The first topic concerns the properties of substances and has several parts.

### Part One — What Makes Physical Bodies Substances

#### Introduction — The Nature of the Natural Body

As you learned at the beginning of this journey, the realities of things are their particular existences: the actual forms and individual identities of things. Essences are universal ideas that correspond to these identities in the outside world. In the mind, essences can be universal and shared. To exist outside the mind means that an essence truly applies to particular existences. In themselves, essences are neither existent nor nonexistent, neither universal nor particular.

When we say that an essence has existence, we mean that the mind describes it through the general, self-evident idea of existence. We do not mean that some actual existence is lodged in the essence; as you have learned many times, that is impossible. In short, existence itself is real in the outside world. It can be prior or later, cause or effect. Every cause is higher, stronger, and more complete in existence than its effect, while every effect is lower and more limited than its cause.

On the side of causes, the chain of existence reaches a height of greatness, power, and purity that encompasses every level and realm. Nothing is absent from this existence, and no particle in the heavens or on earth escapes its knowledge, which is its very being. On the side of effects, by contrast, existence becomes so limited that a thing is not even fully present to itself. This is extended existence, with dimensions in space and time. It has so little unity and fullness that its existence is mixed with nonexistence, its presence with absence, and its unity with multiplicity. It is like a body: each part is absent from the others, and the same is true of the parts within each part. In the end, the whole is absent from itself. This is the lowest level of existence among substances.

Existence cannot stop at any cause, since every existent is the source of an effect, and every effect is another, weaker form of existence. If this chain did not end in something whose existence included a measure of nonexistence, it would have no end at all. Then prime matter—the pure capacity and possibility to receive forms—could not exist, though, as you will learn, it must exist. This means that bodily form, though dark and limited, is still one level of the light of existence.

If the chain of existence did not reach this level, its absence would be an evil, and its not being brought into existence would be a withholding of generosity by the Creator. That cannot be true of the source of overflowing goodness, which is free of limitation and possibility, as reason shows. Also, if this dark substance did not receive existence, the flow of existence would stop at a finite number of beings. Yet proofs show that the series of things that depend on one another in existence cannot end at a finite point. The door of mercy and generosity would then be closed to successive beings in time, especially human souls, which arise within a chain of causes and conditions.

Further, if the creative act did not reach bodily substance, all possible beings would have to be intellects. For everything besides intellects—souls, natures, forms, and accidents such as quantity, quality, place, and time—can exist only with a body or through a body. These and other philosophical principles show by reason that extended substances exist. This is so even before we consider what the senses tell us: between surfaces and boundaries there is an extended substance that supports sensible qualities. This extended substance is the body in the category of substance. The word body is also used, in a different sense, for a kind of quantity, as we will explain. In the first sense, philosophers define a body by saying that it is a substance in which three dimensions can be imagined.

Avicenna is said to have been unsure whether this definition of body is a true definition or only a description. Fakhr al-Din al-Razi rejected it as a definition. He argued that neither substance nor the ability to have dimensions can be a part of the essence of body. First, substance cannot be the broader kind of body, he said, for several reasons he had already given. Substance means “what exists without being in a subject,” but existence cannot be part of an essence. Nor can “not being in a subject” be part of an essence, since it is a negation, and negations do not belong to the realities of existing things. Also, if substance were a broader kind, it would need a defining feature. If that defining feature were an accident, substance would be made up of an accident; if it were itself a substance, it would need another defining feature, and so on without end.[^V05-1]

Second, the ability to receive, imagine, or allow dimensions is a relation, not something that exists in its own right outside the mind. If it did exist, it would have to be in a subject, since it is an accident. Then that subject would need another capacity to receive it, and that capacity another, without end. In this chain, each capacity would depend on an earlier one. This kind of endless sequence is impossible, especially since the chain is confined between two things: the capacity itself and the subject that has it.

Here is the answer to the first objection. As we have already explained, al-Razi and many others assumed that “what exists without being in a subject” is the definition of substance. They took it to mean something that already exists and has no subject. Their objections to substance being a broader kind depend on this assumption. But the most general kinds have no definitions because they are simple, and nothing simple has a definition. What is given as the definition of substances is a set of properties and consequences that can serve as a description. The fact that a description is not a broader kind does not show that the thing described is not a broader kind.

As for the second objection, al-Tusi says in his commentary on *The Directives* that the defining feature is not “able to have dimensions,” since that phrase cannot be predicated of body. The defining feature, he says, is “the able thing,” which can be predicated. But this is not an answer. In definitions, terms such as “able” should be understood through their simple meaning. The difference between such a term and the noun from which it is derived is a matter of how we consider it. “White,” taken simply as white, is as simple as whiteness. If we take “white” to mean something that must have no other feature with it, it is an accident and cannot be predicated. If we take it without that condition, it can be predicated as an incidental feature. The same applies to a part of a definition: “rational,” when considered as a constituent of a definition, is not predicated; considered without that condition, it is a predicable defining feature. So it is not right to distinguish a derived term from its source by saying that one exists and the other does not.

A better answer is that most defining features in definitions do not mean the words themselves, but the underlying features that can only be expressed through those words. For example, “rational” in the definition of a human being means the power of reason, the capacity from which understanding universal ideas arises. It does not mean the act of understanding itself, which is a relation and cannot belong essentially to the category of substance. The words used as defining features stand in for the underlying features they point to.

This is supported by Avicenna’s statement in the Metaphysics of *The Healing*: when sensation is included in the definition of animal, it is not truly the defining feature but a sign of it. The defining feature is that the animal has a soul that perceives and moves by choice. The animal’s essence is not the act of sensing, imagining, or moving by choice; rather, its soul is the source of all of these.

Perhaps those who define body as “long, wide, and deep” mean something similar, since we have shown that these are accidents. Bodily form can exist without any particular line or surface: a sphere and a hollow ring, for example, have no straight lines or flat surfaces.

An oval or lens-shaped body can also lack both straight lines and flat surfaces. A cube has no curved surfaces. And though no body can in reality lack all dimensions, the mind can imagine one without deciding whether its dimensions are limited. To imagine an unlimited body is still to imagine a body, together with the additional feature of having no boundary. If someone imagines a body spread in three directions without also imagining or deciding that it has an end, that person has still imagined a body and has not left out anything needed to understand what body is. Its having a limit is not essential to its meaning.

Someone who imagines an unlimited body has not made a mistake in imagining the two simple ideas involved. The mistake comes only in joining them in an assertion. If having a limit were part of the essence of body, then in this example the person would not have imagined the essence of body, though in fact they have. This will also show, as we will prove later, that bodily substance differs from geometrical body, which belongs to quantity. The two cannot be separated in existence, so their difference cannot be proved by showing that one can exist without the other. It is already clear that body cannot be defined as something that actually has these dimensions, or as something that actually receives them.

### A Closer Examination

Someone might defend the definition by saying that a body cannot lack the possibility of having dimensions and lines. These lines must either be imagined within the body’s own continuity, meaning that continuity is already present, or else they would be imagined in something other than continuity. But whatever is continuous in itself, whether it is prime matter or something else, must already be connected for dimensions to be imagined in it. Otherwise the imagined dimensions would have no basis.

So the connection must exist before the lines are imagined. If it did not, imagining dimensions would be impossible, contrary to what we have assumed. And if imagining the dimensions depends on the connection already existing, the connection cannot itself depend on our ability to imagine them, or both would depend on each other. This would be circular. Therefore, the connections in many directions must exist in the body before we imagine lines; they must belong to the body and cannot be separated from it.

The answer is that the body has one connection of its own, which makes it possible to imagine intersecting lines. If this is what you mean by the connection that exists before dimensions are imagined, you are right: this one connection is the body. But if you mean that many distinct directions already exist in the body, one for each possible line, that is wrong for two reasons. First, if the directions already existed in the same number as the lines we could imagine, there would have to be infinitely many directions, since we can imagine infinitely many lines.

Second, a direction is only the endpoint of a pointing, as we will explain. A line determines a direction; without a line, that direction would not exist as that particular direction. So it is right to say that connection existed before we imagined lines, and that we now describe it as extending in this direction or lying in that direction. It is not right to say that this particular direction existed before we imagined it. When a line appears on a surface, the surface was connected before the line was imagined, but that line was not there yet.

Someone might then object: if the body’s lengthwise connections exist only as possibilities, then the corresponding separations also exist only as possibilities. The body is therefore potentially connected and potentially separated. But what is only potential does not exist; so the body is neither connected nor separated. That is impossible.[^V05-2]

The answer is that “connection” is used in two different senses: for lines and magnitudes, and for the connecting form that makes a body a body. The line-like connections are potential; the bodily form is actual.

The lines are potential in the body, but the form that makes it a body is actually present. This form is part of what makes the nature of body what it is. So we have established that a body does not have to receive dimensions through a capacity that comes after the body and what it receives already exist. Nor must the body actually contain dimensions. Otherwise a sphere would not be a body. What we mean is that it has the basis for receiving dimensions, as we also learned when discussing the definition of substance as “what exists without being in a subject.”

Another approach: some later philosophers found it difficult to defend the traditional definition of body, so they changed it to: “a substance that is long, wide, and deep.”

There are problems with this. Each of these words has several different meanings. “Length” can mean any line, as in Euclid; the longer of two lines bordering a surface; the largest of three perpendicular dimensions, whether lines or not; the first dimension imagined; the distance from an animal’s head to its feet or tail; or the distance between heaven and earth. “Width” can mean the surface itself, the shorter of two dimensions, the second dimension imagined, or the distance from right to left. “Depth” can mean the thickness between two surfaces, the third imagined dimension perpendicular to length and width, the distance measured from above—whose opposite may be called thickness—or the body itself.

As you know, a body does not have to contain any line, as with a sphere at rest. Nor does being a body require that it have a surface, even though no body exists without one. It need not have many surfaces or lines: a sphere, oval, or lens-shaped form may have none of these straight features. Nor must the surfaces or dimensions in a body differ in size; a cube is an example. A cube does not stop being a body if its six bounding surfaces are removed. Likewise, a body's being a body does not depend on its being between heaven and earth or inside the outermost sphere, even though every body must either contain or be contained by another.

It follows that actual dimensions, in any of these senses, are neither part of the essence of body nor a necessary feature of body as body. No body in existence must have them in every sense of the words. We should therefore interpret the description in light of the earlier account: a body is a substance in which one can draw or imagine a line in any first direction, called length; another line perpendicular to it at a point, called width; and a third line perpendicular to both at their point of intersection, called depth. No fourth dimension can be perpendicular to all three at that same point.

So when the definition calls body “long, wide, and deep,” it means that body is of the kind we have just described. Other dimensions and surfaces, whether actual or imagined, are not part of what makes it a body. They are additional features, necessary or incidental, that follow from body, as we will explain. The word “connection” is also used for both the form of body and for geometrical magnitudes; it is shared between them, as we will explain.

### A Further Point

You have learned that describing body as long, wide, and deep means the same as the account just given. Since this is not clear from the words themselves, later philosophers changed the definition to say that a body is something in which three dimensions can be imagined, each at right angles to the others. They added three conditions: possibility, imagination, and perpendicularity.

They added “possibility” because actual dimensions are neither part of body’s essence nor necessary for it. Without this condition, the definition would apply only to bodies that actually have dimensions and would exclude a body in which none had ever been imagined. Adding the condition means that every body, even if it lacks actual dimensions, can have them imagined in it.

Al-Razi quotes Avicenna as saying that the possibility here is general possibility. It includes dimensions that must exist, as in the celestial spheres; dimensions that exist but are not necessary, as in the elemental bodies; and dimensions that do not exist but could, as in an imagined perfect sphere. If possibility meant only the readiness of something that does not yet exist, someone might object: once one or all of these dimensions actually exist in a body, the possibility that was part of its definition is gone. The body would then no longer fit its own definition.

There are several answers. First, the dimensions can be imagined in infinitely many ways. Even when one or three are actually present, the body remains able to have other dimensions imagined in it, since it can be divided without end. Any one point among the infinitely many possible points can be imagined as the meeting place of three perpendicular dimensions. If possibility means a real readiness that exists while the thing is not yet actual, then the existence of one or three dimensions does not end every such readiness. It ends only the readiness for those particular dimensions.

The possibility here is not a mere absence. It is a real capacity, as we have explained. When one particular capacity is fulfilled, that does not eliminate the general capacity.

The readiness meant here is something real, not simply the denial that a dimension must be present. Losing one particular readiness does not remove every readiness for the same kind of dimension. A denial, by contrast, is ended as soon as what it denies occurs: the statement that no motion occurs today is overturned by one instance of motion, but the possibility of motion is not ended by the occurrence of one movement.

Second, in Avicenna’s account, “something in which dimensions can be imagined” describes the person doing the imagining, not the body itself. Even if the imagined dimensions became actual in the body and the body lost its readiness for those exact dimensions, the person’s ability to imagine other dimensions would remain.

Third, even if the possibility belongs to the body, it is ended by imagining the dimensions, not merely by the dimensions’ actual existence. These are not the same thing.

Besides, “possibility” does not have only the two meanings of general possibility and readiness. It can also mean that something can actually occur in reality, whether or not it has a prior readiness. The phrase “can be imagined” was added to include the celestial sphere, whose dimensions are necessary rather than arising from a material readiness. And possibility is still needed, since the dimensions may never actually be present, even at any time. If we left out the possibility of imagining them, the definition would imply that body ceases to be a body when no one imagines dimensions in it. That is impossible.

“Imagining” here does not mean making up something impossible. It means the kind of rational supposition used in mathematics. This keeps the definition from applying to separate, immaterial substances. The condition that the dimensions be perpendicular to one another excludes surfaces, since in a surface only two lines—not three—can meet at right angles.

We have not yet established the way surfaces exist or proved that they must be accidents. So the most general definition of body, when people disagree about what it is, must use terms they can accept in common. Otherwise they might as well define body using a single dimension, since extended substance is limited to body. But this can be known only after proving what body is, which is the very question under discussion.

The three perpendicular dimensions are included either to rule out the view of some Mu‘tazilites, who say bodies are made of substantial surfaces, or to give a complete picture of the essence of body and show that body can have dimensions in this arrangement, even if it can also have other dimensions not at right angles. This also makes clear that a cube does not get its bodily nature from the dimensions it happens to have, nor from the possibility of having those particular dimensions. Its bodily nature does not disappear when those dimensions change or when that possibility is fulfilled. What matters in the description is that someone could imagine a dimension within the body's depth, another at right angles to it, and a third at right angles to both. This remains true whether the dimensions are actually there, whether anyone is imagining them, or whether anyone has in fact imagined them.

### A Difficulty and Its Solution

Someone might object to this description for three reasons. First, it also applies to prime matter, which can receive dimensions through bodily forms. The ability to receive dimensions through an intermediary is narrower than the general ability to receive them; anything that has the narrower ability also has the broader one.

Second, the imagination can have three dimensions in this way, as do the geometrical quantities that exist in it, but imagination itself is not a natural body.

Third, possibility and its related ideas are not real features in the outside world. They are descriptions without independent existence. If a definition can be given through what does not exist, then we could define simple things that can only be known through their properties in the same way. But body is a compound essence: it belongs under the broader kind of substance and has a defining feature. It is also made up of matter and form, and a compound in existence must be compound in essence.

Here is the answer to the first objection. Dimensions are accidents that exist in body because body is their subject. Prime matter does not exist independently in itself, ready to receive something that can only belong to a thing once that thing is fully constituted—whether the reception is direct or through an intermediary. Prime matter can exist only by receiving what completes it, makes it actual, and gives it an external, specific essence.

If “through an intermediary” means that the feature is attributed through a relation, rather than that the intermediary is a real cause of its existence, then it is true that prime matter receives dimensions through bodily forms. It is like saying that someone sitting in a boat moves indirectly. But this kind of attribution is figurative. It is not like saying that water becomes hot through the fire, where the intermediary truly causes the quality. Definitions must apply directly to the things they define and not directly to things outside that kind. An indirect or figurative application to something else does not invalidate the definition. This resolves many objections of the same kind. Aristotle defined the continuous as what can be imagined to have parts meeting at a shared boundary, and described the moist as what can easily take on shapes, and the dry as what can do so with difficulty. People may think that prime matter is a counterexample to all of these definitions. The same distinction answers each objection.

Another answer is that body exists outside the mind as two parts: matter and form. Matter is the part that gives body its capacity to receive things, while form is what makes those things actual. Form does not contribute to the capacity to receive.

But you may say that only matter is receptive, whereas form contributes nothing to that capacity. If so, the definition seems wrong in both directions: it applies to matter when matter is joined to bodily form, yet does not apply to the complete body, since the form is not what receives the dimensions.

We reply that “receiving” here means simply having a feature, whether or not this involves being changed or affected over time. That kind of changing readiness belongs especially to matter, but the broader sense of having a feature does not. Separate beings have features of perfection without undergoing change. So having the three dimensions imagined in it does not require matter to be changed or affected. If body were only bodily form, without prime matter, it would still be able to have dimensions imagined in it. This is why some people who deny prime matter have defined body in that way.

Or we can say that the possibility of receiving dimensions belongs to body, even if the actual capacity to receive them comes from one of its two parts. For example, the possibility of becoming human belongs to semen, though semen is not a human being. So the possibility of receiving dimensions is a feature that necessarily belongs to body without requiring that its actual reception arise from the body's whole essence. Actual passive reception, which requires change, belongs to matter.

As for the second objection, “receiving dimensions,” “being able to imagine them,” and “being possible” refer to the ordinary external world in which we live, speak, teach, and learn. Imagination may contain many things, dimensions, and great bodies, but they exist in a different way from this world. The dimensions of this world are the ones we can point to with our senses. The world of imagination is another world; its heavens, earth, dimensions, bodies, individuals, and qualities differ in nature from those of this world. The Qur'an points to this when it says: “On the day the earth is changed into another earth.”

A grave in this world may be the same for everyone, while in the next it may be a garden or a fire. Ablution here is ablution; there it is a heavenly maiden. Struggle here is struggle; there it is light. A house here is lifeless; in the next world it is alive. Let us leave this kind of argument aside: people have grown deaf to hearing it, blind to seeing another realm, and their hearts are clouded by anger and hostility toward those who believe in it and live by it.

The answer to the third objection is that the definition is not given by “receiving” itself, since that word cannot be predicated, nor by “what receives” simply as a receiver, which would be only a conceptual description. Instead, the definition describes a particular kind of being: a substance that can have dimensions imagined in it, or in which dimensions exist as something that can be imagined. The word “which” here carries the meaning of existence.

Defining something through a way of existing is not defining it through a merely conceptual feature. Existence is what is real in itself, not a universal essence. The same is true of many definitions of real things: an animal is a body capable of sensation and movement; a human being is an animal capable of understanding universal ideas—that is, of grasping and imagining universal meanings.

What al-Razi says applies to simple and compound things alike. We cannot define real things only through negations, whether they are simple or compound. A definition is meant to give the mind something to grasp, while negation only removes something from thought.

#### Chapter 1 — Different Views on Bodily Substance and How It Exists

No reasonable person disputes that body exists as a substance with what allows it to have length, width, and depth in some sense. But it is not self-evident whether body is connected in itself, whether it is simple or made from two substances, or whether it is made from a substance and an accident. That is why people disagree about how body exists.

Some say it is made of substantial units that have position but cannot be divided in any way—not in imagination, by supposition, by cutting, or by breaking. They split into two groups. One says there are infinitely many such indivisible units in every body, even in a mustard seed. This was the view of al-Nazzam and his Mu‘tazilite followers. The majority of theologians say that the number of such units is finite.

Others say that body is continuous in itself. Most philosophers say that it can be divided without end. The author of *The Book of Religions and Sects* says it can be divided only a finite number of times, until it reaches something indivisible. Democritus, one of the early philosophers, says that body can be divided only by external separation—by breaking or cutting. In his view, an individual body is so small and hard that its smallness and hardness prevent either kind of division.

Those who say body can be divided without end are themselves divided into three groups. One group says body is a simple substance, extended in three directions and connected in itself by a substantial, self-subsisting continuity. This is the well-known view of Plato and the Stoics, as well as those who follow them, such as the martyr and philosopher Shihab al-Din Yahya al-Suhrawardi in *The Wisdom of Illumination*.

A second group says body is made of two substances: one is the form of continuity, and the other is the substance that receives it. These are the followers of the First Teacher, Aristotle, and their successors among the Muslim philosophers, such as Abu Nasr al-Farabi and Abu Ali ibn Sina.

A third group says that body is a compound, but of a substance that receives and an accident, namely measurable extension. This is the view of the Illuminationist philosopher in *The Tablets of Light* and *The Throne*. Some readers criticized him because they thought the two books contradicted each other. In one, he called body simple and its magnitude substantial; in the other, he said body is made of a substance, which he calls matter, and an accident, which is magnitude. He had also allowed one natural species to be composed of substance and accident.

The commentators on his books—Muhammad al-Shahrazuri, author of *The History of the Philosophers*; Ibn Kammunah, commentator on *The Tablets of Light*; and al-Shirazi, commentator on *The Wisdom of Illumination*—all say that the two accounts do not contradict each other in their intended meaning. They explain that the difference is in how the terms are used.

When a candle changes shape, two kinds of magnitude are involved. One remains fixed and does not grow or shrink as the candle takes on different shapes; this is a substance. The other changes as the dimensions extend in different directions; it is an accident in the first, substantial magnitude. Together they make up the body. In the terminology of *The Tablets of Light*, the substance is matter; in the terminology of *The Wisdom of Illumination*, the substantial extension is body. Relative to the forms and specific kinds that shape it, this extension is called matter. So there is no contradiction between calling body simple and its magnitude substantial in one book, and calling body compound and its magnitude accidental in the other. The body and extension meant in each book are different. The appearance of a contradiction comes from using the same words in different senses.

### Discussion and a Further Point

The great Illuminationist philosopher speaks clearly in some parts of his larger work, *The Path* and *The Discussions*, against any continuity or extension other than the kind that belongs to quantity. He rejects the continuity that makes body what it is. In *The Tablets of Light*, however, he gives no indication that what he calls matter is a substantial extension that extends by itself or a magnitude that exists independently. Instead, he gives it the properties of matter as the philosophers in Aristotle’s school understand it: something simpler than body as body, and able to serve as a broader kind for simple or compound kinds of body. He says that body contains something that can receive both continuity and separation, while continuity itself can receive neither. So the receiver must be something else. He also says that extension is part of the essence of body; otherwise we would not need it in order to understand body. The receiver of extension, which he calls matter, is another part of body.

This clearly shows that his account of matter in this book agrees with that of most philosophers. But in *The Wisdom of Illumination*, he plainly says that what is called matter is a substantial magnitude that exists independently. The contradiction between the two books therefore remains, both over whether magnitude is substantial or accidental and over whether body is simple or compound.

The matter he describes in one book could be body, since he says it is connected in itself and is a substantial magnitude. Body is complete in just this sense, or through something that follows from it. But the matter he establishes in the other book can only be a part of body. Body cannot be complete or even understood without something like an extended form; a mere receiver of extension is not enough.

You will later see the difference between extension and continuity as the philosophers in Aristotle’s school use the term for what makes body what it is, and continuity as a kind of quantity that belongs to body. The Illuminationist philosopher rejects the first meaning altogether and takes it to be body itself. This is a clear account of the different views people have held about body in general. We will now give reasons for the philosophers’ view that body is made of two substances: a material substance and a formal one, God willing.

#### Chapter 2 — The Continuity That Makes Body What It Is

The first thing you need to understand is the meaning of continuity and of what is continuous, both when these words name a real feature in itself and when they describe a relation to something else.

There are two kinds of continuity that are real in themselves. The first is a thing’s being such that, by its very nature and essence, three intersecting dimensions can be drawn from it, without deciding how large they are or where, among the levels of quantity, they fall. In this sense, one continuous thing is not greater or smaller than another, nor equal to it. A continuity of this kind is not part of another continuity. It is neither an equal nor a measure, nor something measured or counted; it is not a root or a square, neither separate nor less or greater than another.

In this sense, continuity is a defining feature that divides the category of substance, and it belongs to body in itself, as we will prove. Once we have shown that body is not made from indivisible substantial units with position, body itself can be called continuous and extended, apart from all accidents and external features. Its continuity is not some additional thing that makes it continuous or gives us a subject of which to predicate continuity. This is true whether body is only an extended form, as one of the two great philosophers held, or is made of an extended form and another substance that receives it, as the other held.

You might say: if body is continuous in itself, then we can imagine one part here and another there, and divide it into measurable parts. It would then be a kind of quantity, since this kind of divisibility belongs to continuous quantity itself and to things through it.

I answer that being continuous and extended in itself does not yet mean being divisible into measurable parts. That becomes possible only when its quantity and size are determined. Unless the extent of its spread is set—either at a particular limit or without limit—we cannot distinguish one definite part from another, either in reality or in imagination. We cannot divide it by breaking, cutting, imagining, or noticing two features that remain in place, such as two colored patches, or two changing features, such as two points of alignment or parallel position. All these ways of dividing require a determinate magnitude, whether the thing divided is magnitude itself or something else, such as matter or body.

By its nature, body is only extension in all directions, without any definite size or measure. The quantity and measure of that extension come after continuity itself. We know that the quantity of continuity is not the same as continuity, because we can understand the nature of body without thinking about any specific magnitude or number. But in the outside world, body must have some determinate magnitude and number. These are requirements of its existence, not parts of its essence or requirements of its existence in the mind.

Avicenna says in *The Notes*: “When we say ‘a part of a body,’ we mean a part of the body’s magnitude. Body, just as body, is neither a part nor a whole. Likewise, when we say ‘two bodies out of five,’ we mean two of the five numbers applied to bodies, not that body as body is one or many.”

He also says in *The Healing*: “Body, in its true sense, is the form of continuity that can be imagined to have three dimensions. This is different from magnitude and from geometrical body. A body, insofar as it has this form, is not different from another body by being larger or smaller, nor is it equal, counted, used as a measure, a square, a root, a partner, or separate from it. These features belong to it only insofar as it has a measure and a dimension. This is a different way of considering body from the one we have described.”

The second kind of real continuity is when a thing has parts with different imagined positions, and there are shared boundaries between them, each boundary being the end of one part and the beginning of another. A feature of continuity in this sense is that it can be divided without end. It is one of the defining features of quantity and makes up every kind of quantity other than number, whether that quantity is fixed or changing, and whether it can be divided in every direction or only some directions.

Both meanings of continuity are real. Avicenna uses “continuous” for both: the first is the defining feature that makes body a substance; the second is the defining feature that makes quantity continuous. In the Metaphysics of *The Healing*, while explaining that magnitudes are accidents, he says: “As for continuous quantities, they are continuous dimensions.”

He also says: “Geometrical body is the magnitude of continuity, which is body in the sense of form.” This shows that, for him, continuity in any directions and at any size or extent makes body what it is and belongs in its definition. The measure of this continuity, however, is a kind of quantity. It is also called body, but in a different sense. We sometimes distinguish the substance by calling it natural body, and the accident that exists in it geometrical body: the first belongs to natural philosophy, and the second to mathematics.

Continuity can also describe a relation between things, in two senses. First, a magnitude or something with a magnitude may have the same endpoint as another, whether the two are real or imagined. In this sense we say that one is continuous with the other.

Second, one body may move along with the movement of another, and we say that it is continuous with it in this sense. The first relation belongs to continuous quantity. The second belongs to discrete quantity in general, as when the two lines of an angle meet, or to things in matter, as when the parts of a body join, or flesh joins to sine and sine to bone. In short, it applies to anything that touches something else and is not easily separated from it.

To sum up: the light of understanding has made clear that a natural body, simply as a natural body, extends and spreads in three directions. It has this by its own nature, as something that makes up its essence. At this level, it is neither limited nor unlimited in how far it extends. Either possibility, if it can occur, is a feature of its existence, not part of its essence, as we have explained.

So in this respect and at this level, one body is neither equal to nor larger or smaller than another. Once its dimensions are determined, however, it can be divided into parts that meet at shared boundaries. It then has continuity in the sense that makes quantity continuous and makes equality and difference in size possible.

Do not imagine that there are two extensions and two things that extend, one substantial and one accidental. There is only one thing extended in itself. Considered without any fixed measure, it is the substance that makes natural body what it is; at that level, it cannot be measured. Considered with a fixed extent, it can be measured once or many times, within or without limits if that is possible. In this respect it is geometrical body.

Surface, too, can be considered in two ways. In one sense it is the boundary of natural body and is not a kind of quantity, though it is an accident outside the essence of body. In the other sense, it is the boundary of geometrical body and a specific kind of fixed magnitude.

The same is true of a line. We can see the difference between the continuity that makes a body one substance and the measurable size it happens to have by considering what remains when one and the same wax candle changes shape—when it is rounded or made into a cube. The individual continuity in the first sense remains, as does the area measured in the second sense. The latter is a specific kind of geometrical body, since every level of quantity is a complete kind, distinct from the level above or below it. When the shape changes but the area stays the same, different individuals of equal quantity replace one another, because equality means sameness in quantity.

This shows that geometrical body is an accident in natural body. In the ordinary course of nature, they can be separated in the mind but not in existence. Both can likewise be separated from matter in imagination, but not in existence. What depends on matter in imagination and in reality is the particular change and response of each. The nature of these ideas will become clear in the chapters that follow, God willing.

#### Chapter 3 — How all extensions and continuities depend on continuous substance

Reason, by the nature God gave it, rejects the idea that magnitude or continuous quantity could belong to something that is not itself extended and continuous. As Avicenna says in his Persian book *Danishnama*: “A body is continuous by its very nature. If it were disconnected, it could not have dimensions.”

Dimensions, whether in time or space, depend for their existence on something continuous in itself, apart from any fixed extent or measured spread. Its nature is not itself a magnitude. If it were already made up of separate things, then, after continuous quantity was added to it, either those things would remain separate or they would not. If they did remain separate, the continuous thing would itself be separate: many underlying things would make the quantity belonging to them many as well. But then it would not be continuous at all, contrary to what we assumed.

The arrangement found in an army and similar groups is not an external feature belonging to each individual soldier. It exists in the mind, when the mind considers them together as one thing. So fixed magnitudes are features of continuous substance: sometimes directly, as with geometrical body, and sometimes through something else, as with surface and then line. Continuous movement along a distance can likewise be measured by time, because it is continuous across that distance. All kinds of continuous quantity depend for their existence on continuous substance itself.

Strictly speaking, even separate quantity—the number of individuals belonging to one kind—depends on dividing continuous substance. Multiplicity can belong to a kind that is one only if that kind can also exist as a connected whole. If multiplicity were part of its nature, or necessarily followed from it, then every individual would be many individuals, and there could be no single individual at all. That is absurd.

If neither unity nor multiplicity necessarily belongs to the kind, then it must be able to exist either as one connected thing or as many separate things. You have already learned that what makes continuity possible is not something outside the nature of natural body; it belongs to body as continuous substance. You will soon see that something else in the body makes separation possible: its formal part prepares it to be divided. So the quantitative parts of every kind depend for their existence on substantial continuity. Keep this principle in mind.

#### Chapter 4 — The different ways of dividing magnitude into parts

There are four kinds of division.

The first is actual division in the outside world. It produces a real plurality of parts.

The second is division in the imagination. It produces several parts in the mind, but only a finite number are imagined at once, even though more parts can always be imagined.

The third is division by the intellect. It takes in, all at once and in a general way, every division that could possibly be made. This is how the intellect grasps the many possible instances of a universal essence.

The fourth is division based on a difference between two features of a thing. These features may be fixed or changing. A difference between fixed features can bring about actual separation; a difference between changing features lets the mind attribute separation according to the thing’s actual state.

When division of any of these kinds occurs in a body, it is the continuous form that is divided, at the level of its geometrical magnitude. But actual separation requires the matter to be prepared for it: matter is what receives division and remains through it, as you will learn. In truth, therefore, actual separation belongs to matter, even though the body’s form prepares it for division through its geometrical size.

Imagined division belongs to geometrical magnitudes simply as magnitudes, without any special preparation in the matter. Intellectual division belongs to them as continuous in the first sense we discussed: as one continuous substance.

The word “part” is used in several ways. It can mean what a thing is made from, what it can be divided into, or one of its measurable parts. These meanings are related only loosely, by shared wording or figurative use. Measurable parts are not truly parts of a continuous whole. They are called parts because, if we imagine that a continuous thing had parts as distinct individuals with their own positions—not as part of its nature—then the measurable pieces we call parts would be the closest fit.

Their existence comes after the existence of the whole, so they are not truly parts that make up its essence. Each measurable part is one by its own continuous unity, and is called a part only in relation to a number of other connected units. That is why parts and the whole share a definition, a name, a concept, and the name “body.” Remember this; it will help you answer Democritus’s view.

#### Chapter 5 — Proving that body is one continuous thing, and how this reveals the nature of bodily form

Know that the science that establishes what things are and how they exist is universal science, or first philosophy, not natural science.[^V05-3] In the same way, created realities can be brought into existence only by active intellects, not by bodily powers that receive and undergo change. Knowledge is the form and reality of what is known.

When a person’s knowledge grows strong and the eye of insight becomes clear, that person sees that God, exalted is His name, is the agent and the end of all things. His power reaches into things, and His light flows through them. Every question about what exists is ultimately answered by knowledge of God. Whoever truly knows God comes through that knowledge to know all things, both universal and particular. Any knowledge of a universal or particular thing belongs to divine science, because it comes from studying existence itself, what follows from it, its features, and the features of those features, until we reach particular things.

This is knowledge of particular things through universal principles: the kind of knowledge called divine science, or first philosophy. But human minds cannot take in the remote, higher causes that lie far above particular things in this lower world, and far below the First Truth in rank. So we cannot know changing particulars through those higher causes, let alone through the Cause of all causes. This is why the sciences are arranged in levels. A lower science takes its starting points from the findings of a higher one, and each science has its own work to do.

The friends of God and those who know Him deeply, however, take God as the source of their knowledge of natural, mathematical, and divine things, just as He is the source of their existence. It is related that the Commander of the Faithful said: “I have seen nothing without seeing God before it.” This is what the verse points to: “Is it not enough that your Lord is witness to all things?”[^V05-4]

In short, we can have certain knowledge of things that have causes only by knowing their causes. There are four: the agent, the end, the form, and the matter. Some things have all four causes. Others have only the first two; the sciences that deal with these are called the sciences of separate beings. Natural science studies things with all four causes when they are particular things; when they are not particular in this way, the study belongs to mathematics.

The nature of body in general, the existence of matter and form, their particular forms, and the way they necessarily go together are all questions for divine science. These questions concern body in general, not matter or form in themselves. Since a body’s continuity and endless divisibility cannot be reconciled with its being made from indivisible units that have position, the question of whether such indivisible substances can exist belongs to this science, not to natural science. It is mentioned at the start of natural science only as a principle to be examined there.

The exception is when natural arguments establish that bodies are continuous by looking at their movements, powers, or connected actions. One and the same question can belong to two sciences in different ways. For example, the rotation of the heavens is proved in mathematics from its visible effects, through a geometrical demonstration; in natural science it is proved from causes, through an explanation of nature, since a simple nature produces uniform effects.

Likewise, something may first be shown to exist from the principles proper to one science. Its existence can then serve as the subject of further questions about its properties and states. Those properties, along with what follows from them, may in turn help us see what the thing is and prove its existence again in a clearer way. This often happens when we consider the same thing from different angles.

With that in place, let us say this. If a substance occupies a place and has a position, it must have a side facing upward and another facing downward, and likewise sides facing each pair of opposite directions. It must therefore be divisible, at least in the imagination. It has different parts according to whether it faces each side of the world—in reality, in the imagination, or in thought.

You may object that one indivisible thing could have different alignments and relations to things outside it without becoming many in its own nature. At most, its boundaries, ends, and accidents would be many. A change in accidents does not always mean a change in the thing itself.

I answer that it is impossible for there to be different alignments and relations without any kind of difference in the thing described by them. If a thing actually touches several different things, and can be divided, each of its parts meets one of them rather than another. The part touching one cannot be the very same part touching the other. So differences and multiplicity in spatial relations require differences in both the thing being related and the things it is related to.

For this reason, the philosophers count position among the features that are many and make things many by their very nature: position entails difference, even if only in the imagination. Just as one thing, taken as one, cannot have several positions, it cannot have several different relations to things in different positions without some multiplicity in itself to make those relations possible.

It is even more absurd to allow one thing with a position to have several boundaries and ends without any plurality or multiplicity in it, whether in reality or even in the imagination.

The philosophers have strong arguments against the idea that bodies are made from indivisible atoms, and for the existence of body as one continuous substance. Their arguments take several forms: some begin from the different directions and boundaries of a body in space; some from geometrical figures; some from movement and alignment; and some from shadows.

The first approach includes two well-known arguments. They are simple and do not depend on proving the existence of circles, spheres, triangles, movement, or time.

Suppose an indivisible atom lies between two other atoms. If it prevents the two outer atoms from touching, it must be divided: it touches each outer atom on a different side. If it does not prevent them from touching, then the middle atom’s presence makes no difference. The same reasoning applies to every atom placed between others. Then nothing would act as a barrier in the world, and there would be no size or volume. But atoms cannot occupy the same place.

The second argument imagines an atom placed above two other atoms, at the point where they meet. If it touches both of them, either wholly or partly, then it can be divided. If it touches one of them wholly and not the other, then it is not at the point where they meet, though we assumed that it was. If it touches part of each, then all three atoms must be divided. One can distinguish ten possible arrangements here, as some careful thinkers have shown.

Another argument begins by joining one part to another. Either they meet completely, so that the two occupy no more space than one; then joining parts creates no size or magnitude at all, and there is no body. Or they meet only partly. In that case, the joined thing has two ends, which means it can be divided. This argument is easier than the first two for showing that a body is not composed of these atoms, though it is not as useful for proving that no indivisible atom can exist at all.

Another argument imagines a thin surface made from indivisible parts, with length and width but no depth. When the sun shines on it, or it falls into a beam of sight, one face is illuminated or seen while the other is not. So it must be divided.

The second approach uses geometrical shapes, angles, and chords. It includes many arguments, as anyone who studies Euclid can see. In my commentary on *Athir al-Din’s Guide to Wisdom*, I set out a number of good geometrical proofs that bodies are continuous and can be divided without end.

#### A Difficulty and Its Answer

A commentator on *The Aims of the Wise* says that Euclid’s proof of these shapes depends on drawing an equilateral triangle, which in turn depends on drawing a circle. But, he says, there is no way to prove such shapes to those who believe in indivisible atoms. The usual way to define a circle is to imagine a fixed straight line turning around one fixed endpoint until it returns to its starting place. This produces a curved line whose points are all at the same distance from the fixed endpoint. But this explanation does not prove anything to atomists: it is only an idea, and does not show that the shape is possible, let alone that it actually exists.

Even if they accepted the circle, the argument would work only if lines and surfaces were not made of indivisible parts, or if those parts could not move in the way described because that would lead to an impossibility. The same difficulty applies to proving the sphere.

Here is the answer. The critic is right that proving circles and spheres by this kind of movement depends on continuous magnitude, as Avicenna and other philosophers also say. The compass draws only an ordinary, approximate circle, not a true one. But movement is not the only way to prove that a circle exists. The philosophers have two other ways, neither of which first assumes that atoms are impossible.

Avicenna proves the sphere in *The Healing* and *The Salvation* by a causal argument. It begins with the fact that bodies have a simple nature, and that simple bodily nature produces the same effects in every direction. Among the shapes of simple bodies, only a sphere fits this uniformity. Other rounded shapes—oval, turnip-shaped, or flattened—differ in their distance from the center and in their dimensions. A simple nature cannot produce such differences.

Once the sphere has been proved, a circle can be shown to exist by cutting or imagining a cross-section through it.

Avicenna then says that atomists, too, must accept the circle. Imagine a roughly circular figure made of atoms, with one point on its edge lower than another. If we lay a straight line from a point at the center to one point on the edge, and then to the lower point, its length will differ. If we compare the lengths by adding indivisible units, the difference can be completed by one or more units. If the difference is not exactly one or more units, then it is smaller than a unit. If the line cannot quite reach the lower point, a gap remains. We can repeat the same comparison within that gap, without end. That would mean that the gaps divide without end, which contradicts the atomists’ view.

So the existence of circles, spheres, and similar shapes does not depend on first denying atoms, even though most theologians who believe in atoms also deny those shapes. For if the circumference of a circle consisted of indivisible atoms, their outer faces would either meet one another just as their inner faces do, or they would not.

If they did, then the inner surface of the circle—the concave side—would have the same area as its outer, convex surface. But these cannot be equal. If you doubt this, consider what follows: the circle around the inner surface, then the next circle outward, and so on, would all have to be equal, until the outermost circle and the one around the center were equal. That is plainly false. The reasoning is simple: if the outer and inner surfaces are equal, each corresponding pair of boundaries must be equal.

If the outer faces did not touch, then the atoms would be separated by gaps. If a gap could not fit an atom, the atoms would have to divide. If it could fit one, the outer surface would be twice the inner surface, which the senses show is false.

The same argument applies to a sphere. So we have shown that whenever circles and spheres exist, indivisible atoms cannot exist. But circles and spheres do exist. Or, put the other way around: if indivisible atoms existed, circles and spheres could not. That conclusion is false.

We should understand that there are two ways to establish truths: proof and debate.[^V05-5] A proof aims to establish the truth, whether or not anyone else accepts it, and so helps the one who proves it reach a fuller understanding. Debate aims to guide people and benefit society by leading people toward the truth and protecting their beliefs from error.[^V05-6]

Many writers have ignored the rules of sound debate. For example, when they try to disprove indivisible atoms by showing that the view leads to a contradiction, they point to the existence of circles. They say that if circles were made of indivisible parts, those parts would have to divide.

The atomist can answer that although division is impossible for him, it is not impossible under an assumption that is itself impossible—namely, that circles exist, since their existence would entail division of an atom. But if the circle is proved from philosophical principles about nature in bodies, and about simple natural bodies producing round shapes through their simple capacities, that proof may be sound in itself but will not convince atomists. In the atomists’ view, those principles rule out an agent who acts by free choice; that is why they refuse to grant them. It is a mistake to try to trap them with an argument built on premises they reject. Logic counts as a fallacy the use, in debate, of a premise the opponent does not accept, even if that premise is true in a strict proof.

The same problem arises in some arguments against indivisible atoms that use shapes other than a square or the right isosceles triangle formed by drawing a diagonal across a square.[^V05-7] Those who deny the continuity of body accept only these figures. So an argument based on some other shape is neither a proof nor a sound debate.

It is not a proof because someone could deny that the impossible result really follows from the assumption. The result may indeed be impossible, but its impossibility does not have to follow from an assumption that is itself impossible. One impossible assumption may lead to another impossible result.

It is not a sound debate either, because the opponent does not accept its premises. They may depend on continuity, or on other philosophical principles. For example, al-Khafri argued from an isosceles triangle whose base is made up of fewer indivisible units than either of its two sides. He compared it to an arc in the sky and to two arcs of the equator and the zodiac that meet at the points marking the spring and autumn equinoxes. The angle between the sides keeps getting smaller until it is the size of one indivisible unit, then smaller still. This would require an indivisible unit to be divided.

A similar problem occurs in Hunayn ibn Ishaq’s argument that if body were made of indivisible atoms, the diameter of the outermost sphere would have to equal three such atoms.

Here is the argument. Imagine three touching lines, each made of indivisible substances, with the middle line running through the diameter of a square. Let the two sides of the square be lines AB and CD. Draw a line AD between two opposite points. It passes through the center and touches the square at two opposite points, even though it also passes through the three connected lines. So its length must be three indivisible units, which is what the argument was meant to show.

The flaw is clear from what we have already said. Although a diameter made of three indivisible units is impossible in reality, the opponent can deny that it follows from the assumption. If body is made of indivisible atoms, and the lines in the argument are made of those atoms, the line through the square cannot pass across all three lines in that way unless the units along the sides and the diagonal are equal.

The same equality would have to hold between the units along the drawn line and the number of units in the three touching lines. Some commentators have strangely said that this argument comes from Avicenna’s *The Sources of Wisdom* and *The Healing*. But Avicenna does not discuss the impossibility of atoms in the metaphysics of *The Healing*, much less this argument. In his works on physics and elsewhere, he instead imagines a surface made from four substantial lines, each of four units. This leads to the conclusion that the diagonal equals the side. That is a good way to confront atomists, since it uses a square, which they accept.

The third approach uses movement and differences in speed, without pauses. For example, imagine two indivisible units moving toward one another, one above each end of a line made of four units. If they move at the same speed, they must meet at the boundary between the second and third units. Or imagine two units moving from the ends of a line made of three units. They will meet in a way that divides all of them.

Another argument begins with the fact that a fast object catches up with a slower one. Suppose both are moving at the same time, with the fast one behind the slow one by a certain distance. When the fast one covers one unit, the slow one must have covered less. Then that unit must be divisible. If it has covered the same distance or more, the fast one will never catch up—which contradicts what we see.

The philosophers also argue from alignment. Everyone knows that the sun’s position can be tracked by the boundary between shadow and light. The shadow moves more slowly than the sun. So when the sun moves one unit, the shadow must move less than one unit. Otherwise, the shadow’s circular path on a small object would be as large as the sun’s path.

They also argue from contact. Imagine a straight ladder leaning against a vertical wall on level ground. The ladder and the two sides of the right angle make a right triangle. Let each side be five cubits long. By the rule for right triangles, traditionally called the Bride’s Figure, the ladder is the square root of fifty cubits. Now slide the bottom of the ladder away from the wall, so that its top descends by one cubit. The bottom must move less than one cubit. If it moved a full cubit, the two sides would be four and six cubits, and the square of the ladder would be fifty-two, even though the ladder remains the square root of fifty. That is impossible. So the bottom moves by less than one cubit.

If we replace the cubits with indivisible units, then when the top moves by one unit, the bottom moves less than one unit. That means a unit must be divided.

The fourth approach uses shadows. If we stick a piece of wood upright in the ground, it must cast a shadow when the sun is on the eastern horizon.

The shadow keeps shrinking until the sun reaches noon. When the sun moves one unit, the shadow must lose some of its length. If it loses a whole unit, the shadow would have to be one quarter of the heavens in length, which is impossible. If it loses less than a unit, then a unit has been divided.

Some have also argued that body must be continuous because, if it were made of indivisible parts, those parts would make up its essence. We would understand them before understanding the body, and their existence would be self-evident to many people. Since none of this is true, they say, the body cannot be made from indivisible parts.

The answer is that this confuses parts that make up an essence with external parts. The properties just described belong to conceptual parts, such as a broader kind and its defining features. Even then, the definition must capture the essence of the thing, which is not always so. External parts, on the other hand, may need to be established through proof. For example, philosophers prove the existence of matter and form. The same is true of conceptual parts if the essence is not understood, as with the claim that the soul belongs to the broader kind of substance.

Someone else argues that if an indivisible part existed, it would have to be finite, and so it would have a shape—either a sphere or a polygon. Either way, it would have boundaries, and boundaries entail division. Division is obvious for a polygon. For a sphere, if spheres are joined together, there must be gaps between them, each smaller than a sphere.

The answer is that shape belongs to something extended in length, width, and depth. An indivisible part has no extension. If it had any extension in one direction, it would be a line; if in all directions, it would be a body. Even if we granted that it had a shape, that would be true of bodies, not of indivisible parts.

Some imagine that the shadow of every body becomes twice its length at some point. Then half the shadow would be the shadow of half the body. If a body whose length is an odd number of indivisible units cast such a shadow, half of it would be the shadow of half its length. The body would be divided, and so would one of its indivisible parts. We answer that the general claim is false: it holds only for things that can be halved.[^V05-8] As with the argument just before it, the mistake is to treat a property of the whole as a property of each indivisible part.[^V05-9]

#### Chapter 6 — A point that disproves al-Nazzam’s view

Al-Nazzam agreed with the philosophers that a body can be divided without end. But he, like other Mu‘tazilites, did not distinguish between division that is possible and division that has already happened. He treated all the possible parts as already actual. That leads to a part that cannot be divided, and so he ends up in the very position he was trying to avoid.

We can disprove his view first by pointing out that there can be a body made from a finite number of parts, even if it exists within a larger body. There is no plurality without units. Take a finite number of them and join them together: they make a volume, since they are measurable parts in different positions.

We can then extend this point to all bodies by comparing the parts of one body with those of other bodies, and comparing their volumes. As the number of parts increases, so does the volume. The ratio between the volumes must therefore match the ratio between the numbers of parts. Since volumes and dimensions are finite, the number of parts in every body must also be finite. If not, the ratio of one finite volume to another would be the ratio of a finite number of parts to an infinite number.

An objection is that the two ratios need not match. The increase in volume may come from an increase in the number of parts, but the relation between volume and number might still be different. For example, as the angle of a triangle widens, its opposite side grows, but not in a constant ratio. The relation between the angle and its opposite side may be an irrational one, found in magnitudes but not in numbers.

The answer is that, since al-Nazzam takes bodies to be made from indivisible units, the units in any two bodies have a common measure: one indivisible part. Their ratio must therefore be numerical. It cannot be irrational. The difference between numbers and magnitudes is that numbers must end at one, while magnitudes can be divided without end. But if magnitudes are made of units just like numbers, the only difference left is that the units of one are spatial and the units of the other are mental.

As for the triangle, the opposite side does not grow simply because the angle widens. The two sides enclosing the angle must also grow in proportion. Only when the two sides grow as the angle opens will the opposite side increase in the corresponding ratio. This argument may be a disputation over the premise, but it is enough to show that the objection does not settle the question.

Another problem with al-Nazzam’s view is that it makes it impossible to cross a set distance in a set time. To cross even half of a distance, one must first cross half of that half, and so on without end. These are ordered steps, and it is impossible to complete an endless number of ordered steps. So on his view no distance can be crossed.

For this reason, al-Nazzam accepted the idea of a “leap.” But that does not help. What is crossed cannot be less than what is leapt over; at best, it is equal to it, or perhaps greater. And every part of a distance, however small, can be divided in the same way. What we observe in movement is that an object crosses a distance, not that it skips over it. Consider running a finger across a smooth surface, or drawing a line of ink across white paper: every part is felt or colored.

If the relation between what is crossed and what is leapt over were like the relation between a finite and an infinite, then we should feel the surface or see the ink only in that relation. But we do not. For the same reason, his appeal to overlapping parts fails.

Some have tried to defend him by saying that crossing a set distance takes a time that has infinitely many parts, each matching a part of the movement and a part of the distance. This does not mean that time itself is unlimited, they say, since a finite movement and a finite time can each contain infinitely many parts, just as a finite body can.

They claim this is like the philosophers’ view: a finite distance can be divided without end and still be crossed in a finite time, since time too can be divided without end. But they have missed the difference between what is possible in principle and what is actual. It is obvious that something that happens one step at a time from beginning to end cannot consist of an infinite number of actual steps, just as what lies between two limits cannot be infinite.

To accept their view is to open the door to sophistry and turn away from truth. Anyone who sees clearly can recognize that the view fails even without considering the crossing of a distance, though the contradiction is more evident when we consider something with one continuous extension. But if God has not given someone light, what light can that person have?

It is also strange that the very argument used to support al-Nazzam’s view becomes, in the hands of many, an argument against the philosophers’ view that bodies are continuous.

#### Chapter 7 — Problems that follow from denying that bodies are continuous

Our senses show that this view is false, yet those who believe in indivisible atoms accept the problems it brings: the parts of a spinning wheel come apart; a moving object stops; things leap across space; parts overlap; and the fast cannot catch the slow. Faced with these problems, some theologians gave up their insistence on indivisible atoms and returned to the view of careful thinkers such as Imam al-Razi, while others suspended judgment.

In *Nihayat al-ʿUqul*, al-Razi says that some scholars suspended judgment on this question because the arguments conflict. Imam al-Haramayn had stated in *al-Talkhis fi al-Usul* that the question lies beyond what reason can settle; so had Abu al-Husayn al-Basri, the most accomplished of the Mu‘tazilites. “We too choose to suspend judgment,” al-Razi concludes, “and so we do not need to answer their arguments.”

A historical account tells of a debate in the court of Sahib ibn Abbad between those who said the parts of bodies were finite and the followers of al-Nazzam, who said they were infinite. The first group argued that if a body had infinitely many parts, no finite distance could be crossed in finite time. For movement requires each part to leave its place and enter the place of another part, while another part moves into the place it left. If the parts are infinite, crossing the distance must take infinite time. The atomists answered by proposing a leap. They were then told that if a body contains infinitely many parts, its volume must also be infinite. They accepted that its parts could overlap.

The followers of al-Nazzam then challenged the atomists. When a wheel turns, a part near the hub moves more slowly than a part farther out. When the outer part advances by one unit, the slower part must either cover less than one unit, dividing an indivisible part, or pause while the outer part moves. The atomists accepted that the wheel’s parts come apart as it turns, moving along concentric circles, one above another. Each group ridiculed the other for accepting leaps or the breaking apart of the wheel. The atomists also accepted that a moving object must stop while the fast catches the slow, as described earlier.

The absurdity of accepting the wheel’s parts coming apart becomes clearer if we consider something all reasonable people agree is strong and cannot break apart: a rotating heavenly sphere, which God describes as “seven strong ones built above you.” Or consider a body whose parts would scatter if they came apart, like a compass. If it knew that its parts were separating, its life and movement would be destroyed, as would those of a human being spinning around.

The problem of a moving object’s stopping becomes clearer if the difference between the two speeds is enormous, as between a horse and the sun. Imagine that, when they begin moving, the point in the fourth sphere aligned with the horse lies ahead of the sun’s center by an arc of that sphere. This leaves the atomists no way to escape the first problem by saying that a free agent wills the wheel’s parts to separate at one moment and join again at another. They do not understand how far beyond their imagining the Creator’s will is.

It is remarkable to think that the parts of a spinning top and a millstone—both lifeless bodies—would need such awareness and guidance. The slower part would have to know that it must stop so as not to lose alignment with the faster part. It would have to know exactly how long to stop, based on the difference between the distances traveled, and how to keep that ratio steady through every pause, in every part moving around each circle.

Even trained mystics could not manage a fraction of this awareness, though they claim to have visions and miracles. Nor could two people walking toward the same place, one closer than the other, decide to arrive at exactly the same time. The nearer person would not know how long to pause, and the farther one would not know how much to speed up.

The second problem is that we do not notice an object stop while moving because, they say, the pauses are too brief. But if the pause-time is to the movement-time as the distance covered by the fast is to the distance covered by the slow, then the movement-time must be much shorter than the pause-time. We should not sense the movement at all—or at least we should see the object move at one moment and stop at another.

What further confirms how mistaken they are is that their view leads to an effect without its cause when the sun moves but its shadow stands still,[^V05-10] and to a cause without its effect in the following example.[^V05-11] Imagine a well one hundred cubits deep, with a board fixed halfway down. Tie one end of a fifty-cubit rope to the board and a bucket to the other end. Then lower a hook on another fifty-cubit rope until it catches the first rope at the board, and pull it. The hook starts moving from the middle of the well at the same time as the bucket starts from the bottom. They reach the top together, though the bucket travels one hundred cubits and the hook only fifty. Yet the hook’s movement is a full part of the cause of the bucket’s movement. If the hook pauses while it is moving, the bucket’s effect would exist without its complete cause.

#### Chapter 8 — The atomists’ objections and what lies behind them

Know that the atomists’ argument rests on this idea: if a body can be divided without end, then a tiny body, such as a mustard seed, and a huge one, such as a mountain, must be equal in size, since both can be divided without end. Their measures would therefore both be infinite, because an infinite sum of magnitudes must be infinite.

This objection fails because a body is not already divided into parts. It can be divided only in principle. A body’s possible divisions can continue without end and still differ greatly in size, just as there can be endlessly many hundreds and thousands, with an obvious difference between them. Neither body has any parts that are already divided. If we divide each into the same number of parts, each part of the mustard seed is smaller and each part of the mountain is larger. This remains true no matter how many parts we imagine.

This argument does apply to al-Nazzam’s view, since he says that a body is already divided into infinitely many parts. But some writers object that an infinite sum of equal or increasing magnitudes must be infinite, while an infinite sum of decreasing magnitudes can remain finite.
