For example, all the nested halves of a cubit, if they existed, would add up to just one cubit. They say that bodies can be divided into infinitely many parts that keep getting smaller.

The answer is that if there are actually infinitely many parts, then joining one finite group of them to another must make the whole larger. If this is repeated without end, the total must become infinite. To deny this is simply to refuse the result.

It is true that an endless series of nested halves would add up to one cubit if those halves existed. But they exist only as possible divisions. Their actual existence is impossible, and an impossible assumption can lead to another impossibility. Besides, if the parts become smaller in one direction, they grow larger in the other, since the two series correspond to each other. As the objectors admit, the combined amount must then be infinite. They could escape only by saying that growth without end in one direction makes the total infinite, while growth without end in the other does not. There is no reason for that distinction. The actual parts would remain the same in either imagined series. The matching of the two series, and other arguments, also disprove the existence of infinitely many actual parts, since such parts would have to be ordered.

Another objection, closely related to the first, says that if a mustard seed were divided into parts without end, those parts would cover the whole surface of the earth. This objection is weak. The earth has a limited surface, which could be covered by many very small parts, provided their number were also limited.

A further objection says that if a distance could be divided without end, a moving thing would have to cross half of it, then half of that half, and so on. It could never finish. The time needed to cross all those halves would also be infinite. People have used this argument both to support al-Nazzam and to criticize the philosophers. The answer is that the distance crossed is one thing. It is not actually divided into infinitely many parts; it is divided only in thought. The same is true of the time taken to cross it. As we have already seen, this argument applies to al-Nazzam.

Abu Rayhan al-Biruni raised another objection to Aristotle in a letter to Avicenna. If a body is continuous and can be divided without end, he said, a moving thing should never catch another moving in the same direction, even if the one in front is much slower. Imagine that the sun and moon have a set distance between them. As the moon moves, the sun also moves, but by a smaller amount. The same is true no matter how often the distance is divided. Yet we see one body overtake another.

Avicenna reports that Aristotle had raised this objection against himself and answered it. Avicenna did not accept that answer. He later wrote that Aristotle’s reply, and the explanations given by commentators, seemed plainly sophistical and misleading.

Here is the sense of Avicenna’s own answer. Saying that a body can be divided without end does not mean that it is actually divided forever. It means that each part has a middle and two ends. Some parts can in fact be split between those ends; others are too small to be divided in fact, though they can still be divided in thought. Anyone who says that every part is already divided faces al-Biruni’s objection. But if some parts are actually divided and others only in principle, the objection does not follow. A moving thing crosses a finite distance made up of parts that are actually divided.

I would add that, while two bodies are moving, neither has a single, fixed point that marks its exact position at every instant. Each has a general position, not one fixed individual point. The distance between them is likewise not a specific, fixed distance at every instant, but a general one. Only in each imagined instant can we assign a precise position to each body and a precise distance between them. Just as the instants in continuous time exist only in thought, so do the exact positions and distances we assign to the moving bodies.

There are, of course, points in the heavens where circles cross, or where a pole or the place of greatest distance from another circle gives us a definite boundary. But these boundaries are finite in number. The distance between any two of them is a single magnitude in nature and quality; it is divided only in thought.

Another objection says that when a sphere rolls along a flat surface, it touches the surface at one point after another. This seems to mean that the points in the line are passed one by one and that the line is made up of those points.

The answer is that, while the sphere is still, it touches the flat surface at one point. While it moves, however, its contact is a changing line whose parts come into being one after another. At each imagined instant, the sphere touches the surface at a point. But those instants, like the points imagined in the line, exist only in thought, not as separate parts in reality.

To infer that the points must be next to one another because the imagined instants are next to one another assumes what is being argued about: whether movement and time, like bodies and distances, are made of indivisible parts. An instant in time has the same relation to time as an imagined point has to a line. The moving instant is like a point moving around a circle or drawing a line.

People have offered strange answers to this objection. Some say that the sphere and surface are strongly connected, and that their essential parts must touch. Others say that contact changes only through movement, which takes time, not in an instant. So, they argue, the points and instants do not have to follow one another without a gap. The sphere stops touching one point over a period of time, and touches another point in the instant between.

They then add that, since indivisible parts cannot exist, the loss of contact has no first moment. This, they say, avoids the problem.

Others say that only one point is in contact at any given time. One point disappears and another appears, just as they say one instant disappears and another follows. Neither answer is right.

The first answer leaves this question: during the time between the first and second contacts, are the sphere and surface touching or apart? They cannot be apart. Imagine the sphere is iron, or so heavy that it cannot be lifted off the surface without an outside force, and assume that no such force is acting. If the sphere still touches the surface, then it must touch either along a line or at a point. If along a line, a curved line must lie against a straight one. If at a point, that contact exists only in an instant. So we return to the original problem: what happens in the time between the instant of the first contact and the instant of the next?

The same applies to the claim that instants come one after another. We are left only with the truth already stated. The second answer also fails. It is impossible for points to be next to one another in time, even if they do not exist at the same instant. That would mean that a magnitude could end in something that cannot be divided, even in principle, as Muhammad al-Shahrastani argued.

Further, as we will prove, events that come one after another in time exist together in the timeless order that contains time. So the points, each assigned to a different instant, exist together there and are in fact next to one another. The instants cannot be indivisible either. They match a movement, which matches a distance. Since what matches one continuous thing must also be continuous, time and movement must be continuous too. If either were made of indivisible parts, so would the other be. But we have already shown that bodies are not made from indivisible atoms.

A further objection says that the divisions a body could undergo in the future must either be finite or infinite. If they are finite, the process must eventually reach a part that cannot be divided. If they are infinite in fact, the same problem arises as in al-Nazzam’s view.

We answer that the possible divisions may be finite in number without ending at any fixed stage. The same is true of future time and events. People say that the events and days to come after today are finite, as is the amount of time in them. Yet after any group of future events, days, and hours, another event, day, and hour will follow. So “finite” here does not mean that things stop at a last point.

In everyday speech, something that never stops at a last point can be called endless. But people often confuse this meaning of “endless” with having an infinite size or number. This confusion of words causes many errors.

Another objection says that an endpoint must have an indivisible place in which it exists, such as an indivisible atom or something like it. Otherwise, if the place were divided, the endpoint would also have to be divided.

We answer that dividing a place does not always divide what is in it. This would follow only if the place held the endpoint simply because of its size. Relations can be divided when their places are divided, but some relations cannot.

The first kind includes the alignment of one body with another or the way one rests against another. If the body is divided in half, a part of that alignment or contact belongs to each half. A third or a quarter of the body carries a third or a quarter of the contact. This is because contact with half a body is half of the contact with the whole, and the same is true for a third, a quarter, and so on.

The second kind includes relations that do not belong to a thing simply because of its size. Fatherhood, for example, does not belong to a father merely because he has a certain size and shape. It depends on his desire and action in producing what can become another member of his kind. Sonship, likewise, comes about because a smaller body is affected by another person and, through many changes in kind and degree, becomes the matter for its own body.

These two relations do not belong to father and son merely because they have bodies, so they do not grow or shrink with their bodies, nor are they divided as their bodies are. The father’s hand is not a father to the son’s hand. Fatherhood and sonship belong to a living being, as a living being, in relation to another of its kind. Being alive does not require division. So the features that follow from life do not have to be divided either.

Even if, as some philosophers hold, living beings differ in how fully they have life, this difference is not the same as difference in the size of their bodies. A large camel or elephant is not more fully alive than a human being or a monkey simply because it is bigger.

So we say that the body has boundaries only because it ends; boundaries do not belong to it simply because it is a body.[^V05-12] For this reason, some philosophers say that boundaries are a kind of nonexistence: the body’s ending is a lack of further body beyond it.

A surface divides in two directions, but not in a third. It exists where the body is cut off in one of its three dimensions. Since it belongs to the body through the end of that dimension, it cannot be divided in that same direction—not by itself, since in that direction it is a lack, and not through division of the body, since it belongs to the body because the body ends there, not because it extends there. But it can be divided in the other two directions, both in itself and as the body beneath it is divided.

It divides in itself because, when the body ends in the third direction, the surface has a definite nature in the other two directions. It divides along with the body because it belongs to the body not through one of its other two dimensions ending, but through the third dimension ending while the other two continue. Otherwise, it would be a line or a point. A line, in turn, divides in one direction, by itself and along with the body beneath it, but not in another direction. The same reasoning shows why a point has no direction in which it can be divided. Understand and reflect on this.

Another objection would make an angle leap from one kind to another. This is a hard problem, and difficult to solve. It concerns the fact that quantities such as angles and figures are continuous.

A plane angle is a quantity of surface between two lines that meet at a point. Some say it is a feature of the surface between the lines. On either account, philosophers agree that the area between the lines can be divided without end.

The two lines may be of the same kind: both straight or both curved. They may curve in the same direction or in opposite directions. The angle may be formed where the curves bulge outward or where they hollow inward. But the lines cannot lie exactly on top of one another, since then there would be no angle.

The lines may also differ: one straight, the other curved. The curve may bend inward, as when a straight line meets the outside of a circle, or outward, as when a circle’s diameter meets its circumference. Euclid proves that the first angle is smaller than every angle formed by two straight lines, while the second is larger than every such angle.

With that in mind, imagine a line lying against the tangent to a circle. If it moves toward the circle while its point of contact stays fixed, then as soon as it moves at all, the angle it forms with the straight line is larger than the angle formed by the straight and curved lines. It does not first become equal to that angle. This is a leap in movement. The same happens if a diameter moves even slightly while one end stays fixed: the angle becomes wider than a right angle without first being equal to it, because the increase is greater than the angle’s shortfall from a right angle. The same problem arises in reverse when either line returns to its starting place.

This first argument creates the problem. Many clever people have found it difficult to solve and have offered faulty answers. Some say that an angle is a quality, and changes in quality can happen by a leap. Others say that one side of the angle moves in a direction that lies between the two sides and across the plane, while the angle itself can be divided only in the direction between the sides, not from the point to the base. Both replies are clearly misleading.

Our teacher and master, may God preserve his guiding light for those who follow him and let his wisdom shine on those prepared to receive it, has offered an answer that heals this problem. I will repeat it with gratitude.

An angle whose sides differ has two aspects. It is a surface, and it is bounded by a straight line and a curved one. The movement just described follows the first aspect, not the second. An angle between two straight lines can never be equal to one bounded by a straight line and a curve. If the straight side of a straight-sided angle is laid against the straight side of the other angle, the other sides cannot line up: a straight line cannot be laid exactly over a curve. So the straight-sided angle cannot match the one with different sides.

In general, the nature of an angle differs when one side changes from straight to curved, since those are different kinds of line. That difference is part of what each angle is. And since movement follows a connected path through one kind of thing, it must pass through parts of the same kind. What moves cannot reach a part of another kind along that path.

For example, a line cannot change smoothly into a surface, nor can a surface change smoothly into a body. In the same way, as a line moves and grows, it cannot reach a surface; as a surface moves and grows, it cannot reach a body. It cannot reach what differs from it in kind, nor can it take part in a path made up of parts of that different kind.

So an angle formed by a curved line and a straight one does not grow until it becomes equal to an angle formed by two straight lines. The angle between a circle and its tangent cannot grow until it is equal to any straight-sided angle. Nor can the angle between a diameter and a circle’s edge grow until it equals a right angle. Nor can the angle between a tangent and the perpendicular diameter shrink until it equals one of the larger angles between straight lines. There is no leap.

#### Further Discussion and Clarification

A questioner might ask: if one angle is larger or smaller than another, should it not also be possible to say that they are equal? If one is larger, it must contain the other plus something more. That would make equality possible in principle.

We answer that “larger” and “smaller” can be used in two ways: for quantities that have a common unit, or by a looser comparison between unlike quantities. Quantities have a common unit when one can be measured by the other—for example, one magnitude may be a third or a quarter of another. Their relation is then numerical.

A relation between quantities that do not share a common unit is not numerical. They may differ in size without being the same kind of quantity. A straight line, for example, is the shortest line between two points, even though it is not the same kind of line as a curved one. The fact that one is shorter does not make the two kinds alike.

Equality means being the same in size and kind of quantity. So two equal things must belong to the same kind of quantity. In this way, an angle between a straight and a curved line can be compared with a straight-sided angle as larger or smaller, but they cannot be equal.

For example, suppose a small arc of a circle grows as a compass moves until the arc reaches halfway around the circle. It then becomes longer than the diameter, though it began shorter, without ever being equal to it. The point is that the arc and the straight diameter are unlike kinds of length. Keep this in mind.

As for the claim that a sixth of the circle’s circumference equals its chord, which equals the radius in an equilateral triangle, the chord is counted as sixty units because the radius is divided into 120 units. The arc is also counted as sixty units because the circle’s circumference is divided into 360 degrees.

The reason for this is that “sixty” for the arc is a true count, but “sixty” for half the radius is only a chosen measure. The diameter is not really made of 120 units; people use that number because it makes calculations easier. Its actual measure is 114 and a fraction. So the arc and its chord are equal only by convention, not in reality. The supposed problem does not follow. The result that does follow is not a problem.

The ancient geometers, including Archimedes, established relations between unlike magnitudes through comparisons of greater and smaller, not by claiming equality. So they allowed two unlike magnitudes to differ in size without being equal or having any of the other numerical ratios. The rule that ratios require like kinds applies to numerical ratios, not to ratios of magnitudes that cannot be measured by a common unit.

There are many other objections of the same kind. I will not go through them one by one, both to keep this discussion from becoming too long and because their answers depend on a fuller account of movement, which will come later. Wait for that discussion. Examples include the claim that a moving point must meet a part that cannot be divided, that when a point moves with a cone it must pass through indivisible positions, that if instants did not exist then adjacent instants would follow, and that a body’s continuity would require time to be made of instants. These claims also rely on the idea that the present instant is indivisible, separate from what came before and what follows. If it did not exist, they ask, how could another instant come after it? If it did exist, they say, time would be present in an instant. The same question arises about the first instant of a movement. If movement occurred in that instant itself, time would have to be contained in an instant. If it began in another instant with an interval between, the first would not really be its beginning. The first instant of movement follows the last instant of rest.

Another objection says that if a body is continuous, two movements that start and stop together cannot differ in speed. At every imagined instant, each moving thing occupies a position; so their positions would match, making the movements equal in speed. But both the argument and its conclusion are false, as the earlier objection about the mustard seed and the mountain has shown.

#### Chapter 9 — Why a body can always be divided by separation

Some early philosophers, including Democritus, said that what we see as individual bodies—water and air, for example—are not simple things. They are made from small, hard, similar bodies that touch one another. These building blocks cannot be physically divided, though we can divide them in thought. Calling them bodies is what distinguishes this view from the belief in indivisible atoms.

They disagreed about the shapes of these bodies. Most said they were spheres, because they are simple, and accepted the existence of empty space. Others said they were cubes, triangles, or squares. Some said they had five shapes: fire was made of solids with four triangular faces; earth of cubes; air of eight-sided triangular solids; water of twenty-sided triangular solids; and the heavens of twelve-sided solids.

Al-Razi reports these views. Avicenna says in *The Healing* that some atomists thought the small bodies had different shapes, while others thought they were all the same.

Some later writers argued against this view as follows. Since the small bodies are alike in nature, whatever is true of one must be true of all the others and of the whole made from them. If a whole can be split apart, then each small body must also be able to split apart. This argument needs care.

First, it assumes that the small bodies are the same in essence. Their opponents admit only that they are alike in nature, and that admission at most lets us press them in debate. It does not make the argument a proof. Someone could claim that no two small bodies have the same essence. Then we would not have shown that every body can be split apart, and the argument would not establish matter, much less prove that it is found in every body.

Second, the argument works only if the bodies we see and the small things that make them up are alike. That has not been shown, and the atomists do not admit it. The visible bodies have different natures. If their parts all had the same nature, then the whole and its parts would not have the same nature.

If “the whole” means only a number of material parts, with no attention to the form that makes them one thing, then that collection has no single, real nature. We cannot compare it with another thing and say that the two have the same nature. This argument seems to come from Avicenna’s statement that every kind of division makes the thing divided into two, with each part having the same nature as the whole. But he meant division applied to one individual body, not a mere collection of parts. This is enough for our purpose: we need only establish matter, as the words of Avicenna will show, with some further explanation.

Here “nature” means the first source of a thing’s own basic quality, whether that quality is movement, rest, or something else. The term is broader than “natural power.”

There are three main kinds of division. The first happens by breaking or cutting. The second happens in thought or imagination. The third comes from differences between two features of a thing. Those features may be fixed, belonging to the thing in itself, such as black and white; or changing, belonging to it in relation to something else, such as touching or being aligned with it. We have already discussed these kinds of division.

We can tell them apart as follows. If division leads to actual separation, it is the first kind. If it exists only in the mind, it is the second. Otherwise, it belongs to the third. By “only in the mind,” we mean division that is not grounded in a real difference. Without that restriction, the third kind would be counted as mental division too, especially the kind based on changing relations.

We have already explained the difference between the two kinds in this third group. One exists only in the mind, though the mind draws it from something real. The other is an actual difference between features. Both can be forms of connection in a wider sense, like the connections between parts of an animal’s body.

Some people define an indivisible atom as something that cannot be divided by breaking, cutting, imagining, or reasoning, but say nothing about division based on differences between two features. That definition is weak. Its aim is to define the indivisible unit, not to describe every way division can happen.

Others say that when part of a body is heated, lit, or touching another body, the body has not really separated into two bodies. When the heat, light, or contact goes away, it becomes one body again.

They also say that if such divisions were real, a moving body would divide the distance into infinitely many separate parts as it passed through them, then the distance would become one continuous thing again once movement stopped.

This is a misleading way to deny that real differences exist. We know that the part of a body that is black is not the part that is white, even without imagining or inventing a division. This is especially clear when the black and white are inside the body. The black surface is not the white one; hot water is not cold water.

Who says that a moving body has to meet many separate parts of a distance, so that the distance itself becomes many? Who says a continuous distance has actual boundaries? This is one of the later writers’ errors: they claim that at every moment of movement, one indivisible part exists. We will explain the truth about movement and time so clearly that no one will have room for this confusion.

Avicenna says in the logic section of *The Healing* that this is actual separation. In his commentary on *The Directives*, he also says that division based on a difference between two features is real, even though it does not lead to breaking the thing apart. This supports what we have said.

Every body that occupies space has, or can be thought to have, two ends, made distinct in one of the ways just described. The two parts match each other in nature and each matches the nature of the whole they make up. This is because continuity means a single unity in existence. A single existence cannot belong to things that differ fully in their nature and are complete in themselves, each independent of the other. This differs from matter and form, or a broader kind and its defining features, which depend on one another because they are incomplete and general on their own.

So either every body of the atomists has the same nature as another body—or at least one does—or they all differ in nature, with nothing shared between any two.

On the first possibility, consider joining two halves and separating two bodies. If joining and separation can happen, then matter is established. If they cannot happen, either they are impossible by their nature or they are prevented by something else. If they are prevented by something else, the point is established: the body can be joined or separated in principle, though an obstacle prevents it in this case.

This is enough to establish matter. We need only show that joining or separation is possible, even when it does not actually happen because of a lasting obstacle, as in the heavens, or a temporary one, such as the small size or hardness of a body. Mathematicians call the smallest body the senses can detect the “first sensible.” They also speak of the shortest time in which its movement can be detected. Plato describes this in *The Laws*: if a moving point travels along a great circle or a long line within that time, the senses see all the parts as if they were present together, though in reality they are not.

If joining or separation were impossible by nature, then the kind to which the body belongs would have to be limited to one individual. But that contradicts what we see. So if the small bodies are alike in nature, one can be joined to or separated from another in principle, and the material basis for all bodies is established.

Now suppose that the small bodies all differ in nature. Even so, the shared bodily nature found in every body is one complete kind in the outside world. Individual bodies differ only because of features added to them. We have already explained the difference between “body” as the matter of specific kinds of things and “body” as a general kind in its own right. If we use “body” in the first sense, the distinguishing features are additions to it. If we use it in the second, they complete its meaning. Many people confuse these two meanings.

In short, the extended form that makes something a body is one shared kind in all bodies. What follows from it must apply to all bodies. If the joining of two parts were essential to this extended nature, then every body and extension would be one continuous thing. If separation were essential to it, there could be no body or extension that was one continuous thing. Both conclusions are plainly false.

This proves that every body can be divided by separation without reaching a final point. There is no need to claim that all bodies look alike or have the same nature. Avicenna used one proof for both the bodies of the atomists and the heavenly bodies. He treated the obstacle to dividing the heavens as something that lasts, and the obstacles in smaller bodies as things that can pass. In both cases, the obstacle lies outside body simply as body. For the heavens it may belong to what makes them a special kind of body, but not to what makes them body at all. If an obstacle is essential to a kind, then that kind can exist in only one individual, and cannot be divided.

#### Another Summary

Even without assuming that separate bodies resemble one another, the proof still works. Consider one body and its ability to be divided in thought. What can be done to the whole in thought can also be done to a measurable part, and vice versa, because the whole and part share the same extended nature. The measurable parts are instances of that nature.

The whole exists as one actual thing; its part exists only as a possibility and is made distinct in thought. The whole and part therefore share a nature, so features that apply to one can also apply to the other. It follows that the continuous can be separated, and the separate can be joined.

**Question:** If a part of a body can have the same size as the whole, then there is no difference between a part and a whole.

**Answer:** The whole, precisely because it is whole, cannot have the same size as one of its parts. That difference is what makes the whole a whole and the part a part. If it were otherwise, their roles could be exchanged.

**Question:** What about the heavens? A part of them cannot be separated, though the whole can.

**Answer:** The heavens, as heavens, do not have measurable parts. Their special nature is spiritual and is not spread through the body. What can be divided is the matter of the heavens, not their nature as heavens—just as what can be divided in an animal is its bodily frame, not its animal life.

Here is a well-known objection that has been difficult for many capable thinkers to answer. It says the proof uses the same word for two different meanings as though it had one meaning in both cases. That would make the proof a word trick, not a real argument.

According to the objection, what bodies made of atoms can have, and what their parts can have, is only a natural state of being separate and a created state of being joined. By contrast, the possibility of joining separate bodies and separating their parts is a condition that arises later. The word “can” also means different things: in the first case, it means already having a quality, which can be actual and necessary; in the second, it means a new readiness that cannot be actual at the same time as the action itself. The objection concludes that the rule for one shared nature only permits each body to be joined and whole when it first exists, and its parts to be separate and many from the start.[^V05-13]

Yet that is not enough to establish the existence of matter. We must also show that something can become connected after being separated, or become separated after being connected. What requires matter is a genuine readiness for a condition that has not yet come about, a readiness different from the condition already present. This is not merely the possibility of a thing in itself, which can accompany its actual existence when considered by the mind in a particular way, as you will learn, God willing.

Our teacher and master, the foremost of the distinguished scholars—may God increase his standing—answers that if extension admits division in the imagination, then its own nature also allows real separation, even when a lasting or temporary obstacle prevents it. If division were impossible by its very nature, imagining such a division would be a pure invention, no different from imagining a division in something entirely free of extension. But imagined division is an idea drawn from the thing as we understand it, especially when it arises from a difference between two of its features.

#### Discussion and Clarification

This would be disproved by time, which they say is continuous and can be divided in thought, but cannot be split into separate parts. Also, even if imagining a division meant that division could happen outside the mind, that would not prove that the division is possible by nature, much less that the matter is ready to receive it. The mind can first imagine something possible, then learn from proof that it is not.

And if an extended thing cannot actually be separated, that does not make imagining its division the same as imagining the division of something without any extension. There is an obvious difference: one is extended, the other is outside the category of extension. If something has no extension or position, dividing it is an invention of the mind.

There are two further arguments against this view. The first is that bodies truly expand and contract, as experiments show: a narrow-necked bottle draws in water after air has been sucked from it; and a sealed, hollow vessel makes a loud noise when it is put in a fire, even though empty space is impossible. The second argument is that if each of these bodies were simple and had one nature, it would be spherical, as we will explain. There would then be empty gaps between them, which is impossible. But if each body were made from bodies with different natures, it would not be one connected thing. That is also impossible. Further, its parts would tend to separate and return to their natural places and shapes, which would bring back the same problem of empty space.

### Art Two — Matter: what it is and whether it exists

#### Chapter 1 — What We Mean by Matter, and Why It Must Exist

No one with even a little discernment denies that bodies contain something that forms and features come to occupy, and that undergoes one change after another. Most people need no argument to accept the existence of something that fits the name “matter”: something that can receive these changes and features, and their disappearance. Anyone capable of serious thought knows, by reflection or direct insight, that earth becomes a drop of reproductive fluid, which becomes the body of a person or animal; a seed becomes a tree, and a tree becomes ash or charcoal.

When we say that a human being was created from water or an animal from clay, we can mean only one of three things. First, we might mean that the seed remains seed and the clay remains clay, while each is also a human being or an animal. That would make one and the same thing both seed and human body, or both clay and animal, at the same time. Second, we might mean that the seed or clay disappears completely, leaving nothing behind, and a human being or animal then appears. In that case, the human being would not have been created from seed, nor the animal from clay. One thing would have vanished altogether, and another, made up entirely of new parts, would have appeared in its place. The third possibility is that the underlying substance loses its seed-like or clay-like form and takes on a human or animal form instead. Everyone rejects the first two. Ordinary people do not believe them. Anyone who plants a seed expects something to grow from that seed; anyone who marries in the hope of having a child knows that child comes from the parents. We distinguish our own children from other people’s children by their origin, just as we say a crop grew from the seed that was planted and a chick came from its egg.

We see the same thing in farming: wheat produces wheat, and barley produces barley. In reproduction, human beings produce human beings; a purebred horse does not produce a different kind of horse, and a donkey does not produce a horse. A crossbreed has traits from both kinds. If someone stubbornly denies this, a thoughtful person will not take the denial seriously. Plain human insight contradicts it. Such a person would have to reject many of his own judgments, though he may be too shameless even to admit it to himself. Anyone who has no shame before himself has none before God, who is closer to him than he is to himself.

So, as a concept, matter is not in dispute. Despite their differences, thoughtful people agree that there is something underlying bodies, which receives one form and feature after another. But they disagree about what this underlying matter is. The Illuminationist philosophers and their followers identify it with body itself: for them, body is one thing and has no composition at all. They call it “body” insofar as it is a substance, and “matter” insofar as it receives forms and dimensions.

Aristotelian philosophers and those who follow them say that matter is a simpler substance, which is completed by another substance that exists in it, called form. Together they make one substance that can receive dimensions, accidents, and further forms: the body. On their account, a body is made up of matter and a continuous extension that can have length, width, and depth. Democritus and his followers say that bodies consist of many simple bodies that cannot be split apart. Another group, the theologians, with their many different schools, say that bodies consist of indivisible atoms. These atoms are joined together to make a body. For them, this joining does the work that form does for the Aristotelians. But joining is an accident that cannot exist on its own; it needs a subject. Form, by contrast, is a substance in its own right, and matter is the subject that receives it. Some who say that bodily extension is an accident in matter, and that a body is made of matter and this accidental extension, are closer to the atomists’ view than to the Aristotelian one. The difference is that the atomists say the subject is a single individual, while the theologians say there are many. Both nevertheless say that an accident completes the substance made up of the other part.

The theologians’ view stands to this view as Democritus’ view stands to that of the Illuminationists. In each pair, both sides agree that matter is a substance but not itself a body; they differ over whether its parts are actual or only potential. The Illuminationists and Democritus, by contrast, both say matter is bodily, but differ over whether all its parts actually exist or exist only potentially. For the Aristotelians, matter is pure potentiality. For the other groups, it is both potential in one respect and actual in another. You will see that calling matter a substance does not mean that it must be a fully formed, actual thing.

You might object that Muʿtazilite books say that, for al-Nazzam and his followers, the basic constituents of bodies are colors, tastes, smells, and other accidents. If so, then matter, for all theologians, is not a substance.

I agree that the Muʿtazilites describe these as the basic constituents. But for al-Nazzam and his followers, they are substances, not accidents. As best we can reconstruct their view, things such as imaginary and intellectual states—the “states” and “effects” discussed in their books—as well as opinions, beliefs, pains, and pleasures, have no part in the essence of a body. Al-Nazzam and his followers, however, treat colors, lights, tastes, smells, sounds, and qualities perceived by touch, such as heat and cold, as substances, even as bodies. It is reported that al-Nazzam explicitly called each of these a subtle body made of joined atoms.

He says that when these subtle bodies come together and interpenetrate, they form the dense body we call an inanimate object. The spirit, by contrast, is one subtle body, and all animals belong to a single kind. Most theologians treat all these qualities as accidents. But Dirar ibn ʿAmr and al-Husayn al-Najjar say that a body is a collection of such accidents, while other theologians say that it is a collection of substances in which those accidents exist.

Not long ago, a man devoted to debate insisted that water is nothing but accumulated wetness, earth accumulated dryness, and the other elements are made in the same way. This is the most outrageous claim ever attributed to al-Nazzam. Some books, including *al-Mawaqif*, say that a body is not a collection of accidents, contrary to al-Nazzam and al-Najjar. That is not quite right. In those books, “al-Nazzam” should be replaced by “Dirar,” as other books correctly have it.

One might defend the wording in *al-Mawaqif* by saying it is discussing what everyone agrees is a body. Al-Nazzam says that such a body is a collection of accidents that are themselves substances or bodies. In that sense, his position agrees with al-Najjar’s and differs from the others only in what he means by the parts. But the arguments against them do not fit that reading. The argument against al-Najjar—that an accident cannot exist on its own and must ultimately depend on a substance—does not address al-Nazzam’s view. Nor does the argument against both—that substances are all alike while bodies differ—fit al-Nazzam’s view. He says that each quality, such as blackness, is a body made of substances that resemble one another, even if they do not resemble the substances that make up sweetness or heat. This also shows that the argument that bodies endure while accidents do not would not refute his view, since he does not accept that bodies endure.

The reply that substances are not all alike is only a debating tactic. It does not work against those who deny that they differ. Even if it were meant to trap them on their own terms, it would not settle the matter. The more likely view is that philosophers and theologians alike consider bodies alike insofar as they are bodies. For the philosophers, that sameness is what allows them to establish a specific form for each kind of body; for the theologians, it supports the existence of a freely choosing agent. Al-Nazzam is the one who says that bodies differ in their very nature.

How feeble the defense in *al-Mawaqif* is: it tries to support al-Nazzam’s view, patch its flaws, and make it seem sound. But no perfumer can undo what time has spoiled. It says that anyone who believes substances are all alike must include a collection of accidents in the essence of a body, making those accidents the source of bodily differences. The author seems not to distinguish sameness in meaning and in the mind’s analysis from likeness in actual existence and in the way things are composed. Even if the mind divides something into parts, and those parts share a general kind, this does not mean that they are alike as actual things.

You have already seen that a defining feature need not be a kind of accident or substance. If bodies are all substances in thought, and “substance” is their general kind, then they must differ by specific features that exist together with them but are parts of their meaning in thought, not separate things in the world. Tastes and smells are not such features. But if bodies are of one kind in reality, or if each is made in reality from substance or body together with something else, as the different schools of philosophers and theologians say,[^V05-14] then the shared element—whether body or substance—can be treated as a general kind in thought, in a certain sense, even though it is one and the same in reality. In that case, only some particular kinds of things include it in their essence. It does not follow that tastes and smells are part of what body is as such, as al-Nazzam holds.

How strange that, while trying to repair his view, the author of *al-Mawaqif* fell into another trap: his explanation also makes bodies unable to endure. If they depend on a collection of accidents that do not endure—and he admits that accidents do not endure—then the bodies themselves must disappear whenever those accidents do. He says that accidents do not endure, but substances do. If substances were all alike in themselves, however, differing bodies could not be made from substances alone. They would have to be made from substances together with accidents, and then bodies could not endure because the accidents do not endure. Is this not a contradiction?

#### Chapter 2 — How the Aristotelians Understand Matter

The Aristotelians define matter as “the substance that receives form.” At first glance, this definition seems to apply to the soul too, since the soul is a substance that receives forms. The better course is to specify that matter receives sensible forms. Some also restrict the definition further, saying that matter’s only nature is its ability to receive. There is a flaw in this view—not the one the Illuminationist philosopher raises in *al-Mutarahat*, which we will discuss later, but another one. Within the soul[^V05-15] there is a material intellect,[^V05-16] which is only receptive: it is a pure capacity for intellectual forms, as we will explain. But this intellect is not the soul itself. The soul, as soul, is a form that brings the bodily matter to completion. It cannot be a capacity for intellectual perfection, since one and the same thing cannot, in the same respect, be both actual and potential—even if the two states concern different things. Nor can it be the form of one thing and the matter of another, even if those two things differ.

A thing can have opposite features in relation to two different things only if its own nature is not wholly one of those opposite features. That is the case here. For the Aristotelians, matter is nothing but receptivity and potentiality. Souls and forms, by contrast, are actual in themselves. They depend on potentiality and receptivity, and are mixed with them only because their existence is joined to that of matter outside them. This is the basis of their account of a body as made of matter and form.

The Illuminationist philosopher objects, at the beginning of his book’s section on nature, that the capacity to receive and readiness to receive cannot themselves be substances. The receiver must first have a reality of its own in order to receive something else and be called receptive of it. The bearer of forms cannot itself be the readiness, since readiness is always readiness for something that already has its own reality.

It is possible to call the substance that bears a form “matter” because it receives that form, just as the soul is called “soul” because it governs the body. On that view, these relations belong to the meaning of the names, not to the substance’s underlying reality. But this claim needs closer examination. Matter’s receptivity and readiness stand to it as God’s agency and creative power stand to Him. Proof shows that the chain of causes must end in a cause that acts through its very being. God is not a god or a cause because of some added quality; otherwise He would need something else in order to have that quality, and a prior godhead as well. That would lead to a circle or an endless chain, both of which are impossible, as we will establish in its proper place.

In the same way, the chain of need and potential dependence must end in something whose very nature is need and dependence. When we say that God’s attributes are one with His essence, we do not mean that concepts such as “giver” and “powerful”—which exist only in the mind—are identical to the essence of the One whose nature is pure existence and beyond our grasp. We mean that His very being is the source from which these attributes are understood, just as He is the source from which things receive existence. Matter’s potentiality is one with it in the same way. Matter expresses the need and limitation found in things as they exist in the world. In the same way, a thing’s essential possibility expresses the need and limitation of its nature as it depends on its own essence.

A thing’s existence may depend essentially on something else without making it a member of the category of relation. Relations such as father and son are general concepts in the mind; each is understood in relation to the other. That is not how actual existence depends on something else. If it were, every existing thing would belong to the category of relation. Consider the maker and what is made: the maker is a maker through its own being, and what is made is something bestowed through its own being. By the simple act of creation, it is made. God, through His holy essence, gives existence to things, yet He is above every category, especially the category of relation, which is one of the weakest kinds of attribute.

As for the soul, if its very existence depends on something else through its desire to govern the body, or through a higher or lower link in the order of existence, then it is like other beings that essentially depend on something else, though the relation is different. But if the soul can exist without that relation, comparing it with matter’s dependence on another is a comparison with no real point in common.

The Illuminationist philosopher also says that a substance cannot have a nature made up of potentiality and readiness, or be potentiality itself. A part of a substance cannot in every respect be an accident, or the thing would not be purely a substance but a combination of substance and accident. The answer is that we often describe the real nature of an essential feature by means of its accidental effects, as we have said more than once. This is how we define powers by what they do. We identify an active power by its characteristic action and a receptive power by the way it is affected.

In fact, definitions such as “the intellect understands universal ideas,” “the animal power senses and moves,” and “the plant power nourishes and grows” all serve as definitions, even though the qualities named may seem, on the surface, to be relational accidents. The true features that make these things what they are are what we express through those signs and effects.

The same is true of matter and of all receptive powers. We can know simple forms of existence only through their effects, or through direct intellectual insight. A strict logical definition, made of a general kind and a defining feature, applies only to a universal essence of a kind. Sometimes existence itself is simple, while the essence linked to it has a definition made up of two parts. Each part belongs to that essence but not to the existence itself. Together, however, they point to what that existence really is, because they follow from its very nature. So we are forced to state them when referring to it, even though existence itself has no definition. Avicenna explains this in his *Eastern Philosophy*.

A similar answer appears in *al-Mabahith* by the author known as Sharaf. He asks: “If possibility is the defining feature of matter, and matter is a substance whose defining features must also be substances, then possibility must be a substance, though this has been disproved. If possibility is neither its defining feature nor something that follows from it, then matter must have been possible before possibility, even though it cannot be separated from possibility.”

He answers: “Matter’s defining feature cannot be known. Matter, considered just as matter, is neither possible nor impossible in itself. Rather, possibility belongs to it: when we think of matter, we think of possibility with it, so the two cannot be separated.”

When examined closely, this is the same point we have made. Matter is a simple substance, and the essential features of simple beings can only be described by effects that the mind draws from their very nature. Matter’s essence is nothing but the capacity to receive forms and the potential to take them on, as the proof for its existence will show.

This potentiality is the whole of matter’s nature and perfection, insofar as it is the capacity for substantial forms. In the same way, motion is the fulfillment of what is potential among accidental qualities, insofar as they are potential within it.

The Illuminationist philosopher raises a third objection: readiness cannot be the bearer of what it is ready for. It is said that readiness for something disappears as soon as that thing comes about. So if matter is readiness for a form, or if readiness is part of matter, it cannot remain once the form is present. But we are looking for something that bears the form.

The answer is that matter’s unity, as we will explain, is not a definite individual unity. Matter has in itself the potential to receive every form and accident. Whenever one of these becomes actual, the corresponding potentiality is lost. Matter is therefore not potential for the form it already has, but for other forms.

That is why philosophers say that matter has a general, not individual, unity. When some philosophers say that the matter of the elemental world is one individual, they mean that its individuality comes from the whole arrangement of forms and accidents that affect it. The entire collection of substances and accidents is certainly one individual. To clarify: since matter is intelligible in itself, its unity resembles the unity that general ideas have in the mind when the intellect considers them across many different kinds. One idea is present in the mind, but it is given particular instances by the mind. Matter’s intellectual, individual unity is like the unity of a general kind in thought, shared by many things. Avicenna and other philosophers often say that the general kind found in compound things is ready to receive its specific features. Taking on one feature does not destroy its potential to receive others. Its unity is indefinite and can go along with multiplicity; its particularity does not prevent it from being general and shared. Matter is similar. It is not potential for just one form. At each stage, joined to one form, it becomes potential for another.

Philosophers also distinguish potentiality from readiness in three ways, as Avicenna explains in one of his letters. First, potentiality can be directed equally toward two opposites. Every person, for example, has the power to feel joy or sorrow, though some are more ready for joy and others for sorrow. The same is true of anger, fear, and other feelings. Second, potentiality can be remote, while readiness is close at hand. Third, potentiality can be stronger or weaker, while readiness is a fully developed, single power.

In short, readiness is a power brought close to completion in relation to one of two opposites. Prime matter is potential for everything. But some forms that come to exist in it prevent other forms from coming about until they disappear. Other forms do not block further forms, but something else must be added before matter is fully ready to receive them. This is remote potentiality.

Near potentiality is different. It needs no further active power to prepare it before it can be affected by the power that will bring about the change. A standing tree is not yet ready to become a key. It must first be cut down, sawed, and shaped. Only then is the wood ready for the further work that makes it into a key.

So matter is not a single readiness for one form that disappears when that form comes to be. It is a general capacity for every form. In itself, it is one in meaning, not one individual thing. Nor is it a separate substance that exists on its own and has a fixed unity. Its existence depends on the existence of some form in general, which gives its general nature a definite instance and a distinct existence, brought about by an intellectual cause.

To make this clearer, consider the body. It carries a continuing capacity for changing forms and shapes, in its organs and in other respects, along with successive states of readiness. As long as it is in this world and remains incomplete, it has a continuing power to reach greater completion, stage by stage and state by state. The forms of its organs and parts change, yet the body keeps its individual unity. The body’s general forms and its general capacity remain the same individual from the beginning of life to its end. The elemental world is similar: its matter has one individual unity and is a continuing potentiality, while forms change and potentialities multiply. We will explain this more fully in the discussion of how matter and form depend on one another, if God wills.

This shows the mistake made by some later thinkers, including al-Khafri. They claimed that matter is one individual thing to which forms and features come in succession, like waves on the sea. It would make more sense to reverse the comparison. Matter’s existence depends on form, so how could the thing that depends be one individual while what it depends on is many?

It has been proved that what is actual in general comes before what is potential. A particular potentiality, however, comes before the actual state for which it is a potentiality. Every potentiality depends on an earlier actual state; it is a possibility of something that comes later. The potential to become an adult, for example, follows the form of a child and exists along with it. The potential for childhood follows the form of seed, the potential for that follows the form of blood, and the potential for that follows the form of food.

This sequence continues through the forms that accompany simple bodies, then returns to the forms of compound things, sometimes directly and sometimes through one or more simple forms, until it reaches a compound being. Potentialities depend on forms in one respect, and forms depend on potentialities in another. The relation is like the sea’s tides, the rising and setting of the heavenly bodies, or the opening and closing of a hand.

One might object to our definition of matter as “a substance that receives sensible forms” by pointing out that some bodily forms are not sensed—especially the forms of the heavenly spheres. By “sensible,” we mean capable of being pointed out by the senses, even if it cannot be perceived by any of the outer senses. The word “sensible” is often used in this wider sense, to distinguish what can be grasped by the senses from what can only be grasped by the intellect.

We could also define matter as a substance that receives bodily forms. Then body can be defined as a substance that can be pointed to by the senses or in which three dimensions can be imagined crossing at right angles, as we explained earlier.

#### Chapter 3 — Proving the Existence of Prime Matter

Consider the views we have ruled out. Some say that bodies are made of parts that cannot be divided in size. The theologians treat those parts as substances, following thinkers who lived before Plato and Aristotle, when philosophy was still developing. Al-Nazzam, Dirar, and al-Najjar treat them as accidents.[^V05-17] Democritus and his followers say that the parts cannot be separated, although they can be divided in other ways. Once these views are set aside, what remains is clear: a body is continuous in itself, as our senses show, but it can also be separated. Separation and joining are not merely parts moving apart or coming together. They are the loss of unity and the beginning of separation, or the loss of separation and the beginning of continuity.

In general, a body’s individual unity must be the way it is continuous. A number’s unity, by contrast, consists in its separation into many units. When the many parts of a number cease to be many, its separated identity disappears. In the same way, when a body loses its unity of continuity, its continuous identity disappears. So if a body is continuous and can be separated, and if what receives a state must remain when that state changes, then something in the body must receive separation. Continuity itself does not remain through separation, since unity and multiplicity are opposites. If there were nothing in the body that could receive separation, it would have to be either the continuity itself receiving its opposite,[^V05-18] or nothing at all.

In the first case, separation would completely destroy the body’s continuous identity and bring two new continuous identities into existence from nothing, with no underlying link between them and the original body. Then, when the two parts joined again, if there were nothing but continuity to receive the new continuity, the thing would have to receive itself. Or joining would destroy two identities and bring one new identity into existence, with no common underlying thing. These first two possibilities are plainly false.

The other two possibilities are also false. There is an obvious difference between separating a body and destroying one body altogether, then creating two new bodies. There is also a difference between joining two bodies and destroying them, then creating a third—just as there is a difference between pouring the water in a jug into cups and pouring the water in the cups back into the jug. The thing that remains through both separation and joining is what we mean by matter. It is pure potentiality. In itself, it has no continuous identity that would prevent many parts from arising and separating while it remained unchanged. Nor does it have a separate identity like indivisible atoms, which would prevent unity and continuity from arising in it. Its unity and continuity come when a continuous form exists in it; its multiplicity and separation come when separation occurs in it.

The lowest levels of actual existence are continuous and separate things, neither of which contains its own parts as a whole. Separate things are lower in rank and more limited. They receive existence from its source only in a degree too low to be substances that exist on their own as bearers of themselves. As we have proved, separate atoms cannot be substances. A unit in a number has no independent existence; it is an accident that depends on something else. A number made up of units therefore has only an accidental kind of existence. The same is true of magnitudes that do not endure, as we will explain.

So the lowest kind of substantial form is the continuous identity of a body. The matter of a thing has less existence than the thing itself. Its relation to the thing is like the relation of an unfinished thing to what completes it, as we established in our discussion of the four causes. What, then, of something that is matter for a form that is already the weakest of forms? It can only be pure receptivity, with no share in actuality. Such matter must be a capacity that depends on some actual state, whatever that state may be, and must take on some definite form, whatever that form may be. Its nature is completed by separation just as it is by joining, by multiplicity as it is by unity, by corruption as it is by generation, and by evil as it is by good.

Its continuity and endurance come from its dependence on forms in general. Their kinds endure as individual instances follow one after another, sustained by a holy source that preserves the world of bodies and their forms and necessary features. It preserves their kinds through the succession of their instances, sustains their existence and good, and makes up for their defects, evils, and losses by bringing similar things after them.

#### Discussion and Clarification

Ibn Sahlān al-Sāwī, author of *al-Basāʾir*, objects to this proof: “Separation is a privation, and what does not exist needs no receptacle. Otherwise, there would have to be infinitely many existing receptacles for nonexistence before anything existed.”

We answer: if separation means that two continuous identities come into existence, then it is something positive. But if it means the absence of continuity, that does not mean that everything without continuity is separate. The intellect is not separate, nor is a point. Separation must also belong to some subject that remains and is ready for the opposite state.

Sight, for example, belongs to an organ that can take on the form of what it sees and receive the colors it perceives. Blindness is the loss of that capacity in an organ that can, by nature or in a particular case, receive sight. Blackness does not receive whiteness; its subject does. In the same way, continuity does not receive separation. Its subject does.

As you have seen, the subject that receives continuity is pure capacity. It needs only the slightest readiness to receive anything, since it is wholly bare of form in itself. Its relation to continuity, its loss, and its return is like the relation of a unit to a state it can receive again and again. This differs from other, secondary subjects. They have forms that define them, giving them a certain readiness. To change from one opposite state to another, they may need a strong cause to act on them. They can also be affected in a way that destroys their readiness for the other state or for the opposite in general. This is true of something shaped by a complete form, with no higher form available to it; or of something shaped by a heavenly or stellar form, which has no opposite or counterpart and cannot be corrupted except by total annihilation, as the great teachers of our faith and philosophy hold. In this respect, prime matter’s relation to joining and separation is not like the eye’s relation to sight and blindness, or a human being’s relation to knowledge and ignorance.

#### Another Discussion

They say: “Even if separation needs something to receive it, the body itself is that receiver. There is no need to posit anything else besides prime matter.”

We answer that we have already explained that the continuity that makes a body a body is not the changing dimensions of a candle. Those can change without changing what it is to be a body, though its measured size changes. If the continuity that makes it a body disappears, however, the original body no longer remains to receive separation. So what receives separation must be something distinct from body as such.

We can show this in another way. Body can be separated, but continuity itself cannot be separated. So body is not continuity itself. Yet body cannot be understood without continuity. Therefore continuity is not outside the nature of body. The first argument shows that continuity is not all that body is; this argument shows that it is not outside body’s nature. Whatever is neither outside a thing nor the whole of it is one of its parts. So continuity is part of body. It follows that matter is not the body itself but another part, distinct from continuity. The nature of body is made up of continuity and what receives it: continuity is the substantial form, and what receives it is prime matter. That is the point we wanted to establish.

#### Another Discussion

The proof just given assumes that a body is made of two substances, and that there is a substantial continuity—an extended substance. We do not accept this. We accept only the continuity that divides the category of quantity. The only thing established by disproving indivisible atoms, we say, is that body is a substance whose nature is to be continuous and extended, and to receive dimensions. It has not been proved that extension itself is the nature of body, or one of its parts.

Nor should we accept the claim that reshaping a candle changes its dimensions while its continuity remains the same. When its shape changes, parts that were separated become joined, and parts that were joined become separated. When an elongated candle is made round, parts that were apart come together; when a round candle is made long, parts that were joined separate. So it is not true that one continuous extension remains throughout, while dimensions are redistributed.

Another argument: the continuity that disappears through separation and returns when the parts are joined must be an accident. When separation and continuity occur in succession, the body retains its nature and kind; what changes does not alter the answer to “What is it?” Anything that does not change the answer to “What is it?” must be an accident. So continuity is an accident.

A third argument: you say that a body has a substantial extension, which is its bodily form, and an accidental extension, which is its measured size. But extension, as extension, has one nature and one meaning. One and the same nature cannot be substantial in one case and accidental in another. If reshaping one candle shows that one instance is accidental, then every instance must be accidental.

These three arguments all deny substantial extension in a body. In truth, they deny form rather than matter. The author of *al-Talwīḥāt* held that a body is made up of a receptive substance and an accidental extension, which is its measured quantity, because he allowed a bodily substance to be made up of a substance and an accident that exists in it. The Aristotelians answer his objections. Their first answer is that body, as body, cannot be understood without the capacity to have three dimensions joined together.

That is why body is defined through this capacity. If it were not continuous in its own nature, it could not receive quantity. As Avicenna says in the passage quoted earlier from *Persian Philosophy*, if the nature of body were not continuous and one in itself, but got its continuity only from an accident, then, in its own existence, body would have to be made of either a finite or an infinite number of indivisible atoms. Or it would have to be something like an immaterial being, outside place, dimensions, and directions, to which continuity, divisibility, or a relation to dimensions and directions was then added. Both possibilities are false.

So body must be continuous by its very nature. It can receive dimensions only if it has a continuity that belongs to it in its own nature. What belongs to a substance in its own nature must be either its whole nature or one of its parts. Since a part of a substance must itself be a substance, continuity must be a substance.

Someone might reply that this argument works only if it has been proved impossible for a substance to depend on an accident that forms one of its parts, while the other part is a substance—just as those who say body is made of matter and accidental extension would claim. They regard the extension as part of the nature of body, even though it is still an accident. We might answer that the argument concerns only the substantial part, which must be extended in itself, regardless of any accidental extension. But then the same objection applies to the matter you have established: for you, matter is connected through a continuity that it receives from form, while in itself it is neither continuous nor separate.

The argument that body must be continuous in itself, or else made of indivisible parts or something outside place and space, also applies to you. We answer both objections in the same way. If something is not continuous in itself, that does not mean it must be made of separate atoms, nor that it is actually outside place and space. It can simply lack continuity in its own nature. Only if its lack of continuity in its own nature meant that it must lack continuity, quantity, and dimensions in reality would either conclusion follow. But this is not so. In reality, what is capable of receiving continuity cannot be without some continuity or the separation opposite to it. It cannot exist apart from both.

This shows that being capable of receiving dimensions does not require the receiver to be continuous in its own nature. Still, we can answer the objection in another way. What you describe—a thing that is continuous in reality but not in its own nature, neither separate nor located nor immaterial—is possible only if that thing has no independent existence, either in itself or in its own nature. It must be potential and possible in itself, pure capacity, made definite by whatever joins with it: it is continuous when continuity joins it and separate when separation does.

This amounts to admitting that the substance which is neither continuous nor separate in itself is matter. It depends on one continuity when the body is one and on many continuities when the body is divided. It cannot do without something that makes it continuous or separate in reality. The continuous form is therefore a substantial form: its unity makes matter one when there is one body, and its multiplicity makes it many when there are many bodies. This avoids claiming that matter can be without body or place in reality, or that, apart from forms, it is either a collection of indivisible parts or something immaterial. As we will explain, the existence of matter follows the existence of bodily form. If continuity is a substantial form and matter depends on it, then matter has no actual state in which it exists outside place and dimensions.

But if, as the author of *al-Talwīḥāt* claims, matter were a substance in its own right, apart from continuity, and continuity were an accident that did not complete matter but only completed the body, then the problem would be unavoidable. This is what led the Aristotelians to establish that form is a substance. The explanation also proves our claim: if there were no substantial extension in reality, none of the continuous quantities—whether stable or changing—could exist at all. Keep this distinction in mind.

The answer to the second objection is that a body does not remain the same individual through the succession of continuity and separation. The truth is the opposite: it does not remain one individual, though its kind remains. This does not prevent continuity from being a substance.

The claim that anything whose change does not alter the answer to “What is it?” must be an accident is too vague. It needs to be clarified. If something does not change while the thing itself remains the same individual—with the same nature and identity—then that feature is an accident of its nature. But if the thing itself changes from one individual into another, that feature may be essential. When separation occurs, the body does not preserve the same individual identity. That identity disappears, and two new identities take its place. The fact that a substantial kind endures while its individual instances come and go does not show that those individuals are accidents.

What we have shown is that individual instances and individuating features are accidental to a nature: anything that can change without changing the answer to “What is it?” is accidental in the sense that it is predicated of a thing from outside. This does not mean it is an accident in the sense of something that needs a subject in order to exist. As explained earlier, something may be accidental to a thing and still be a substance in itself. Each individual continuity is accidental to the nature of body, yet body is constituted by the nature of continuity, and matter depends on continuity for its existence. That is how substantial forms work, as we will explain.

We can answer the third objection in two ways. First, we do not accept that extension and continuity have one nature and one meaning. As we have already explained, the word is used in two different senses. One is a substantial concept: it is the feature that completes the nature of body and divides the general category of substance into kinds. The other is an accidental concept: it divides the category of quantity into kinds.

Second, even if extension has one meaning, it can be taken as a general, undetermined nature, without a particular boundary or size. In this sense it makes body complete in its nature and gives matter a definite existence. Or it can be taken as defined by a particular size and measurable area, finite or—if an infinite size is possible—unbounded. In this sense, it is an accident outside the nature of body. When a candle changes shape and its particular dimensions change, the body’s nature remains, though its measured size changes.

I do not mean that the general feature of being measurable changes; it remains while the candle’s particular dimensions change. The measured size changes through real expansion and contraction. To explain this fully, and keep our understanding from being led astray by imagined mistakes, we need to clarify what geometric size is as an accident of body, and how it differs from substantial continuity, which is part of what makes a body what it is.

Philosophers disagree about the nature of geometric size and have divided into several views. One says it is a continuous accident in which we can imagine three lines crossing at right angles, and that its continuity differs from the continuity of a substantial body. On this account, a body has two continuities: one is a substantial form, the other an accident. The first is quantity; the second is what is measured by quantity. One can be divided by the intellect into general parts that are not fixed in size; the other can be divided in the imagination into particular parts, each with a definite size and measured in three dimensions. Yet the two share the same location and can both be pointed to by the senses. This view is clearly mistaken, as the third objection shows.

A second view says that size is substantial continuity itself,[^V05-19] and that a natural body has extension only because of it. We have already rejected this. A body is continuous in its own nature, and the feature that distinguishes it from nonbodily substances is simply that it can have three dimensions. The proof has shown that, if no substantial continuity existed, no thing could have the kind of unity that belongs to something continuous, whether that unity belongs to it in itself or through something else.

A third view says that geometric size is just the three dimensions of a body—length, width, and depth—taken together.[^V05-20] But these dimensions do not exist as three separate things in the body, while geometric size is an actual thing in the world.

A fourth view says there is one continuity in a body, belonging directly to its bodily form, and belonging to its measured size only indirectly. On this view, geometric size is either the extension of the body as it is defined and bounded in its directions; if so, it is not in the category of quantity.
