Or it is bodily form considered together with those boundaries; then its continuity belongs to it directly, not through something outside it, because it includes bodily form within itself. This seems to be the view chosen by careful thinkers, and it fits what Avicenna says in *The Healing* and *al-Taʿlīqāt*, as well as what his student says in *al-Taḥṣīl*.

They explain it this way: a body has only one extension in the directions of space. When we consider that extension in itself, without fixing its size or the particular way it is measured, it is bodily form, a substance. When we consider it as having some definite size, whether general or specific, it is geometric size. On this account, geometric size is not merely an accident but a combination of substance—the bodily form—and an accidental feature, the extension’s particular size.

The answer is that even if the old philosophers were right that a concept made up of substance and accident is not itself an accident—since it has no subject of its own—a combination of the concept of substance and an accident may count as an accident if the subject is taken as that which receives the accident. For someone who accepts prime matter, the combination of bodily form with its accidental size could be an accident, since matter is the subject: form alone may not complete it, but matter does not depend on that whole combination in order to exist.

There is a problem here. The real issue is not simply whether the combined idea can be called an accident. The main problem is that the members of one category cannot belong inside one another. This account makes a single nature a combination of two categories, substance and quantity. It would no longer be one genuine kind, with one real definition, but a construct of thought with only an imagined unity.

#### Summary

The true meaning of geometric size is an extended thing whose extension is fixed so that it can be measured by a unit cube, or something equivalent. Its being a substance is no part of this meaning, just as the fact that something is a substance or an accident is no part of the meaning of a derived word. In existence, a thing must be one or the other, but that is not part of the word’s meaning.

The mind can consider this extended thing apart from those two relational descriptions: as part of body, or as a form received by matter. What is extended and fixed in size belongs directly to the category of quantity. Its meaning is complete without considering how it is part of a body or how it completes matter. It is not a compound of the meaning by which it completes body and some other accidental meaning, which would force it to be a mixture of substance and accident.

The same is true of “white.” The old philosophers say that whiteness belongs directly to the category of quality. In reality, of course, white things are bodies. But “white” does not mean a body that has a quality; nor does the concept of whiteness include either bodily or substantial nature. It is a meaning that is not limited to either. Nor is whiteness in reality anything other than what acts on the sense of sight by spreading light, whether it exists in a body or not. Even if whiteness needs a bodily subject in order to exist, this does not mean that its own nature depends on the subject. If a whiteness without a bodily subject could exist, it would still be whiteness and white in itself. The whiteness that depends on an elemental body for its existence is also white in itself, not because it is bodily. It needs a subject because its existence is weak and accidental. A body that has whiteness is still a body and belongs to the category of substance.

If we leave out the fact that the white thing is a body and consider only what is white, it belongs to the category of color, and thus to the category of quality. Geometric size is similar. Extension that can receive dimensions, when considered as what completes a body, belongs to the category of substance. When considered simply as a measured extension, with length, width, and depth in some proportion, it belongs to the category of quantity. The same applies to particular geometric shapes: sphere, cube, cone, cylinder, and others. A sphere, considered as a quantity—a shape bounded by one surface, with all points on that surface equally distant from a point inside it—belongs to quantity. But considered as something that exists on its own and can be pointed to by the senses, it belongs to substance. The fact that it has a spherical shape or a particular size is not part of what makes it a natural body or a substance.

The same distinction applies to other solid shapes. Thank God for helping us resolve this objection.

#### Another Discussion

Suppose that a body does have a substantial continuity. Still, it is nothing but geometric size; there is no other kind of continuity in it. This size can receive separation. The thing that receives separation is not matter, and it does not help to say that geometric size disappears when separation occurs, because the continuity that separation destroys is only accidental, not substantial.

As we have already explained, the word “continuity” can mean a real feature that does not require a relation between two things: the substantial extension. This is a special meaning, and not what most people understand by the word. It can also mean the familiar relation between two things that are joined. We can use it whether the things actually exist as separate and then come to be joined, or whether we imagine parts within one connected body and say they are joined, or whether two different qualities belong to neighboring parts of one body. Continuity in this relational sense is certainly an accident. It is the opposite of separation, so it cannot be part of a purely substantial nature.

Someone might therefore say that continuity in the first sense is body itself and is geometric size, and that it is not opposed to separation. Rather, it is opposed to continuity in the second sense. These two states follow one another in the same subject.

The subject remains the same in both cases.

We have already explained that this does not follow merely from calling something “extended” or “having extension.” Such words do not have to mean that there is something other than the feature named. Even if they did, this would be only a way of speaking, not a fact about what things are. Scientific truths do not depend on rules about words. We say “distant distance,” “large body,” “long line,” and “dark night,” though the words that describe them do not add something to the thing described.

You might ask: “If one and the same body receives different sizes, larger and smaller, when it expands or contracts, does that not show that size is an accident?”

We answer that true expansion and contraction depend on matter, and proving that they occur requires first proving matter’s existence. If size is no more than the substantial continuity that makes up the whole nature of body, then we cannot explain a body’s size increasing or decreasing without saying that matter has been added or removed. On that account, a body grows in size because more material parts join it, and shrinks because some leave it. Expansion and contraction would then mean that a subtle body enters among the parts of another body, or that they separate or come together. They would not mean that the very same individual body moves into a greater or smaller size while remaining numerically one. There is no proof for those two changes, and in questions of reason, proof is what matters.

The argument from a bottle that draws up water when inverted, or a sealed vessel that makes a loud sound in a fire, is very weak. In particular, bubbles have been seen to show that air is escaping when the bottle is inverted. Nor can we assume that the suction removes no air in proportion to the water it draws in, which would be required to prove expansion. The Illuminationist philosopher also says in *The Wisdom of Illumination* that oil can seep through glass. It is no less possible for air, which is much finer than oil, to do the same.

If you argue that bodies share the same bodily nature but differ in size, then size must be distinct from body, the answer is this: in *The Wisdom of Illumination*, the shared bodily nature is itself the shared size, common to what is small and what is large. Their difference in size is only a difference in how large or small each instance is. Just as the difference between a small and a large size is not something added to size but is a difference within size itself, so the difference between a small and a large body is simply a difference within bodily nature.

This difference is one of greater and lesser, stronger and weaker, within the very nature of a thing. That is the view of the divine philosophers and the ancient Stoics, as we explained at length earlier.

The Aristotelians answer the objection in one of two ways. The first is to distinguish two meanings of extension: one that makes a body what it is and is not a measurement, and another that belongs to the category of quantity. There are several ways to establish this distinction. One is that, when separation occurs, something substantial disappears from the body—either all of its substantial continuity or one of its parts. Before separation, the body was one individual with one existence. Once it is divided into two bodies, one at rest in the east and another moving in the west, one white and one black, that original existence no longer remains as the same individual. It has ended, and two new individuals have come about. It is impossible for one and the same individual to be both moving and at rest, or both black and white. Yet common sense tells us that some underlying connection remains between the first body and the two bodies that arise from it. That enduring subject is what we call matter.

Once we establish the existence of a receptive substance that can be present with a large or a small continuous body, and that always has some kind of continuity, we can see that what completes matter is continuity in general, whatever its size. The feature that completes a fully formed substance must itself be a substance, as we will explain. Particular sizes do not complete matter, so they are accidents.

We have already explained the exact difference between extension as the substantial nature that completes body and gives matter its existence, and extension as a quantity outside the nature of body, which matter does not need in order to exist. The second way to answer the objection is to prove that bodies truly expand and contract by presenting evidence other than the fact that bodies share a bodily nature but differ in size. The argument just given is weak. On reflection, the objection is powerful: different instances of one nature may vary in completeness and degree, especially when that nature belongs to the different ways things exist.

Number and size, and similar features, are particular states that belong to the existence of bodies. Existence itself varies in its nature without anything being added to it to make the difference, the individuality, or the degree. Yet denying that quantity can increase or decrease through real expansion and contraction is hard to accept, given the many observations and signs that point to such changes, as well as the impossibility of empty space. A sealed vessel cracks when heated; meat is drawn into a cupping glass after the air is sucked out and remains there as long as the opening is sealed. Many ordinary observations make the mind certain that one and the same body can increase or decrease in size. We will return to this point and explain it further.

It should now be clear that Aristotle’s followers, including al-Farabi and Avicenna, distinguish two meanings of “extended.” One is bodily form: the thing that is extended without any fixed size. The other is the quantity that allows us to imagine shared boundaries between parts of one continuous thing: an extension with a particular size in three directions.

The first makes body what it is; the second is an accident in body. The first kind of extension does not make one body greater or smaller than another, or make something a part or a whole, a measure or what is measured, touching or separate. The second kind does all these things. It is often said that the Aristotelians believe in two extensions, each with its own existence. This is not so. On their view, there is only one extension in a body. Taken simply in itself, without a definite size, it is a substance that makes the body what it is and gives matter its existence. Taken as a particular, measured size—finite or infinite—it is a quantity that does not make body what it is and does not give matter its existence.

These two meanings can be distinguished as we have explained, and by proving that bodies truly expand and contract—not by pointing to the changing shapes of a candle. In real expansion and contraction, the size itself changes. In the candle example, only its features change: the way it spreads out and the particular lengths, widths, and depths of its dimensions.

The Illuminationist philosopher rejects extension in the first sense in *The Wisdom of Illumination*. He gives three arguments. First, if an actual body were completed by substantial extension, then that extension would have to be either universal or particular. It cannot be universal, since a universal as such does not exist in the world, and what does not exist in the world cannot complete something that does. Nor can it be particular. If it is the extension already shown to be an accident, and there is no other extension in a body, then a body has no substantial extension. If it has both accidental and substantial extension, that is impossible, since extension has one nature and one meaning. It cannot be substantial in one instance and accidental in another, given that both answer the same question: “What is it?” If one instance is proved to be accidental, all must be.

The second argument is that if every body and every part of a body had substantial extension, then the whole would have more extension than its part. It would therefore be capable of division into quantities.

The third argument is that if a body expands, then either its substantial extension remains unchanged—though it is clearly a quantity—or the extension itself becomes greater. In the first case, the extra size of the expanded body would not be covered by its bodily form, which is impossible. In the second case, substantial extension itself becomes greater, so it is a quantity and therefore an accident.

A reply to the first argument says, in effect: if “universal” means a universal that exists only in the mind, then substantial extension is not universal in that sense, since it exists in the actual body. But if “universal” means a nature that can be called universal when it exists in the mind, then extension is universal in its nature and particular in each body.

The Illuminationist philosopher says that if the extension in question is the one shown to be an accident, and there is no other extension in the body, there can be no substantial extension. We answer that what has been shown to be an accident is a feature added to extension: its fixed dimensions, whether in general or in a particular way. That feature is not the same in meaning as the concept “extended.”

I should point out the weakness of this reply. First, as we have said, the meaning of quantity is not simply a fixed size added to substantial extension. It is a continuous thing that can be divided into particular parts with definite shared boundaries. Second, as we have established, a thing becomes an individual through its own way of existing. Some philosophers agree with this, including Aristotle; Avicenna’s wording sometimes suggests it, while others explain individuality in different ways. But accidents that depend on a thing’s individual identity do not produce that identity. They are signs of it, as we explained in our earlier discussion of individuation. If the extension that makes an actual body what it is existed in the world as a particular thing, then its individuality would not come from these accidental features, except as signs of it.

If this substantial extension were a particular thing in the world, independently of all those accidents and external features, then, if it were identical to quantity, it would be an accident. That would make substance identical to accident. If it were not the same as quantity, then the body would contain two separate particular extensions, one a substance and the other an accident. That is the very problem the Illuminationist philosopher raises. Moreover, if the substantial extension became definite on its own, without the accidental one, then it would be either equal to that extension, greater, or smaller. In each case, extension would be quantified in itself, leading to further problems such as two identical things existing together, their occupying the same place, or one of them having no effect.

The right answer to the first argument is the distinction we have established. A body has one bodily extension, understood in two different ways: as extended in itself, without any fixed size, and as a particular quantity with definite dimensions and area. In the first sense, it makes a body what it is; in the second, it is an accident. Often, several different concepts can be drawn from one particular existence,[^V05-21] some of them essential to that thing, others accidental. For example, Zayd’s existence itself makes him an instance of human nature, a particular thing, and something that is generally possible. There is no problem in saying that one existence answers to two different concepts, one essential and one accidental. Extension, considered as one continuous thing at any size, makes a body what it is. Considered as a quality of size and quantity, it is an accident. A definite size does not make something an individual, nor does the lack of a definite size make it universal. A particular size may occur in many individuals; one particular body may also pass through many different sizes as it changes in quantity. The extension that is body itself, which makes a particular body what it is, has a definite nature but is not fixed to one size. What has been shown to be an accident is only a definite size, considered as a quantity that spreads in space and ends at some boundary.

The substantial extension that makes body what it is is not a second extension, existing alongside the measured extension. The two differ in meaning, not in existence. It is this difference in meaning that has led people to think the Aristotelians believe in two extensions and two bodies, one natural and one geometric.

To answer the second argument: substantial extension does not have a whole-and-part relation, or large-and-small relation, in itself. Those relations belong to extension understood as quantity. The philosophers who say that extension is substantial are too careful to overlook that division into parts, wholeness, size, equality, and their opposites belong to quantity. Whether substantial extension can be divided into quantity is the very issue under discussion.

To answer the third argument: motion in quantity and increase in size do not mean that a body has one fixed quantity from the beginning to the end of a change, and that another equal, greater, or smaller quantity is added to it. They mean that, at the successive moments of a change, different quantities replace one another in the whole body. The body does not keep the same size through expansion, only for that size to grow, shrink, or remain unchanged. What remains throughout the change is matter, while it receives one size after another. The path of expansion consists of those changing sizes. The starting point is a small size, which disappears as soon as expansion begins; the endpoint is a larger size, which comes about at the final moment of the imagined process. The same account applies to the strengthening or weakening of qualities as they change. We have already explained this.

It is strange that this great divine philosopher and careful debater established precisely this account in his book *al-Mashariʿ wa-l-Mutarahat*, at the beginning of the section on strength and weakness. How, then, did he make this misleading argument, which leads others astray? Surely he did not mean to.

#### Another Discussion with Him

One of the careful thinkers raises a related objection to the Illuminationist philosopher’s view in *al-Talwīḥāt*. There, he says that a body in the world consists of a substance he calls matter and an accidental continuity or extension. But this accidental extension, which he says completes the body, must itself be either universal or particular—both impossible, for the reasons already given. It cannot be universal. Nor can it be particular, since the specific sizes change while the body remains in both conditions described above. If the extension that has been shown to be an accident is the only one in the body, then it cannot complete the body, since it changes while the body remains. If there is another extension that remains while the accidental one disappears, then the body has two particular accidental extensions, which is impossible.

Whatever answer the Illuminationist philosopher gives to this objection can also be used to answer his own argument. But we must point out a subtle difference between the two views of composition: a natural body made of matter and form, as the Aristotelians say, and a body made of a subject and an accident, as the author of *al-Talwihat* says. Once this difference is clear, someone with sound judgment may say that it is impossible for a body made of two substances, each of which completes the other, to remain the same individual when one of them—substantial continuity—changes. But there is no contradiction in saying that a body remains the same individual while one part of it remains numerically the same and the other remains only in kind, with new instances replacing the old. This is possible because the same individual unity is preserved through the one part that remains the very same individual.

The careful thinker’s objection does not refute the Illuminationist philosopher, because it overlooks this difference. Nor can someone say that this great thinker admitted the existence of substantial extension in *The Wisdom of Illumination*, so he could not then argue against it. The extension that he accepts there is a measured quantity, which the Aristotelians distinguish from the extension that makes body what it is. For them, the quantity is an accident; for him, it is a substance. As we have repeatedly explained, “extension” has two meanings: one is the form that completes body, according to the Aristotelians; the other is an accident outside the nature of body. The Illuminationist philosopher rejects the first in every sense, whether as a substance or an accident. In *The Wisdom of Illumination*, he treats the second as the substance and essence of body; in *al-Talwihat*, he treats it as an accident and part of body. As we explained, he is rejecting form, not matter.

The point of the objection is that the philosopher understood the Aristotelians to mean that bodily form—the first kind of extension—is indefinite in reality. So he asked how something definite and real could depend on what is indefinite in reality. But the geometric size he accepts is not indefinite in reality, even if it is indefinite in the mind. The intellect can draw the nature of a thing from particular instances and consider it in a way that allows it to apply to many. Just as the intellect considers body in general and in particular, so it can consider size in general and in particular. It can analyze a thing into these two meanings: one is measured size that completes the body, and the other is the general extension that completes body as such. Particular sizes complete particular bodies and are their very nature, as he sees it.

What he shows to be accidental in that book is only the particular lengths, widths, and depths. None of those by itself is the size of the body; they are features of its bodily size. Their being accidents does not make bodily size itself an accident. We know that he rejects real expansion and contraction, and all motion in size. So we cannot use the proof that measured size is accidental because different sizes successively occur in the same body. This is how his words can be understood in a way that fits his view.

The correct answer is that the extension which, according to the Aristotelians, makes a body in the world what it is has a definite nature in itself. It is indefinite only as to its size and individual measure, not as to its substantial existence. A body is a definite individual substance in itself, and can still undergo changes in size and quality. While something is changing, it is not fixed as one individual from the category in which the change occurs.

This also answers the earlier objection, if you understand it. The substantial part of extension is not a universal or particular thing in the sense the objection assumes. Wholeness and part, large and small, belong only to extension as quantity. The first question to settle is whether substantial extension can be divided into measured parts.

#### Another Discussion

Let us grant that a body has three things when considered in terms of extension: first, a substance that belongs to the nature of body; second and third, two accidents that add to it. One changes as the body expands and contracts, the other as its shape changes. But why must separation destroy something essential to the body? It is enough that body by nature can receive continuity and separation. It does not follow that what receives them must be one individual by virtue of continuous unity. We would have to prove that individual unity is always the same as continuous unity, and always accompanies it. That has not been shown. A single person or a single bed can have individual unity even though it is made of many connected parts. So being a body does not mean being one continuous thing. It only requires one individual subject that can receive continuity and separation, and that subject may itself be continuous while its own continuity changes.

The objection continues: “Then separation does not end continuity. It ends only continuous unity. What was one continuous thing becomes many continuous things. The substantial extension remains the same individual in both states; only its accident, unity or multiplicity, changes.”

One sharp-minded thinker answers this after making two points. First, a thing’s existence is nothing but the fact that it is actual and definite, whether in the world or in the mind. Existence goes together with individuation, and is identical with it, as Aristotle held. So when something becomes one or many in its individual existence, the other changes in the same way. Before it is divided, a continuous body is one actual thing, with one nature and one individual existence. Its imagined parts do not actually exist as separate individuals.

Indeed, a body can be divided without end in thought. If some of its parts already existed as distinct individuals, there would be no reason why those parts rather than others should be actual. If all of them existed in that way, we would face the same problems as the atomists, who say that a body contains infinitely many parts.

When the body is divided, two individually distinct things come into existence. Either these two already existed while the body was joined, or they did not. The first is impossible, because the parts of one continuous thing are distinct only in thought. If the two parts had already existed as distinct things, those distinctions would either have existed in reality or not. So their existence after separation is either the same as their existence before separation, or not. The first cannot be right, because distinctness and existence go together: the distinctness that comes with separation must be a new existence. Nor can the second be right, since it would mean one and the same thing had one existence, lost it, and then gained another—a contradiction of the premise that a thing’s existence is simply its being actual.

Or perhaps the two parts existed while the body was joined only as possibilities, close to becoming actual. Then there must be matter that can bear their possible existence and individuality while the body is joined. When separation brings their existence or individuality from potential to actual, that matter becomes their bearer and takes on their forms. This matter cannot be the original continuous body, for the reasons already given. So there must be another substance that can receive the original body and the two bodies that arise from it. That is what we wanted to prove.

#### Discussion and Clarification

There is no doubt that the plurality of existences is the plurality—or at least implies the plurality—of individual beings, and that one existence is the oneness—or at least implies the oneness—of an individual. Nor is there any doubt that continuity and separation describe the body’s being one or many. We agree with this. One of our basic principles is that a thing exists only in its own particular way of existing, and existence makes it an individual and gives it its identity.

But we must distinguish between what is continuous in itself and what is continuous only through something else. Division turns one individual into many and brings one existence to an end, giving rise to many. Yet what has continuity and a definite size is only what you call quantity. It can be divided in size by the mind, and in the world through the underlying bearer: the bodily substance.

We have explained many times that extension itself and what is extended without any fixed size are one thing, while an extension considered as having a definite measure or as one or many is another. When we say “extension in itself,” we do not mean that a completely undetermined bodily form exists on its own in the world as a part of body. We mean, as explained earlier, that the nature of extension exists in itself, apart from its particular size and number.

The point is that the individual unity of a continuous substance is not the same as its measured unity. Just as “continuous” has two meanings, “one” and “many” have two meanings as well. One meaning can be one in the world at one time and many at another, because a bearer can receive several different existences.

But the continuity that completes the nature of body—the extension in itself, whatever its size, and whether it is considered one or many in the other sense—can have only one way of existing in its own nature. Because its existence and unity are weak, however, it can receive opposites and changes that belong to other things. In fact, the true bearer of dimensions is confined to one individual, with one measurable size: the sphere of the world, bounded by the outermost heavenly surface. It is one individual whether it has one continuous form or many, whether those forms arise later or are present from the beginning. It has one enduring identity, and also receives changing identities and definite sizes through changes in its dimensions, through continuity and separation, and through changing states, forms, and accidents.

The same is true of the matter of the elemental world. Philosophers say it is one individual, but its individual unity does not disappear when continuous bodily forms come and go through separation and joining, or when the forms of particular kinds of things are generated, corrupted, or changed. You might object: “Since matter is indefinite, can we not say that it remains the same when continuity becomes many or one, unlike body?”

We answer that it is not clear what it means to call matter indefinite in a way that does not also apply to body. The usual explanation, which most people accept, is that matter has a definite underlying nature but is indefinite in its forms. It has two kinds of definiteness: one is essential and enduring; the other is accidental and changes. The same can be said of extended substance: it has a definite nature but is indefinite in whether continuity is one or many. It has an enduring essential nature and a changing measured state, just as the usual account says of matter.

#### A Deeper Conclusion

The strongest way to answer this objection, and to complete the proof for matter through the body’s capacity to receive both continuity and separation, begins with a point no reasonable person can deny. When one continuous body is separated, something that was actually present in it disappears. And when two bodies become one, something appears that was not present when they were separate. We can therefore ask what disappeared from the single body when separation occurred and then returned when the bodies joined. It must be something that belongs to continuity. But is it a real, essential unity, or a unity that exists only as a relation?

If it is a real unity and a genuine continuity, then the conclusion follows: prime matter exists. Both sides agree that if separation destroys something essential to the body—its substantial continuity—then the body must include another part, so that separation does not destroy it altogether and joining does not create a new thing from nothing. If the unity is only relational, then a single body must contain infinitely many relations and unities of continuity, one for every possible division, all lying between its two outer boundaries. The same impossibilities that follow from al-Nazzam and his followers’ view would follow here as well.

Through God’s grace, this is as far as our argument goes in proving that matter exists because a body can undergo both continuity and separation. A question now arises. One of Avicenna’s early students objected: “Existence, unity, and other general features cannot be divided by size, and neither their existence nor our understanding of them depends on matter. How, then, can they depend on matter and be divided by size? And how can unity itself be divided and one identity become many?”

These features are attributes that belong to subjects. If the subjects can be divided, then the features must be divided too. But if these features did not exist in subjects, they would be separate substances—indeed, separate intellects.

Avicenna replied in one of his letters: “These concepts are not necessarily separate ideas. They can exist in matter. Existence and unity in matter can be divided, just as a general kind can be divided into its specific kinds. But the claim that these features belong to subjects and therefore must be divided needs examination. It is true that they belong to subjects. But it does not follow that they must be divided in every subject because they are accidents. They must be divided when they are accidents of material, bodily subjects. In those subjects, unity is continuity. Continuity is ended by separation, but it remains continuous when we imagine the body divided into two parts sharing a single boundary. In that way, it is one and also contains an imagined division.”

This makes clear that existence and unity in a truly continuous thing are continuity itself, which can become many through separation. Separation is nothing but its existence becoming many and its unity recurring in many instances. Yet existence and unity in general apply to all things. This supports the view that existence exists in itself, which we established and explained at the start of this book, and the view that the unity of each thing is its existence. Keep this as a settled truth, beyond doubt or reproach.

It is also clear that existence, unity, and their consequences—like all real features shared by and belonging to all things—are broader than either what the intellect alone can grasp or what the senses alone can perceive. Sometimes they appear in the intellect, where they can be divided only into parts with different meanings, such as general kinds and specific features. Sometimes they appear in simple, continuous things, where they can be divided only into measured parts that share boundaries. Yet in their own nature, as we have explained, they are free of essence and matter; they cannot be divided either by meaning or by position.

How lofty they are, how widely they embrace all things without mixing into them, and how pure they remain, distinct from everything without being separated from it. How deeply even careful thinkers have missed the point: they noticed the ways existence and its features apply to everything, but did not rise from there to understand how the source of existence encompasses all things in a greater and higher way—not as a general idea of the mind applies to its individual instances, but in a way far beyond that.

Avicenna glimpsed another kind of encompassing. In *al-Mubahathat*, in a passage following the discussion quoted above, he says: “Intellectual meanings are forms. This does not mean that they can never be divided in any way. It means that they cannot be divided into parts that are the same in meaning but many in position. An intelligible meaning, as intelligible, can be divided only into parts that differ in meaning, so it cannot exist in bodies. But these meanings are not necessarily intelligible in themselves; they can be intelligible or not. In that case, they may be divided in a certain way. It is possible for what is one through continuity and bodily existence to be divided into two or more similar beings, and this does not prevent it from being bodily unity.” That is the end of his words. Understand it and take it to heart.

#### Another Discussion

If a body’s becoming many after being one means that it ends and must have matter, then the matter of the many bodies must be either one or many. If it is one, then the same thing would have to exist in several places and different directions at once. If it is many, then that multiplicity must either come about when the body is separated or be present in matter from the beginning.

If it comes about at separation, it must begin either after the matter of the single body has disappeared, or while that matter still exists. In the second case, the same underlying thing would be one individual at one time and many individuals at another. In the first, we face an endless chain of matter: every new thing, they say, must have been preceded by matter that could receive it, and that earlier matter would itself have had to begin from other matter. This also contradicts what we set out to establish: something must remain through joining and separation, so separation is not total destruction and joining is not creation from nothing.

If matter is many from the beginning, then a single body would contain infinitely many parts of matter, since it can be divided without limit. If the matter-parts were finite and stopped at a fixed number, the body’s divisions would also have to stop once every part had been separated. But we have already shown that they do not. That is impossible.

We answer that matter, though one in its own nature and individual identity, is not ready in itself to be pointed out by the senses, to receive dimensions, to occupy different places, or to be joined or separated and become one or many. It becomes ready for these features only indirectly, after a bodily form gives it a definite state. It is one with one continuous body and many with many bodies. Considered by itself, as the mind considers it, it is free of all these features. This does not mean that matter is immaterial in itself, as we have explained. Nor is it made of finite or infinite indivisible parts. As we have indicated, bodily form comes before matter in existence. In reality, matter cannot avoid having some of these features through bodily form, even though it has no form when considered by itself. To consider matter without any form is only an idea invented by the imagination.

You will learn that what is actual in general comes before what is potential. When one body is continuous, matter has its own individual unity, and a unity of continuity through form. The first unity depends on the second, or on the separation that is its opposite. When separation occurs, the unity of continuity disappears, but matter and its individual identity remain. This differs from the bodily substance, whose continuous unity is its individual unity. As you have learned, its existence is continuity itself, and continuity is its individual identity. So when separation occurs, that individual body does not remain.

The matter of the two parts that arise when the body is divided is one in its nature but has two parts in its new state. Matter remains through every stage and keeps its nature in both separation and continuity. It does not begin when either occurs, which would lead to an endless chain of newly created matter. Nor does it become many whenever separation occurs, which would mean that every body contained infinitely many parts.

The passing away and coming into being, continuity and separation, belong only to extended substance itself. Matter in itself requires none of them, nor does it resist them. The matter of two bodies—one in the east and one in the west—has an intellectual unity that can coexist with their being in two different places. It has a kind of unity that can go along with multiplicity, just as the number ten is one number made up of many units, and movement and time remain in one sense while continually changing. This is because their unity, stability, and existence are weak.

So when matter is said to exist in different places and directions, this means that it can receive many bodies and kinds of bodily form, each of which can be pointed to by the senses.

Matter’s ability to receive places and directions as one individual does not conflict with the plurality that comes about through separation. Continuity is different. Some philosophers say that there are two kinds of unity: one is simply the absence of multiplicity, while the other is a positive feature that can itself be divided. Matter’s unity seems to be of the first kind. It is not a positive feature in matter but the absence of many matters; it is simply matter’s nature not to be many in itself. The unity of bodily forms is of the second kind: it is a real unity that can disappear through separation and become many. If it were not real, how could separation remove it from matter and joining bring it about?

#### Chapter 4 — Another Argument for the Existence of Prime Matter

A body, simply as body, exists as one continuous thing and has an actual form. Yet it is also able to be joined or divided, and to receive things it does not now have, such as color, motion, heat, and the specific forms that complete it. In this respect, it exists only potentially. So, considered simply as body, it has both an actual and a potential side. In one respect it is actual; in another, it is only able to become something.

One thing cannot be actual and potential in the same respect. Potentiality means that something is missing from a thing; actuality means that some reality is present in it. The same aspect of one thing cannot explain both. So the body that is actually continuous cannot be the very same thing, in the same respect, as what is able to be divided, moved, or given a color, soul, or form. Being a continuous substance is one thing; being a substance able to receive other things is another.

If a body can receive what is opposite to continuity—separation—then it must also be able to receive continuity. The possibility of one of two opposites entails the possibility of the other. If one were necessary, its opposite would be impossible, but here both have been shown to be possible.

A body is continuous before it is divided. Yet there must also be something in it that can receive continuity. If that receiver were continuity itself, or something that had to be continuous, the body could not possibly be divided. But we know that it can. The fact that a body can receive something other than its present continuity shows that it can receive continuity too. So a body, simply as body, is made up of two things: one that accounts for its potentiality and one that accounts for its actuality. These are matter and form.

To state the argument as a proof: a body is actual by its own nature, and whatever is actual by its own nature is not potential. So a body, as such, is not potential. This conclusion can serve as a premise in a second proof: prime matter is potential, and no body that is actual in itself is potential. Therefore, prime matter is not the body itself.

To make the point clearer, a body surely has the capacity to receive many things. What is that capacity? Is it the continuous substance itself, something joined to it, or something that exists on its own? If it were the continuous substance itself—the thing that lets us imagine three dimensions—then the body's continuity would also be its capacity to receive all the many things that may come to it. Anyone who understood bodily continuity would then have to understand that capacity as well. But this is not so.

If bodily continuity itself were the capacity to receive one thing after another, then the form of a body would be an accident. For that capacity is an idea that points to a lack or a relation, and relations are among the weakest kinds of qualities. And if continuity itself carried the capacity for those things, it would have to remain when they became actual. The receiver must exist along with what it receives. Then continuity would remain even after separation, which is impossible.

I do not mean that the capacity to receive must remain after the thing received has come to be. A particular capacity disappears when what it was a capacity for becomes actual. But if this capacity existed on its own, then possibility itself would be a substance, even though possibility is an accident. The bearer of these capacities is therefore neither continuity nor a separate, self-standing capacity. It is what can receive continuity and separation, along with all the countless other states and forms a body may take. That bearer is prime matter.

This argument and the one before it begin from much the same point. But the present argument has drawn several objections. One of our predecessors makes this one on behalf of those who disagree: “We grant that body, or continuity itself, is not a capacity for something else. But that does not mean the capacity cannot be in it. A quality can depend on something without being identical to it.”

If they say, “If the capacity for separation were in continuity, then continuity would remain when separation came about,” we have already answered this in the earlier argument. If they say, “If a capacity exists in continuity as something real, then one thing would be actual and potential at the same time, which is impossible,” we reply that the same thing cannot be actual and potential in the same respect. But it can be actual in one respect and have the capacity for something else in another. Its actuality may come from its own nature, while its potentiality comes from something else that it lacks. There is no contradiction between having one thing and lacking many others. Arguments often go wrong when they ignore these different respects. The bearer of a capacity need not be the source from which that capacity comes.

#### A Deeper Conclusion

Every feature that really belongs to a thing, whether it is a positive quality or an idea we draw from the thing, must have a source in that thing. As we have explained, an effect follows from its complete cause, and a feature follows from what truly bears it. So a quality, whether it is something present or something absent, cannot come from its opposite. Blackness cannot come from whiteness or from anything that requires whiteness. Nor can absence come from existence or from anything that entails existence.

In the same way, potentiality and readiness cannot come from a form simply as form, or from anything that is complete by form. The source of potentiality must be something material, something indefinite and incomplete.[^V05-22] You may already know this from our discussion of causes and effects, especially the relation between a cause and what it brings about. Still, let me make the point clearer.

Every real aspect of a thing must have a source in it. Potentiality is a kind of absence: a thing lacks something that it could have, though it does not yet have it in fact. So this absence must belong to something that can bear it. That very bearer can then receive what it lacks when it moves from potentiality to actuality. If continuity itself bore the capacity for separation, continuity itself would be potential for its own opposite. But that repeats the earlier proof, which depends on showing that separation is opposite to continuity. So let us ask directly: what is the true bearer of this potentiality?

Natural things have four basic causes: matter, form, the maker, and the end or purpose. Every capacity to receive something must be connected to one of these. Take the matter of a chair, which can receive the form of a chair. This capacity cannot belong to the maker or the purpose, because they bring about actuality, not potentiality or receptivity. Nor can it belong to the form of the chair, since the form is already actual. The thing described as receptive must be the chair’s matter; it can receive the form because it is not yet complete.

Once this is clear, we can ask about the matter of the chair’s matter: is it the receiver simply because it receives, or is there a formal aspect to it that can receive?

The form of wood, for example, is already complete in its own kind. It cannot be the potential side that receives the form of a chair. The receiver must be the matter of the wood, not its form. We continue in this way until we reach a receiver that is not itself one of the actual forms. The body's continuity, simply as bodily continuity, is a form: it is what lets us picture the body’s dimensions. So there must be something else that accounts for its capacity to receive and change.

That capacity cannot be a new natural form that comes into being in the receiver, because then it too would need a cause and a receiver, and the same question would arise again without end. Rather, it belongs to what the receiver is: it is part of its nature and needs no further receiver in reality, except when the mind considers it separately.

We have now shown that the bearer of the changes a body can undergo is not continuity itself, but what receives continuity. This proof is close to the earlier one, but it brings out a deeper point: one of the two parts of a body is pure receptivity, a capacity that has not yet taken on a form. This must be so if we are to reach a point near sheer absence, below which nothing remains. The Generous Sustainer has then given all that can exist. This also explains why things may fail to exist, or exist imperfectly, with defects or damage: their matter cannot receive what would be more complete, noble, and excellent than what came to be.

This shows that the world has the finest order that can be imagined and the greatest perfection possible.

A second objection is that this argument also seems to apply to the human soul, and to every immaterial substance that directly governs or moves a body. The soul is a formal substance, yet it can receive changes and feelings, and new intentions and ideas. This seems to disprove the premise that whatever is actual cannot also be potential. The soul is actual in its own nature, but every soul also has some capacity for something else. So it seems that something actual can be potential too.

We answer that the human soul and things like it are immaterial in their essence but material in their actions and qualities. Just as one thing can be called a substance in one respect and an accident in another, so something can be immaterial in one respect and material in another. The soul is actual through its own nature, which depends on its complete maker. It is potential through the actions and effects that depend on matter being prepared as an instrument for them.

The reason is that the soul is very weak and incomplete when it first comes to be. Though simple, it has two aspects that reason can distinguish, as we distinguish a thing’s general kind from what makes it the particular kind it is. Since the soul comes from the source that gives existence and is actual in every respect, it is actual in its own nature. But since its beginning in this world is tied to matter, it starts below its full perfection. It needs qualities and abilities that develop through matter. The matter joined to it bears its potential for perfection; its actuality comes from its own nature, given by the Generous Giver.

This confirms that all actuality and perfection come from God, the Necessary One, while potentiality and absence come from prime matter, which is brought into being through the intellects because of the possibility within it. Matter is the source of evil, absence, and limitation. Their deeper source is the possibility built into every being that could exist, because it falls below complete existence.

This also answers the dualists, who said there were two eternal creators. They saw good and evil, benefit and harm, light and darkness, souls and bodies, spirits and forms, life and death, knowledge and ignorance, day and night, brightness and shadow, actuality and potentiality, and all the other opposites in the world. They thought these must come from two creators. But God is pure light, with no trace of darkness; pure necessity, with no possibility; pure life, with no death; pure actuality, with no potentiality; and existence that gives without withholding. God is far above what wrongdoers say of Him.

A third objection concerns the intellects. They act on what is below them and are acted on by what is above them. They therefore seem to have both an active and a receptive side, and so to be made of two parts: one that acts and another that receives.

We answer that what intellects receive from above is nothing other than their existence itself.[^V05-23] Their reception is not a capacity that existed before they came to be, and so it is not the opposite of action. The word “reception” can mean two different things. One is potentiality, which is opposed to actuality and leads to an external composition of parts. The other is the way an effect receives existence from its cause. Intellects act on what is below them through the very existence they receive from their causes.

A fourth objection concerns prime matter itself. It is a real substance, actual in its own way, yet it is also ready to receive things. It would then have to be made of a form that makes it actual and a matter that makes it potential. We would then have to ask what receives that matter, and the argument would go on without end.

Avicenna answers that the actuality of matter is the actuality of its potentiality. Its substantiality is its ability to receive things. It is not actual in the way a fully formed being is, and these two aspects do not make it two separate parts. They are separate only when the mind considers them. This can be seen in two ways. First, if the two aspects belonged to two distinct things outside the mind, we would have to ask what receives them, and this would lead to an endless series of receivers. We must therefore reach a receiver in which the two aspects are distinct only in thought. Second, matter’s substantiality and receptivity are related differently from the matter and form of an ordinary compound. They are related like the general kind and distinguishing feature of a simple thing. Matter is a simple kind of substance: its general kind is substance, and its distinguishing feature is that it is ready to receive something specific. Otherwise, it would be made of receptivity and something else that makes it receptive.

This distinguishing feature need not come from forms that arrive later. It may come from something higher than matter and form, such as the relation to a particular source of action. The differences among the kinds of matter that underlie the heavens, and between them and the matter of the elements, come from differences among their immediate active causes. The separate, holy intellect that gives each matter its individual unity brings about that difference. A natural form of a certain kind is then joined to it: either the same individual form remains with it, if it can last forever, or forms of the same kind take its place, as happens with the elements. We will explain this more fully when we discuss how matter and form depend on each other.

In short, prime matter in a natural body is potential in itself, yet actual in being potential; and it is actual in itself, yet potential for everything or for certain things, in the ways just described.

Someone may say that receptivity, readiness, and possibility are not essential features of matter. They describe how something relates to other things. A capacity is always a capacity for something else, and the thing it concerns must already exist in its own right before that relation is added. There is no problem with including a relation in the meaning of a name, but it is not part of the thing’s essential nature. Matter may be called matter because it receives a form, just as a soul or an angel is called a soul or angel because it governs a body or a realm. Governing belongs to the meaning of the name, not to its deepest nature.

Nor can the distinguishing feature of matter simply be potentiality and readiness, someone may object. A pure part of a substance cannot be an accident. And readiness cannot be the bearer of what it is ready for, since it disappears once that thing comes to be. Matter would then disappear when the form arrived, though we are asking what receives the form.

These objections have been answered before, but I will answer them again in a different way. Scholars often use words for things that are relational or accidental, while intending the underlying things that give rise to them. They may describe the parts that distinguish kinds of substance by words that express relations, such as “able to speak” for the human being and “able to move by choice” for an animal. What they mean are the sources of these qualities. Avicenna calls these sources the true distinguishing features, and calls the concepts we use to describe them the logical distinguishing features.

In the same way, when we speak of readiness and of what is ready, we do not mean a mere relation. We mean the underlying receiver. It is not the receiver in the sense of something that has a prior capacity to receive. Its relation to receiving is like the essential features of a thing. The source of potentiality and receptivity must be something that has not yet been fully determined. The source of existence itself is existence; the source of being a pair is a pair. It is not a thing that happens to have existence or pairhood as an added feature.

Keep this point in mind. If we overlook it, we can badly misunderstand what things are. These abstract names give us a way to notice realities outside the mind, which we cannot grasp in thought without such names and descriptions. As for the claim that a capacity disappears when what it is a capacity for comes to be: this is true of a particular capacity for one particular thing. But a general capacity for infinitely many possible things disappears only if they all come to be at once, which is impossible. That would also put an end to the unlimited power and capacity of God, which has already been proved to be without limit.

As for the claim that an accident cannot be part of a substance, if “accident” means something that belongs to a thing without being needed for that thing to exist, then we do not accept that matter’s distinguishing feature is an accident in this sense. If “accident” means something that is not itself a substance by its own nature, even if we can use the word “substance” for it in a secondary sense, then the claim that a substance cannot be made up of it has not been proved. As we have explained, some realities that are not self-subsisting do not fit neatly into either the category of substance or the category of accident. This is the best answer the philosophers in Aristotle’s school can offer. God guides us to the truth.

#### Chapter 5 — Another Argument for the Same Point

A body has a nature made up of a general kind and a distinguishing feature. The general kind is “substance”; the distinguishing feature is “something with three dimensions,” taken in general. Whenever a thing has a definition made up of a kind and a distinguishing feature, and the feature could disappear in the world while the meaning of the kind remains, the two must correspond to two parts outside the mind. Those parts give rise to the concepts of kind and feature: an external matter, which gives rise to the kind, and an external form, which gives rise to the feature. In the mind, when each is considered on its own, they are called the matter and form of thought.

Body fits this description. Its distinguishing feature—being extended in three dimensions, and therefore continuous—can disappear while it remains a substance. So a body must be made of matter, which is prime matter, and form, which is bodily form. This is what we set out to prove.

This proof begins from much the same place as the last two, so many of the same objections apply, even if they use different words. I will not repeat them all. But someone may object: “If kind and distinguishing feature are one in a real thing, then they must come into being and disappear together. If a body loses its ability to have dimensions, then it must lose its substance too. Your proof depends on the extension disappearing while substance remains, but this is not so.”

We answered this kind of objection in our earlier discussion of what things are. When a distinguishing feature disappears, the kind may disappear as a kind, but it does not follow that it disappears as matter. Some people claim that when a tree is cut down or an animal dies, its body vanishes completely. They say a wholly new body then comes into being, because growth and life—the features that distinguish plants and animals—have disappeared. They also claim that the cut tree’s body is numerically different from its body before cutting, and that the animal’s body, which remains after the soul leaves, is numerically different from the body it had a moment earlier. This goes against what both reason and our senses tell us. It is no less absurd than saying that something jumps from one place to another without passing through the space between, or that the parts of a millstone come apart while it turns.

The right answer is to say that kind and feature are one in what they bring about and in their existence, since they are parts of one real thing. But they differ in how the kind continues: it can remain as the material part even when it no longer functions as one part of a compound.

When a house is torn down, the bricks remain, though they are no longer parts of the house. Being a part of the house, like being a kind or a broader kind, is a relation. That relation ends when the thing it refers to ends.

#### Chapter 6 — A Further Argument, Called “the Proof of the Most Particular”

Here is the argument, shortened and stripped of unnecessary detail. Everything that can exist depends on the Real One, who is one in every respect. Among the things that can exist are extended bodies. Every effect must have some connection to its cause that makes it possible for that cause to produce this effect rather than another. But bodily form has no such connection to the Real One, who has no hint of composition. It also has no such connection to the intellects, in which no part can be set apart from another. So there must be something between them and the intellects that has a different kind of connection to each. That is matter. In itself, matter is not extended, so it can come from what is separate from bodies. Because it can receive extension, it can then serve as the means through which extended bodies come to be.

#### Discussion

This argument assumes that matter exists before bodily form in the order of reality. But the reverse is true: actuality comes before potentiality. As we have shown in our discussions of what things are and of cause and effect, form comes before matter in existence, just as a distinguishing feature comes before the complete reality of the general kind, even though the general kind comes first in our understanding.

Al-Taftazani states this clearly in his commentary on *Sharh al-Maqasid*: “Form is a substance that stands on its own, and the matter that receives it depends on it.” We will see this more fully when we discuss the relation between matter and form. The argument is right that body as such has no direct affinity with a separate intellect. But matter has even less: body is actual in some way, while matter is potential in every way. In the language of the Illuminationists, the separate intellect is pure light; in our terms, it is pure existence, though it still depends on the Light of Lights and its existence is given by the Necessary One. Matter is pure possibility and darkness. Nothing could be more unlike one actual being than matter is from a separate intellect. Even opposites must share a nearby general kind.

What links matter to what is separate from bodies is not a direct likeness. The link is a series of intermediate beings in which possibility and need grow stronger, step by step, as existence descends. They need more causes because their existence is less complete. This process reaches a continuous being whose imagined parts differ in position and boundaries. It is not fully present to itself as one whole. Its unity can become many; its continuity can be broken; and it can persist only if another form preserves it. That preserving form may remain as one individual, as in the heavens, or be replaced by forms of the same kind, as in the elements. Through this kind of being, matter comes to be. Matter itself has no actual existence, unity, or continuity, and is not itself what changes from one to many or from continuity to separation. Its nature, as we have said, is simply the capacity for existence and nonexistence in something else, for unity and multiplicity, and for continuity and separation.

Matter, then, is pure possibility and potentiality. Its nature depends on something that has actual existence and stands on its own. Like an accident, matter depends on something else, though it has a general unity that joins it to bodily substances and allows it to be spoken of as one of them. Its nature is the meaning of “substance,” but this does not mean that it is itself an independently existing substance.

#### Chapter 7 — Another Argument from the *Eastern Investigations*

This argument begins with the claim that a heavenly body must have a particular shape and size, because it cannot come to be or pass away. What makes this shape and size necessary? It cannot be body as such, since all bodies share that nature, and then every body would have to have the same shape and size. That is false. So the cause must be something else: either something present in the body, something that serves as its subject, or something separate from it.

If it is present in the body, then unless it must always be there, it cannot explain why that shape and size are necessary. But if it must always be there, we can ask what makes it necessary, and the same question returns. Either this leads to an endless chain, which is impossible, or it ends in something that must belong to body as such. Then every body would have that shape and size, which is false.

The cause might instead be something that is neither in the body's nature nor its subject. It could be another body, a power in a body, or something separate from bodies altogether. Another body will not work: if it caused that shape and size simply because it is a body, then every body would cause them. And if it did so through an extra power, that is the next possibility.

If this power must belong to its subject, we must again ask what makes it necessary. If it does not have to belong to its subject, then when it leaves, either it ceases to exist or it remains. If it ceases to exist, the necessity it caused must also cease, which is impossible. If it remains after leaving its subject, then it does not depend on that subject in order to exist. In that case, it has no necessary connection to one subject rather than another. It should affect all similar bodies in the same way. So we still need something to explain why only some bodies receive a heavenly nature from it. The same question then returns about that further cause.

If the cause is separate from bodies, its relation to all similar bodies is the same. It could not give a heavenly nature to one body rather than another without a special link, and we would have to ask what makes that link. We are left with this possibility: there is something that serves as the subject for both bodily nature and heavenly form, and its own nature requires both. That subject is matter. If the body's form needs a subject, it is called prime matter. And since heavenly bodies need matter, bodies made from the elements must need it too. This completes the argument.

The thinker whose proof I have just set out says: “I presented it to many sharp-minded people, and none found a fault in its premises.” But he later reports a doubt. Perhaps body is not one shared nature in all bodies; perhaps the bodily nature of one differs from that of another. This would block the conclusion that every body must share the heaven’s shape and its other features. He spends a long time developing this objection, but there is no need to repeat it here. We will show that bodily nature is one kind, shared by all bodies, and that differences come from features added to it.

I find it surprising that a thinker so widely read and skilled in debate took this argument to be strong and free of objection. The doubt he raised is very weak. We will soon show beyond doubt that all bodies share the same bodily nature. Many natural-philosophy arguments depend on this: the existence of specific forms in bodies, matter in the heavens, the natural shape and place of every body, and the study of souls, powers, and qualities.

Here are the main objections to the proof. First, the same question arises about why one part of the heavens is at a pole and remains still, while another is on the equator and moves quickly. Matter cannot explain the difference if there is one matter throughout each heavenly body. If the poles and stillness, or the equator and its motion, are explained by divine care—the knowledge of the best order—or by a will that some philosophers attribute to God without a further reason, then that same explanation can account for the heavenly nature and its features.

Second, matter cannot make a shape or size necessary. Matter receives forms and states; it does not cause them to be necessary. Some other cause must make the heavenly form and its features necessary, and matter receives them.

Third, the argument presents a real puzzle that must be solved before we can see the full picture. I suspect this is the argument that the Imam, after taking it to be a proof for thirty years, later recognized as a fallacy and wept over for some time. Here is the answer. Of the possible causes listed above, we choose something present in the body and inseparable from it. If someone then asks why it belongs to heavenly body rather than other bodies, even though all share the same bodily nature—we answer that when something present in a subject completes its nature, like a specific form, it comes before that subject in the order of existence. The subject’s nature depends on the form, not the other way around. The form is the true source of why it belongs to the subject; the subject depends on the form. This is like the relation between bodily nature and matter: body as such is the source of its being a substance, and matter follows from it.

The same question can be asked about every specific form and the matter that receives it, or about a distinguishing feature that belongs to one kind rather than another. For example, the ability to reason belongs to human nature. If it belongs to humans simply because they are animals, then all animals would be able to reason. If it comes from something present in this particular kind of animal, we can ask why that thing belongs to it. If it can leave, then the power of reason could disappear while human nature remains. If it cannot leave, we ask what makes it necessary, and the same chain of questions returns.

Suppose, instead, that the feature followed from the general nature shared by every kind. Then every instance of that general nature would have it. A cause wholly separate from the different kinds would also stand in the same relation to them all and could not explain why only one kind received it. Nor does appealing to a subject resolve the matter: we would still have to explain why that subject is different. We cannot simply appeal to matter here, since the elements share one kind of underlying matter and can change into one another.

But if the thing that belongs to a shared nature follows from a distinguishing feature, then its presence in that kind has a reason: the distinguishing feature itself. The general kind belongs to that feature because of what the feature is. A feature that necessarily belongs to a nature needs no further cause beyond that nature.

This is different from a quality that does not complete the thing’s nature and that the thing does not need in order to be the kind of thing it is. Such a quality needs an outside cause, as heat does in fire or whiteness in snow. There the quality follows from what the thing is, and there is no problem in saying that the same quality can follow from different kinds of thing.

We must leave further discussion for another place. The argument’s answer—that the subject explains why a heavenly body has its particular features—does not work for a general kind, such as a created being or a feature that distinguishes a fixed kind. But when we ask why an individual has its own particular qualities, the subject that can receive them may be the right answer. An individual needs a subject in order to exist as one particular thing, even if the quality that completes it is a form of substance.

The true form that completes a material subject is the form present in it, not one particular individual form considered apart from its subject. So the form cannot come before its subject in its individual identity. The subject is what makes it individual. If we then ask why this subject is special, despite being like other subjects, we arrive at the question of how a new thing can be connected to what is eternal. We will explain this later, if God wills.

#### Addendum

If matter and form are essential parts of a body, why do we not use them as answers to the question “Why?” Why do we instead use them to answer “What is it?” and claim that no proof is needed for them? The answer is that we must distinguish parts of a definition from parts that exist in the world. The parts of a definition are the general kind and the distinguishing feature, as the mind draws them out by considering what is vague and what makes the thing definite. Matter and form are the parts that make up a thing’s existence.

Only the parts of a definition are basic to what a thing is, taken simply in itself. They are not proved by asking why something exists or why it is what it is. But the parts that make up a thing in its particular way of existing can be shown by proof. In relation to a general definition, they are like added features; in relation to the thing’s actual existence, they are its necessary parts. The parts that make existence complete can be proved by asking why, just as the parts of a definition answer what something is.

This shows that the way a cause exists in relation to an effect is like the way a definition belongs to what it defines. So proving that a body is made of matter and form does not mean we can skip the proof. First, we learn from the body’s defining qualities that it is a substance. Then we use argument to discover its parts in the world: the capacity for receiving, which appears in the definition of body, points to prime matter; its dimensions point to its form, the extended substance itself.

### Part Three — The essential connection between matter and form

#### Chapter 1 — Bodily Nature Cannot Exist Without Matter

The proof that matter exists through potentiality and actuality shows that no body is without matter. Every body has some potentiality: it is not fully complete, or it is limited in its position, actions, or changes from one state to another. This is true even of the heavens. They may be actual in their substance, quantity, quality, place, position, and all their stable features, but they remain potential in how they are positioned in relation to other things. They cannot occupy every possible position at once.

We have seen that potentiality comes from something whose nature is pure receptivity and possibility. This must belong to bodily nature. As we have already shown, bodily substance does not exist on its own, and potentiality cannot exist on its own apart from what has it. The thing that has potentiality cannot be a separate intellect. Nor can it be the soul as soul, since a soul exists in relation to a body. The first and basic bearer of potentiality is body simply as body.

This is a strong proof. It does not depend on proving that all bodies share one specific kind, though that too is true. Avicenna gives another argument in *The Healing*. It begins from the claim that bodily nature is one reality shared by all bodies.

A body can differ from another body because one is hot and the other cold, or because one has a heavenly nature and the other has a different nature. But these qualities and natures come after the body has already become the kind of thing it is. Body as body does not need them in order to be body. This differs from size or quantity. Quantity does not have a complete nature until it is specified as a line, a surface, or a three-dimensional solid.

Quantity in general has no existence or stability unless it is a line, surface, or solid. Body as body is different. It can exist as body through the causes that produce it, without anything added except later stages of completion, such as being nonliving, plant-like, or animal. An inanimate nature, for example, can complete a body as a particular kind without needing another form. If another form is added, the body becomes more complete than it was. Bodily form works in the same way.

The forms that complete bodies therefore do not join them because bodily nature is a vague general kind. A general kind, simply as such, is not fully formed in thought or reality. Take quantity in general: if the mind removes all its distinguishing features, it has no existence outside the mind and is only something the mind uses to understand quantities.
