If quantity is taken without removing or adding any feature, it is still incomplete. Bodily nature is not like this. When we consider bodies with different forms and features after setting those differences aside, we still understand a complete substance that can receive dimensions. Reason allows us to think that it could exist in the world simply as that.

Bodily nature must therefore be a specific kind of thing. Otherwise, after setting aside the different forms and features, we would find either several separate natures, or different basic features other than those forms, or one nature different from the shared substance that receives dimensions, or that substance together with some other nature serving as its distinguishing feature. All these options are false. So all bodies share one specific nature: bodily nature, the substance that can receive dimensions.

Once this is established, many further points follow. One is that bodies need matter because they are bodies. Avicenna gives two ways to show that this applies even to bodies that cannot actually be joined or separated because something outside their extended nature prevents it—whether that obstacle belongs to them by nature, as in the heavens, or can later be removed, as in small hard bodies. First, consider extended nature itself. Either it needs no matter, or it does. If it needs no matter by its own nature, it cannot exist in something as a form, because to exist in a subject is to depend on that subject. If this independence were essential, nothing could take it away. Yet we know that extended nature exists in some bodies as something received in a subject. This contradicts the claim that bodily nature is independent of matter. If it is not independent by nature, then it depends on matter wherever it exists—whether the body can actually be divided or not.

Some people think this proof leads to impossible results: that many identical forms would be in one subject, one form would exist in every subject, one matter would receive every form, and every body would contain all forms and matters. But this objection misses the difference between a shared nature and one particular example of it. The general nature of body depends on general matter; the particular body depends on its own particular matter. The nature that needs matter is not the same as the nature in a particular body.

This is like triangles. The nature of a triangle requires its angles to equal two right angles, but each particular triangle has its own specific equality. The general nature requires general equality; each particular instance has a particular equality. So too, bodily nature in general needs a subject in general, while a particular body needs a particular subject. Particular instances and their features belong to the nature only in a general way.

Someone may ask: if bodily nature does not need a particular subject by its own nature, but only needs one because of some added feature, why could it not be the same with matter in general? We answer that if bodily nature, with all particular features removed, did not need matter at all, it would be complete and independent by itself. That would be its essential way of existing. The same nature cannot have different essential ways of existing because of causes outside it. This can happen only with accidental states and relations. For example, a person may need food because heat is using up the body’s material; that need comes and goes. A person’s essential independence from clothes does not prevent an unusual cold from making clothes necessary. But eating or wearing clothes is not part of the way a human being exists by nature.

This is what leads some people to imagine a middle ground between being dependent by nature and independent by nature. But a body’s dependence on a subject cannot begin later because of some other cause.[^V05-24] To exist in a subject is a way of existing for the thing that exists in it. Nor can something first depend on a subject and later become independent for the same reason. If bodily nature left its subject, it would take on a definite size and shape and be affected by an outside cause. If size and shape belonged to bodily nature by itself, they could not change while it remained the same. And whatever is changed by a cause must have something that receives the change.

We have already shown that what receives a change must be prime matter. So prime matter would have to be present along with bodily nature, though we have assumed it is separate from its subject. That is a contradiction.

The second argument begins from the fact that every extended body can be divided by actually separating its parts. We used this point earlier to refute Democritus, and completed the familiar proof without leaving it open to objection. Every body, simply as body, can be divided in reality, just as it can be divided in thought. This possibility is enough to show that it needs matter, even if some natural or other obstacle prevents the division, as with the heavens or the small bodies of the atomists.

If a body had no matter, it could not be divided in reality. But it can be, so its ability to be divided shows that matter exists in it. Avicenna adds that perhaps an obstacle that belongs to a body by nature would prevent two instances of that kind from existing. The kind would then exist only as one individual. If extended substance, simply as extended substance, had something in its nature that prevented division, it could not exist as more than one individual. If it did, each individual could be divided, contradicting the claim that division is impossible for it.

By reversing this argument, we can show that what prevents division is not part of bodily substance simply as bodily substance, even if it belongs to some bodies, such as the heavens. The heavenly form may prevent a body from being divided, but that is not part of the general nature of body. It belongs only to certain instances, so it is an added feature and may be removed in thought. The bodily nature of the heavens can be divided in itself, even if its heavenly nature prevents division. Whatever can be divided in reality is never separate from matter. This proves that all bodies need matter.

#### Discussion

If the natures of the heavens—their specific forms, as we will explain—prevent them from being divided, then each kind of heavenly body must exist as only one individual. This is their view. If there were two planets or heavenly spheres of one kind, parts from one could be joined in the same way as imagined parts within it, and parts of the two could be separated in the same way as the two bodies. So the nature of each kind would have to allow joining and separation, even though its nature is supposed to prevent them. They therefore say there cannot be two heavenly bodies of the same kind. The same applies to each heavenly body according to its special nature, though it can be divided as a body.

But there is a counterexample. A body with a planet, a small circle, or an off-center circle has two separate sides in one heavenly body. The two sides of a planet’s path or of the small circle are separate parts of one body. The two sides of an off-center circle are even more clearly distinct, like the two ends. If the proof against there being two heavenly bodies is sound, it should also show that one heavenly body can be torn apart and rejoined. That would contradict their claim that this kind of body cannot be divided or joined. If they answer that the body was made this way from the beginning, the same answer could be given for two bodies of one kind.

#### A Heavenly Insight

The Illuminationist thinker who wrote *The Discussions* raised this objection against the philosophers but did not answer it. God gave me a way to answer it. A heavenly nature does not run through a body in the way human nature does. The form that makes a heavenly body the particular kind it is does not exist in its matter as a form that completes it. Each heavenly body has a separate soul that gives it its features, positions, actions, and characteristic movements. The parts of a heavenly body, whether only imagined or actually separate, are parts of its bodily extension and matter, not parts of its specific heavenly nature. The body's extension can be divided and joined; what cannot split into two is that body's nature as a particular heaven.

So the problem is not that one heaven has become two heavens, or that one heaven has become two parts as a heaven. It is that one extended body has become two parts as a body. The two complementary parts are not two heavens, even though they are bodies associated with the heavens. That is why astronomers do not count them as separate heavens.

#### Chapter 2 — General Conclusions About This Question

One conclusion follows from what we have said: matter is not part of a thing’s essence. If it were, that would be clear without proof. We understand what a body is simply as body: a substance in which three dimensions can be imagined. We can still ask whether this substance has matter that bears it. Only after a proof do we know that another, material substance exists alongside it. We then see that a sensible body is made of two substances: one that exists in another, and the one that bears it. So matter is not part of the definition of body.

The same point applies to the forms that make bodies into particular kinds. A thing may need matter in order to exist without matter being part of what the thing is. Avicenna confirms this in *The Healing*. Bodily nature, he says, is already a complete nature in itself. It does not become a particular kind only when something is added to it. If we imagined that nothing were added to bodily nature, we would still understand it as matter and continuity. If we establish that continuity cannot exist on its own, we do so through other proofs. The fact that a nature cannot exist by itself does not mean that it is not complete as a nature. Black and white, for example, each have a complete and distinct nature, even though neither can exist except in matter.

Quantity in general is different. It cannot have a nature that points to something definite unless it is specified as a line or a surface. It is not that quantity first exists as quantity and only afterward becomes a line or surface. It must be one of these in order to exist at all.

Avicenna also says that bodily form, simply as bodily form, is one simple, complete nature. One bodily form does not differ from another through a difference that belongs to bodily nature itself. Anything that comes along with it comes from outside its nature. So there cannot be one bodily nature that needs matter and another that does not. Bodily nature needs matter because of what it is, not because of some added feature. It follows that bodies are made of matter and form.

Another conclusion concerns this rule: if one instance of a specific nature can exist without a subject by its very nature, then all instances of that nature can. Here “nature” must mean the nature as it exists in the world, and “instances” must mean actual things outside the mind. Otherwise the rule would fail for separate substances and forms, which can appear in the mind as ideas dependent on a mental subject. If the dependence of an idea in the mind meant that every instance of that nature needed a subject, then no substance could exist in the world, since substances are represented in the higher principles. That is impossible.

We have also adopted the early philosophers’ view that one specific nature can exist at different levels of dependence on a subject. So we cannot prove that heavenly bodies need prime matter merely by showing that bodies which can be split apart and joined need it. We must use one of the other two routes.[^V05-25]

#### Chapter 3 — Why Bodily Matter Cannot Exist Without Any Form

We can understand this point through one of the routes used to establish matter: the relation between potentiality and actuality.[^V05-26] If matter were stripped of every form, it would still exist as something actual and as something able to receive other things. It would then have one aspect by which it is actual and another by which it is only potential. That would make it composite, though we have assumed it to be simple. This is a complete proof, and careful examination leaves no objection to it.

Still, the philosophers add another step. They say that matter stripped of all form would have to be a body; but every body is made of matter and form, contrary to the assumption. Here is their reasoning. If stripped matter has a position and place, it must either be divisible—so it is one of the three kinds of magnitude, a line, surface, or solid, though we have removed magnitude and everything that depends on it—or it is indivisible, like the supposed atom. We have already shown that an indivisible atom cannot exist. A point is only a feature of something extended; it cannot exist as a separate thing occupying a place.

If matter has no position or direction, then either it cannot become extended, or it can. If it cannot, it would be an actual intellectual substance, not a material substance able to receive a body. If it can become extended, it must receive dimensions either all at once or gradually, as something moves into a particular place.

Suppose it receives dimensions all at once. If it is already in a particular place when the dimensions reach it, then it was already extended and located before it became extended. That is impossible. If it is not in a particular place beforehand, neither matter nor the dimensions themselves explain why it receives one place rather than another. Yet when the dimensions reach it, it must be in some place. It must then be either in every place or in one particular place.

The first option is impossible: one body can occupy only one place at a time. If it is in one particular place, something must have selected that place. Without a cause for that choice, one place would have no advantage over another.

This applies even to the matter of a clod of earth. In itself, it has nothing that would make it take the form of a clod in one particular place within the earth. Only a relation of position can single out that place. Other causes, qualities, and purposes do not do so: when the form is about to arrive, they stand in the same relation to this particular instance as they do to others. Only a positional relation can make one place the place for it.

Now suppose matter receives its full dimensions gradually, spreading out into them over time. Then it must already have a position and be a body. Anything that spreads through a magnitude has six directions and boundaries: up and down, front and back, left and right. A substance that has dimensions and boundaries is a body, though matter was supposed to be without bodily form.

There is another way to make the point. Even when considered apart from form, matter is a substance that can receive things. By its nature, it must be capable of receiving. But is it actually a receiver when stripped of form? If so, it must already have bodily form, which is impossible. If not, then it exists in itself without dimensions or quantity and has no parts, either actually or potentially.

When quantity later comes to it, however, it has parts potentially while remaining continuously one, and parts actually once it is divided. So the addition of quantity would change what matter is and how it exists. How could something whose nature excludes parts, both actually and potentially, later become something with parts, first potentially and then actually? A change in what makes a thing what it is cannot happen.

This is not like the way a definition relates to what it defines. The mind can consider a thing as a whole and then spell out its parts, such as its general kind and the feature that distinguishes it. The parts themselves have not come into or gone out of existence. The mind has only unfolded what it first grasped together. In the thing that is defined, its parts are present potentially; in the definition, they are made explicit. Quantity is similar: we can consider it while it is continuous or after it has been divided. These are two ways of understanding it, not a change in its nature. But a separate, immaterial substance cannot have parts of quantity, either actually or potentially. If it became extended, its very nature would have changed. Avicenna discusses this point at greater length in *The Healing*.

Bahmanyar gives another argument in *The Attainment*. If matter could receive a form that cannot be divided, that form would oppose bodily form. But bodily form has no opposite. If matter received no form at all, it would not be a substance able to receive; it would be a fully actual substance. If it could be pointed to, it would be a body, contrary to what we assumed. If it could not, the contradictions already described would follow.

There is also this argument: matter, through its readiness, is what makes it possible to divide a quantity in the outside world. If matter could exist stripped of bodily form, then actual division of quantities would not be possible. But actual division is possible. So matter cannot be separate from bodily form.

From Avicenna and his student we can draw an important distinction. Bodily form is not like an accident that comes to something after it has already achieved some kind of existence. Some features complete a subject in a further way, giving it a second standing as a particular kind. The heavenly shape of a body, for example, gives it a further kind of completeness. Other features do not add any completion, like the fact that a triangle’s angles equal two right angles. A triangle is complete whether or not we mention this feature. Recognizing or setting aside such a feature does not change the thing’s existence or completeness.

Natural forms are different. They complete their subject and give it one stage of completion after another. Bodily form itself does not fit either of those cases. There is no subject that exists on its own before bodily form, and then receives it as a further feature. Prime matter has no independent actual existence outside bodily form. In the world, its nature is to be the capacity for existence. This separates it from a subject considered in relation to an added feature, from second matter such as a body that can receive further natural forms, and from mere possibility or essence, which are not actual things in the outside world.

One more argument follows. If prime matter could remain after bodily form was removed, existing as an intellect without place or sensible direction, then after a body was divided its two halves would each have a separate matter. Stripped of dimensions and position, those portions could not differ from one another. So the assumption cannot stand.

To see why, consider the body before division: it has one matter. After it is divided, how could its matter differ from that of either half if bodily form were removed from each?

They could not differ in their nature or essential features, since those are the same in all matter. They could not differ in place or position, since these require a body. Nor could they differ in quantity, since quantity has been removed. If the two matters became one after separation, then the body before separation and the two parts afterward would have the same matter in every respect. It would make no difference whether something had been divided or not, or whether the matter was alone or with another.

Perhaps one of the matters is destroyed and the other remains. But if loss of bodily form destroys matter, that applies equally to both; there is no reason for one to be destroyed and not the other. They also cannot merge into one, since things without quantity cannot be joined in that way. The only remaining possibility is that one matter is part of the other. But then it would have to be an extended part of it, even though both are supposed to be without quantity. That is impossible.

This shows that prime matter, in itself, has no way of existing that is not bodily. Anything we picture in the mind as matter existing apart from a body is not the nature of matter as it really is. It is only an imagined construction.

When we say that matter is potential, we mean that anything that could exist as matter would be potential if it existed. Whatever the mind considers on its own and judges with certainty is being considered as something actual, not potential. The same applies to other things that have an incomplete kind of existence, such as number, time, motion, a letter, infinity, and contradiction.

Here is a related point. Anything that can receive dimensions cannot have a nature that is wholly indivisible and unable to receive division. Otherwise something whose existence is one and undivided would take on a divided existence: potentially divided, as in quantity, or actually divided, as in number. Reason rules this out. A sound mind therefore rejects the idea that bodily form could come to a separate intellect or an indivisible atom, if such an atom existed. The matter of bodily form and quantity must itself belong to the realm of extension and bodily existence.

Someone might then ask: if we consider the matter of a body before it receives bodily form, is it already continuous in itself? If so, it is the form of body, not its matter, because a body is nothing but a substance that is continuous in itself. It would then be complete as a body and would need no further bodily form. Or perhaps it is an immaterial intellect or an indivisible thing with a position. Both choices lead back to impossibilities we have already proved.

This is the central reason some philosophers deny that prime matter exists. We answer that this objection has already been resolved. It overlooks what matter is and how its existence depends on form. Bodily form does not relate to matter as an outside feature does to a thing that already exists. Nor is it like a further natural form that completes an already existing subject. Prime matter has no independent actual existence; form gives it its existence in the world and makes it complete. Matter is the form’s existence in a diminished and incomplete state—not a lesser essence, but a lesser degree of existence.

As already mentioned, later natural forms act on second matter, such as a body that can receive a more specific natural form, and give it a further stage of completion. Before the new form arrives, the body already exists in its own right as a particular kind of thing. It serves as a subject because it exists apart from the later form. It serves as matter because, in relation to that further completion, it is still incomplete and potential.

Prime matter is different. In itself it has none of these stages of completion or kinds of being. It has no reality apart from what is joined to it. First it exists through bodily form, then through further forms that complete it, as we will explain.

#### Chapter 4 — How Matter and Form Depend on Each Other, and Why Form Comes First

We have shown that bodily form cannot exist without matter, and that matter cannot exist without form. Their connection is essential, not accidental. Whenever two things are essentially bound together, their relation must be either one of mutual dependence, like paired relations, or one of cause and effect.

They are not simply paired relations. If we consider what each is in itself, neither can be understood only by reference to the other. The words “matter” and “form” are relational in meaning: matter is what can receive something, and form is what completes something. But we are asking about the realities themselves, apart from the meanings of their names. If we were only speaking about paired concepts, their connection would be obvious and would need no proof.

Could each then cause the other? No. There is a difference between two things each requiring the other to exist and two things each causing the other. The first relation is possible; the second is not. A third cause could bring about both, with one depending on the other. But if both arose separately from a third thing, with no further connection between them, that would not explain why they are essentially bound together.

So one of them must be closer to the third cause and must help bring about the other. It cannot be matter. Matter is receptive and ready; it does not act or produce. Something that is ready to receive an effect cannot be the cause that produces that effect.[^V05-27] If it could, then it would always produce the thing it was ready for, without first needing to be ready for it. Also, for matter to cause form it would have to exist actually apart from form. But matter by itself is only potentiality. It cannot cause what is actual.

From this it follows that a body cannot be the cause of existence—not as a whole and not through either of its parts. Matter is pure receptivity, and what includes matter cannot cause existence through that receptive aspect. Form also cannot cause something through matter. If form could act without matter, it would be even less in need of matter to exist. But existence is what makes causal action possible. We have already seen that form needs matter in order to act. So, if its action on something had to pass through matter, matter would be the immediate cause of that thing, which is impossible.

We can therefore say that bodies, considered in themselves, exist at the same level and are not essentially bound to one another. Still, they can become connected through later features and conditions. Bodies whose natural motion is straight, such as the elements, depend on the sphere that sets the directions—not because it is a heavenly body, but because it gives direction and motion. This points to a sacred, separate being, wholly free of bodies and their accidents. Bodies are limited in size and number, and an endless chain is impossible, so the chain ends in a being necessary in itself, neither a body nor something bodily.

This is one route to proving that the Necessary Being exists. Also, since every body is made of matter and form, if one body caused another, it would first have to cause the existence of its own two parts, which are prior to it. This cannot be. Matter does not differ in itself from one body to another. We have also shown that the actual existence of bodily form is the same across its instances. A cause must be higher in existence than its effect, but neither part of the body is higher than the other. That is a contradiction.

Someone might object that forms differ because the conditions of their matter differ. But this does not work for two reasons. First, these conditions would then be the earlier forms in matter, and the same question would arise again: how can one form cause another when matter only receives?

Second, even if we grant that forms differ because of conditions acquired through matter, then the causal relation would be between those conditions, not between bodily forms. That would be a different question, outside our present point.

In fact, on closer inspection, the forms cannot affect anything by themselves. A bodily form is a physical capacity. Just as it cannot exist as a particular without matter, it cannot make something else exist without matter. Existence is needed for causal action. Let us return, then, to our question.

If matter is not the means by which each comes from the other, form must be the means by which matter comes from a cause. But form cannot be an independent cause or an independent tool. Those options fail for the same reason that form cannot be an unrestricted cause, as we have seen. A tool is what a cause acts through on something close to it. A mediator is itself an effect that stands between the cause and what it brings about; it is the immediate cause of one and the remote cause of the other.

There are two kinds of bodily form. Some leave matter and are replaced by another form. Others remain with it. The first kind cannot be an independent cause of matter. If it caused the matter from which it then departed, the matter would disappear when the form did. The next form would need another matter already there to receive it. That would mean each matter had to come from an earlier matter, and the same question would repeat without end. This is impossible, since we are asking about the first matter. It would also make matter the form of another matter.[^V05-28]

The problem grows if we ask about matter before form: the cause must exist as a particular before its effect exists. But a specific form that can appear in many individuals becomes particular through features that can change. Every change of this kind requires something receptive, and reception requires matter. If matter must already exist for the form to become particular, then matter would have to be there before it came to be. That is impossible.

Form must therefore be one part of a complete cause, working with another particular cause to give matter its existence. Matter comes from that cause through an unspecified form: when one form leaves, another similar form takes its place and does for matter what the earlier one did. The giver continually provides forms of the same broad kind, so matter always has a form through which it can exist. We will describe this Giver of Forms later. It must be a spiritual substance; otherwise, we would have to continue asking what causes it until we reached a sacred, separate being.

Since form serves as a means of making matter complete, it must first be complete in itself—in the order of dependence, though not necessarily earlier in time. It is actual either through itself or through its source; then it makes matter actual. Form’s existence is therefore prior to matter’s. Bodily form gives the body its actuality and completion. Its potentiality and incompleteness belong to matter. Matter becomes actual through form. Form is first as form, then matter is actual as matter, and then the particular form is actual as this form.

The second kind of form—the kind that always remains with matter, since no cause of separation or joining has arisen—is, at first, something one might think could be a direct cause or tool that makes matter complete. But reason and proof rule this out. If that form directly caused one matter and not another, though both had the same specific nature, there would have to be a reason for choosing one. We have also seen that individual extended substances become particular only through receptive features that are not essential to their nature. Otherwise their form would exist on its own like an external intellectual form. If this form were the complete cause of matter, it would have to exist before matter, complete in itself and through the causes that make it particular. These two points would mean that matter existed before itself by two levels of dependence. That is impossible.

There is another proof. Causal action depends on existence. The particular existence of bodily form depends on matter, because it comes through receptive conditions that arise and change over time. These conditions have specific features that allow them to continue and persist. They also share a common feature that always belongs to the form’s nature. In that respect, only matter is receptive and dependent. Yet this common feature is general and never exists apart from a succession of similar forms and passing events. Whatever depends on such events for its particular existence depends on matter, even if only indirectly. How could it cause the existence of something it needs in order to become particular, when particularity itself comes before causal action?

Particularity is either the same as existence, as the stronger view holds, or follows it so closely that both are present together. If bodily form caused matter, matter would have to exist either before form or before something that exists together with form. What is prior to something that exists with a later thing is also prior to that later thing. Matter would then be prior to itself by two steps, an impossibility.

There is a further problem. A particular form always has a definite limit and shape. These are features of matter. If they belonged to bodily nature itself, then every body would have the same limit and shape. But they differ. So these features, which come from matter, must either exist at the same level as the particular bodily form or before it. If they come before it, then what comes before what is already prior to a thing is also prior to that thing. If the form caused matter, it would have to precede what precedes it, once or twice over. It would be prior to itself by two or three steps. Since that is impossible, the assumption that form causes matter is also impossible.

So neither kind of bodily form can be an independent cause, mediator, or tool for bringing matter into existence. Form can only work together with the active cause as one part of a complete cause.

#### Chapter 5 — How an Effect Can Exist Alongside Its Cause

We have learned that form comes first in the order of existence, and matter comes second. Causation means that one thing is prior to another in the order of existence. Form exists in matter by being present in it, so form and matter exist together. This does not prevent form from giving existence to matter or matter from receiving existence from form.

When a cause is fully at work, its effect follows from it, and the effect exists alongside the cause. Existing at the same time does not mean being equal in rank or independence. In the same way, when form exists as form, something else follows from it and exists alongside it. Sometimes a thing gives existence to another that remains separate from it; sometimes it gives existence to something that is joined to it. Reason allows both kinds of cause, and reality confirms them.

The same is true of substances and their accidents. A substance, once it has become actual in itself, can serve as the means by which accidents exist in it.

#### Chapter 6 — How One General Nature Can Cause One Particular Thing

We have established that form and matter do not exist at the same level: form has a degree of priority that matter lacks. We have also seen that form is not a complete cause or an active cause of matter. There must be something beyond form on which matter depends and from which it receives existence. This must be a divine, luminous substance and a sacred act. It cannot be a soul, much less a form or bodily nature. Yet its giving cannot be complete without form. The work is done by the two together. With the permission of the Lord of separate forms, this intellectual light gives bodily form and makes matter actual through form taken in its general sense. It does not make form actual through matter.

Matter has no reality of its own apart from forms, as we have said. The way this works is that when one particular form leaves the matter it had occupied, the source preserves that matter by bringing another form after it. The next form is like the previous one in being a form, and in that respect it works with the sacred source to preserve matter. But because the next form differs from the one before it, it makes matter actual as a substance different from the one that existed through the earlier form. Form is therefore, in one respect, a means between cause and effect. It depends on its cause and is prior to matter in the order of existence. It is more fully realized and established than matter, the pure potentiality it makes actual.

You may ask how one general nature can be the source of one particular existence, or how a unity shared by many can cause one individual thing, when a cause should be more fully realized and more particular than its effect.[^V05-29]

There are two answers. The first, given by Avicenna and other early philosophers, is that reason allows a general unity, whose oneness is preserved, to work with one numerically particular thing as the cause of another particular thing. Here the unity of the kind is preserved by one numerically particular separate being. That separate being is the true primary cause. Its causal action is completed when one of a number of accompanying forms is added, whichever form it may be. This does not multiply the separate cause. Rather, the particular form creates a suitable connection between what is actual in every way—the separate cause—and what is pure potentiality in itself.

In short, the general nature of extended forms is the direct cause of matter. No particular form’s individual features enter into this causal action except indirectly. The complete cause of matter is therefore made up of a general nature together with one particular, continuing cause. It remains one particular cause; it does not multiply as the individuals of the general nature multiply. The individual forms are not parts of the cause. In this way, one particular cause can give rise to one particular effect.

The second answer occurred to me, with God’s help. Matter is not a fully particular individual in the way people suppose. Its individuality is of another kind, and its particularity is incomplete. It is determined by forms in general, not by one particular form. Its particularity is therefore like that of a mental idea of a general kind, when the mind considers that idea in itself and sets aside the features that distinguish its subclasses.

Matter’s individual unity is not so fixed that it cannot depend on the shared, specific unity of form. Rather, matter becomes particular through that unity, as reflection makes clear. Its need for a separate sacred cause does not come from the way it becomes actual or particular, as though it required a numerically particular cause rather than a general one. Form needs that cause in order to remain in existence, together with one of its particular instances, as we have said.

This becomes clearer if we consider how matter and form depend on each other for their particularity without falling into an impossible circle. Matter is prior in its nature and particularity to the particular form that exists in it. But matter becomes particular through the general form itself, not through the particular identity of the form. Forms, by their natures and not by their individual identities, are prior both to the particularity and to the nature of matter. A particular form, in turn, depends on matter that has become particular through form—not on a particularity that existed before form. Keep this distinction in mind.

#### Discussion

You may ask: if every specific nature that can have many real instances becomes particular through matter, then matter itself must become particular through another matter. This would lead to an endless chain of matter. If instead matter becomes particular through form, we have a circle.

The answer is that the two claims mean different things. A form becomes particular through matter in the sense that it depends on matter as something able to receive particularity, or on the features that come with matter and make the form particular. Matter’s nature is receptivity and readiness; it does not itself cause particularity. That is what the philosophers mean when they say that any kind that can have many instances needs matter in order to become particular: it needs something receptive that can receive this particular existence.

But how could what receives particularity also cause it? Matter does not. The near cause of a form’s particularity is the set of features around it—its position, place, time, and similar features—which are called the marks of particularity. By contrast, matter becomes particular through the general form because form acts and gives particularity, while matter receives it. So neither an endless chain nor a circle follows.

#### Discussion and Resolution

Someone may object that matter and form cannot each become particular through the other unless they are joined. But for them to be joined, each must already be particular, since what is only general does not exist and cannot be joined to anything.

One reply is to reject this assumption by comparing the case to existence and essence: their union does not wait for each to exist separately first. But that comparison does not work. The claim that they must first exist separately is obvious here, while existence and essence are not two different things joined together outside the mind. Their difference comes from the mind’s analysis, as we have already explained.

The better reply is to reject the first assumption: it is not true that anything considered in general is nonexistent. What does not exist is a general thing considered specifically as universal, with universality as one of its features. But a thing considered without deciding whether it is general or particular—without adding either universality or particularity, and without considering their absence—does exist, according to the philosophers who hold that natures exist in the outside world through the existence of their individual instances.

So an essence can be considered without making it universal or particular, and it can also be considered specifically as universal. These are clearly different ways of thinking about it. Considered in the first way, it exists both in the world and in the mind. Considered in the second, it exists only in the mind. Our argument needs the first sense, not the second. So what the argument requires is not a problem; the problem someone imagines does not follow.

The two replies just discussed—one acceptable, the other not—were given by al-Tusi and Fakhr al-Din al-Razi. Perhaps al-Razi used his reply because he did not accept that natures exist in the outside world.

#### Addendum

Matter and form each cease to exist if the other ceases. But this does not mean that they have the same rank in existence. The same is true of any cause and its effect. When a cause is removed, its effect is removed. But when an effect is removed, the cause does not have to be removed with it. If the cause is also removed, it is prior to the effect in the order of dependence, even if both cease at the same time.

Here the order is reversed in this respect: the effect’s removal comes later, while the cause’s removal comes first in the order of dependence. Yet they can still cease at the same moment in time. It is like the movement of a hand and a key it turns: the hand’s movement is the cause, and the key’s movement follows it; when the hand stops, the key stops with it. In reasoning, we can consider the order in either direction, as in proofs that proceed from cause to effect or from effect to cause.

We have discussed this before in the discussion of nonexistence.

### Part Four — The different natures of physical bodies and the forms that make them what they are

#### Chapter 1 — What the Term Means

The philosophers in Aristotle’s school held that each kind of natural body has, besides extension and the capacity to receive dimensions, a form through which it becomes a particular kind. They called this a *specific form*—the form that establishes and completes a kind. What belongs to one kind of body differs from what belongs to the others.

They also saw this form as the source of a body’s distinctive effects and of the movements and rests that belong to it by nature. In that respect they called it a power or a nature. They also called it a perfection, because it turns a general kind into a particular, composite kind. These are different names for one thing found in every natural body. Any one of these descriptions can help show that it is a substance and lead us to understand what it is.

#### Chapter 2 — Proving It from Its Role as the Source of Movement and Effects

There is no doubt that different effects, each belonging to a different kind of body, must come from different principles within those bodies. We know that bodies have different effects. Water and air, for example, separate and join easily; earth and fire do so with difficulty; the heavenly bodies do not separate or join at all. Each kind of heavenly or earthly body also has its own shape, place, position, and natural quality. It tends to keep these once it has them, seeks them again when something forces it away from them, and returns to them as quickly as it can once the force is gone.

We also know that a heavy element moves toward the center by its own nature, while a light element moves upward toward the heavens by its own nature—or through some special principle belonging to each and included in what it is.

These principles cannot be bodily extension shared by all bodies, or their effects would all be the same. Nor can they be matter, which, as we have learned, is purely receptive; the question is what produces the effects. Matter is also one and the same in the earthly elements, so this would lead to the same problem as before: all bodies in this world would have the same effects.

They cannot be separate intellects either, since a separate intellect has the same relation to every body. Nor can these effects come directly from God without any intermediary. His relation to all things is one, while the more perfect things proceed from Him before the less perfect. This does not conflict with saying that God acts freely, so long as we do not allow a choice with no reason behind it or reject wisdom and order. When certain bodies have particular qualities and effects, the Wise Creator must give them some determining principle. That principle is what we call the specific form.

In fact, people who say that God’s free choice means a will with no reason or wisdom leave no room to prove specific forms—or any unseen powers and qualities, and even the qualities we can sense. If such arbitrary will is possible, we cannot rely on what we sense, reflect on premises, or reason about things we cannot observe directly. We could not even be sure that our minds had not been made to see a valid argument as invalid, or an invalid one as valid, or to see everything as the opposite of what it is.

Many people hold this view because they have turned away from wisdom. Their earlier bad actions, habits, and character—love of status, the wish to outdo their peers, and other deep-rooted faults—have made them unwilling to learn and hostile to wisdom. They fear that someone will unsettle the false beliefs they received from their teachers by imitation, without understanding. Those beliefs, together with their habits and their attachment to shallow ideas, have led them to embrace what they call the “free agent.” May God protect those seeking the next life from their harm to faith. They block the road to the next life for Muslims. In this community, they play the role that the sophists played among the ancients, as anyone who considers the matter fairly can see.

One careful thinker said that views like these drove wisdom from the face of the earth and caused sacred knowledge to fade. There are several objections to the argument just given.

The first objection is this: why could the principles behind these particular effects not be particular accidents? Not every cause of an effect in a body has to be a substantial form. Forced and natural inclinations can cause movement, yet they are not substantial forms. Heat in a hot piece of iron causes something else to burn. In some cases, movement causes heat. Neither heat nor movement is a substantial form. There are many such cases in which accidents produce effects. Why could the forms you speak of not be accidents of this kind?

Someone might reply: these accidents do not produce the effects; they only prepare things for another cause. But the same could be said of what you call forms. This is supported by what Avicenna says in one of his works. He argues that nature cannot be the direct source of qualities and accidents that arise in the matter within which it exists—such as the movements and qualities, including heat and cold, that are commonly attributed to fire and water. He writes: “The source of a bodily action exists and is sustained through a body. It cannot act without a positional relation between itself and what it acts upon.”

If powers impressed in bodies cannot act without their bodies serving as intermediaries, then nature, which is itself a bodily power, can act only through its body. But an action mediated by its body can be completed only in things outside that body, not in the body itself. How could nature act on its own body, when its body would have to serve as the intermediary? A body cannot be an intermediary between the nature within it and that very body. So nature cannot directly act on its own body.

When we say that nature is the source of movement, heat, or cold, we mean something else. When a nature arises in a body, it prepares the body to receive these effects. Once the body is fully prepared, the Giver of Forms bestows them on it, by the permission of its Creator, whose power is exalted. Nature itself comes to the body only after the body has been made ready to receive it. In the same way, other powers come to the body only after it is fully prepared to receive them. The readiness becomes complete after nature has arisen in the body. Since nature must be present for that readiness to develop, people call it the cause or source of the effect.

The same is true elsewhere. If we look closely, we find that some states and qualities exist before others, and that their presence is a condition for the later ones. Matter becomes fully ready to receive one thing because another, which serves as a condition for that readiness, is already present. In this sense, the earlier thing is called the cause of the later one. The relation of the soul to its powers and qualities is like the relation of nature to the effects just described. That is the end of Avicenna’s statement.

His point is clear: these things prepare bodies for effects; they do not produce the effects themselves. If the heat in fire and the cold in water are not caused by something present in those bodies, the same might be true of heat produced in another body by fire, or cold produced in another body by water. The same could be said of other effects: turning food into a substance like the living thing that eats it, growth, reproduction, and taking on a particular form.

So all that has been established about what they call forms is that they are conditions that prepare bodies for effects. They could still be accidents, like inclination, heat, and cold. The powers of nutrition, growth, and formation are accidents too, yet philosophers call them active and, in the account given by Aristotle’s followers, credit them with producing forms and other effects. If they can call these powerful agents accidents, then the principles under discussion are even more likely to be accidents.

Here is the reply. The argument for specific forms rests on the claim that different effects in bodies require different principles, and that these principles must be substances. The argument does not depend on whether the principles act as efficient causes or merely prepare things for action. Different effects still require different determining principles. If those principles belong essentially to bodies and are the basis of their essential differences—so that bodies have different natures because of them—then the principles are substances. If they are accidents, like other effects that arise in bodies, they need further principles to determine them.

We must then ask what determines those principles. Either this continues without end, or it circles back on itself, or it ends with principles that are not effects of bodies but substantial forms that establish different kinds of natural body. The first two possibilities are impossible, so the third must be true. That is what we set out to prove.

When philosophers say that some powers or qualities act and produce effects, they are speaking loosely. They have already explained that the true cause of existence must be free of any tie to matter. They also say that what is called nature’s action consists in movement and change in bodies; other effects come about through an intermediary, and the Giver of Forms is their source. This point is missed only by those with little understanding of philosophy, such as physicians, materialists, astrologers, and their followers.

The second objection is this: we cannot assume that a separate intellect relates equally to every body. Perhaps it has a special relation to some bodies and not to others. Even if its relation were equal, it would not follow that it could not produce different effects. Different bodies and their matter may have different capacities to receive them, just as a separate intellect bestows different perfections on them.

The reply is that if bodies all share the same bodily nature, each must still have some special readiness, condition, or other feature that gives it this capacity. Readiness, like potentiality, is not itself an actual thing; it arises from some particular feature firmly present in the body that is ready. If that feature is an accident, it must be an accident of this particular body. But the question is what makes this body particular. If its particularity is itself an accident that comes after the body exists, the same question arises again. This leads to an endless chain or a circle. So in the end, the particular feature must belong to the body itself and make it what it is.

Once the matter is understood in this way, no one can say: if bodies need specific forms to explain their different qualities and effects, then we still have to explain why each body has its own specific form, given that all bodies share the same bodily nature. The forms themselves explain how a body with general bodily nature becomes a particular kind. Their existence makes the general bodily nature into a specific kind; their individual identities make up the particular natures of those kinds.

Most people miss this subtle point, and so they get confused about the distinguishing features of both composite and simple kinds. They ask what makes “rational” belong to the animal nature in a human being, or “neighing” belong to the animal nature in a horse. They do not see that animal nature is a general feature that follows from what makes a human rational or a horse neigh. The better question is why a distinguishing feature brings its general kind with it, not what makes it a particular share of that kind.

The third objection is that a separate intellect might have different aspects, through which it relates differently to different bodies and gives them different effects. Or there might be many separate intellects, one for each kind of body.

This was the view of the early philosophers, including Plato and his teachers Socrates, Empedocles, Pythagoras, Agathodaemon, Hermes, and other great Greek and Persian sages, the masters of the wisdom of the East and the pillars of Persian philosophy. As the divine philosopher Suhrawardi reports in works such as *The Paths and the Discussions* and *The Wisdom of Illumination*, they held that each kind of heavenly body, star, simple element, and compound body has a guardian in the sacred world: an intellect that governs and cares for that kind. They held the guardian to be the source of nourishment, growth, and reproduction in living bodies. In their view, these varied activities cannot arise in plants from one simple, unconscious power, or in us from our own souls; otherwise we would be aware of them.

These sages would be surprised by someone who says that the remarkable colors in a peacock’s feathers are due only to the differing mixtures within each feather, with no universal cause, fixed order, governing intellect, or guardian of that kind. The great Greek and Persian sages trace every kind of body and its features to such guardians. They say that complex structures, mixtures, and other features are shadows of the spiritual light that shines from these luminous guardians. Even a simple feature, such as the scent of musk, is the shadow of a luminous form in the guardian of its kind.

They also say that oil is drawn toward the flame in a lamp neither because nature abhors a vacuum, as they explain elsewhere, nor because fire has a special power of attraction. It happens through the overall governance of the guardian of the fire’s kind, who preserves its shape and other features.

The Persians called this guardian *Ardibehesht*. The Persians were especially emphatic about the guardians of kinds and their governing powers.

Hermes and Agathodaemon did not offer a proof for these guardians. They said instead that they had repeatedly witnessed them directly, through spiritual exercises, discipline, observation, and the shedding of their bodily attachments. If they speak from such experience, we cannot debate them as though they had made an ordinary argument. Followers of Aristotle do not dispute what Hipparchus and Ptolemy reported observing with their instruments; Aristotle himself relied on observations made in Babylon.

If the observations of one person, or a few people, made with physical instruments about the heavens are accepted—and later observers build astronomy and astrology on them—then we should also take seriously what the leading philosophers and sages say they saw through the mind, during their seclusion and spiritual practice: many intellects and their luminous forms and relations. These are like luminous spheres, of which the visible heavens are shadows. Their positions and other features are shadows of the intellectual forms and their luminous relations, as we have explained before.

The defender of Aristotle’s school of his school says there is no proof that the number of intellects is limited to ten, fifty, or any other number in the vertical chain of cause and effect. Nor must the spheres be arranged in the same order as the intellects. There may be many intellects in a vertical order, with another layer of intellects arranged side by side, like branches growing from higher roots. The celestial and earthly bodies—simple and compound—come from these branches, from the noblest down to the least noble. Both groups are numerous, as the Qur'an says: “None knows the hosts of your Lord except Him.”

They also say that the guardian of a kind is not the soul of that kind. A soul suffers when its body suffers, but the guardian does not suffer when the kind suffers. A soul is linked to one body; the guardian cares for every body of its kind. The soul and the body it governs together make up one animal of one kind, but the guardian of a kind is not like this. And if the guardian gives existence to that kind, it does not need the kind in order to become more complete. A soul, by contrast, needs a body in order to develop. Its relation to a body comes from a lack in the soul’s own being, which it makes up through that relation. But a being that creates a body does not depend on the body’s relation or distractions. A separate cause is complete by becoming like the First Cause, whose existence depends on nothing. A bodily relation would be a deficiency for it. The principle that gives a body its kind and brings it into existence cannot itself be confined by an accidental relation to that body. This is clear to anyone with even a little insight.

Here is our reply. We agree with you that separate intellectual forms give existence to the different kinds of body. We have discussed this in our account of essences, defended the existence of Plato’s luminous Forms, and established the point at length. Even so, we know directly that the effects we observe come from bodies around us, or from a separate cause through a near, accompanying principle that directly governs their movements and effects. Fire burns, air moistens, water cools, and earth solidifies, and so on. If bodies had nothing but matter and bodily form, they could not produce these effects.

Indeed, Suhrawardi himself proves in works such as *The Illuminations* that a purely separate being cannot directly move a body as its immediate cause. It acts at a distance, like a final cause that draws an active cause toward itself. A teacher’s soul, for example, moves the student’s body because the student’s soul is drawn to the teacher and seeks to become like him.

For this reason, the philosophers say that the motions of the heavenly bodies need intellectual principles as their goals, drawing them through desire, and bodily movers—powers and natures—that directly move them through changing activity and response. Otherwise there would be no likeness at all between what gives and what receives, and no middle term between what is purely separate and what is purely bodily.

So bodies, too, must contain powers that receive influence from separate principles and act within material bodies. These can only be bodily powers, plant souls, or animal souls. All are called specific forms, though they differ in rank and ability.

#### Chapter 3 — Another Route to Proving Natural Forms: They Make Matter What It Is

The proof is this. We know directly that every kind of body has something specific to it besides matter and bodily extension, something that cannot be separated from it. It must be either a feature that depends on a subject or a substance that makes a body the kind of thing it is. It cannot be merely a dependent feature, because it makes something that cannot stand on its own into a complete thing. A principle that gives such completion is a form, as we have already seen. Just as matter cannot exist without bodily extension, it cannot exist without becoming a particular kind of body. We cannot picture a body that is neither a heavenly body nor an element, an animal, or a tree. So general body exists only through a specific form. The form makes body actual in a particular way. Since what makes a substance complete in its own existence must itself be a substance, the form that specifies body must be a substance.

The form must either exist in matter or have matter existing within it. The second is impossible: our direct experience shows that it is not so, and it would reverse the relation between matter and form. When one form replaces another, as happens among the elements, matter remains in some way through both states. So the form exists in matter. A substance that exists in matter is a form. That is what we set out to prove.

The Stoics object to this argument in several ways. The first objection is that the argument moves too quickly from a body’s need for specific forms—because it cannot exist without them—to the claim that those forms are substances. Even if these forms have a nature and exist in their own way, their inseparability from a body does not show that they are substances. A body cannot exist without some quantity, shape, or place, though you admit that these are properties dependent on a body rather than things that exist on their own. It also cannot exist without unity or multiplicity, which you too regard as accidents.

You cannot reply that these features can change while their subject remains, so they are accidents. The same applies to forms that change in matter while matter itself remains. The only proofs you have offered for matter’s need for form are that matter cannot be imagined without some form or other. That does not show that it cannot exist without one particular form, or without any form at all. In the same way, a body cannot exist without some shape or other, some quantity or other, and so on.

Also, if the fact that general body cannot exist in the world except through a specific form proves that the form makes body what it is, then the features that make a particular kind—such as humanity—specific, and that distinguish one individual from another, should also make humanity what it is. That is an even stronger case: what makes a kind specific completes it more fully than what makes a general kind specific. If you call what specifies general body a form, why not call what specifies a particular kind a form too, since the kind cannot exist without it?

The author of *The Paths and the Discussions* gives a series of related questions and answers in defense of those who say that forms are accidents. To give the reader the full shape of the debate, we will first set them out and then state what we think is right.

He asks: “The features that specify kinds depend on the kind they specify, even though they make it specific. Why not say the same of the forms in bodies?” The answer is: “They depend on bodily nature and make it specific, just as you say that features which specify kinds depend on the kinds they specify.”

He asks: “A specific kind is complete in itself.” The answer is: “The same can be said of general bodily nature.” If you argue that body is incomplete because it needs specific forms, then a human being is incomplete too, because it needs features that make it a specific kind.

He asks: “If humanity were limited to one individual, it would need no feature to distinguish one individual from another.” The answer is: “If body were limited to one individual, it would need no feature to distinguish one body from another.”

He asks: “A body must be somewhere, or have a position or place.” The answer is: “If we supposed that only a human being, a tree, or some other bodily kind existed, each would still have to have a position and a certain size. If we suppose that body alone exists, with no place or position, then if this is impossible, it is equally impossible for only one body to exist. The same applies to a single kind such as humanity or trees.”[^V05-30]

He asks: “Nothing in the nature of a human being as human prevents there from being only one human being in existence; if something prevents it, that cause is external.” The answer is: “The same can be said of body precisely as body.”

He asks: “The features that make a kind specific arise from external causes or happen by chance; they are not part of the kind’s essence.” The answer is: “The forms you call specific also come to bodies or matter from external causes. Matter itself does not require that it become water or air. Some such forms come to it for external reasons, and they do not make up the essence of what bears them.”

He asks: “The forms make what bears them complete in existence, unlike the features that distinguish kinds.” The answer is: “The whole question is how you know that the forms you call specific make body complete in existence. If you know it because they make body specific, the same can be said of the features that make kinds specific. If these features do not help make a thing complete, you cannot use this argument in cases such as the plurality of necessary beings, where you say that each needs a distinguishing feature. You would first have to establish that the feature is an essential difference or an external addition.” That is where his discussion ends.

#### A Conclusion from the Throne

To answer this objection, we must first establish a principle. There are two kinds of features that matter can take on. The first kind are perfections through which matter develops and toward which nature tends. For example, a natural body becomes a plant, then an animal, then a human being. The plant form perfects earthly bodily nature and makes it a particular kind. It then has effects different from those that follow from general bodily nature, such as natural shape, place, size, and position.

The animal form—the soul that senses and moves—completes the body more fully than the plant power. Once the body is prepared to receive it, it becomes a living, growing thing, more complete and nobler than it was in the two earlier stages. It has the general features of bodies: shape, place, size, and sensible qualities. It also has the features particular to plants: growth, nutrition, and reproduction. And it has the features particular to animals: walking, appetite, anger, eating, mating, and the rest. This continues until the body reaches its highest perfection, beyond which it has no further bodily perfection. At each level it has both the general and the particular features of that level.

The second kind are features that do not perfect matter. They cannot be final goals, or even intermediate goals, toward which nature moves. They are more like necessary features of bodily existence in general: a particular size, shape, or place.

Bodily nature itself calls for such features, without thereby becoming one particular kind of thing. They multiply individuals; they do not make new kinds. They distinguish one human being from another, or one horse from another, just as they distinguish one body, plant, or animal from another. We hold, along with the careful philosophers in Aristotle’s school, that the first kind are specific forms and bodily natures. The second kind are external accidents.

The first kind are prior in existence to the natural body considered as matter, and they make the kinds of body they belong to complete. They must therefore be substances: what makes a substance complete in its existence and gives it its definite nature must itself be a substance. The second kind come after a kind is already complete and attach to it as further features, so they are accidents. Think this through carefully.

#### A Rule from the Throne

When something has a natural unity and is made up of two parts, one of which is certainly a substance and the other of which may or may not be, we can work out the second part’s status by looking at its rank and strength of existence. Consider the effects that follow from its existence as such. If it exists more fully than the part already known to be a substance, as its effects seem to show, then it is more properly the source of the whole’s completion and the part that is prior and causal. The part whose status is already known stands in the opposite relation to it. This assumes that every combination into a natural kind or subkind must have some relation of priority and dependence between its two parts. Without that, it would be a kind or subkind only because the mind happened to put it together.

But if the part whose status is uncertain has a weaker existence than the part known to be a substance, and a lower rank, then it does not complete the whole. It must exist after and depend on the other part, and be caused by it. In that case it is either matter or an accident that exists in the other part. If it were a substance that needed nothing from the other, and the other substance also needed nothing from it, there would be no natural bond or unity between them. If the uncertain part is an accident that exists in something else, we are back where we started. Think carefully about this.

#### Chapter 4 — A Third Method for Showing That the Specific Form Is a Substance

The specific forms are parts of the essences of natural bodies. When they change, the answer to “What is it?” changes too. So they are not accidents. When accidents change, they do not change what the thing is at its core. Whatever changes the answer to what a thing is must be a substance, not an accident; otherwise a substance would receive its definite nature from an accident.

The ancient philosophers reply that some accidents do change the answer to “What is it?” Before a piece of iron is made into a sword, if we ask what it is, the answer is “iron,” or “a piece of iron.” Once it has the form of a sword, we do not answer “iron”; we answer “a sword.” Yet it has acquired only accidents, such as shape and sharpness.[^V05-31] The same is true of clay made into bricks and used to build a house. We no longer answer “clay”; we answer “a house.” But the clay has acquired only the accidents of arrangement and shape. This shows that a change in the answer to “What is it?” does not tell us whether what has changed is a substance or an accident. So this line of proof fails.

No one can say that these shapes are substances; that would go against common sense. Nor can a collection of accidents become a substance simply by being joined together. Wasn’t a garment made from cotton, with twisting and weaving added to it? Once it has become a garment, if we ask what it is, we cannot answer “cotton”; we have to say “a garment.” The same is true of other examples. So some accidents do change the answer to “What is it?” A change in the answer does not show whether the thing that changed is a substance or an accident. This route of proof therefore fails.

#### Another Discussion

The usual definitions of substance and accident, and the distinctive feature of each, do not depend on whether the answer to “What is it?” changes. The proper distinction is this: the subject of an accident is complete in itself, while the subject of a substantial form is made complete by the form it receives. An accident exists in a subject that does not depend on that accident. A form exists in matter that depends on the form.

But this does not settle the matter, because this dependence is dependence in existence, not dependence in essence. That is different from what is being argued here. A form cannot be part of the essence of the matter in which it exists. How could something be a part of the definition of what bears it? What we understand by the nature of a subject is not the same as what we understand by one of its features. If a subject needs the form it receives, it needs it to complete its existence, not to complete its essence.

So the question returns to the earlier method: does a subject need some features that cannot be separated from it, and not others, only because they make it specific and cannot be absent? Or is its own nature incomplete, so that it needs a substantial form to complete it? This is the same point we have already examined from both sides.

That is all the defender of the ancient view has to say against the philosophers in Aristotle’s school in this discussion. We will postpone our reply until we have presented another route to the same conclusion, with similar proofs and objections. We will also mention the objection made by some followers of those who deny specific forms, and give an answer suited to readers who want a closer analysis.

Then we will return to the proof God has shown us, the insight He has opened to our hearts through His mercy and favor. We will offer some of it without hesitation, even if it departs from the familiar view.

#### Chapter 5 — A Fourth Method for the Same Point

These forms are parts of the essence of each kind of substance. The essence of fire, for example, is not just bodily nature; it is made up of body and something else, and together they make the reality of fire. The same is true of water, air, stone, plants, animals, and other things.

An opponent may say that a part of a substance must be a substance in every respect, while a part that is a substance in only one respect is not. The first claim is accepted; the second is not. A white man, or a hot body, can be called a substance: we can say “this white thing is a substance” or “this hot thing is a substance,” because each is a substance in one respect, not in every respect. In the same way, we can say that water is a substance because it is a body and because it bears a form. Water is not a substance in every respect; it is made up of a substance and an accident. We call the whole a substance because of one of its parts, just as we do with the white man or hot body.

It should be clear that you cannot know water without knowing its parts. You cannot judge that water is a substance unless you already know that its parts are substances. So knowledge that its parts are substances must come before the judgment that water is a substance in every respect, rather than a combination of substance and accident. Once this is clear, the argument that “a part of a substance is a substance in every respect” assumes the very point it is meant to prove.

If substancehood belongs to the parts of a thing’s parts, then just as we cannot understand a whole without its parts, we cannot understand those parts without their own parts. What comes before something that is already prior must itself come before that thing. We have established that a part of a substance is a substance. So water cannot be understood unless its parts are understood, and its parts cannot be understood except as substances. If so, then whenever we understand water as a substance, we already understand that all its parts are substances. How can we then use an argument to prove that one of its parts is a substance?

In short, you cannot prove that a part of water is a substance unless you have already established that water is a substance in every respect, rather than a combination of substance and accident. But you can establish that water is a substance in every respect only after you have proved that each of its parts is a substance. The argument therefore proves its conclusion by relying on that same conclusion.

This completes the account given by the author of *The Paths and the Discussions* on behalf of the ancient philosophers. We have kept his wording, since changing it would add nothing when the aim is simply to convey the meaning. Now, as God wills, let us give the answer we promised.

#### Chapter 6 — Answering the Objection to the Last Two Arguments

First, keep in mind a principle accepted by careful thinkers: a natural kind has a real unity of its own. It is not a collection brought together by human work, like a house or a tool. So a natural kind cannot be made from parts that belong to different basic categories. This applies both to simple elements and to natural compounds. Their forms come to their matter from the Giver of Forms when the matter has become ready through its changes and through the natural causes and powers within it.

Every division into basic categories respects this unity. A thing belongs to one category: substance, quantity, quality, or another category. A supposed compound of substance and quantity is not itself a substance or a quantity. The same is true of a supposed compound of substance and quality. It would be two things—a substance and a quality—not one natural thing. Nor does the pair itself exist as a third thing with its own cause and purpose. Giving it a name or treating it as one in thought does not make it one in reality. Human-made objects are different: they can have an artificial unity, an intended purpose, and a maker, all of which depend on human thought and practice.

For this reason, philosophers said that words such as “white,” “black,” “Roman,” and “African” do not name things that exist on their own. What exists in each case is two things: a body and a quality, or a body and a relation to something else. A substance joined to an accident does not form a third category or a single nature with its own kind and defining feature.

For example, when we call a body “long,” we call it a quantity because length belongs to it. When we call it “white,” we call it a quality because whiteness belongs to it. It may be described under many categories, but it belongs essentially to only one: the category that makes it what it is. The others do not belong to its nature in itself. A single natural thing cannot have its essence made up of two different categories.

Now consider fire, air, stone, plants, and animals. Each has a definite nature and a real unity, not one created by our way of looking at it.
