Each exists in its own right as one substance, but its nature is not simple. It consists of the bodily nature shared by all bodies and another principle that makes it a particular kind. If this second principle belonged to another category, such as quality, the two would be only an accidental collection, like a stone placed beside a person. Such a pair would not have one natural essence, and could not be a kind of any basic category. But fire, air, earth, water, trees, and animals are natural kinds. Therefore each must include a specific substantial part besides the shared bodily nature. This is what we call its specific form.

The relation of this form to body, considered as matter, is like the relation of a defining feature to a general kind. The form completes body as a particular kind, just as a defining feature completes a general kind. For this reason it is called the body’s perfection. A thing’s perfection must belong to its own kind, not come from an unrelated kind. The perfection of a substance must complete its substancehood and be more fully substantial than what it completes. How, then, could it be an accident that depends on it? We have already established that these specific forms are the principles of the essential differences between kinds of body.

As we have discussed, the general kind and its defining feature belong to the natures of things made from external matter and form. These parts remain in the thing, both in the mind and in the world. So the earlier argument—that whatever changes the answer to “What is it?” must be a substance—was not meant to apply in every possible case.

Suppose, for example, that “black” were defined as “a color that constricts sight,” while other colors had other definitions. If the answer to “What is it?” changed from one color to another, that would not make the color a substance. The argument applies when a thing has a real natural unity and belongs essentially to the category of substance. It helps us decide whether a feature is part of that thing’s nature or only an added feature. The fiery nature that makes fire burn belongs to the essence of fire; whiteness does not belong to the essence of a white body.

I do not mean that a phrase such as “white animal,” which joins an animal to whiteness, is not defined with whiteness included. In that phrase, the two are taken together, and whiteness acts like a defining feature. I mean that an animal of one natural kind has its own definition, which remains unchanged whether it happens to be white or not. The examples of a sword, a bed, and other human-made things do not challenge this point, because they are not natural kinds. Iron and wood have natural kinds of their own, with defining features whose change changes what they are. No one doubts that fire, air, earth, water, pearls, rubies, human beings, and horses are natural kinds, each with four causes in their own right, not merely by accident.

Two of these causes make a thing exist: the active cause and the purpose. They do not come from human work. The other two make up its nature: matter and form. They belong to the thing itself and are joined by nature, not by a maker’s decision or a way of thinking about them. These forms are the natural kinds’ essential differences.

Since these forms make matter into different kinds of body, the forms themselves do not come from principles already present in matter. They are given by a source beyond it. Readiness and lack of readiness are not complete things that make kinds what they are. They follow from features already present in a body. Their role is only to prepare matter to receive a form. The differences between forms come from different separate principles, not from different kinds of matter or from differences in matter’s readiness. As we have seen, those differences in matter arise after the forms. They account for differences between individual things and the ways they come to exist, not for differences between their essential kinds.

The true source that gives existence to everything is God, who necessarily exists and sustains all things. He acts in keeping with His knowledge of the best and most complete order. Intellects, souls, and other active principles bring about the many distinct kinds of reality. Matter, motion, time, and readiness are receptive conditions through which many individuals of those kinds can come to exist, along with their different ways of existing. This is the view held by the monotheist sages and the careful philosophers.

The claim that “a part of a substance is a substance” means this: if proof, insight, or direct understanding shows that something is a substance, then its part must also be a substance. The part is prior in existence and has even less need for a subject. We do not have to understand every part of a thing before we can know that the thing is a substance. We know that a natural body is a substance with three dimensions before we know that it is made from matter and form, both of which are substances—or that one part may be a substance and the other an accident.

So the objection is mistaken when it says that we can know a thing is a substance only after knowing that every part is a substance. Nor does “a part of a substance is a substance” mean that the whole must be a substance in every respect before any of its parts can be one. A thing is not a substance in one respect but not another. “Substance” is the general kind that makes its members what they are. It is part of their essence and cannot be absent from them, whatever way we consider them.

When we call a hot or white body a substance, we call it a substance because of its body. We call it hot or white because of its quality. Substance is said of the body essentially, but of the heat or whiteness only because it is joined to the body. The two together are not one thing in a single category. Each belongs to a different category, as we have already explained.

Our discussion concerns what is a substance in its own right, with “substance” said of it essentially, not because of something else. A thing that is a substance in its own right remains one when we set aside added qualities and other descriptions. A part of a substance that belongs to the category of substance must therefore be a substance too. Otherwise, the whole substance would stand on its own while depending on a subject for one of its parts—even though that part is its cause and is prior to it in existence. The cause should have a stronger and more complete existence than what it causes. The opposite would be impossible.

It is also a mistake to say that one substantial part can be an accident existing in the other substantial part. This confuses a natural unity, in which matter and form become one thing through nature, with a human-made combination, in which a subject and an accident are joined by an outside cause.

If one part is a substance in its own right and does not need a subject, it is already complete as a kind. It does not need another part added to it. Anything added to it would come after it exists, so the two could not become a single natural kind. They would remain a substance joined to an accident: the substance would be prior and complete, and the accident would come later. But if their combination made a third substance, that substance would not exist until the accident arrived. The accident would then cause the substance to exist before the substance existed, which is impossible.

Nor can the accident be part of some other subject. Then it would not be a part of this natural kind at all. So a single, naturally unified thing cannot be made up of parts from two different categories. We have already shown why such a combination cannot have one real existence.

#### A Conclusion from the Throne

In an earlier discussion of essences, God sent me a dream and inspired me with a conclusion that has since become clear in my heart. It shows that specific forms are the concrete existences of natural bodies. Existence itself, simply as existence, is neither a substance nor an accident.

The defining features of substances in natural compounds are one in nature with the forms that exist outside matter. A general kind is not the kind above its defining feature; rather, the general kind is a feature that applies both to that defining feature and to other things. So natural forms are not substantial essences. They are what gives a substantial body its definite existence. They are not accidents either. They are more basic than the categories of substance and accident.

Their simplicity, brightness, and role as the source of effects in the world give a thoughtful reader a clue to what they are: real existences that complete natural kinds and distinguish them from one another. They exist as distinct realities in themselves. This is like existence spread across the forms of simple and compound things: its individual instances differ in rank and degree through existence itself, not through features added to it. Differences between essences follow from differences between these individual existences and their ranks.

The need of these forms for matter does not come from their own nature. It comes from features that attach to them—such as size, quality, position, and place—which require matter. These are the features philosophers call the marks of individuality. Think carefully about this.

#### Chapter 7 — The Natural Form Does Not Give Body Its Existence by Taking Turns with Other Forms

We have learned that bodily form changes when a natural form changes. The relation of natural form to body is like the relation of a defining feature to a general kind. The defining feature is prior in existence to the general kind. So when the prior feature changes, what depends on it changes too, but not the other way around.

Avicenna also says in *The Remarks* and elsewhere that whenever a new natural form arises, it comes with a different size and different dimensions. A new essential continuity comes with it. So we should not say that different natural forms take turns making one and the same body exist, as bodily extension gives matter one body after another. Body cannot be to natural forms what matter is to bodily forms. Body is the extended part that can be thought of as having three dimensions. When the extended form changes, the natural form changes too, because the body itself changes. Each new natural form comes with a different body. The body is therefore not like prime matter, which remains itself while receiving different boundaries and forms in turn.

Here is why. Every feature that exists in something else—whether an accident or a form—depends on its subject for the conditions that make it an individual. But an accident also depends on its subject for what kind of thing it is, because an accident describes its subject. The subject makes the accident what it is and makes it this particular accident. A form, by contrast, makes the existence of its subject complete. Body has one shared nature across different sizes and measures. Prime matter remains the same individual as the kind of extended form that makes it body remains.

That is why the kind of bodily form makes matter actual, while particular bodily measures make it this individual body. This differs from natural forms of different kinds. Each natural form makes body a particular kind. When one kind of natural form changes into another, body as a natural thing changes too. Prime matter remains, as long as some bodily form remains. Since prime matter is wholly potential and vague in its nature, any bodily form can make it this particular matter. But a body, considered simply as body, is a definite kind, and it needs a particular form in order to be this individual body. When that form is lost, the body is lost. The marks that make prime matter an individual are not lost so long as some bodily form remains.

#### A Consequence

For this reason, philosophers say that when a tree is cut down or an animal dies, the body that existed with its plant or animal form ceases to exist, and another body comes to be. Prime matter, by contrast, continues. This is one point of disagreement between the two schools, because it follows from their disagreement over whether specific forms are substances or accidents.

The same issue arose in our discussion of essences. In a compound, the general kind and its defining feature are made actual together, just as in a simple thing. The followers of Illumination instead say that each is made actual separately. Yet these are parts that can be predicated of the whole, and predication means that they are one in existence. They hold that when a tree is cut down, its defining feature—the power of growth—goes away, while its general kind, body, remains; and the same happens when an animal dies or a body is divided. We have already explained this point. Keep that explanation in mind.

#### Chapter 8 — The Natural Form Exists at a Higher Level Than Bodily Form

We have already set out principles that lead to this conclusion, but let us explain it another way. It is impossible for a body made from prime matter and general extension to exist as a fully actual thing and only afterward receive a particular size, shape, place, position, or a natural ability to resist or allow being split and joined, either easily or with difficulty.

As body simply, made up only of matter and extended form, it would have only a general place, shape, and size. But general features exist only in the mind. Anything that exists in the world must have particular features and forms. It cannot exist as a complete actual thing stripped of the features that make it a body of one particular kind. Nor can it exist before one of those features, even by a natural priority rather than in time. Something that can exist only together with one of several different features cannot exist on its own, apart from all of them, or come before them.

So the natural form cannot come after bodily form, and the two cannot be equal in rank, with neither prior to the other. If they were, each would make matter actual on its own. But one simple matter cannot be made actual separately by two forms at the same level. If one form were enough to complete it, the other would add nothing. This is like saying that one general kind is completed by two separate defining features. The terms “sensitive” and “able to move by choice” do not pose this problem for an animal, because they are two descriptions of one defining feature, as has been explained elsewhere.

The extended form must first be completed by a natural form. It then makes bodily nature a particular kind. That form gives matter and the body it makes actual a kind of existence different from the body made actual by bodily form alone. The body here is a third meaning, distinct from matter and from the general kind “body.” It is the real unity of matter and both forms, joined by nature, not by anyone’s way of thinking. This is unlike a black person or a white person: body and heat, or a person and writing, can exist separately, but matter cannot exist separately from its form.

This natural union makes the general kind into a specific kind. The union of body with heat does not make the body into a specific kind, since the body is already complete before the heat comes to it. Think of writing: does it make the human being it is added to into a kind of human being that needs writing in order to exist? No. Before someone becomes a writer, that person already exists as an individual and can have other features, such as whiteness. So we have established that matter and bodily form alone are like a general kind: they need something to complete them as a natural kind. The extended form is complete as an idea in the mind, but in the world its existence is incomplete. It needs a natural form to make it into a complete kind, able to produce the effects that follow from existence. This continues until the thing reaches its full perfection as a genuine natural kind.

If this is so, a specific natural form must complete it. What completes a thing must be prior to it in existence, and stronger and more complete. So the natural form comes before bodily form as its cause, and before the real body made from all three parts by nature.

This raises a familiar question: if prime matter is one, why does it receive one natural form instead of another? We have met a similar question before: if different forms explain why bodies have different effects, why are the forms themselves different when all bodies share the same bodily nature? The objection depends on forgetting that specific forms are substances and exist before body in the sense of matter, which has the same general nature in all bodies.

But “body” as a general nature predicated of all bodily kinds is not one and the same thing in every kind. Each kind becomes definite through a form, and “body” means something different in each. The forms themselves, or the defining features they give, do not need other forms or features to distinguish them. They are simple natures, not compounds of matter and form, or of a general kind and a defining feature. In short, existence, through its different levels of priority, completeness, and deficiency, is the source of every difference and every real kind, quality, or feature, whether essential or accidental.

When matter and bodies differ by kind—such as the matter and bodies of the heavens, or the matter and natures of the earthly elements—the explanation is clear: those kinds of matter are not prior in existence or in time to their particular natures. But if we speak of matter as one in kind through shared bodily nature, or as one individual, as some people claim, and say that it takes on one form after another, then it does so because it receives different readinesses from higher causes. These arise through the motions of the heavenly bodies, as will become clear later.

#### Chapter 9 — Natural Forms and Bodily Powers Do Not Exist Only at the Edges of Bodies

Forms and powers cannot exist in a body only at one of its indivisible boundaries, such as a point or a surface. If they exist in body as body—in its own divisible nature—they must spread through it. If one existed only at a boundary, it would have to have an existence separate from the body, and then it would not be the body’s boundary. Also, some bodies, such as a sphere, have no straight edge or point. Before a heavenly body begins to move, it has no fixed point or line as its pole or axis. If its moving power existed only at that point or line, the power would not exist until two stages after the motion had already begun. That is impossible. So the power cannot exist only at an indivisible part of the body.

We have also learned that size and position belong to bodies by nature and, some thinkers say, are even part of what makes them bodies. Anything that exists in body as body must have size, position, and similar features. Otherwise it would exist in body only because of some added meaning that is not divisible, like being someone’s parent. So natural forms and bodily powers have these features in an accidental way. Their relation to size and position is not like their relation to color or movement. Size and position are essential to body: if they disappear, the body and the powers or accidents in it disappear too. This is not true of color and movement. The essential features of body do not affect the powers and forms in it in the same way that a color or movement does.

If they did, removing a feature of body would remove the thing that depends on it in the same way. But if a body loses its size, position, or shape, the body itself and every power or accident in it cease to be. If its size is halved, half of its color is halved too. The same is not true of color and movement: if the color disappears, the movement need not disappear; if half the color disappears, half the movement does not necessarily disappear.

#### An Insight from the Throne

We should also know that in some things unity is the same as being divisible. This is true of number. In other things, unity means that they can be divided: this is true of time and size. Number is what can be divided into units; time is a continuous flow of change; and size is a continuous quantity that can be divided.

In other things, unity is different from division. It may exclude division, as with the Necessary Being and the intellects, or it may exist alongside division in different respects. The second applies to things that have both completeness and deficiency.

A heavenly body or an animal, for example, has a bodily aspect that can be divided, actually or potentially. In that respect it is incomplete. It also has a soul or intellectual aspect that cannot be divided, actually or potentially. That is its completeness. A heavenly body as a complete heavenly body cannot become two heavenly bodies; an animal as an animal cannot become two animals. Its bodily nature can be divided because bodily nature alone does not make it a complete thing. As we have learned, its unity is also an openness to multiplicity.

From this we see that body simply as body is like a general kind, while natural forms have both an aspect that can be divided and an aspect that cannot. These aspects are stronger or weaker in different forms. The lower a form is, the more closely it is tied to body and prime matter, and the more open it is to division. The higher it is, the more unified and complete it is, and the less it can be split or forced apart. It is also closer to the world of forms without bodies or dimensions. The same is true of qualities and other accidents. Black and white, for example, are not like a sphere or circle: a sphere or circle has real unity and wholeness, but blackness and whiteness have unity and wholeness only by comparison and in thought.

Every part of a shape in a shaped body has its own part of that shape, and the full shape exists in the whole body. The whole body, which has natural unity, does not exist in every part of itself. Blackness and whiteness are different.[^V05-32]

As for bodily powers, if a power has a full meaning beyond simply being a power in a body, it exists throughout the whole body, with a part of it in each bodily part. A soul, for example, has different powers in different bodily parts: its nobler powers are in nobler parts such as the heart and brain, and its lower powers in lower parts such as the heel and the base of the spine.

But if we consider equal-sized, similar parts of the body, the soul has a similar power spread through all of them. This seems to be what is called *nature*: the first source of the movement and rest that belong to the body itself, not to it by accident. Or it may be what serves nature as its tool, namely inclination. This nature is one of the soul’s powers, spread through living bodies. In simple bodies it is the specific form and the last defining feature. In complex bodies, especially living ones, it is not the form that makes the body a kind; it works like the body’s other powers and qualities.

The soul, considered in itself as a complete nature, is not spread through the body and has no parts in its parts. In this respect it is like separate beings, which do not depend on matter. But considered as a power spread through the body, it is divided along with it. The soul is therefore like something made up of matter and form, or like an animal made up of body and soul. It has two aspects: one spread through the body, like the other natural forms; and one that is separate from it, like the beings that do not depend on matter.

This describes souls that exist as separate beings. In plants, and perhaps in some animals such as snakes, the power of unity is weaker and is overcome by division. If such an animal is cut in two, each half can retain a power of movement and sensation for a long time. The same is true of the growing power in each cut piece of certain trees when it is planted.

#### Chapter 10 — How Natural Things Exist

From what we have already discussed, we know some of the near causes of natural things: prime matter and form. We will establish later, in our discussion of the universal motions, that they also have separate purposes in the world of intellect. These are two more distant causes of things below the heavens.

If these things could exist through the two separate causes alone, with no need for matter and form within them, they would last forever and be complete from the start. Their first state would be the same as their last. But they are made up of matter and form, and their forms oppose one another; their basic qualities can also decay. Matter is receptive: it can take one form or its opposite. So each body has a claim to remain by virtue of its form, and a claim to change by virtue of its matter. Its form seeks to remain as it is; its matter can receive a form opposed to it.

These two claims cannot both be fulfilled at once, because matter cannot take opposite forms at the same time. We can say that all the forms in this world first existed in the intellectual world, created by God, as we will explain later. Their separate existence comes from the First Source. But their existence in matter cannot be brought about all at once, because one body cannot receive two opposite forms at once, much less all forms.

With wise care, God therefore set a circular motion in place, along with continuous time and matter that changes from one form to another. Each form receives its due for a time: it exists and is preserved for a while, then decays and gives way to its opposite. That opposite also remains for a time, then passes away. Neither opposite has a greater claim to exist or last than the other. Each receives its share of existence and duration.

Their matter is shared, and each kind has something of the other within it: each has a claim to receive what belongs to the other, and the other has a claim in return. Justice requires that matter pass from one to the other in turn. For this reason, no individual thing can last forever as the very same individual. A verse says: “Its charm lies in this chill: just as you make yourself at home, it tells you to rise and leave.”

So what lasts through time is the kind, not one numerically identical individual. The same is true of prime matter, the first motion, and time: each continues as one general kind, not as one individual that remains unchanged. Each kind continues through a series of individuals. One set of individuals exists for a while and then passes away; another set of the same kind takes its place and lasts for a while; then it too passes away. This continues as long as God wills.

#### Chapter 11 — The First Forms, Those That Follow, and How Perishable Things Persist

Some forms belong to the elements themselves. Others arise when the elements are mixed. Some mixtures are more complex than others. An element is destroyed only by something outside it, because nothing within its whole body is opposed to it. A simple mixture contains only a few opposing qualities, and their powers are weak. So the force that destroys it from within is weak too; it is destroyed only by something outside it. But in mixtures built from other mixtures, the many opposing qualities and their combinations make these conflicts stronger.

These complex bodies are also made from unlike parts, so they can contain opposing qualities that break them down from within as well as from outside. Water and stones, and things like them, are destroyed only by outside forces; they do not decay from within. Plants and animals, by contrast, are broken down by things that oppose them both within and outside them.

So when God’s care means that one of these forms should last for a time, He gives the body a power that continually replaces what is lost. This can happen only by drawing another body into it. That body loses its former form and takes on the form of the body it joins. The living body is given other powers, including the power to nourish itself and the powers that serve it. It draws in something opposed to itself, sheds that opposition, takes in the substance of what it draws, and gives it the form it already has. This continues until, over time, the power is exhausted and the body loses more than it can replace. Then it dies.

In this way, bodies are protected from what would destroy them from within. They are protected from outside harm by other means. Some are part of the body: a plant grows leaves to shield itself from harmful heat and cold; an animal can move by choice. Other protections are attached to the body or remain outside it. Plants have leaves; animals have hair, claws, nails, and teeth. Human beings use clothing and weapons. Animals use shelters and dens.

As we have learned, what lasts is often the kind, not the individual. So another power is needed to replace individuals that die. It produces new matter, separate from the matter of the earlier individual, and gives it a form that is numerically different from the earlier one. This preserves the kind through its many individuals. The power is called the generative power. It too has supporting powers, whose details we will discuss later, God willing.

### Part Five — Why natural bodies continually change and pass away

We have already established this claim, proved it, and made its path clear in earlier discussions.

### Stage Four — What things can become, what they already are, motion, and rest

We discussed the way nature exists as the near cause of motion, especially in the section called “Eastern Wisdom,” where we explained how what changes is connected to what does not change, and proved that there is motion in substance. We also defended the view that material substances grow stronger in their very existence. But here we want to add another kind of explanation, to make the point clearer and stronger.

This view may seem strange because it differs from what most people assume. They think philosophy teaches that bodies—especially the heavens, stars, and elements as a whole—have always existed. So they have judged the greatest philosophers of the past unfairly, claiming that they believed the heavens and similar things would last forever. This criticism does apply to some later philosophers, whose understanding was limited and whose minds were not fully clear.

What has come down to us from the early philosophers says the opposite of what later thinkers commonly claimed. We will see from the statements we quote in these chapters that the early philosophers did not believe the world or the heavens had always existed.

#### Chapter 1 — A Hint from the Throne

We have already suggested that the purpose of the heavens’ turning, the planets’ motion, and the world’s ongoing activity is to make the whole world good and happy. Creation begins in God’s generosity and mercy. He brings each thing to its perfection and end, moves it toward its goal, raises what is lower toward what is higher, and gives each kind of matter the form suited to it, making it the best it can be.

Just as a natural body comes to rest when it reaches its proper place, and nature reaches its goal when it gives rise to the soul, the soul reaches its rest when it reaches the level of intellect. This is the soul’s end, just as God is the end of intellect and everything below it. There, we find lasting rest, complete peace, and the greatest good.

This is the highest purpose of the world’s order: the motion of the heavens and planets, the changes of bodies, and the sending down of angels and messengers with books, revelation, and news. The aim is for the whole world to become good, with evil and deficiency removed. It returns to the One from whom it began, so that its end joins its beginning and its cycle comes full circle. Wisdom is then complete, and creation reaches its perfection. The world of change and decay comes to an end; this world passes away, and the great Resurrection begins. Evil and its people disappear, unbelief and its followers come to an end, falsehood is erased, and truth is made clear by God’s word. This is the highest purpose and the greatest knowledge.

From this account we see that bodies, their natures, and their powers are all subject to passing away. None of them lasts forever. Keep this hidden knowledge safe; only those made pure can touch it.

#### Another Secret

This concerns the fading and passing away of the natural world. All the pleasures of the senses—the bright lights and colors, the lovely smells and tastes, and the moving sounds found in nature—come from the soul’s giving them life, by God’s permission. They come from the soul and return to it. That is why our souls can draw these forms away from their matter through the senses, then shape them as they wish through imagination and the power that works with it. Everything that remains in nature also exists in the soul, though in a lower form. Nature has muddied and clouded it by mixing with it, because nature is lower in rank.[^V05-33]

We have explained that bodily existence is mixed with nonexistence: its core is mixed with husk, and its clarity with murk. We call these clouding elements evil and harm because they block what is good. They produce opposing things, evils, deficiencies, troubles, and hardships—everything found in this natural world, the world of change and decay and opposition. This is true whether things are joined together, as in the heavenly world, or can be separated, as in the elements and compounds.

Every perfection and pleasure found in this world has its source in another world, where it exists in a fuller, stronger, higher, and purer way, and brings greater delight. How could anyone suppose that pleasures and good things are present in the lower world but absent from the higher one? If what we have said is true, everything returns to its source, and everything incomplete moves toward its perfection. The happy return to their families in joy. The wretched suffer for a time, their skins renewed as they are burned, until they rise to the home of bliss or fall into the fire. “As for the one who transgressed and chose the life of this world, Hell will be his home. But as for the one who feared standing before his Lord and restrained himself from desire, Paradise will be his home.”

#### Another Secret

From what we have explained and proved, you can see that bodily nature, whether heavenly or earthly, slowly fades and disappears. If you look at your own state, and at the state of anything with a soul, you will see that the natures, powers, and bodies the soul uses gradually melt away and lose their qualities in its service and in obedience to the higher command. The same is true of everything lower in relation to what is higher, every branch in relation to its root, and every effect in relation to its cause.

Think about this further. A soul that has risen even a little above nature and the senses is not content to remain with nature or to return to it. It is made to love lasting life and the highest state. If this desire did not belong to every soul by nature, every human soul—and even every animal soul—would not wish to be the leader of its kind, ruler of its land, and head of its family. Every soul is naturally drawn toward the level of intellect. Its longing for that level is stronger than its longing for the senses, nature, and lower things, including bodily pleasures, unless something blocks it or turns it away from its original nature.

When a soul is sound and not sick, it longs for what is above it, because higher things are more like it. Lower things and their deficiencies are more like nature. The soul therefore keeps striving for lasting life and resists change and passing away. As we have learned, lasting life belongs to intellect, not nature. Through this natural striving, the soul reaches its goal—the level of intellect—leaves nature behind, and joins intellect.

We have also established that the soul can join intellect. When the soul leaves nature, nature does not remain as something separate from it; that would be a foolish claim, as we have explained more than once. Once the soul leaves the level of nature, nature itself ceases and passes away, because nature depends on the soul, just as the soul depends on intellect and everything depends on the Living, Self-Sustaining One.

Nature seems to sense that it will fade, while the soul will not. So it draws the soul toward itself and makes itself appealing, keeping the soul from becoming pure and joining intellect. This too serves God’s wisdom: for a time, the soul is occupied with managing the natural world, until God brings about what He has decreed. The argument from final purpose therefore shows that the whole natural world will pass away.

#### An Objection and an Explanation

Someone might ask: Why must nature pass away? Why can it not rise to the level of the soul, as the soul rises to the level of intellect?

The answer is as follows. The soul has two aspects. In itself, it has the potential to become a lasting being of intellect. In its relation to nature, it is a changing substance. The first aspect seems to make up the soul itself, since it is the higher aspect. The second belongs to the soul only through its connection to something lower and changing.

When those added relations fall away, the soul returns to its first source and its intellectual home. Nature, by contrast, is spread through the lowest reaches of existence, far from the world of lasting light. It belongs to a place of change and passing away. It does not know or long for intellectual things, so it cannot rise to the world of lasting existence. Its status is like that of a natural kind. General kinds found in nature do not exist on their own; they exist only through the individual things that make them real. So nature too exists bodily, and all bodily existence is continually renewed and does not last unchanged. Nature is aware neither of itself nor of anything else. It has no longing for the world of intellect, so it cannot endure. It also cannot last without the soul, as we have already explained.

We have learned that the soul does not remain with nature forever. It rises beyond nature and leaves it behind, since nature is a trial and hardship for the soul. The soul was sent down into nature because of disobedience and a deficiency that affected it in its first life, and a wrong it committed at the beginning of its existence. It must pass through this trial until its defects and the stains of its sins are washed away, so that it can leave the prison of nature.

When a prisoner leaves, the prison does not remain as a locked home for him. Nor is there any need for a prison once its prisoner has gone. The Imam al-Sadiq points to this when he says that the Hour will not rise while anyone on earth still says, “God, God.” So God’s wisdom and unchanging law require nature to vanish, fade, and pass away. Clear proof shows that the passing away of nature and bodies is possible and required by divine wisdom. When nature—whether heavenly or earthly—changes and disappears, the soul leaves it, just as a person leaves a ruined house. God says: “When the sky is split open and obeys its Lord, as it must, and when the earth is stretched out and casts out what is within it and becomes empty.” The sky’s splitting points to the end of its nature when its soul returns to its Maker and answers the call: “O soul at peace, return to your Lord, well pleased and pleasing to Him. Enter among My servants; enter My Garden.”

#### Chapter 2 — Every Moving Thing, Whether Body, Nature, or Soul, Will Pass Away; Everything Perishes Except His Face

Know that God, exalted is He, is the maker of everything. We have already explained, in our discussion of causes and their effects, how the true end of each thing leads back to its maker. There is no need to repeat it here; anyone with an awakened heart can return to that discussion. In short, God is pure good, with no evil in Him, and pure existence, untouched by nonexistence. Everything seeks what is pure good and existence, and draws near to it by nature or by choice. Or it seeks what leads to that good and draws near to what leads to it.

This is rooted in every part and level of the world: what we sense and what we understand, what is on earth and in the heavens, what is made of parts and what is simple. God says, “Everyone in the heavens and on earth submits to God, willingly or unwillingly, as do their shadows.” He also says, “There is nothing that does not praise Him.” If you look closely at the parts of the world, you will find that each has a natural direction or an inborn movement toward something higher. It seeks a more lasting life and a nobler state, and tries to escape a lower existence mixed with nonexistence and decay.

Every existing thing has an inborn love and longing for what lies beyond it. In some things this is obvious; in others, it can be seen with the senses or revealed by proof. Careful observation, guided by sound insight, confirms it in all things. So does the proof already mentioned: existence is pure good, and good acts for its own sake. We have also explained that what is higher does not act for the sake of what is lower. God acts for His own sake; all other things act for the sake of what is nobler than themselves. Even fire does not burn wood for the sake of the wood, and water does not wet a cloth for the sake of the cloth. Each acts to complete its own nature and preserve its own being.

The soul, too, does not manage the body merely to make it healthy, balance its mixture, or keep its parts in order. It does so to reach its own perfection and goal, and to attain what it seeks and desires. Every lower thing has a path toward a higher perfection, which it follows by nature or by choice. Whenever something can be thought of as having a kind of perfection, it naturally moves toward it or longs for it. If this inborn desire had no real end, it would be pointless and useless. But nothing in existence is pointless, and nature does not work in vain. Every lower thing therefore has the potential to reach something higher.

This potential is either simply part of a thing’s nature, as it is in things brought into being directly, or it depends on being prepared, as it does in things that come to be through change. For things of the first kind, once the potential exists, its cause is present, and no obstacle or force prevents it, the goal is reached. Separate beings face no obstacles or conflicts of the sort found in the world of motion, so they always reach their goal. In this world, obstacles and evils do exist, but they are neither constant nor common. They come from chance causes, not from what things are by nature. As we proved in our discussion of cause and effect, chance causes are rare and do not last. They occur only in the world below the heavens.

Heavenly natures, by contrast, fulfill their proper roles. Through their universal souls they are always reaching their intellectual beloveds, and through their individual souls they reach their aim gradually, moment by moment. The first goal of one particular nature is another particular nature, and so on without end. The goal of universal nature is another intellectual nature above it in cause and rank.

With this in mind, we say that every sensible nature, whether heavenly or made of the elements, has a corresponding nature in the divine world: lasting patterns that exist apart from matter and are present in God’s knowledge. Plato and his followers called them luminous archetypes. They are the lasting realities of which our changing sensible forms are only images, like originals and their shadows. These higher forms are the sources of the changing images because they cause them, are their goal, and in a sense are their forms. These higher patterns are fully realized beings of intellect, while the things we sense have abilities that are not yet fully realized.

In their natural existence, these things move toward their archetypes and long for them. Because they exist as particular things at particular moments in time, they receive what comes from the archetypes little by little, as their readiness changes. Their forms take shape in matter gradually, one after another. Each separate form has many aspects and ways of being, which only God fully knows.

In their intellectual existence, however, they have reached their archetypes and are joined to them, as a thing is joined to its goal when it reaches the end of its motion. Those intellectual forms and divine patterns are always connected to their source and goal, beholding the beauty of their Maker and immersed in the divine. They are lost in the light of God’s oneness and self-sustaining life, without turning even for an instant toward themselves. Possibility there is never separate from necessity, potentiality never separate from actuality, and deficiency never separate from perfection. Their individual selves are wholly absorbed in the First Beloved. There is no difference between them and their Beloved, as the sacred tradition says. They have no sense of self or otherness.

Satan and his followers—the people of illusion and veils—are not among these beings and do not belong to their rank. Otherwise, they would not have shown pride and self-importance or refused to bow. All those higher beings bow, kneel, and submit. Nor can anyone deny that these separate forms have divine love and longing, since we have shown that what is lower is naturally drawn toward what is higher.[^V05-34]

Someone might object that love does not always lead to union. If it did, there would be no lover who remained far from the beloved. Or perhaps, even if the lover reaches the beloved, the goal is only to become like the beloved or come close to it, not to join or become one with it.

We answer that an inborn desire placed in a thing’s power means that, when left to itself, it can reach what it longs for, unless something outside its nature blocks it. Failure to reach its goal because of an obstacle occurs only in the world of chance and opposition. Even there, it happens only to some individuals, whose particular circumstances prevent them from reaching what their kind naturally seeks. It is not a failure of their kind’s nature. Things that rise above the world of coming-to-be all reach the goals set by their nature.

The suggestion that the goal might be something merely relational, such as becoming like or close to the beloved, does not hold. If this means only a relation, then it is not a real goal, because a relation has no existence on its own, especially among higher beings. If it means an added quality, that is no better. A quality is lower in rank than a substance, so it cannot be the true goal of a substance. A thing’s goal must be higher and nobler than the thing itself. Otherwise the same thing would be both higher and lower in the same respect. Someone might say that the goal is for the substance to have that added, perfecting quality.

Even if we grant this, it does not hurt our argument. If the substance has that quality, either it has reached the highest good and perfection, beyond which there is nothing nobler, or a still greater good and perfection remains above it. In the first case, our point stands. In the second, it has another goal that its nature makes it seek and approach. As we have already explained, every existing thing other than God, the Self-Sustaining One, has a goal above it. The same question then applies to that goal, and so on, until the chain ends at the highest goal, with nothing above it: God, the goal and maker of all things, exalted is He, the One who overcomes all.

It is therefore established that nothing has always existed except God, and that everything returns to Him. Glory be to the One who holds the rule of all things; to Him you will return.

#### A Hint from the East

Every individual thing, whether necessary or possible, has intelligible consequences and effects that follow from it, such as being a thing, being caused, and existing. This is especially true of the divine reality, the source of every existence and every identity, and the origin of every idea and essence. If this is so, then the divine essence must have rays, lights, and effects, since all existence comes from the light of His rising and the brightness of His appearing.

Most philosophers call these lights the active intellects. The followers of Aristotle call them the forms of knowledge that exist in God Himself. The Platonists and their followers call them luminous archetypes or divine forms. Most theologians call them eternal attributes; the Muʿtazilites call them states; and the Sufis call them names or fixed realities.[^V05-35] Each group turns toward its own direction.

How could these rays be separated from their source? If they were separate in existence, they would no longer be rays. Think of the sun’s rays in the visible world—though God is far above any comparison. There is a difference between these two kinds of rays: the rays of the sun of intellect are living, speaking, and active, while the rays of the sun we see are lights that serve something else, not themselves. They are not alive or active. The divine forms are also connected to their source and goal through intellect. The sun’s rays, by contrast, have a spatial relation to the sun. How could the two be the same?

#### Chapter 3 — Prophets and Philosophers Agree That the World Began

Know that the view we have explained—that the whole world, the heavens and everything in them, the earth and all that belongs to it, came to be—is the true belief shared by every community with a true revealed law, and by all who have followed the divine path, in the past and today. In every age, those who believe in one God and seek the truth have shared one faith and one way in the foundations of belief, the principles of religion, and the questions of where all things began and where they will return. The prophets, the friends of God, and their followers have had one religion; no disagreement among them is reported on the basic truths.

Anyone whose religion is not the religion of the prophets has no part in true wisdom. Nor should someone be called wise without a firm grasp of reality. Wisdom is among God’s greatest gifts, and one of the noblest treasures and joys of the human soul. It sustains the higher world and brings joy to all existing things. One of the greatest misfortunes is to turn away from wisdom and deny it. God says, “Whoever turns away from My reminder will have a troubled life, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.” He also says, “No indeed! On that Day they will be kept from their Lord.” Their hearts have been covered by what they used to do.

We have good reason to believe that the greatest philosophers agreed that the whole world began. We know their words and writings. The finest thinkers in every age have praised them, and respected people of many groups have testified to their self-discipline, clear hearts, freedom from the hold of the senses, turning away from worldly life, return to their true home, and effort to live in the likeness of the first principles and the Creator’s virtues. They held that the whole world came into being: its substances and their properties, its heavenly spheres and angels, and its simple and compound things. But this question is so difficult that other readers have not understood their writings without falling into contradictions or departing from sound reasoning. Truly, reaching the truth here while following philosophical rules and preserving God’s oneness and freedom from change and multiplicity is among the highest achievements of reason, close to inspired understanding.

Here we will quote statements from some of the early philosophers. They show that these thinkers reached the truth and agreed with the messengers of God that the world began and will pass away. The leading philosophers recognized by one group are eight: three from Miletus—Thales, Anaximenes, and Agathodaemon—and five Greeks—Empedocles, Pythagoras, Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle. May God make their souls holy and let us share in their good prayers and blessings. The light of wisdom shone in the world through them, and knowledge of divine things spread through their efforts.

All of them were wise, disciplined, devoted to God, turned away from worldly life, and focused on the life to come. They are called the sages in the fullest sense. No one after them was called a philosopher in that same broad sense; later thinkers were known instead for a particular craft or way of life, such as Hippocrates the doctor, Homer the poet, Archimedes the engineer, Democritus the natural philosopher, and Yudhasaf the astrologer.

The five Greek philosophers had much to say in many fields of knowledge. We will quote what has reached us from these five Greeks and the three from Miletus about the beginning and passing away of the physical world, as well as statements by other philosophers who came after them. The eight leading sages were like the roots, first principles, and fathers of wisdom; the others were like their students and children. The eight drew the light of wisdom from the lamp of prophecy, and none of them disagreed about the basic truths.

Their philosophical discussions dealt with God’s oneness; how His knowledge encompasses things; how existing things come from Him and the world takes shape; what the first principles are and how many there are; and what the return after death is, how it happens, and whether the soul lasts on the Day of Resurrection. The claim that the world has always existed arose only after later thinkers changed their teaching, left their path, failed to study their words closely, or lacked the understanding to grasp what they meant.

Thales of Miletus was the first to practice philosophy in Miletus, after coming there from Egypt. He was an elder who spread his wisdom. He said that the world has a maker whose nature the intellect cannot grasp directly; we know Him through His effects and through the things He creates and brings into being.

He also said that the truth that cannot be overturned is this: the Maker existed when nothing had yet been made. He then brought into being what He brought into being. Before creation, there was no image of things within His essence. Before creating, He was simply Himself. If He was simply Himself, there could not yet be a direction or side, as if there were Him and an image, or a here and there, as if He had a form. Pure oneness rules out both. Creation means bringing into being what was not. Since He brings all existing things into being, He does not need a prior model of what is to come into existence.[^V05-36]

Thales said that the first source contains the forms of all existing and knowable things. Every form in the world comes from it, as an image comes from its original pattern. It is the source that gathers forms and brings existing things into being. In the intellectual world and the world we sense, every existing thing has a form or pattern in this first source. That is the end of his statement.

I say that this philosopher’s words contain several noble insights. First, God existed when nothing else existed, and He will remain when nothing else remains, as in His words: “Whose is the kingdom today? It is God’s, the One, the All-Powerful.” Second, the effect that comes from Him is the existence of things, not their definitions or kinds. His words that He “brings existing things into being” show that He gives existence itself in a single act. Many of the points we have discussed, including the beginning of natural kinds, depend on this.

Third, the whole intellectual world is one substance that comes from the Necessary Being through a single source, and yet contains the forms of all things. In philosophical language, it is called the First Element; in revealed language, the Supreme Knowledge. There are other benefits too, but explaining them would take us too far.

It is curious that Thales is also said to have claimed that water is the source of existing things. Water can take on every form, and all substances, from heaven and earth, were created from it. Perhaps he meant the all-embracing existence that flows through all things, which the Sufis call the Breath of the All-Merciful. This may fit the saying of God, “His Throne was upon the water.”

Another of these great sages was Anaxagoras of Miletus. His view of oneness was like Thales’s, though his words contain symbols and figures of speech that some might mistake for saying that God has a body—far be that from the truth. It is said that he called the First Truth “still and unmoving.” In their language, stillness can mean that something exists necessarily by its own nature. It can also mean that a fully separate, higher being does not turn its attention toward what is lower. Movement can mean acting—that is, bringing things into being over time—or coming to exist after not existing.

Porphyry reports that Anaxagoras said that the source of things is one body, the foundation of all things and without limit, from which all bodies and bodily powers come.

I say he may have meant by “the first body” the first existing thing, calling it a body because it brings other things into being, completes them, and gives them form. Perhaps he meant prime matter, which is made up in its very nature of extended substance. It is the receptive source of created things, with an unlimited capacity to receive, given to it by the First Agent, whose power to act is unlimited.

It is also reported that Anaxagoras said that things were first still, and then intellect arranged them in the best order. It placed them above, below, and between; in motion or at rest; moving in a straight line or in a circle. It arranged the heavens to move in circles and the elements to move in straight lines. This order made visible the things held within the first body. Some say that the arranger was nature; others say it was God.

The principles we have already explained show how Anaxagoras’s words can be understood. Think carefully, and you will see their depth. When he says that things were first still, he means that they existed in the intellectual world before taking on their natural forms.

Anaximenes of Miletus, known for his wisdom, said that this world will fade and fall into ruin. Since it is the lowest and heaviest of the worlds, it stands to them as the husk stands to the kernel—and the husk is thrown away.

He said that this world lasts only as long as it contains a little light from that other world. It will last until the part mixed with it is made pure. Once that part is purified, the parts of this world will fade and decay. Darkness will remain, and impure souls will remain in that darkness, without light, joy, rest, peace, or comfort.

He also said that every created thing whose form appears in the act of creation already had its form in the First’s knowledge, and that these forms are without number. The One, through His oneness, created the form of the element. Intellect then came from it by God’s creation and arranged within the element forms of different ranks, according to their degrees of light and kinds of effect. These ranks became many forms all at once, like images appearing in a polished mirror, with no time passing and no order of one thing after another. Matter, however, cannot receive everything at once. It can receive forms only in a certain order and over time. So the forms came to be in matter one after another.

His words show that he believed this world will decay, fall apart, and pass away. They also show that God’s knowledge is eternal and belongs to His own being, without making His qualities many. What He knows, however, comes to be in matter, which is open to change and receives the bodily forms one after another.

One of the great sages was Empedocles, one of the famous five leaders of the Greeks. He lived in the time of the prophet David, from whom he learned, and he also studied with the sage Luqman. He later returned to Greece. It is reported that he said the world is made from the four elements, with nothing simpler than them; that things are hidden within one another; and that coming-to-be, passing away, change, and growth do not really occur.

I say that both of his claims can be understood in a sound way, as we explained in our treatise on the world’s beginning. The heavens may also be understood as made of elements, though the elements in them exist in a higher and nobler way than those below. Some complete knowers have held this view. As for his claim that things are hidden within one another, most readers think he means that fire contains actual water, hidden from sight and unable to cool or moisten, and that other bodies contain one another in the same way. But Empedocles was too great a thinker to hold such a view. He meant something else, expressed in a symbol too subtle for most people to grasp.

On the return after death, Empedocles said that this world will remain as we know it while souls are caught in nature and spirits are entangled in bodies. In the end, they will call out to the universal soul. The universal soul will plead with intellect, and intellect will plead with God. God will answer intellect; intellect will answer the soul; and the soul will pour all its light into this world. Then individual souls will shine, and the earth and the world will shine with the light of their Lord. Particular things will see the universal realities, escape the net, and join their universal forms. They will settle in their own world, happy and blessed. “Whoever God gives no light has no light.” That is the end of what is reported from him.

I say that this speech shows that the whole world will pass away. Proof has shown that bodies cannot exist without souls and spirits, and that bodily nature depends on the soul. Aristotle says as much in several places in his book known as *The Theology*, where he points out that the earth has life and a soul. Empedocles’s words show how the things of this world return to their Maker, as the revealed laws teach and as devoted sages, those who have seen deeply, and firmly grounded scholars have explained. The things that come to be move back toward God because they are naturally drawn toward the good and their goal. The great Resurrection brings all creatures back to their Creator and causes all things—even the heavens and angels—to pass away, leaving only the One who overcomes all.

Pythagoras was another of these great sages. He lived in the time of Solomon and received wisdom from the source of prophecy. He was a wise and virtuous man, with sound judgment, a bright intellect, and keen understanding. He claimed to have seen the worlds through his senses and insight. Through discipline and purification, he said, he heard the heavens’ music and reached the rank of the angels. “I have never heard anything sweeter than their motions or seen anything more splendid than their forms and arrangements.” His views on divine things and the first principles of existence, including his claim that they are numbers, are well known. We have given a sound interpretation of his claim that number is the first principle. He also said that the nobility of every existing thing depends on how much unity it has: the farther it is from unity, the lower and more deficient it is; the closer it is, the nobler and more complete it is.

Someone once asked Pythagoras why he believed the world would end. He replied, “Because it reaches the cause for which it exists. When it reaches that cause, its motion comes to rest.” This is a brief answer of remarkable depth, as though it came from the source of revelation and prophecy. It explains both why the world began and why it will pass away. It also points to the great Resurrection, as Empedocles’s words did.

Pythagoras also said that what exists in this world contains only a little of what we sense, because it is an effect of nature. The worlds above it are so much more beautiful and noble that words cannot describe the world of the soul and intellect. So direct your effort and longing toward that world, where you can live free from corruption and passing away. You will reach a world made entirely of beauty, splendor, joy, honor, and truth, where happiness and pleasure never end.

I say that his words clearly show that this world can be corrupted and pass away; it cannot last forever. Whatever can pass away began from nothing and will return to nothing.

Another sign that Pythagoras believed the world began comes from the poets Herennius and Zeno, who followed his view of the maker and what is made. They said that God created intellect and soul at once, then brought into being everything below them through their action, gradually. At the beginning, intellect and soul did not die or pass away.

I say that their meaning is this: the world can pass away because it comes into being gradually and is renewed little by little. Everything in it comes to be and then ceases to be, like motion, which does not remain at all. Intellect, and the soul in its higher aspect that faces intellect, remain through God because they are hidden beneath the powers of the higher world. The soul’s lower aspect, which faces nature, also passes away.

Among these great leaders and fathers was Socrates, the wise, devoted sage from Athens. He learned wisdom from Pythagoras and Archelaus, and focused on divine and moral questions. He practiced self-discipline and worked to refine his character, turning away from worldly pleasures. He withdrew to a mountain cave and warned the rulers of his day against idolatry. The crowd rose against him and forced their king to order his death. The king imprisoned him and gave him poison. His story is well known.

One of Socrates’ beliefs was that God’s knowledge, wisdom, generosity, and power have no limit. The human mind cannot describe them; if it could, they would be limited. We must say, then, that they are without limit or end, even though the things we see are limited. Their limits come from what matter can receive, not from any limit in God’s power, wisdom, or generosity. Matter cannot receive forms without limit. So the forms are limited because matter is limited, not because the Giver is ungenerous. Divine wisdom therefore made them limited in their own nature, shape, place, and location, but not limited in time at their end, except in relation to their beginning. Since an individual cannot remain forever, wisdom ensures that the kind remains by replacing one individual with another. An individual is preserved through the survival of its kind, and the kind is preserved through the many individuals that come after one another. God’s power has no limit, and His wisdom has no final boundary. That is the end of Socrates’ words.

I say that his words show that every bodily individual in this world began and can pass away. They are all material and limited in their power and causes. Matter’s inability to sustain one individual forever applies equally to heavenly bodies and to things made from the elements. Every individual can therefore pass away. What remains of a kind is not one numerically identical thing, but its meaning and definition as held in knowledge and intellect. As we have explained, a kind does not exist or come to be on its own; it is a reflection that follows existence and is like its shadow.

As we have said, beginning and coming-to-be describe existence. They are opposites and cannot belong to the same existence at once. But a single nature can be considered ancient in one respect and newly coming into being in another, when we distinguish what it means from the particular instances in which it exists.

One of the five great Greek sages was Plato, known for his wisdom and belief in one God. Aristotle, Theophrastus, and Timaeus, who saw and studied with him, report that he said the world has a creator who brings it into being. This creator is eternal and exists necessarily through Himself, and He knows everything through its universal causes. He existed eternally, with no trace or shadow of the archetypes beside Him, as we have already quoted.

Plato is also said to have included time among the first principles, calling it duration. He gave every existing thing an individual counterpart in the divine world. These individuals are called divine archetypes and first principles. The archetypes are simple; the individuals in this world are compounds. The embodied human being we sense is an individual of the simple, intelligible Human Being, and the same is true of all material forms we sense. Plato therefore said that the things in this world are effects of things in the other world, and every effect must resemble its cause in some way.

I say that, with God’s help, we have revived Plato’s teaching about these archetypes. We have strengthened its proof, repaired its foundations, and answered the objections and attacks made against it by those who came after him. Plato said that these forms are universal, eternal, and lasting because every form that God brings into being is already in the First’s knowledge, and His forms are without limit. If they were not eternal in His knowledge, they could not last. If they did not last as He lasts, they would fade when matter fades. If they faded with matter, there could be no hope or fear. Since the forms we sense can hope and fear, Plato took this as proof that they endure. They endure because, in that other world, they have an intellectual form that they hope to join and fear falling short of.

Plato also said that since thoughtful people agree there are things we sense and the senses that perceive them, as well as intellect and what it understands, and since everything we sense is limited by time and place, there must be corresponding intellectual forms.

His luminous words give us several sound philosophical principles. First, he proves that the sensible world, with all its forms and qualities, began and will pass away. He says that every form joined to matter fades when matter fades. Matter is open to passing away and nonexistence; forms are renewed, one after another. This is why he included time among the first principles. Natural forms exist over time. Their earlier nonexistence helps make way for their later existence, and the passing away of earlier forms is the direct beginning of later ones. When some ancient thinkers called nonexistence a first principle, they meant this: every existence that unfolds gradually includes a lack, and is mixed with nonexistence and decay. Its lasting is mixed with passing away.

Each individual or part is marked by the absence of other individuals and parts. The whole is absent from each of its parts, and each part is absent from the whole. So the whole is, in a sense, absent from itself, and the same is true of each part. Second, Plato shows that every form we sense has an intellectual form of the same kind, its own reality and essence in God. These are the forms known as the Platonic archetypes. Later thinkers could not understand these luminous archetypes as Plato meant them or accept that they exist. Aristotle may have leaned toward them in some of his books, though he denied them in most; perhaps he thought there was a reason to do so. What we have explained in this book is enough for anyone who wants to see clearly.

Third, Plato points out that these sensible forms and passing beings return to the intellectual forms and become one with them. This is like sunbeams joined to the sun, our senses joined to our intellect, or the body joined to the soul so that body and action come from the soul. The body is broken down by natural heat and other causes, while the soul remains. Plato used the hope and fear found in some sensible forms to show that they last through their connection to intellectual beings that remain with God. They therefore have two states: a passing, sensible one, and a lasting, intellectual one. In short, Plato used the thing’s own goal as the middle point in his proof that it will pass away, just as we did earlier. Deriving a proof from a thing’s goal is one of the strongest methods.

Fourth, Plato says that these intellectual forms are themselves God’s qualities and knowledge, which follow from His existence, though His greatness comes from Himself, not from these forms. This does not mean that there are many eternal beings or that God is made more complete by anything apart from His single, self-sufficient essence. These forms do not exist on their own, separate from the Real’s existence. As you have learned, they are overcome by His power, absorbed beneath His greatness, and pass away from themselves while lasting through God.

For this reason, the Greek sage said: “The true Maker has no form or shape or power within Him. He is above every form, shape, and power, because He creates them.” He also said: “The First Maker is not one thing among other things, yet He is all things, because all things come from Him.”

The early sages spoke truly when they said, “The master of all things is all things.” God is their cause because He brings them into being, and He is the cause of their longing for Him. He is unlike all things: nothing He has created is within Him, and He resembles none of them. That is why He is loved and longed for by the higher and lower intellectual forms. The lover strives to reach Him and be with Him. As we have already proved, the simple, undivided being is all things, and everything returns to Him.

Plato is also reported to have rejected the idea of events with no first event before them. If we say that something came to be, we have shown that it has a beginning. What is true of each event must also be true of the whole series. He also said that the forms of things must come to be, but that their matter and first element existed before them. Some readers thought he was saying that matter always existed.

I say that his first argument is not based on applying a rule about the whole to every single part.[^V05-37] His point is that coming-to-be means being preceded by nonexistence. If the whole is preceded by its parts, and the parts are preceded by their nonexistence, then the whole is preceded by nonexistence too. In fact, it is preceded by the nonexistence of all its many parts. The common claim that an endless series of individuals follows one after another is mistaken. Such a series exists only as an idea; it cannot be real. Anything real must be limited. An order between two or more things depends on their existing together.

As we have learned, matter cannot take two forms at once, much less an unlimited number of forms. They do not all exist together. Even imagination cannot picture an unlimited size or number. A kind’s survival over time comes only from its intellectual existence in God’s knowledge. As for Plato’s second claim, he did not mean that the first element, or prime matter, is a complete thing that continues as one individual. Prime matter is only potential; it becomes actual in the world through forms that replace one another. It has one general continuity, while its individual states come and pass as the forms change. As we have explained many times, nothing in nature—neither form nor matter—exists forever as the same individual.

Plato is also said to have asked Timaeus: “What does not come to be? What comes to be but does not last? And what exists in actuality and is always the same?” By the first he meant God; by the second, the things of time that never remain unchanged; and by the third, the intellectual principles, divine forms, lights of the higher world, and unchanging knowledge. He also asked: “What comes to be but does not exist? And what exists but does not come to be?” By the first he meant time and the things that continually arise in time, which do not deserve the name of existence.

As you have learned, these forms have a weak kind of existence, mixed with nonexistence. By the second, he meant the separate forms, which are above time, motion, and nature, and deserve the name of existence because they last with God.

Aristotle says in the first book of the *Metaphysics* that in his youth Plato studied with Cratylus. Plato wrote down Cratylus’s claim that everything we sense is corruptible and beyond the reach of knowledge. He later studied with Socrates, who sought definitions without looking at the natures of things we sense.[^V05-38] Plato thought Socrates studied things beyond the senses because definitions do not apply to sensible things; they apply only to lasting universals.

Plato then called universal things “forms” because each is one. He believed that sensible things exist only by sharing in forms, which are patterns and images that come before them.

I say that when he said that all sensible things are corruptible, he included both the heavenly bodies and the elements below. So he believed that the heavens and everything under them began to exist. If the source means “universal things”—and this is not a copyist’s mistake for “particular things”—he means the general meanings and kinds of sensible and other things, which have no separate form in themselves. The word “they” refers to the forms. Plato did not think these general meanings had a separate existence or a single identity in the world. Otherwise, the fact that definitions remain true of changing sensible things would mean that some part of the world had always existed. As we have already explained, general meanings exist in the world only through individuals, and they do not have one numerical identity that lasts while individuals change. By “sharing in forms,” he meant the intellectual forms that sensible things have in the separate world. These sensible things depend on those separate forms as their cause and goal—not on general ideas in the mind, which do not exist on their own.

#### A Report with a Lesson

Know that Abu al-Hasan al-ʿAmiri, in his book *From the Present to the Everlasting*, relates the views of the early philosophers. He says that Plato’s view on whether the world had always existed or had a beginning is disputed. In his book *On the Care of the Body*, Plato is said to have claimed that the world is eternal, was never made, and will always last. Proclus relied on this statement and wrote a book arguing that the world had no beginning. John Philoponus wrote a reply to him.

Plato then says in his book *Timaeus* that the world was made: God turned it from disorder into order. All its substances are compounds of matter and form, and every compound can be broken apart. If Aristotle had not explained why Plato used both descriptions, we might think he was confused. Aristotle says the word “made” has more than one meaning. When Plato says the world is eternal and was not made, he means it had no earlier time and was not made from something else. When he says it was made and God turned it from disorder to order, he means that its existence depends on the ordering that joins form to matter. Neither matter nor form exists by itself, apart from the other. So God brought them into being together in an ordered whole. By His creative act, He turned the world from nonexistence into existence.

Plato says this plainly in *The Laws*: the world has a higher beginning, but no beginning in time. A Maker brought it into being without using time. If someone asks why He did so, we answer that He wills by His own nature to give existence, and has the power to bring into being what He wills.
