Plato also says in the book called *Phaedo* that the soul’s substance is unmade and does not die. Yet in *Timaeus* he says it is made and mortal, and does not last forever. Aristotle explains the difference this way: by “unmade,” Plato means that the soul did not slowly change from potential to actual; it was made all at once. By “does not die,” he means that it does not die in the home of reward. By “made and mortal,” he means that it can change from ignorance to knowledge and from vice to virtue, and that it cannot last forever unless God keeps it alive.

Plato says in *Timaeus* that the Creator told the spiritual beings: “You are not immortal by your own nature, but I will keep you alive through My divine power.” That is the end of al-ʿAmiri’s report.

I have several objections to this account. First, Aristotle’s attempt to reconcile Plato’s two statements about the world is not convincing. His explanation of the first statement works if Plato meant the separate, immaterial world. But his explanation of the second does not fit. Plato says that God turned the world from disorder to order; this plainly means that it existed in time before it was ordered. It cannot mean only that the world depends on its Maker, because that dependence rests on its being possible, not on matter’s readiness to receive a form. Besides, a thing’s definition does not exist before the thing itself, so we cannot say that it was first nonexistent and then brought into existence by an ordering cause.

This is especially clear because Plato explains the world’s coming-to-be by saying that its substances are compounds of matter and form and that every compound can be broken apart. Dependence on something else applies to simple things too, not only to compounds. So Plato’s words show that material substances have a special kind of coming-to-be because they are compounded, unlike simple intellectual beings. His statement that every compound can be broken apart also clearly means that the world will be ruined, destroyed, and dissolved. If it had always existed, it could not be open to decay and passing away.

Second, Aristotle’s explanation of Plato’s claim that the soul is unmade and does not die is not reliable. He says this means the soul was not gradually made and continues after death in the next life. He explains the opposite statement—that it is made and dies—by saying that its qualities can change and that God keeps it alive, though it cannot survive on its own. This is far-fetched.

The first part of his explanation is doubtful for two reasons. First, Alexander of Aphrodisias reports that Plato believed simple souls, which have only the lowest level of intellect, perish and do not last after the body. Avicenna leaned toward this view in some of his letters, though in most of his books he denied it, claiming that the soul is a form separate from matter from its beginning and cannot perish because it has no matter.

Second, the truth, as we will explain in its place, is that the soul’s substance, as it relates to the body and completes it as its form, comes into being gradually, like other sensible natural forms. But once the soul grows stronger and its existence becomes actual rather than potential, it frees itself from the possibility of decay. It begins in matter and lasts without matter.

The other part of Aristotle’s explanation is also doubtful for two reasons: it is far from the plain meaning and flow of Plato’s words, and it conflicts with Plato’s view that incomplete souls perish, as we have mentioned. It is better, then, to reconcile Plato’s statements in another way.

Third, we have proved that the whole world, with its sensible substances in heaven and on earth and all their qualities, comes into being gradually, and does not remain unchanged even for two moments. The divine world is vast and contains all the realities and forms of this world in a fuller and nobler way. They last through God’s existence—indeed, because they are held by God. These are not two different kinds of lasting. If God’s forms had definitions separate from their existence, then they would last because God keeps them. But as pure existences, each dependent on its complete maker, they are divine lovers who have reached their Beloved: faces turned toward their Lord, eyes beholding Him. They differ in strength and weakness, and in how near they are to Him, but they have no lasting apart from God’s lasting.

This truth is proved by reason and clear to those who know through direct insight, but only those firmly grounded in the way things are made and brought into being have fully understood it. You must free and purify yourself to join them and grasp their depth.

With this established, the right way to reconcile the two statements by Plato is to understand his claim that the world is eternal and was not made as referring to the divine forms. They are the true realities of existing things, lasting in God’s knowledge. His claim that the world was made and God turned it from disorder to order refers to the sensible forms. They are material and come to be in an ordered way through potentiality and actuality, matter and form, as we have indicated. The individual states of both matter and form begin in time and are preceded by nonexistence.

Fourth, the statements by this philosopher that we will quote show that he agreed with his teacher about the coming-to-be of this world and the lasting of the divine world. What al-ʿAmiri attributes to Plato seems to be a mixed-up account made by some common philosophers who had not reached the insight of the firmly grounded or understood the way of the complete sages.

Fifth, we have two other ways to reconcile Plato’s statements about the soul’s beginning and lasting. One is that human souls have two lives after this passing life: a purely intellectual life for those who draw near to God, and an imaginal life for others. When Plato says the soul dies, he means it loses the ability to reach the intellectual life and happiness of the complete. When he says it does not die, he means it remains after the body, though with a life of sense and animal feeling, not intellect.

The second way is this: the soul’s life is its existence, and this particular kind of existence does not truly remain. The soul changes from this existence into another. So when Plato says its substance is unmade and does not die, he means its intellectual reality, which is not part of the world of coming-to-be because it is a form in God’s knowledge. When he says it is made and dies, he means the soul as a living nature and the form that joins it to an earthly body.

We have established that every form joined to matter comes to be and passes away. In short, every soul tastes death; then you will return to God. After that, God will bring about the next life. So both of Plato’s statements are true, and he was neither confused nor contradictory.

Another sign that Plato believed in the world’s beginning comes from Abu al-Fadl ibn al-ʿAmid. He says that philosophers believed all the heavens have souls and life. Plato held that the heavenly bodies live, but not forever and not by their own power. He said that when God told these noble bodies to honor and praise Him, He told them He had not created them in a way that made them last by themselves. But after creating them, He joined them to His will, which makes them last. Aristotle, by contrast, argued that the heavenly bodies do not come to be or pass away.

Aristotle’s proof was that coming-to-be and passing away occur in matter that can receive opposite forms. The heavenly bodies have no opposite, and neither does their motion. So, he said, they neither come to be nor pass away. That is the end of his statement.

I say that these two philosophers need not disagree, as we have already pointed out. We will make this clearer when we soon explain Aristotle’s words.

One of these honored early sages was the greatest philosopher, Aristotle. We have already quoted his words showing that no body can stand still and remain unchanged, since its nature is to flow and decay. Those with insight can see from his words that he believed no bodily substance had always existed. Another proof that he understood the gradual coming-to-be of bodily substances appears in his well-known book *The Theology*. There he quotes his teacher Plato:

“Plato distinguished intellect from sense, the true natures of things from the things we sense. He said the true natures last and do not change, while sensible things pass away and come to be. When he had finished making this distinction, he began to say that the cause of both the lasting true natures without bodies and the sensible things with bodies is one: the First True Being.”

Plato then says that the First Being, the cause of lasting intellectual beings and passing sensible beings, is pure good. Nothing is good except through Him. Whatever good exists in the higher or lower world does not come from its own nature, nor from the nature of the intellectual beings. It comes from that higher nature. Every intellectual or sensible nature that appears receives its good from God, the Maker of both worlds.

He also says: “The First True Being first gives life to intellect, then to the soul, then to natural things. He is the Maker, who is pure good.” That is the end of Aristotle’s report of his teacher’s words.

I say that his words clearly show that this world will decay and pass away. As for what he says about intellectual beings being necessary to sensible things, while God is not necessary to either but holds everything together, the point is this. Every passing natural kind has a lasting intellectual form in God, which completes that kind and cares for it. There are many of these forms because there are many natural kinds. God, however, is one, and His sustaining relation to all things is the same.

These intellectual forms are necessary to their own sensible kinds, but God is not tied to any one kind. The other causes are each tied to their own effects; God is not tied to any single intellectual or sensible kind. He orders everything, holds it together, and protects it from falling apart or disappearing, according to the different ranks of its existence and its lasting. Pure intellectual beings last through God’s own existence. Sensible beings last only by one individual following another, through continual coming-to-be in which nonexistence is mixed with existence.

When Aristotle says that sensible beings are held together by coming-to-be and reproduction, he means that heavenly bodies exist through a continuous, connected process of change. Things made of the elements exist through separated individuals following one another, as with the different kinds of animals and plants.

In the tenth sermon of *The Theology*, Aristotle also says:

“When the First Being came from the true One, it stopped and turned its gaze toward the One to see Him. Then it became intellect. When the first being that came from the One became intellect, it could reflect the One’s actions. It had looked toward Him and seen Him according to its own capacity. The true One then gave it many great powers. When intellect became powerful, it brought forth the form of soul without moving, in imitation of the true One.

“The true One brought forth the being of intellect while at rest. Intellect, also at rest, brought forth the image of soul. But the soul, being the effect of an effect, could not understand how to act without motion while at rest. It brought forth an image through motion. It is called an image because it is a passing act, not something fixed or lasting. It came about through motion, and motion cannot produce something fixed and lasting. Otherwise, the effect would be nobler than its cause, while the cause—motion—would be passing and brief. That would be very wrong.”

These are his words. They clearly say that heavenly bodies and other bodies are passing images. Their existence changes, and their substances and natures do not last.

At the beginning of this book, Aristotle also lists the main questions in nature. He says that nature is an image of the whole,[^V05-39] that it agrees with the soul while being lower than it, that it acts and is acted upon, that matter is acted upon but does not act through souls, that nature itself is not in time,[^V05-40] and that its effects in the universal souls are in time. He asks whether nature acts on one thing after another and must therefore be under time,[^V05-41] or whether it is not under time,[^V05-42] adding that shared things are under time. Active ideas act on things together, while passive ideas do not act all at once; they act on one thing after another.

Another statement by this great philosopher shows that he believed the world began. He says that bodily forms, meaning bodily shapes, do not come from one another. Instead, each follows another and takes its turn in matter. This shows that a form can be lost and pass away. If something ceases to exist, it must have had a beginning, because passing away is an end, and an end implies something that came before. So what begins and ends must have been brought into being from nothing. Its matter can receive it; matter is not naturally unable to receive these forms. But the matter that carries them has a beginning and an end too. It is therefore something brought into being from nothing.

The Maker, however, has no beginning or end, because decay is an end, and an end implies a beginning. If substances and forms had always existed, they could not change. Change is the passing away of the form that gave a thing its existence, and the thing moves from one state to another. This repeated change shows that it passes away and that its states began. If one part of the world can come to be and pass away, then the whole world can do so. It has a beginning, can decay, and can change. A beginning and an end both point to a Maker.

Aristotle also reports that an unbeliever once asked him: “If the Maker has always existed and nothing else existed, then He brought the world into being. Why did He bring it into being?”

Aristotle replied: “The question ‘Why?’ cannot be asked of Him. It asks for a cause, and a cause must be greater than the thing it causes. The Maker is not made of parts, so no cause can be assigned to His own being. There is no ‘why’ for Him. He acted because He is generous.”

The man said: “Then He must always have acted, since He has always been generous.”

Aristotle answered: “The words ‘always’ and ‘without a beginning’ mean that something has no first moment. But an act of making has a beginning. So to say that it has no beginning and also has a beginning is a contradiction.”

The man asked: “Will this world end?”

Aristotle said: “Yes.”

The man said: “Then God’s generosity will end.”

Aristotle answered: “He will end this world in order to bring it into a form that cannot decay, because its present form can decay.”

These are his noble, luminous words.

I say: people of faith and insight, has any philosopher or theologian after Aristotle made a stronger case for the world’s beginning and its link to the true Maker, without any change in God’s essence, will, or relation to the world?

#### An Explanation and a Reminder

Aristotle’s words contain subtle hints and important points that some readers may miss, so we will explain them. When he says that one form does not come from another, he means that an earlier form is not the cause of a later form in its own right. A cause that makes something exist must exist together with what depends on it. So anything that comes before another thing in time cannot be the cause that makes it exist.

When Aristotle says that if something passes away it must have had a beginning, he is showing that coming-to-be means being preceded by nonexistence. A thing ends when it reaches its goal, and the way it ends shows the way it began. If its goal is nonexistence, then it began in nonexistence too.

Someone might ask: How can nonexistence be a thing’s goal? The answer is that nonexistence can be a goal in an indirect sense, just as it can be a starting point. When a thing reaches its proper goal and perfection, it no longer remains in its earlier, incomplete state. The same applies to the nonexistence that came before it.

When Aristotle says that something made is “made from nothing,” he distinguishes between what a thing comes from and what makes it what it is. What it comes from must be separate from it; what makes it what it is must exist together with it. The first is a cause that prepares the thing in time; the second is the cause that makes it what it is. So every thing that comes to be needs a cause present with it, not only something that existed before it. The earlier thing is gone when the new thing comes to be. How could what no longer exists make the new thing exist? Every thing that comes to be therefore needs a maker outside time. Nothing that exists in time comes to be all at once; it comes into being bit by bit. As long as it is coming to be, it remains partly potential. But what is only potential cannot bring itself into actuality.

That is why Aristotle says the Maker has no beginning or end: He is outside the order of time. And when he says that if one part of the world can come to be and pass away, then the whole world can too, he means that the whole exists through all its parts. If one part passes away, the whole is even more open to passing away and coming into being again.

This is especially clear of a whole that exists in time, such as time itself and the whole series of things that happen in time. Its parts do not all exist together. Once all the parts have passed, the whole has passed too. The whole exists with its parts only in our minds; there is no complete set of all past and future things existing outside the mind. Each part of time and each event exists only at its own time.

Some people say that time and the things that happen in it exist as a whole in eternity. We answer that the whole is absent in eternity, because none of its parts are there. Each part exists at a certain time, but the whole is absent at every time. As we explained earlier about continuous size, each part is absent from the whole that contains it. When Aristotle says that an action “has no beginning” while a maker’s action “has a beginning,” he means gradual action, not creation from nothing. He is speaking of the gradual effect that belongs to the category of acting.[^V05-43] His point is that bodily forms come to be gradually. Gradual action cannot remain unchanged even for two moments, let alone last forever. Its coming into being includes its passing away. When Aristotle says that God destroys the world to make it into a form that cannot decay, he means every bodily form has an intellectual goal. It returns to that goal and becomes one with it.

From Aristotle’s words we now understand how the Maker brought the world into being and why it will pass away. We also understand how the heavens will be ruined and the skies rolled up, as a scroll is rolled up, in the words of God: “As We began the first creation, so We will repeat it. This is a promise binding on Us; We will surely do it.” God moves, turns, and governs the heavens. He is wise and freely chooses what He does. Every agent who acts by choice has a purpose. God’s care for what He will make comes before it exists. Because of this care, He acts through causes and moves things by giving them a goal to seek. Every mover stops acting when its action reaches its goal.

So when, by God’s permission, the mover of the heavens and the source of temporal things reaches its purpose in moving them, it must stop moving and turning them. Once the mover of the heavens and the angelic powers that draw them toward their goals stop, the heavens stop turning and the planets stop moving. The world is ruined, its order breaks down, the seasons cease, and new living things and crops no longer come to be. All things pass into the next world.

This is because motion is the form of the things that come to be. If motion ends, all of them end. When it runs out, the world of coming-to-be runs out, and the Resurrection begins. This explanation of the world’s end, the heavens’ ruin, and the folding up of heaven and earth is enough for people who worship God.

#### An Objection and an Explanation

Someone might think that the mover has not reached its goal and has not attained what it seeks. We answer that if the mover knows it will never reach its goal, it should stop acting anyway. Something that cannot be reached cannot be an inborn goal of the agent, as we have assumed here. A being might aim at something unreachable by chance or force, but neither kind of action is constant, and neither belongs to natural action. The mover of the heavens is a noble angel and a wise agent. The action of a wise agent cannot be without a goal. So the heavens and everything in them exist for a goal known in advance and reached in the end.

The view we mentioned earlier—that God does not act for a purpose—does not conflict with our argument. That view concerns His act of creation in the broadest sense: bringing something into existence from nothing. We are discussing action in time, its goal, and the purpose that belongs to the immediate agent. This also resolves the apparent conflict in Aristotle’s words. He says that no “why?” applies to God, yet also says that God destroys this world to make it into a form that cannot decay.

From the clear statements and hints we have quoted from this great philosopher, it is now plain that he believed the world began and would pass away, and that the heavens would be ruined and disappear. God will inherit the heavens and the earth.

The common belief that Aristotle held the world had always existed may refer to something other than the world of bodies and bodily things. If he meant the intellectual world—the divine forms, pure knowledge, and levels of God’s sustaining power—then the claim that it is eternal is true. This does not mean that there are many independent eternal beings. These forms are not separate from God’s own reality; they are aspects of His divine perfection, His knowledge, and His decree. But if he meant separate, independent beings, such as active intellects, then the claim that they last forever is false. They are overcome by the light of God’s oneness. Still, we should think well of Aristotle. His deep knowledge and sound proofs on other questions, and the fact that Plato his teacher and Porphyry his student held the first view, support this reading. God knows what is hidden.

Another sign that Aristotle believed this physical world began and will pass away is found in the seventh section of his book *On Knowing God*. He says:

“When matter received form from the soul, nature came into being. Nature then shaped matter and necessarily made it able to come to be and pass away. It became able to do so because of the soul’s power and the higher causes placed within it. The action of intellect stopped at nature and at the source of coming-to-be. Coming-to-be is the last of the causes that give forms, and the first of the causes that bring things into being. The active causes that give substances their forms did not have to stop before reaching nature. The reason they did so was the First Cause, which made intellectual beings into active causes of the secondary causes that work in the world of coming-to-be and passing away.

“The sensible world points to the intellectual world and the intellectual substances within it, with their great powers, noble qualities, and good that wells up and overflows.” That is the end of his words.

I say that when he says nature “necessarily” became able to come to be and pass away, he means that it was made this way simply by being made. It was not first one way and then changed by some outside addition. His word “necessarily” means that coming-to-be and passing away belong to the very nature of bodily forms.

When he says nature became able to come to be through the soul’s power, he explains why nature is renewed and also continues. These two features seem opposed, but nature has both potentiality and actuality, passing away and stability. The causes of nature also have both aspects. The soul’s power includes deficiency and longing, but also completion and intellect. Longing is a movement of desire toward completion, and this accounts for nature’s renewal and passing away. The intellectual completion in the higher causes accounts for nature’s stable, continuous renewal.

When Aristotle says that intellect’s action stops at nature, he is pointing to two ways of bringing things into being: the first gives existence without a previous physical thing to work on, and the second forms new things within the natural world. The first ends with nature, while the second begins from it. As we have learned many times, nature’s existence includes both stability and renewal; its renewal is itself its way of continuing. Change is essential to nature and happens by necessity. In other things, change is only an added feature. Nature is therefore the last stage of creation and the first stage of generation. These two aspects do not make nature two things in the world, because nature’s stability is the same as its continual change and passing away. This is like the way matter can be potential and actual, or the way many units form one number.

Aristotle calls the causes of generation “secondary” or “accidental” because a later form does not come from the earlier form. It comes from a higher cause that is not in time. He may still call the earlier form a cause in another sense: it came before the later form in time. This is why philosophers distinguish causes ordered by their very nature from causes that only prepare something in time. When Aristotle says the sensible world points to the intellectual world, he means that every sensible form in this world has an intellectual form in the divine world and in God’s knowledge. Many early philosophers, including Plato and Socrates, held this view. We have already built a firm case for it and made its path clear many times.

#### Chapter 4 — Other Philosophers on the World’s Beginning and the Passing Away of Heaven and Earth

One of them was Zeno, the elder son of Phares. In our discussion of motion, we quoted his clear statement that the world began: things persist and pass away. They persist because their forms are continually renewed; they pass away when an earlier form fades as another takes its place. He said that both form and matter are subject to passing away. Anyone who understands how matter and form depend on one another can see why both are renewed at every moment.

He also said that the First Creator’s knowledge contains an image of the bringing of every substance into being and an image of its passing away. His knowledge has no limit, and the forms in it that show creation and passing away have no limit either. So worlds are renewed at every moment and in every age. He then explained this renewal in the way we quoted earlier.

By “without limit” he does not mean an actually infinite number of things. Proof shows that such an infinity is impossible. The phrase may mean an infinity in potential, as in the continuous succession of sensible forms and individual things, each taking the place of another. Or it may mean the increasing degrees of intellectual existence, as separate forms give rise to individuals and motions without reaching a final degree. In either case, he does not mean an endless chain of intellectual forms arranged one above another; that chain must have a limit.

Zeno also said that the Sun, Moon, and stars draw their power from the substance of heaven. When heaven changes, the stars change too. Their images, both as lasting and as passing away, remain in God’s knowledge, and His knowledge preserves them forever. God can also bring the world to an end on a day of His choosing.

By “the substance of heaven,” I think he means its intellectual reality: the form of heaven as it exists in God’s knowledge, its face turned toward the holy realm. Change affects heaven only in its material aspect, which belongs to nature and passes away. Its intellectual aspect remains, as God says: “Everything perishes except His Face.” The day on which God can end the world is the Day of Resurrection, which is said to last fifty thousand years.

We have already set out sound principles that can explain these words and make his meaning clear, so I will not expand on them here. Democritus and his followers also held that the world began, though few people have understood the symbols and figurative language in his writings. Some views attributed to him seem to conflict with sound philosophy, such as the claim that things are made of tiny bodies or that chance governs them. But he denied only that God acts for the sake of an end. Every wise philosopher agrees that what is possible cannot exist by itself; it must depend on what is necessary. Democritus and others call events “by chance” when they do not follow from a thing’s own nature but happen because of something else. In that sense, one could say that the world exists by chance.

One scholar writes that the parts of Democritus’s words that have reached us show a strong spiritual path, refined insight, and high, sacred vision. Much of what is attributed to him is sheer fabrication. The early philosophers used riddles and symbols to express sound ideas, and later writers attacked the surface meaning of those symbols, whether through carelessness or to gain influence. One of Democritus’s symbolic sayings is that the First Maker[^V05-44] is neither merely an element nor merely an intellect, but the four mixtures—the elements—from which all things begin. Simple things were brought into being all at once; compound things are continually formed and pass away, though their kinds endure.

He also said that the world as a whole does not pass away, because it is joined to the higher world, just as the elements in these things are joined to the subtle spirits that dwell within them. The elements may seem to pass away, but their pure spirit does not. They pass away only to the senses; from the point of view of intellect, they do not pass away. So the world does not pass away, since its purest aspect is joined to the simple worlds above.

These are powerful and carefully reasoned words. To understand them takes a clear mind, sharp insight, and deep study of divine things. It is surprising that philosophers so learned and perceptive missed his meaning and harshly criticized him for saying that the elements were the first things created, followed by simple spiritual beings. They said he was moving from what is lower to what is higher, and from what is clouded to what is pure. But he clearly did not mean the sensible forms of the elements.

He plainly says that these elements pass away. How, then, could he contradict himself? I would explain his meaning in full if that would not make this discussion too long. Even so, his words clearly support the point we are making: this world is renewed, and its sensible individuals and material identities pass away, while their pure forms remain with God, the Living, Self-Sustaining One. By “simple things” he means intellectual forms that endure; by “compound things” he means bodily forms, whether heavenly or elemental, since they are composed of matter and form—or, as we have learned, of existence and nonexistence, woven together in things extended through space and time.

The Fadāmian philosophers also held that the world began and would pass away. They said that every compound thing is dissolved. A compound cannot consist of two substances that agree in every respect; if they did, it would not be a compound. So when a compound is dissolved, each substance returns to the source from which it came. Whatever is simple and spiritual returns to its spiritual world and endures. Whatever is dense and coarse returns to its own world. As each dense thing is dissolved, it returns to something subtler, until nothing dense remains and it joins the first subtle thing. It then remains joined with it forever.

When the last things join the first things, the First is the source of every created thing, with no other substance between it and what it creates. Each created thing is then connected to the light of its maker and remains forever. These philosophers’ words are noble and profound. They express two important points that I have often repeated. First, the bodily world passes away: its form is lost, its matter decays, its existence is undone, and its compounds dissolve. Second, the refined and purified sensible forms join their intellectual forms and return to the First Divine Cause. Everything returns to Him and moves toward union with His enduring Face: what is incomplete returns to what is complete, and the branch to its root. As God says: “Glory to Him, in whose hand is the dominion of all things; to Him you will be returned,” and, “All things return to God.”

Heraclitus, another philosopher who held that the world began, said that the first of all things is the True Light. Our intellects cannot grasp it, because they themselves were created from that first light, which is truly God. He also said that Love and Strife were the beginning of creation and of this world. Empedocles agreed, saying that Love and Domination were the first powers of creation.

These words point to the world’s beginning. Anyone who loves must love something beyond the self. The lover moves toward the beloved, returns to it, and is absorbed in it. Everything incomplete longs for an end, and everything with an end moves toward it. The beloved draws the lover to itself and erases the lover’s traces, until the lover returns to it. As God says, “He is the All-Powerful above His servants.” This does not mean that what is higher turns toward what is lower or is changed by it. The higher moves and draws the lower toward itself, erasing its traces and bringing it back without itself changing or coming into contact with it. In short, every caused thing longs for what will complete it. The whole world, with everything in it, will one day return to God and become one with Him, just as it began from Him.

Epicurus is also counted among those who said the world began, though he disagreed with the early philosophers about its first principles. He said there are two: the Void and Form. The Void is empty place; Form is above place and the Void. Existing things are made from them, and everything made from them dissolves back into them. They are both the beginning and the end of things; perhaps, he said, everything perishes.

I think by “first principles” he meant the principles that make up a thing, which are joined to it, like matter and form—not the causes of its existence that are separate from it, like an agent or a goal. Matter and form belong within the existence and nature of this world; the agent and the goal are outside it. By “Void” he meant prime matter, which in itself lacks form and therefore has no form of its own. It has spatial extension through the bodies that receive it. By “Form” he meant the nature that gives bodies their kind. In its intellectual or soul-like existence, this nature is above matter, just as a cause is above what it causes. Matter and form are both principles from which a thing is made, but not principles by which it is made. Form gives a new thing its actuality and perfection; matter gives it the capacity to receive form, and its need for that form.

When form is complete, it is the thing’s perfection and goal. When matter is cast off and discarded like a husk, it is the goal of the thing’s nonexistence and the return of its need and deficiency. That is why Epicurus said things begin from these two principles and return to them. His words show that bodily things and other sensible forms are continually renewed from the separate forms that remain with God, then return to them. Sensible things pass away as matter decays, while their forms remain with God through His own enduring existence, not through any independent existence of their own. “Everything perishes except His Face.”

A Greek sage expressed the world’s beginning in another symbolic saying: “Your mother is loving, but poor and foolish; your father is young, yet generous and measured.” By “mother” he meant matter, and by “father” he meant form. The mother’s love is matter’s readiness to receive; her poverty is its lack of actuality and its capacity to receive; her foolishness is its inability to remain in one state. The father’s youth means that form is renewed in existence at every moment. His generosity means that form is the source of a thing’s qualities and effects. It gives existence according to its intellectual reality in God’s knowledge, as one aspect of God’s giving and mercy toward those ready to receive it. Each recipient receives forms in a measure suited to its times and circumstances. As God says: “There is nothing whose stores are not with Us, and We send it down only in a known measure.”

The sage also said that the soul is a noble and precious substance, like a circle turning around its center, though it is a circle with no physical dimensions. Its center is intellect. Intellect, in turn, is like a circle around its center, the First Good. Yet the circle of intellect never moves; it rests, still and fixed, like its center. The circle of the soul moves around its center—intellect—in search of completion. The lower world circles around the soul and longs for it. Its own movement is a longing for the soul, just as the soul longs for intellect and intellect longs for the First Good.

Because the circle of this world is a body, and a body longs for what lies outside it so that it can reach and embrace it, the noble outermost body moves in a circle. It seeks the soul from every direction so that it may reach it, rest with it, and become still. These are the sage’s words. They plainly say that the sphere will come to an end and its motion will stop, nature will fade and run out, and the bodily world will pass away and move to the next life, as we have explained.

Alexander of Aphrodisias was one of the foremost and best-known philosophers. He was among Aristotle’s leading students, in both thought and learning, and his words are especially strong.

In *The Healing* and *The Beginning and the Return*, Avicenna praises Alexander and compares him favorably with earlier thinkers. Alexander agreed with Aristotle on many questions. For example, he held that God knows all things, universal and particular, in one way; that His knowledge does not change when what is known changes; and that it does not become multiple. He disagreed with Aristotle on some points, at least as they appear on the surface. We have already explained his statement that some souls will perish, as you will see.

One view Alexander held on his own was that every star has a soul and a nature, and moves by its own soul and nature; it is not moved by anything else. His words also show that everything beyond the outermost sphere began. He said: “The sphere surrounds everything below it, and time runs through it, because time counts motion. Nothing else surrounds the sphere; if it did, time would run through that too. So the sphere cannot decay or come into being. It cannot come to be or pass away, and whatever cannot come to be or pass away is eternal.”

His words seem to mean that whatever lies within eternity and time belongs to the things that come to be and pass away. All the heavenly bodies and the elements are material and have the potential to change, so time applies to them. Their existence changes, and they can come to be and pass away. If the thing that surrounds everything is itself a natural body with the power to move and change, it too must have a renewed form that comes to be and passes away. It would need something else to surround it and mark the time and place of its existence. If it is not such a body, then it does not surround everything by occupying a place, as a roof encloses a room. It surrounds the heavens in a soul-like way. In its intellectual reality, it does not belong to the visible, sensory world, but to the unseen world of divine governance and decree. What is with God lasts, as He says: “What you have will run out, but what is with God remains.”

Porphyry, one of the great philosophers devoted to God and firm in knowledge and oneness, wrote the *Isagoge* and belonged to the school of Aristotle. In my view, he was one of Aristotle’s greatest students and the best guide to the core of his teaching and its deeper hints. I rely on him more than on other commentators, such as Alexander of Aphrodisias and Themistius, in questions about the soul, knowledge of God, the return of the soul to the world of truth and the home of reward, and the way back. Avicenna relied more heavily on Alexander and Themistius because Porphyry rejected the idea that the knower and what is known, or the soul and the Active Intellect, become one.

We have already explained this issue fully. Porphyry said: “Everything that comes to be does so when its form changes; it perishes when it loses its form. Whatever is one and simple has one simple action; whatever is many and compound has many compound actions. The action of each existing thing matches its nature. God’s action through Himself is one and simple; His actions through intermediaries are compound. He also said: Whatever exists has an action that matches its nature. Since God exists, His unique action is to draw things toward what resembles Him—that is, toward existence.” These words clearly and indirectly show that the bodily world began and will pass away. The first statement says this outright: things come to be when their forms change, and perish when their forms leave.

The second statement makes the point indirectly. Every body has a nature that is the source of its motion, and motion is continually renewed and comes to be. So its near source must also be renewed. Every natural body is therefore renewed, has come to be, and will pass away. Porphyry’s words about God’s unique action—drawing things toward what resembles Him—also point to this world changing and returning to God.

Proclus, who was associated with Plato, is another respected and well-known philosopher. People have widely believed that he taught that the world had always existed and that he attributed this view to the early philosophers. This belief arose because his book presents nine arguments for the world’s eternity. If I were not concerned about making this discussion too long, I would quote them and answer each one, leaving no room to doubt how the world came from God. Each argument has a sound interpretation and a reasonable answer.

In *The Book of Sects and Creeds*, al-Shahrastani says that one of Proclus’s defenders excused these arguments by explaining that Proclus addressed people in two ways: in a simple, spiritual language and in a compound, bodily language. The people of his time who challenged him understood only the bodily language. Their opposition led him to express philosophical ideas in ways that would reach people at different levels of understanding. A wise person should present wisdom in many ways, so each reader can benefit according to their understanding and readiness. This leaves opponents with no sound objection to his words.

Proclus taught that this world lasts forever and does not pass away. In his book, he says that when the worlds became connected and natural powers came to be, shells formed and cores arose within them. The shells pass away, but the cores endure, because they are simple and their powers are undivided. Thus the world is divided into two: the world of form and core, and the world of cloudiness and shell. The two are joined, and the end of this world is the beginning of the other. From one point of view they are not different, because this world is joined to what does not pass away. From another point of view, the shells pass away and the cloudiness is removed. How could the shells fail to pass away and disappear? Until the shells are removed, the cores remain hidden.

He also says that this world is compound, while the higher world is simple. Everything simple lasts forever without fading or changing. Proclus’s defender says that these are the words we should accept from him. Those who attributed the earlier argument to him either failed to understand his purpose, for the reason already given, or were jealous of him because his thought was clear, his outlook broad, and his powers strong. His defender points to a passage in which Proclus says that the first principles from which the worlds were made endure and do not pass away. They remain throughout eternity and hold things together. Yet they all come from the One, who cannot be described or defined. He says that all things take their form from that One, and that the One is their source and final end. Nothing is greater than it except the First, the One. Its power brought forth the first principles, and its ability gave rise to these beginnings.

Proclus also says that the Real does not need to know itself in order to be truly real. Whatever is truly real is below it, because the Real has made it real. The Real from which the nature of life and permanence flows is what gives this world its permanence, both at its beginning and after its shells pass away. It purifies the simple inner core in human beings, which had been covered over by the shell. He also says that the shell and impurity of this world have disappeared; it has become simple and spiritual. The pure, luminous substances within it remain at the level of spiritual worlds, like the higher worlds. But there also remains a substance made entirely of shell, impurity, and filth. These are his words.

These carefully formed words make it clear that Proclus’s view—though people have called him a believer that this world has always existed—is the same as Plato’s and the earlier philosophers’ view: the world began and will pass away, while the divine world and its divine forms remain. Proclus saw that the end of this world is joined to the beginning of the other, as what is known is joined to the cause that gives it existence, as a thing is joined to its goal, and as people reach their completion and perfection. Since that divine world lasts through God and endures through Him, Proclus sometimes called this world eternal in that sense. We speak in the same way when we say that our bodies last through our souls, even though the body is continually changing and passing away.

So when Proclus says that this world is eternal, he does not mean that the sensible forms of the heavens and other natural things last forever as individual things. He means that their simple intellectual forms remain in God’s knowledge and do not pass away when their compound, sensible forms do. Passing away implies an end and a return; coming to be implies a beginning and a cause. Every changing thing must finally return to One who has neither beginning nor end. This is sound only if we understand that these enduring intellectual forms are not beings with an existence separate from God, which would make many eternal beings; nor states within God’s essence, which would make His essence multiple; nor identical with God in His own essence, which would involve a change from necessary existence to possible existence, or the reverse. All of these are impossible. The truth is that what is necessary is forever necessary, what is possible is always possible, what is real has always been real, and what is false has never been real. Only those with direct spiritual insight and a firm grasp of God’s oneness can know this. Still, as we have shown, every inborn longing and desire must necessarily lead to what it seeks.

#### Chapter 5 — Sayings of Muslim Mystics on Nature’s Renewal and the Passing Away of the World

We have already quoted many verses from the Qur'an that point to this teaching, which lies at the heart of both wisdom and religion. We also find it in reports from the Prophet and his family, the people of purity and prophecy, peace be upon them all. One example is this saying of Imam Ali in *The Path of Eloquence*: “I warn you about this world. It is a place from which people move on, and a home that makes its residents unwelcome. Its settled people are travelers, and its residents will leave. It rocks those who live in it like a ship tossed by storms on the deep sea.” He also says: “What can I say about a home whose beginning is hardship and whose end is ruin? Those who pursue it lose it; those who turn away from it receive it.” These words, like the saying before them, are full of wisdom. Their full meaning becomes clear only to someone who has mastered the principles we have explained and has trained the mind deeply in the search for divine knowledge.

As for the words of the Sufis and those who have direct insight, the discerning mystic Muhyi al-Din Ibn al-Arabi says in one chapter of *The Meccan Openings*: God says, “There is nothing whose stores are not with Us, and We send it down only in a known measure.” That is, He sends things down through His name “the Wise.” Wisdom governs this divine sending down: it brings things from God’s stores into the distinct existence of each thing. Ibn al-Arabi later says, “From the point of view of their distinct forms, things exist after not existing. But from the point of view of their being in these stores with God, they are already there.”

He then comments on God’s saying, “What you have will run out, but what is with God remains.” The saying is true of knowledge. The address refers to the individual substance: what each existing thing has—what God brings into being in its place, such as its qualities, features, and states—passes away from it in the next moment or state. The substance is continually renewed through likenesses or opposites drawn from these stores. This is what the theologians mean when they say that an accident does not last for two moments. It is a sound and clear teaching about how possible things are brought into existence.

He says that a person who relies on reasoning has no knowledge of this. It is a prophetic insight, not a conclusion reached by thought; reason is ruled by its own limits and cannot reach it. In another chapter of *The Meccan Openings*, he tells of an inner journey and a conversation with the Prophet Idris. He says: “I told him, ‘In a vision I saw a man circling the Kaaba. He told me he was one of my ancestors and gave his name. When I asked how long ago he had died, he said forty thousand years.’ I asked him about Adam, since we had already worked out the dates of his life. He asked, ‘Which Adam do you mean—the Adam closest to you?’ I said yes. He replied, ‘You are right. I am a prophet of God. I do not think the world has a total span of time that can be measured. In general, God has always created and will always create, in this world and the next. Created beings have fixed terms when their periods come to an end; creation itself does not. Creation is renewed with every breath.’”

I asked, “How soon will the Hour appear?” He replied, “The Hour is near; people’s reckoning is near, yet they turn away without paying attention.” I asked him to tell me one sign that it was near. He said, “The existence of Adam is one of its signs.” I asked, “Was there another home before this world?” He said, “The home of existence is one. It is neither this world nor the next until you are part of it. The next world is distinguished only by you. As for bodies, they keep taking on forms and changing, coming and going. This has always been so and will always be so.” Those were his noble words.

### Part Six — The Principles and Causes Studied in Nature

The natural philosopher receives these principles from the divine philosopher as a report and accepts them as a starting point. Proving them is the responsibility of the philosopher who studies divine things, who receives them from God through the angels closest to Him, the means by which His command reaches creation. This part has several chapters.

#### Chapter 1 — What Is Nature?

There is no doubt that the bodies around us act, are acted on, move, and change. We see some of these actions and changes come from outside causes: a stone moving upward, fire moving downward, water becoming warm, or air becoming cool. Others seem to have no outside cause: a stone falling downward, fire rising, water cooling, and fire heating. Seeds and reproductive fluids also change into plants and animals. And animals move and rest by choice in many ways, without any outside agent directing each movement or forcing them to act.

These are the impressions we form at first glance. At this stage, we do not decide whether either kind of action is chosen or unchosen, whether it always follows one fixed path or can take many paths, or whether the bodies that move without a visible outside cause may still have an unseen mover outside them.

If such an outside mover exists, is it something that could be sensed, even if its effect cannot be seen—as when someone sees iron drawn toward a place but does not see the magnet pulling it? Or is it wholly separate from the senses and never sensed at all? One thing is plain to anyone who thinks clearly: a body, simply because it is a body, cannot perform a particular action or set itself in motion.

The principle that the natural philosopher must accept, and the divine philosopher must prove, is this: these moving bodies act and move because of powers within them. Those powers are the sources of their effects and actions. One kind of power acts and brings about change in one fixed way, without choice. Another does the same with choice. A third acts in different ways, without choice. A fourth acts in different ways, with choice. The same kinds of possibilities apply to rest.

In this terminology, the first power is called “nature.” We find it in every body we know: a stone falling, for example, or resting at the center of the whole. To say that every body has nature means that every body has a power that directly produces a particular movement or rest in one fixed way, without awareness of it at that level. This does not mean that the body cannot also have a higher power, such as a soul or intellect, or that nature must exist apart from such a power. Nature may be joined to a higher power in some way, or it may not be. The second kind of power is called a “celestial soul,” as when a heavenly body turns around its own center, according to the most careful philosophers.

The third kind is called “vegetative soul,” as in a plant’s nourishing itself, growing, and reproducing. The fourth is called “animal soul,” as in an animal’s sensing, walking, desiring, and feeling anger.

Sometimes “nature” means any power that acts without choice, which would include a plant’s soul. It can also mean the source of any action that takes place without deliberation. In that sense, it includes the heavenly soul and other powers, even a spider weaving its web or a bee building its honeycomb: we say it does these things by nature. But the nature that natural science studies—the source of change in things—is nature in the first sense: the power that every body has.

The natural philosopher does not have to prove that this nature exists. The philosopher must ask the one who studies first principles to prove it. The natural philosopher’s task is to understand what nature is, not to argue with someone who denies its existence. That would be a difficult task, as we explained earlier when discussing the principle that everything moving has a mover.

The natural philosopher approaches this question as someone studying what can receive an effect; the divine philosopher approaches it as someone studying what gives the effect. Nature has been defined as “the first source of movement and rest in the thing it belongs to, in itself and not by accident.” The first teacher of philosophy passed this definition down to us. Avicenna explains in *The Healing* that “first source of movement” means the highest direct source from which the movement of another thing—the moving body—comes. We have already noted that natural philosophers call something an agent when its effect unfolds in time and step by step.

Nature need not be the source of both movement and rest in every thing, and it need not be the source only of movement from place to place. The definition means that nature is the source of any feature that belongs to a thing in itself: its movement, if it moves, and its rest, if it rests. The movement may be a change of place, size, quality, substance, or something else.

“First” means direct and near, with no cause between nature and the movement it produces. The soul may be the source of some movements in bodies that have souls, but it may act through nature. Some people have held that in living things, nature carries out movement from place to place on behalf of the soul. We agree with this for animals’ chosen movements, but not for the fall of their bodies from a higher place to a lower one. As we have hinted before, nature is the direct source of every movement, whether it acts on its own or serves the soul.

Avicenna rejected this idea and thought it unlikely. He says: “I do not think nature could move the limbs against what its own constitution requires, in obedience to the soul. If it could, the soul’s forcing it to act against its own nature would cause fatigue, and the demands of the soul and nature would conflict.” We have already answered this objection. Nature is not one single kind: the nature in the elements and minerals is not the same as the nature that acts between the soul and the movements it directs in place, size, and quality.

It is surprising that Avicenna allows the soul to use nature as an intermediary in movements other than movement from place to place, but does not allow this in movement from place to place. Even where he does allow it, he understands the powers differently from us. For him, plant and animal powers are qualities that act or are acted on; for us, they are substantial forms.

The words “in itself” can also be understood in two ways: in relation to the mover, or in relation to the thing moved. In the first sense, nature moves by its own power, not because something forces it. So when nothing blocks it, it cannot fail to produce the movement that comes from its own power; movement caused by force is different. In the second sense, nature moves something that moves from within itself, not something moved by an outside cause.

“Not by accident” also has two meanings, one relating to the agent and one to what receives the action. In the first meaning, nature is the source of movement in something that is truly moving, not merely moving along with something else. A passenger at rest in a boat, for example, moves only because the boat moves. In the second meaning, if nature moves a copper statue, it moves the copper in itself; it moves the statue only by accident. That is why a doctor does not heal himself simply by being a doctor: he treats himself as a patient, not as a doctor. As doctor, he gives treatment; as patient, he receives it.

#### A Further Point

Some later writers thought this definition was too short and tried to add to it. They said: “Nature is a power spread through bodies that shapes them, gives them form, and preserves that form. It is also the source of the movements and rests that belong to the body.” Avicenna says this addition is unnecessary and wrong. He argues that the power in this definition is an active power. An active power is defined as the source of movement, one thing moving another, as long as the mover is something other than the thing moved. “Power” means a source of movement within a thing, and “spread through” simply means “present in.”

If the thing receiving a power is divisible, then what exists in it is also divisible and spread through it. “Giving form” and “shaping” are already included in the idea of movement; preserving forms is already included in the idea of keeping something at rest. So this writer repeats many ideas without need. He also thinks that saying “power” means nature is a thing in itself, apart from its relation to anything else. But “power” means only a source of movement and rest, so it can be described only by its relation to what it moves or keeps at rest. That is the point of Avicenna’s argument.

I say that nature is the source of its own actions and effects, including movement. This is not like a quality added to a thing after it already exists, as laughter or writing is added to a person. Nature’s existence and its being the source of these actions are one and the same. So Avicenna’s definition is not merely a definition of the word “nature,” as his words seem to suggest; it describes nature’s reality. We should also understand that “nature,” “power,” and “form” are different ideas, but those different ideas do not require different kinds of existence. In every kind of body, this power is one physical reality that produces the body’s own movements, changes, and other effects. It may or may not have its being as part of a soul-like substance. We will explain this later.

#### Chapter 2 — Nature’s Relation to Form and Soul Above It, and to Matter, Motion, and Accidents Below It

Avicenna says in *The Healing*: “Every body has a nature, matter, form, and accidents. Its nature is the power that produces its own movement or change, and likewise its rest and stability. Its form is the essence that makes it what it is. Its matter is what carries that essence. Its accidents are the features that follow or come to it from outside once its matter has received its form and its kind is complete. Sometimes a thing’s nature is the very same as its form, and sometimes it is not.

“In simple bodies, nature is the form itself. When we consider it in relation to movement and action, we call it ‘nature’; when we consider it as what makes a thing the kind it is, we call it ‘form.’ For example, the form of water is the power that makes prime matter into water. This power cannot be sensed, but it produces sensible effects such as coldness and heaviness. Heaviness is the tendency a body has when it is outside its natural place. The form acts in the substance of water in different ways: coldness is how water is affected; moisture is how it affects and shapes other things; movement is what it causes when it is in an unfamiliar place; and rest is what it causes in a place suited to it. In compound bodies, nature is something like part of the form, but it is not the whole form.

“For such bodies do not become what they are through the power that moves them in one direction alone. They need that power, but it is only one part of their form. Their form combines several meanings into one. Human nature, for example, includes the powers found in bodily things, plants, and animals, together with reason. Joined in a particular way, these make up what it is to be human. How they are joined is better explained in the study of the first principles of existence.”

This is a summary of Avicenna’s account. Some parts of it are sound according to the philosophy we have developed here; others are uncertain, as you can see by considering his words carefully. His statement that a thing’s form is the essence that makes it what it is, and that its matter carries that essence, is sound and should be trusted throughout.

This principle supports many of our teachings, especially our account of the return to life. Every specific reality is what it is through its form, which gives it its kind and makes it complete. Several consequences follow. The human soul, in its own reality, includes all the meanings found in animals, plants, and minerals. This also bears on the union of the knower and what is known, and on the possibility that a substance can grow stronger through change.

Avicenna’s claim that a thing’s nature is sometimes the same as its form, as in simple bodies, and sometimes not, as in compound bodies, is unclear. First, by “simple bodies” he seems to mean the four elements. Elsewhere he says that the heavenly sphere and stars have no nature, only an inclination, which is a quality like heaviness or lightness. But we have already shown that the spheres and stars have a fifth nature of their own.

Second, when he says that the nature of a compound is not the same as its form, he may mean that the specific form of an animal or plant is its soul, which differs in number from its nature. Perhaps by “the nature of an animal” he means the forms of the elements that make up its matter. But none of those is the nature of an animal as an animal, or of a plant as a plant. If instead he means the sources of growth and voluntary movement, then in reality and existence they are the same as the animal and plant forms, though they differ in meaning and definition.

Avicenna also says that, in a compound, nature is like part of the form, not its whole. This can be understood in two ways. The first, which we favor, is that the nature he describes is one of the meanings included in a compound’s specific form. The form is one, but it includes this power as the direct source of the movements and changes that belong to the thing in itself. A power of this kind may also exist by itself in matter of the same kind, though not as the very same individual power, if we consider it apart from the rest of the form.

The second reading, which Avicenna seems to have intended elsewhere, is that this power exists in the matter made up of elements, while the form belongs to the compound as a whole. The form and its distinguishing feature can be said of the compound because the form is considered without restriction, and the distinguishing feature is included in it. To say something of the compound means that it is one with it in existence. In this sense, the power is part of the form. This reading is sound too. But deeper thought shows that the form that gives a thing its final kind contains, in one simple existence, all the meanings found in the powers beneath it, but in a higher and simpler way.

The same two readings apply to Avicenna’s claim that a form is made up of several meanings that come together as one. From what he says elsewhere, the best way to understand him is that a specific form, such as the human form, is made up of several powers, arranged in an order. Each is the source of the powers beneath it. They all come together in one because they are linked in a fitting order. Order makes many things resemble one. But deeper reflection shows that the human form—the rational soul—is one, and that within this one form all its animal, plant, and mineral powers are gathered in a noble, unified way. The same holds for each form in relation to the forms beneath it.

Someone might ask: If the form is the active source of all these powers and rules over them, how can it be acted on by them or become more complete through them? I answer that the forms that shape matter and its powers are not as fully separate from matter as intellectual principles are. Each has two aspects: one of need and growth, and one of independence and completion. We explained earlier how these two aspects are joined when we discussed the close relation between matter and form.

The form gives matter its basic reality with help from an intellectual substance that is connected to it in some way. Yet the form needs matter for its changeable features and its particular shape. We are speaking here of form in its basic reality, not of its dependence on matter. From the standpoint of that dependence, it is not form but shape; and matter, from that standpoint, is not matter but a subject that receives it. The same is true of every form and matter, even the soul and body. The soul gives the body and its powers their form because it is joined to the active source, though it does not act alone unless the union is complete. When the union is complete, it no longer needs the body. But while it is separated from the source and remains limited, it depends on the body to seek completion.

Let us return to our subject. We have seen how nature relates to form, and this also shows how it relates to soul. In simple bodies such as the heavenly sphere and what it contains, nature and soul are one in existence. That one reality has different levels and functions: some intellectual, some soul-like and aware of particular things, and some natural, spread through the body and directly producing its regular circular motion without choice. The movement is chosen at the level of the soul, which is joined to nature in a certain way. Nature is the near source of the motion, which comes about little by little and is renewed as the movement continues, as we explained earlier.

Nature relates to matter by giving it form, specifying it, and making it a kind, in different ways that we have already explained. Motion and rest follow from nature without any new act of creation between them. Nature relates to accidents in two ways: it produces or completes some, and prepares the conditions for others.

Some accidents come to a natural body from outside; others come from the thing’s own substance. Some follow matter, such as skin color, height, and bodily shape. Others follow form, such as intelligence, joy, good character, and the power to laugh.

Avicenna says that although these features must exist in matter, their source and cause is form. He adds that we will find accidents that follow form and arise from it in another way, without needing matter to take part; this will become clear in the study of the soul. I say that all accidents follow form, which is their source. But forms vary in strength and weakness, and in how closely they are tied to matter. Matter only receives and is affected. This does not set one accident apart from another: every accident exists through form and comes from it, while matter prepares to receive it. Some forms, such as those of minerals and plants, are close to bodily matter, so their accidents, such as shapes and colors, are close to matter too. Other forms, especially human and heavenly souls, are far from matter, and their accidents arise from them in the same way.

When these higher forms exist, they are accompanied in matter by intermediate forms and powers between them and the lower bodily matter. So the accidents that arise from them are at different distances from matter. This is why people may think some accidents follow form and others follow matter. The truth is what we have just explained.

The highest forms have no matter at all, either in themselves or in the actions that come directly from them. These are the holy intellects. Their attributes are universal meanings and intellectual qualities, such as universal knowledge, power without any trace of change, and a will that is pure care, with no turning toward what is lower. Below these are forms that do not depend on matter in themselves, but are connected to it through their changing actions in time. These include the souls of the heavenly bodies and their changing knowledge and renewed acts of will.

Beneath these are forms closely tied to matter, arranged in levels. At the lowest level, the form is spread through every part of the matter equally, as in the elements and minerals. Their parts are portions of quantity, like parts of a body. The plant form is slightly higher: it is more closely tied to some parts of the plant than to others. The root is essential to the plant’s continued life; if it is pulled up, the plant dries out and dies. Branches can be cut without destroying the whole, as long as the root remains. The plant’s parts also change through nourishment and breakdown, while its soul continues in a certain way. Higher still is the animal form. Some animals seem able to survive the destruction of their bodies as the same individual.

It is now completely clear that a natural thing made of several parts depends more strongly on its form—or what serves as its form—than on its matter. The view of some early thinkers is very weak: they gave matter a greater role than form in making a thing what it is. They argued that if a thing’s nature were its form, then when a tree is cut down and used to make a bed, it should grow branches and produce another bed. But it does not; it returns to its nature as wood.

As Avicenna points out, this thinker seems to take nature as matter—the matter that stays the same through every change. He seems not to distinguish a form made by craft from a natural form, or an accident from a form. A thing’s defining principle must be necessary while the thing exists, not when it does not exist. Form is what makes a thing actually what it is; matter is what makes it possible. Matter does not give a thing actual existence. It gives it only the possibility of existing.

Clay and bricks, for example, have the house only potentially. The house exists in fact because of its form. If the form of a house could exist without matter, it would not need bricks or anything like them. Some forms need matter because their individual existence is weak, not because matter is needed for the basic reality of their kind.

As we will see more fully in our discussion of the afterlife, all these natural kinds made of parts have forms in a world where things have shape and size without depending on the physical matter and unrealized capacities found here. That whole world consists of forms without matter. There, everything is fully real, with no unrealized potential; it is complete, free from deficiency, at rest, and does not need to be renewed. There is no toil or weariness there.

We should also understand several related terms used by scholars: “nature,” “natural,” “having a nature,” “by nature,” “naturally,” and “what acts like nature.” In our view, “nature” is used in three closely related senses. First, it means the source we have defined: what directly produces movement or change, and their opposites. Second, it means what makes up the substance of a thing. Third, it means the thing’s very reality or essence.

“Natural” means related to nature. It may mean what contains nature or belongs to nature: something shaped by nature, or a part of its form. It may also mean what comes from nature: its effects, movements, places, times, and similar features. A “thing that has a nature” is a body that has this source of movement or rest within itself.

“By nature” can describe what exists through nature as its first outcome, such as individual natural things, or as its second outcome, such as natural kinds. “Naturally” means anything that follows from nature, either in a way that serves its purpose, as in individual things and kinds, or as a necessary result, as in features that must belong to it or arise from it. “What acts like nature” means movements and rests that nature itself produces according to its own tendency, not something outside that tendency. A result that does not fit nature’s usual course may still come from nature because of the matter that receives its action. A flattened head or an extra finger, for example, is not what nature ordinarily produces. It happens because of an unusual condition in the matter, such as its size or quality. It is “by nature” in the sense that nature caused it, but it does not serve nature’s purpose; it results from an accidental condition in the matter.

#### Eastern Wisdom

Nature may be particular or universal. Particular nature belongs to each individual. Universal nature exists with an identity separate from the individual matter. Its relation to individual things is like that of a maker to what it makes, or a root to its branches.

This universal is not the general idea that does not exist on its own in the world, but is found only through the things it describes. That kind of universal is the same as those things in existence, though the mind distinguishes it from them when it thinks about them. The universal we mean here is an intellectual form that is separate from individual matter both in its existence and in its meaning.

Avicenna denied that this universal nature exists as a real thing. He says that only the different powers in the things that receive it exist; they are not all one nature that later divides. They may be related to one source, but their relation to one source does not remove the real differences between them. Nor does it make the related things exist on their own, apart from the source. On this view, a universal nature does not exist as a real thing, not even within God Himself.

He says it is impossible for something unlike God to exist within Him. Nor is there a nature waiting to flow from Him but not yet present in the things it will reach. And the nature in each thing cannot have one shared existence without difference, since every nature differs from every other nature in kind or number.

We have already given a full account of why every natural kind has one intellectual form in the world of separate forms. Its relation to the individual natures is like completion to deficiency, or stronger existence to weaker existence. I will not repeat the argument here. It is surprising that Avicenna admits that particular natures have goals, and that the goal of one individual following another is the survival of the kind, yet he also says that a universal nature has no real existence apart from the mind. He says that a universal may be universal within a kind, or universal without qualification, but neither exists on its own in the world; only individuals exist.

He says that one kind of universal is what we understand as the source of the plan that preserves a kind; the other is what we understand as the source of the plan that preserves the whole in its order. But if a universal form has no independent intellectual existence in reality, what does it mean to preserve the world through the relation of individuals to that form?

In *The Divine Science*, when answering an argument against goals in an endless series of living things, Avicenna also says: “The endless individuals that come to be are not the direct goal of nature. The direct goal is for a kind of substance to exist, such as human beings, horses, or bees, and for this existence to remain constant. A single individual cannot last, because everything that comes to be will pass away. So nature preserves the kind. Its first goal is for human nature, for example, to remain, and that goal is one. But for it to remain, one individual after another must exist without end. In this necessary sense, the endless series of individuals is a goal.”

If we consider both of Avicenna’s statements—that natural kinds do not exist independently, and that the kind rather than its individuals is the main goal of nature—we see that they conflict. A true goal, which an agent directly seeks, must exist in reality more fully than what the agent produces only by accident and in passing.

#### Chapter 3 — Nature’s Own Action, Whether Universal or Particular, Aims at Good, Not Evil

Avicenna says that many things outside the course of particular nature still fall within the course of universal nature. Death, for example, is not intended by the particular nature in Zayd, but it is intended by universal nature for several reasons. It frees the soul from the body so that a happy soul can reach its afterlife reward; the body was made for that purpose.
