When souls do not reach happiness, this is not nature’s fault but the result of bad choices.

Death also makes room for other people whose right to exist is like that of the person who has died. If these people lived forever, there would be no room or power for others. Matter has the potential to form them, and they deserve this kind of existence just as much; they have no less right to exist than the earlier people have to live forever. These and similar reasons, Avicenna says, are goals of universal nature. An extra finger also serves universal nature, which gives each matter whatever form it is ready to receive, so that no matter is left unused. If some matter is ready for the form of a finger, it is not denied or wasted. That is Avicenna’s account.

I see several problems with this account.

First, as we have already seen, his words conflict. Here he says that universal nature exists in reality, though earlier he denied this.

Second, death, decay, and the like do not belong directly to nature, whether particular or universal. Death happens because each particular nature moves toward its own completion through a change in its very being, which we mentioned earlier. When it leaves its first form and reaches another form and goal, the first stage of its life ends as the next begins. We say it has died or decayed. Nature does not directly intend death or act to bring it about; death is an indirect result.

Third, Avicenna’s explanation—that universal nature causes death so the human soul can reach happiness in the afterlife—applies only to human souls. It does not explain why animals and plants also die or decay.

Fourth, as Avicenna explains in his discussion of the afterlife, intellectual happiness is pure and belongs only to a small number of human souls, fewer than the rest. But goals that universal nature directly seeks must be reached generally or in most cases, as we explained when discussing goals. If universal nature caused death so souls could reach this happiness, all or most people would have to be happy, and only a few would be miserable. Yet that is not what Avicenna and his followers believe.

The truth is that every nature moves toward its own completion and goal. It fails to reach it because something forces it away, something interrupts it, or its own power falls short. The wisdom in death is not limited to bringing the soul to happiness. Intellectual happiness is not the only kind of completion or full actuality. Every soul separated from its body has another existence, stronger and more lasting than its life here. But stronger existence does not rule out misery and punishment, as we will explain in the study of the afterlife.

#### Chapter 4 — The Subject and Principles of Natural Science

According to the science of logic, especially its account of proof, some sciences are universal and others particular. A universal science studies things simply as existing things and studies the features that belong to them as such. It does not need to treat them as having size, number, or change. Mathematics and natural science study things under those further conditions, so they are particular sciences.

Each particular science has its own particular subject. The subject of natural science is the sensible body, considered as something that undergoes change. This science studies the features that belong to bodies in this respect, whether those features are forms, accidents, or relations. We call them all “natural” because they relate to the power called nature, which we have defined. Some are the subjects of natural science; others are effects and movements that come from nature. Natural things must have principles and causes.

We have established that we can know something that has a cause only by knowing its cause. So we cannot fully understand natural things until we know their principles and causes. Some principles belong to all natural things and are shared by them; others belong only to certain things. The principles shared by all—those of the common subject and its common features—cannot be proved within natural science if they need proof. As we learned in logic, their proof belongs to the universal science. The natural philosopher must accept that they exist and learn what they are. But the natural science may be able to prove both the existence and nature of principles that belong only to particular kinds of things.

Natural things have both shared principles and special ones, such as the principles of growth and sensation. The right way to teach them is to begin with what is more general and work toward what is more specific. This is clear in the principles that make a thing what it is. If you want to know what a human being is, you must first know the broader group it belongs to—the part of its definition it shares with other kinds—before you can identify what makes human beings distinct. So the general is easier for the mind to know than the particular.

Anyone trained in divine philosophy knows that the principles of existence itself, such as the agent and the goal, are also easier to know in themselves than their effects. We have explained this before. The best order of teaching follows the order in which things exist: from higher to lower, and from the broadest and most inclusive to the most specific. So anyone studying natural things should begin with the principles nature shares and the general features that follow from them. Then they should move to particular natures and their features, which arise from principles not shared by all things. Our minds know general things more easily than particular ones.

Nature itself, when it is particular, works in the reverse order. The individual is more familiar to it than the kind, and the kind more familiar than the broader kind. Particular nature does not aim to produce an animal in general or a body in general. It aims first to produce a particular body, then a particular animal, then a human being, until it reaches a specific reality that gathers every perfection available to that nature.

But universal nature, in the sense we have explained,[^V05-45] aims first and directly at the existence of what is higher and nobler, and what contains more within itself. Then, as a consequence, it aims at what comes next, descending step by step until it reaches body and prime matter.

What nature aims to produce is a kind of existence. As we have learned, existence has degrees of strength and weakness, fullness and limitation. Nature does not aim, as some have claimed, at an abstract universal essence, whether a specific kind or anything else. Essence is not made, and is not itself the direct goal. What is made, and what any source of action aims at, is existence. Still, existing things may share a kind or broader kind, and the mind uses these shared meanings to understand them. This is why the general is said to be easier for the mind to know than the particular. The first features we notice in existing things are those shared by all, such as “thing” and “existing.” Then we notice meanings shared by fewer things, such as things that exist in their own right and features that depend on other things, then “body,” and so on, until we reach a complete, specific meaning. People share knowledge of the general and the first ideas, which do not offer much perfection by themselves. They differ in how far they go into the details, learning more specific meanings and kinds.

For some people, this close study of specific things leads them to see with the mind’s eye intellectual forms and complete, luminous realities. These are the sources of the specific kinds. Reaching them is the highest perfection, the greatest result of knowing both general and specific meanings. That is why people say, “Knowledge is the seed of direct vision.” This vision is not sense perception. We sense things before we grasp the general meanings and their distinctions; direct vision comes later.

Some people stop at general categories. They know that something is a body but do not understand its distinguishing features, such as growth, which would let them know what a plant is. Others understand a growing body but stop before they understand sensation and so cannot know what an animal is.

When knowledge reaches the specific natures and their features, inquiry through reasoning comes to an end. What remains is direct presence before the intellectual natures and forms that exist apart from matter. The mind does not suffer from failing to know every particular nature, because such natures perfect bodily things, not the intellect.

#### Chapter 5 — Principles That Natural Science Takes as Given

Natural things have principles and causes, which we will now discuss. The subject of natural science is the natural body insofar as it can change. So its definition includes both what it means to be a natural body and its capacity for change. We have already explained what a natural body is, and we have already explained motion and change. A natural body has principles insofar as it is a body; these form part of the subject of natural science. It also has further principles insofar as it comes to be, passes away, and changes.

Some of the principles that make a body what it is are parts of its being and identity. There are two: matter, as wood is to a bed, and form, as the bed’s shape is to the bed. Matter is pure potentiality. It is called prime matter, substrate, matter, element, or stuff, with each name drawing attention to a different aspect. Form is the bodily form: the capacity of a body to have three dimensions that meet at right angles. It is the weakest of forms, and it remains through the many shapes and sizes a body takes on. Both matter and form are principles of the body simply as a body, and of each kind of body.

The special forms found in natural things—the principles of their kinds and species—always exist together with a natural body. So prime matter, one of the body’s principles and related to bodies as wood is to a bed, bears the same relation to bodies that have those forms. Considered in itself, without being joined to any form, matter is a substance. But it is empty of form and exists only with the capacity to receive forms, either together or one after another. This may belong to a universal kind, whose unity exists only in the mind and is never found as one thing in the outside world. Or it may belong to one particular nature shared by all its parts, whether those parts exist together or one after another. Philosophers have differed on this point.

As we said earlier, prime matter’s unity as one particular thing in the outside world is as weak as the unity of a general kind. This is because matter, without forms, has very little being. Matter is naturally related to the forms that come after it through differences in lack and fullness, weakness and strength, poverty and richness. It is like an outline, shadow, trace, or image of the forms. The form completes the material substance and makes it a different substance in actuality, after it existed only in potentiality.

Natural science should therefore take as given that a body, simply as a body, has two immediate principles of its own: prime matter and form. Form may mean bare extension; it may mean the form that completes a body, such as the form of a particular kind of body; or it may mean an accidental form, as in bodily kinds such as being white, walking, or laughing. It should also take as given that prime matter cannot exist on its own, apart from bodily forms. No matter, considered as matter, can be without a form that makes it exist. If one form left and no other replaced it, the matter itself would cease to exist.

I do not mean that a later form completes the matter in exactly the same way, as one and the same individual form did before. Matter is an indistinct substance with many states. Its underlying unity remains through whichever form it takes on. Natural science should also take from first philosophy the principle that a form that completes the nature of a species cannot change into another species-form, nor can one individual form become a numerically different individual form. In either case, the original thing perishes. This differs from a substance taken as matter for a kind or individual: matter is understood in an indeterminate way in what it helps form, so it can change while the kind or individual remains the same, as long as its form remains the same. For this reason, a formal substance does not undergo motion, while a material substance can.

This is also why bodily form and bodily size cannot move within the category of quantity. A particular kind of quantity must keep its definite extent, and motion requires a subject that remains the same while it changes. What changes cannot be identical with what it changes into.

But motion can occur in what acts like the form that completes geometrical extension, such as the form that makes something a plant. As long as this form remains the same individual form, the plant remains the same individual thing, even while its bodily dimensions change. Some body and some measure are enough to serve as the material part of a particular kind of plant. The same applies to an animal: its plant-like powers may change in number or kind while it remains the same individual animal, as long as its particular animal form—that is, its soul—remains the same.

These are the principles that belong within the body’s makeup. Bodies also have an agent cause and a final cause. The agent cause gives bodily forms to matter and makes matter exist through those forms. The form acts on the matter; the compound that they make takes its nature from both. It acts through its form and is acted on through its matter. The final cause is what those forms exist in matter for. Since we are discussing principles shared by natural things, the agent and goal we consider here must also be shared principles.

Avicenna says in *The Healing* that “shared” can mean two things. First, an agent may do one initial act, from which all its later acts follow. An example, if there is such a cause, would be one that gives prime matter its first bodily form; after that, what comes later completes its existence. A shared goal, in this sense, would be the goal toward which all natural things aim. Second, “shared” may mean universal: a general agent that applies to each particular cause of a particular thing, or a general goal that applies to each particular goal, rather than to the individual things themselves.

I have several objections to this account. First, the first kind of shared agent described here has no sound basis. The first thing produced by the active source in this world is not general bodily form, followed by the particular forms of each kind. Particular, complete natures come before general kinds. We have shown that what acts like form completes what acts like matter. Also, if bodily form existed on its own before the forms of particular kinds arrived, those later forms would be mere accidents: the body would not need them in order to exist.

Second, the final cause paired with an agent of this kind would be the least noble of all goals. It would be the goal of bodily form first, and of other things only through bodily form, just as the agent would first produce bodily form and then the rest.

Third, Avicenna himself says in *The Metaphysics* that what is actual always comes before what is potential, and he gives many reasons for this. In another chapter, on the coming-to-be of the elements, he rejects the idea that a body first exists with only bodily form and then acquires its other forms.

Fourth, the natural philosopher should not investigate principles that natural science is supposed to take as given. On these grounds, the universal agent and goal described in Avicenna’s second sense cannot be principles that natural philosophers simply assume, either.

The right approach is to understand a shared agent and a shared goal in another way, though it is close to what Avicenna meant. The shared agent is the source from which all natural things begin to exist, directly or through other causes. The shared goal is what all natural things exist for and toward which they move, directly or through other things. This agent is shared by all natural things, whether it is one or many, and whether it also acts on things outside nature or acts only on natural things. The same is true of the shared goal.

The first things to come from this source are the noblest kinds of nature joined to bodies, such as animal souls, ranked from higher to lower according to their perfection. Then come complete souls, from the most to the least complete.

Next come mineral natures, then the elements, then forms of bodily extension, and finally the matter shared by physical things.

In the return toward the source, through the changes that prepare things to rise, the order runs the other way. Existence begins with the lowest things, such as bodily forms and the elements, and rises toward what is nobler, such as souls and what comes after them. A full account of the difference between the two kinds of existence would take us too far from the subject here.

It is clear that this agent and goal stand outside the world of material nature and changing things. Whether the agent is one or many, necessary or dependent, has been shown elsewhere. Natural science does not have to prove these points or simply assume them.

Natural science does have to take as given that natural bodies share four principles: matter, form, agent, and goal. But a body insofar as it changes, develops, or comes to be and passes away has another principle as well. Something that changes naturally, or moves toward a perfection in itself or in one of its features, must contain something that remains while one state is lost and another appears. What comes to be, considered as something that changes in itself, must have something that can receive both what it changes from and what it changes into, a form that comes to be, and an earlier absence of that form alongside the form that passes away. Most people can see this in changes to a thing’s features.

By God’s grace, we have shown that the same is true of the inner being of material natures. Nature is renewed, and each part of its existence is formed through the absence of what came before, just as each part’s absence is formed through its successor. So the absence of a form that could be there must be counted among the conditions that make it possible for a changing thing to exist. This kind of absence is necessary for a thing to change. When the change reaches the thing’s very being, privation helps make it what it is, together with the other principles. Remove this absence entirely from something whose very being is changing, and you remove the thing itself. In this sense, that absence is a necessary condition: the changing thing cannot exist without it. If someone objects that a principle must exist in order to help something exist, we do not insist on using that word. Call it instead something required for the thing to exist. Either way, the definition of a changing, developing thing must include both its form[^V05-46] and this absence.

This is not absolute nothingness. It is the absence of one form in matter that is ready and able to receive it. A human being does not come from just any non-human state, but from a non-human state in matter capable of becoming human. Coming-to-be is considered in relation to the form; decay is considered in relation to its absence.

People sometimes say that a thing came from matter and from absence, but not from form. We say that a bed came from wood or from what was not a bed. In many cases, it is right to say that something came from matter and absence; in others, it is not. But we can always say that it came from what was absent. We would not say that a person came from being a writer. The reason is that some changes complete matter with a form whose arrival destroys an earlier form or state. This is what happens when a human being comes from a reproductive fluid: the earlier form passes away. In another case, wood becomes a bed. Its wooden form does not disappear altogether, but it loses a particular shape through cutting and carving. In other changes, no new form completes the matter in a way that removes any part of it. When a person becomes a writer, for example, the person’s human nature and its features remain. In the first kind of change, “from” means “after”; in the second, if we use “from” or “out of,” it means that the new thing is made from the old material. We also say that ink comes from copper sulfate and gallnut.

Avicenna discusses this in detail in *The Healing*. What a natural scientist needs to know is that all natural changes involve a thing’s matter passing from one form to another, more complete form. The first form passes away in its limited state, but as the thing becomes stronger and more complete, it remains in a way through its underlying nature. The words “from” and “out of” may be used in either sense. Some changes that are not natural also work this way, as when a person becomes a writer. Other changes do not: a bed may fall apart, a person may grow old, or an animal may die. In our account, aging and death are not ends that natural change aims at in itself. They are necessary results that accompany development. So they may be counted among things that happen by chance or through force, rather than by nature.

Ancient philosophers also spoke of matter’s longing for form, comparing matter to a female and form to a male.

Avicenna says, “This is something I do not understand.” I have already discussed how to make sense of this claim in the section on causes. If “matter” means any matter perfected by a form—including the matter of a body with its powers, which is perfected by a soul, and even the potential intellect, which is perfected by the intellect in actuality—then it is not very difficult to say that matter longs for form.

#### Chapter 6 — How Matter, Form, and the Absence of a Form Can Be Shared

Most philosophers hold that no single matter is shared by every natural form. Some bodies come to be and pass away; others do not. They are created directly and have no matter in which they come to be. So, they argue, there cannot be one matter that receives the forms of things that come to be and pass away, and also the forms of things that do not naturally perish or come to be through matter. There may be one matter shared by bodies that come to be and pass away and change into one another, but not by all bodies. This is Avicenna’s view.

I say that this difference between bodies—some are created directly, others come to be and pass away—comes from their forms, not from their matter. In thought, one and the same underlying substance could receive the form of a directly created body or of a body that comes to be. The forms themselves may differ: some have an opposite that destroys them; others do not. So the difference between these two groups may lie in their forms, not their matter.

This view is supported by the fact that prime matter, in itself, is only the capacity to receive forms. It has no particular form of its own. Otherwise it would be made up of potentiality and actuality, matter and form, and would receive its own existence from forms. This is why the union of matter and form is a union in one being. In our account of the world’s coming-to-be, we also showed that the individual forms of the heavenly bodies and stars come to be and pass away, though their general natures are not material but intellectual.

As for a common formal principle, Avicenna allows that the bodily form of extension may be one and the same in number, but not the other forms that complete particular kinds. We have already pointed out the problem with this view.[^V05-47] It is strange that Avicenna would not allow matter to be one shared thing that receives different forms, even though the first form that makes matter actual, in his view, is bodily extension. If that form can be shared, why should differences among the further forms make bodily extension itself different? He lets differences among forms make matter differ and cease to be shared by all bodies, but does not let them make bodily extension differ. Yet prime matter has such weak being that many forms do not destroy its individual unity, as we have shown. Bodily extension, by contrast, changes through connection, separation, and other causes.

In truth, bodily extension cannot be the common formal principle of natural things. It changes with every coming-to-be and passing away, every joining and separating. The best candidate for a common formal principle is the one proposed by some ancient philosophers: they took the whole world to be one animal with one soul, the soul of the whole, and one form belonging to the whole. This idea is not far from the truth, especially for those who take the outermost sphere to be one body containing all the heavenly bodies, moving in one circle around the center of the whole. Even if its parts differ in kind, their ordered places—from higher to lower and finer to denser—do not prevent the whole from being one individual, just as a human being is a small world.

Avicenna then says that if bodies had a shared formal principle of this kind, it would always be joined to matter, would never come to be or pass away, and would instead be created directly. But we have already learned that always being joined to matter conflicts with being created directly, without ever coming to be or pass away. Matter is what bears a thing’s potential existence and readiness to come to be. A thing that has no potentiality, readiness, or coming-to-be cannot be joined to matter. Such a thing would be pure form. Anything whose existence depends on matter must involve privation in its makeup, since it comes to exist only through motion and time. It therefore cannot be created directly in this sense.

The forms we have described as possible shared forms must have two sides: one intellectual, created directly, and another natural, tied to matter and to coming-to-be and passing away. The celestial soul is an example. In its intellectual being, it is a form created directly from the divine command. In its natural powers and other features, its existence depends on matter, and it undergoes coming-to-be and passing away.

The absence of a form cannot be shared in the first sense we described earlier: it is the absence of something that could exist, and it may end when that thing comes to be. But there is no doubt that matter, form, and privation are shared in the other sense: every new or changing thing has matter, form, and privation. This is Avicenna’s account.

The common meaning of matter, form, and absence does not itself come into being or pass away, just as a general nature does not. That is Avicenna’s account. I say that things whose existence is continually renewed are different: in them, being and absence are intertwined.

Their absence has a share of being, and their being has a share of absence. Each part’s existence is the absence of the next part, and that next part’s existence is the absence of the earlier one. In the same way, each part’s coming-to-be is the passing away of the other, and its passing away is the other’s coming-to-be. The whole has being at a given time, and it also has absence at that same time. The same is true of a spatially extended body: each part occupies its place while another part is absent from it, and the whole is present in all its space while also being absent from it.

We have learned that bodily nature exists in this way in both respects. So if the whole bodily world had one shared form, as Avicenna allows, we could also say it has one shared absence in the first sense, even though this absence contains countless particular absences.

Avicenna then says that this absence comes to be and passes away only in an incidental way. It comes to be when a form leaves matter, giving rise to an absence of that form; it passes away when the form arrives, so that the absence is no longer there. This absence itself is absent, as its being is only incidental. The form is what makes it absent. Still, its existence depends on the form, while the form’s existence does not depend on the absence.

I say that this account applies only to things that exist all at once and remain in place. But there are also things that come to be gradually and do not remain fixed, such as motion and time, and the changing things that follow them—even natural substances, in our view. In these things, absence comes to be and passes away in its own right, just as being does. Each part of a continuous process has being in itself, and is also, in itself, the absence of the part that follows it and the passing away of the part before it. Each part’s existence depends on the passing away of the earlier part.

That is why the absence of a form is counted among the principles of things that come to be—not every absence, but the absence of an earlier part whose coming-to-be is followed by the next part. Forms come to be in an unbroken succession and pass away in an unbroken succession. Absences do the same. The coming-to-be of form is the passing away of absence, and the coming-to-be of absence is the passing away of form. There is no reason to call our use of “absence” for a thing that comes to be gradually a mere figure of speech. Being unfolds gradually only because absence does too. Both being and absence therefore come to be and pass away in their own right. Still, “coming-to-be” and “passing away” apply more properly to form as form than to form considered as an absence.

You should also understand that the words for these three principles—matter, form, and the absence of a form—apply to their many kinds in a shared but not perfectly identical sense. Their meanings are graded, like the meanings of “existence” and “principle.” The things called matter differ in how they serve as principles: some are earlier or later, stronger or weaker. Everything called matter has potentiality for something else, but it may be simple or compound, remote or close to what it receives.

The same is true of form. It may be a substance or a feature, since here “form” means whatever brings something from potentiality into actuality. And every such absence is the absence of a form in this sense. It is understood in relation to that form, and forms differ in priority, delay, strength, and weakness.

Here we are considering form as one part of a thing that comes to be. It is the highest principle in that thing, even though it may also act as an efficient cause of the other part, or of a feature or motion.

We have already said that natural science does not deal with an agent cause or final cause shared by all things, whether shared in the first sense or as a universal. It does, however, study an agent cause or final cause that is shared by a group of natural things, but not by all of them.

#### Chapter 7 — Which Causes Should Matter Most in Natural Science?

There is no doubt that a thing’s form deserves more attention than its matter when we want to understand what the thing is. Form makes it what it actually is; matter makes it what it could be. The agent cause and the final cause also deserve more attention than matter, because they explain why the thing exists. It may seem that we should look for form before either of those two causes. The science of proof says that one question can come before another, but asking about the agent and the goal is more noble and reaches higher than asking about form. The agent and goal complete the thing’s existence, while form completes its nature and meaning. A thing’s complete being is that thing together with something more: its perfection. Form alone does not complete a thing’s existence unless its agent and goal are also present. Matter is the least important cause.

Most people, however, care far more about matter than form. They seek bodies rather than souls, even if those bodies are forms of matter that is still lower in being. Avicenna says in *The Healing* that some natural philosophers paid no attention to form at all. They thought matter was the only thing to find and understand; once matter was known, everything else would be an endless list of features and additions that could not be measured. It seems that the matter they focused on was shaped, visible matter, not prime matter. They had forgotten prime matter. If they had noticed it, they would have seen that what they took to be matter was really form.

Avicenna says that some of these philosophers compared natural crafts with crafts that prepare materials. A miner works to obtain iron, and a diver works to obtain pearls. The form of the iron or pearl, they said, does not matter to them; they only want the material. But this view prevents us from recognizing the features and kinds of natural things, which are their forms. It also contradicts itself. If it is enough to know matter without form, then we claim to know something that does not exist in that form at all—it is merely potential. How could we come to know it if we had turned away from forms and features, which are what lead the mind to recognize matter? If, instead, these thinkers want to know matter with a form, such as air or water, then they have not stopped considering form.

Avicenna adds that the miner does not take iron as the subject of the craft. Iron is its goal. The subject is the metal on which the craft works by engraving or melting; that work is the craft’s form. Obtaining iron is its goal, and iron then becomes material for other crafts. In short, to seek a thing is really to seek it as a form, not as matter.

Avicenna then describes another group of natural philosophers who dismissed matter. They said that matter exists only so forms can appear in it, and that form is the first thing intended. Once we understand form, there is no need to look at matter, except as a distraction. Avicenna says they go too far in dismissing matter, just as the first group went too far in dismissing form. They are willing to ignore how forms and matter fit together, even though not every form suits every matter, and not every matter is ready for every form. If true knowledge means understanding a thing as it is and knowing what follows from it, and if a specific form requires a certain matter, how can we fully understand the form without the matter?

Avicenna also says that no matter is more widely shared or farther from form than prime matter. Knowing its nature, or knowing that it is potential for everything, teaches us that a form in such matter must either pass away when another takes its place, or may do so, though we cannot be sure. The natural philosopher needs to understand both form and matter to prove things and complete the science. Still, form teaches us more about what a thing actually is, while matter usually teaches us about its potential existence. Together, they give us a full understanding of the thing’s substance.

I say that this second group is not as far off the mark in dismissing matter as the first group is in dismissing form. Their view has some strength.

But Avicenna’s claim that a form’s nature needs a particular matter is not always true. In its general existence, the nature of a form makes the matter what it is, just as the nature of bodily form makes prime matter what it is. A form needs shared or particular matter only through the individual features and conditions of its instances. Taken in general, as the nature of a form, it does not need any matter at all. We have proved that each natural form has a way of existing apart from matter and its features. So anyone who sets matter aside when learning the general nature of each natural kind, apart from its individual forms in the outside world, is not far from the truth. The exact features of individual things, simply as individual things, are not the subject of the true sciences.

The questions studied in these sciences must have answers that do not change. Still, individual things may be studied as examples of a kind of nature, considered in general. Then a good inquiry should include both matter and form. The claim that prime matter is the most widely shared matter and the farthest from form does not clearly prove what Avicenna wants it to prove. Asking about prime matter—whether it is matter for all forms, whether it is potential for everything, whether it is one or many, and what kind of unity it has—is really asking about form, not matter simply as matter. Prime matter, too, has two aspects like matter and form: its broader kind and its difference. Its being a substance is its broader kind; its being matter for things is its difference. Its potentiality for everything is also the actuality through which its simple kind is complete.

Studying prime matter as pure potentiality does not require us to study every kind of thing made from matter and form. We can study many kinds of body without turning to prime matter. If knowing the matter of every kind were required to know that kind, no one could fully understand any kind of body without knowing and returning to prime matter. But this is not so. Many earlier philosophers even denied that prime matter had any real nature.

What seems right to me is this: material kinds, insofar as they exist in a world of change and motion, are made up of absence, coming-to-be, motion, and perfection. They must therefore have both a side of potentiality and a side of actuality. A philosopher should understand both sides of each thing’s lack and perfection: its potentiality and actuality, its matter and form. Its form is the form of something that moves toward it and becomes complete through it.

#### Chapter 8 — How Matter and Form Make Up a Natural Body

Our view agrees with what one of the later great thinkers from our city of Shiraz, may God protect it and its people, came to understand: matter and form unite in one being. To explain this, we need two starting points.

First, as we have said, things differ in how fully they exist. Some are so complete that, in their unity, they are many meanings at once—meanings found separately in other things. Others are less complete. A human being is an example of the first kind: a human being contains together what is found separately in animals, plants, and nonliving things.

Second, there are two kinds of combination. In one, one thing is added to another, and each has its own separate existence. The combined thing then contains several actual things, as a house is made of bricks. This is not a natural combination in itself. It may be artificial, as with a house; merely incidental, as with a stone lying beside a person; or natural only in an incidental way, as when some parts of an animal are joined to others, as we will explain.

In the second kind, something changes in its very being and becomes something else, completing one single thing. There is then one being that is each of its parts and is also the whole. An embryo that develops into a wise human being is an example. In general, whenever natural matter takes on a substantial form, the combination may be called a union into one being. This does not conflict with the philosophers’ claim that a natural compound has external parts. Nor does it erase the difference between simple and compound things, or between a combination that exists in the world and one made only in the mind. In this kind of compound, one part may exist on its own: the part corresponding to broader kind may exist without the part corresponding to difference. That is not true of a compound made only in the mind. Color, for example, cannot exist without its ability to make sight distinguish it from other colors. But animal life can exist without reason, and a growing body can exist outside an animal, without sensation or life.

There are many arguments for this union. One is that matter and form can be predicated of each other: we say “an animal is a growing body,” “a plant is a body,” and “a body is a substance that can receive dimensions.” Saying one thing *is* another means that they are one in being, although the reason we say this is not the same as the reason we distinguish them as parts, as we have shown in our discussion of nature.

In short, the parts of a natural compound are one in reality in this way: the mind can analyze one natural thing into two parts and consider each apart from the other. One part may exist without being the other, and then later become identical with it. This does not mean that both parts have separate existences within the compound, as most people think. Proof shows otherwise. Or the mind may consider one thing that has two meanings, one corresponding to broader kind and the other to difference. In that case, the thing ceases to exist as one meaning and remains as the other. The first way of looking at things applies to natural changes toward greater perfection; the second to changes that happen by chance or through force.

A seed that grows into a plant and a reproductive fluid that develops into an animal are examples of the first kind. A tree that is cut down or pulled up, and water that turns into air, are examples of the second. When a tree is cut, it ceases to be a growing thing but remains a body. In the same way, when water turns into air, it ceases to be water but remains a body. So the parts of these natural compounds are also parts that the mind separates, as with simple bodies. They are not several separate things in the outside world.

If matter and form were two different beings in a body, we could not truly say that one is the other, however we considered them. Some of the most careful thinkers have said that when two parts exist separately, we cannot truly say that one is the other or that they are identical, nor can their whole be identified with either part. Ordinary understanding confirms this. Yet matter can be predicated of the compound when we consider it in a certain way, as we have explained. Most careful thinkers, including Avicenna and those who follow him, say this directly: matter is identical with the compound. Form can also be predicated of it, and each can be predicated of the other. The whole must therefore be one.

A second argument is that the elements are not actually present as separate elements in the three groups of things produced in nature—minerals, plants, and animals. A ruby, for example, does not contain an actually existing part of fire. If it did, the ruby’s form would be present in that part, since most philosophers who accept the ruby’s form agree that it spreads through the whole compound. But if the ruby’s form were present in a part that was still actually fire, one body would be both fire and ruby, which is impossible. It would also follow that the ruby was not made from the four elements, contrary to their agreement. And how could the fiery part remain in a small ruby for so long, surrounded by watery parts, without being put out? In truth, if any part of a ruby were an element in its own right, however small, that part itself would be a ruby. So the elements do not exist as separate actual parts in living things. Their combination with the form is therefore a union of the kind we have described.

The elements turn into mixtures; mixtures turn into reproductive fluid; that turns into an embryo, and so on until an animal comes to be. What comes earlier in this chain does not remain as an actual thing inside what comes later. Otherwise, there would be several actual things in one compound.[^V05-48] Each stage—the food and its parts, for example—is one natural thing, not several things in reality. The mind can divide it by its effects and features, treating some meanings as its matter or broader kind, and others as its form or difference. This is supported by Avicenna’s statement in *The Alai Wisdom* that matter and form are one in their being, though different in meaning.

A third argument is that matter is what makes a thing potential, while form is what makes it actual. They must therefore be one, and form must be that thing itself. This is why philosophers say that prime matter is pure potentiality for everything.

Tusi writes in his commentary on *The Directives* that the levels of honor and freedom from potentiality correspond in two orders, and both lead down to prime matter, whose existence is nothing but potentiality and which is therefore the lowest. The earlier commentator says that matter is potential: once it is actual, it is a body. Bahmanyar says in *The Book of Attainment* that matter is a substance not in a subject; its being a substance does not make it actually one thing or another, but prepares it to become an actual thing through form.

If a body were composed of matter and form as two beings that actually existed separately, form could not be defined as the body’s nature, just as a roof cannot be defined as the nature of a house. Yet philosophers define form as the nature through which a thing is what it is. Avicenna says in *The Healing* that every body has a nature, matter, form, and features; its form is its nature, through which it is what it is, and its matter is what bears that nature. If the body’s matter and form make up one being, then it is right to define the body’s form as its nature: this one being is its form, though the mind may draw from it another, indistinct meaning.

A fourth argument is that the soul is the body’s form and the body is its matter, as careful philosophers have held and Avicenna and his followers have stated. The soul has the particular features of the body. Whatever has the particular features of something is identical with it. So the soul is identical with the body.

The first claim is something everyone can recognize from experience. The “I” we point to is what moves, sits, eats, smells, and tastes. We say “I sit,” “I eat,” “I smell,” and “I taste” without metaphor. These are particular features of the body, and the soul has them. The second claim has been proved elsewhere: one particular feature cannot belong to two subjects. An accident exists only by existing in its subject, and one existence cannot belong to two different things unless they are one in some way. So the soul is the body. If the soul is identical with the body, then every form is identical with its matter. No one accepts a different rule for other forms; indeed, forms other than the soul have an even stronger claim to be identical with matter.

There are several objections. One says that philosophers have declared forms to be causes of matter. If matter and form are one, how can one cause the other? The answer is that they are not causes of one another when considered as one thing. The mind analyzes the one thing into several aspects and judges that one aspect causes another. There is no reason why one being cannot be analyzed by the mind into several meanings, with causal relations among them. This happens with the parts of a definition: when the mind analyzes the definition of blackness, it treats its difference—what makes sight unable to distinguish it—as a cause of its broader kind, color, and treats both as causes of the nature. The mind does this when it considers the meanings separately; when it takes them without restriction, one is predicated of the other and of the nature. Matter and form work the same way. They are one being, but if each is considered only in its restricted sense, neither can be predicated of the whole, because the whole is both. Considered without restriction, each is predicated of the nature and of the other. In that unrestricted sense, matter and broader kind are one meaning, considered in different ways. The same is true of form and difference.

A second objection is that this account works only for compounds whose parts are alike, such as a ruby, where one thing can be matter and form in one sense, and broader kind and difference in another. But it cannot work for a compound whose parts differ, such as a horse, which has bone, flesh, and nerves. How could these different things be identical with the horse’s form, which is one thing?

I answer that everything found alongside a thing’s matter and form does not have to be part of its nature or of its being as that particular thing. Some things are additions or preparations. The horse’s form is one thing: the nature and identity that produce its many actions in matter in an ordered way. The limbs are effects and features of that form; they are not all parts of its matter. This is why some of them may remain after the form passes away. If they were all parts of the horse’s matter in their particular forms, none would remain when the horse died and its form was lost. But that is not what happens. In any case, the form is a cause of its particular matter. When the cause ends, its particular effect ends too. In reality, the horse is one natural thing with many meanings that can be truly said of it: body, growing, nourished, sensing, and more. There is no actual multiplicity in the horse itself. Its real bodily nature is not just the visible collection of limbs that exist in different ways.

We have also said that matter as the bearer of a thing’s potentiality and capacity is not the same as the matter that serves as the form’s present, actual material. For a human being, matter in the first sense is reproductive fluid, or the embryo. Matter in the second sense is indistinct in itself and made definite by form. This answer is better than the one offered by the respected Sayyid Sanad. He says that the horse is one natural thing with no actual plurality, but the horse’s body is not one thing in reality; it is one part of a single natural being, as are its other parts. A ruby is the same: its parts can be distinguished by the mind, but in the ruby they have one nature. He compares this with the great sphere of fixed stars, which is one real sphere, though its stars have different natures.

That view cannot be right. Saying that bone, flesh, and other parts are conceptually divided parts of a horse, though they differ in nature and do not actually exist as separate things, conflicts with plain reason and with what Avicenna and others proved: imagined division of a measured body produces parts of the same nature. They used this to refute Democritus. Avicenna says in *The Metaphysics of The Healing* that continuity may be considered with size alone, or with another nature as well; in the latter case, it cannot be divided into different forms. And treating the entire heavens and stars as one continuous body conflicts with the philosophers’ proof that each star has a motion of its own, different from the motion of its sphere. A third objection says that this union cannot apply to a human being, because philosophers hold that the human soul, its form, is a substance separate from matter, while the body is physical. Their union would make the physical body separate from matter, or make the separate soul physical.

I answer that this would follow only if human souls were separate from matter from the moment they first came to be and became forms that guide matter. But souls are physical as souls at first. When they become stronger and more complete in their own being, they become separate from matter. As a separate form, the soul is no longer with the body. By saying that a human being is a rational animal, we mean an animal capable of understanding ideas in actuality, because each human being can come to understand them. Every human being is therefore potentially both knower and known, and potentially separate from matter, but not separate in actuality.

If, as most people think, a human being were made up of a soul and body as two separate things, several unacceptable results would follow. First, our own experience tells us that the “I” we point to is the one who understands, moves, and sits in a place. If this “I” were the body, then it could not understand, since a body simply as body does not understand. If it were the soul, it could not sit in a place. If it were both together, a combination of something separate from matter and something physical could not be a single knower or sitter. But unity goes with being: what has no unity has no being.

Second, philosophers define a human being as a rational animal, and explain “rational” as able to understand general ideas. This definition cannot apply to the separate soul alone, nor to the body alone, which cannot understand anything, much less general ideas. Nor can it apply to a combination of the two. Third, a true natural unity made from something actually separate from matter and something actually physical is impossible. Sharif Jurjani denied this in his notes on *The Science of the Eye*, arguing that each belongs to a different broader kind: the soul to separate substance, the body to substance joined to matter. They cannot, he said, form one real thing.

He allowed that they might be one only in the mind. If that were enough, then the Active Intellect could be one thing with the bodies it creates and governs. Dawani’s response to these objections is also unsound, as I will explain. On the first point, he says that “I” does not mean someone who sits. Avicenna and others say that “I” points to the separate soul; we wrongly describe it with bodily features because we imagine that it is the body. Everyday speech does not prove what things really are. People speak of God in bodily terms and look toward the sky when they turn to Him, though He is beyond direction and bodily features. Denying our own experience here seems forced, even though some major philosophers, including the author of *The Book of Illumination*, have done so.

It is not a figure of speech in ordinary language when we call a human being growing, located, walking, eating, desiring, or sleeping. Such a claim conflicts with both experience and proof. Experience shows that we ourselves have these bodily features and actions. Proof shows that the “I” we point to is a human being, every human being is an animal, and every animal is a body. These features apply to the body in the real sense, not metaphorically. How divine attributes that sound bodily apply to God is a serious question that only those deeply grounded in knowledge of divine unity can answer.

As for Dawani’s second point, he says that the human being defined as a rational animal is the whole made up of soul and body. Avicenna has shown in *The Healing* that this whole is a body: it is a substance in which dimensions can be imagined. Not every part needs to help receive dimensions. The specific form of a natural body does not do so; only bodily form does. In the same way, we can imagine dimensions in the human whole even if the soul, which serves as its form, does not help receive them.

This whole can understand general ideas because it includes the separate soul that understands them. A derived description does not require its source to belong to the thing in the same way as a part.

On the third point, Dawani says that a true union between what is separate from matter and what is physical is possible when the separate thing governs and directs the physical thing. The fact that they belong to different broader kinds does not prevent it. Both claims fail. If the first were true, we would have to call the whole made up of heaven and earth both heaven and earth, however we considered it. But we can treat something as a whole and as a part only when its aspects exist as one thing, not when they are separate beings.

His claim that bodily dimensions depend on bodily form and not on the form of a particular kind does not help. The whole human being is said to receive dimensions, but we are asking whether matter and form unite in one being. A thing’s form is itself what makes true all the meanings that apply to its matter, along with the added meaning of its form. The form that makes an animal the kind of thing it is also receives dimensions, grows, and senses. The bodily form that remains after death and decay is not the same individual form that was united with the living animal, as we have said.

Dawani’s claim that a derived description does not require the source of the description to belong to the thing may have some truth. But the source must either belong to it or be one with it. A mere relation to the source is not enough. The examples used to support his view—such as a blacksmith, a date seller, or something warmed by the sun—do not prove it. Most are either figures of speech or treat as a source something that is not really a source.

As for his second claim, proof shows that the matter of a thing is what can take on that thing’s form, not what cannot. A body cannot take the form of a separate substance, whatever relation we imagine between them.

One further point. The respected Sayyid Sanad answered the third objection in another way. In his view, neither the soul nor the body exists in actuality on its own. The existence that makes the compound real belongs to something else, which the mind can analyze into soul and body. He applies the same account to every compound of matter and form. He says their union does not make a separate thing physical or a physical thing separate, because matter and form are not two different things in the outside world.

The human being, he says, develops from reproductive fluid through several stages and is one natural thing, with no actual parts in the outside world, whether physical or separate from matter. The mind divides it through its effects into a substance that can receive dimensions, grow, sense, and understand general ideas. Having some of these features—receiving dimensions and growing—requires position, size, and location. Understanding general ideas does not. So a human being understands general ideas insofar as it is separate from these three features. This is what philosophers mean by “separate from matter”: bare or stripped of position, size, and location.

An animal can also change into something that keeps some animal features but loses others, as when a horse dies. Its size, shape, and color remain, but its growth and sensation do not. If a human being changes into something that no longer has size, position, or location but still understands general ideas, then it has become separate from matter. The separate thing that was potential in the human being becomes actual. There is no proof that this cannot happen. The objection comes from mistaking what exists potentially for what exists actually. The separate soul, the form of a human being, is therefore a part that the mind analyzes, not a separate part of the compound. That is his account.

I say that this account is wrong. The union of matter and form means that one existing thing is the form, and that same existence is what serves as matter. Both are present in actuality through this one existence. The mind analyzes them by considering each apart from the other.

For example, a body is matter for the form of a plant. Before it becomes a plant, we can say that it is a substance able to receive dimensions. Once it takes on the form of a plant, it also belongs to other meanings: it feeds itself, grows, and reproduces. All these meanings are actual in one being, not potential, as long as we consider them without separating one from another. Taken without restriction, “body” is a broader kind that applies to what grows. Taken with the restriction that nothing else is present in it, it is matter. The same distinction separates the form from the difference in the meaning “growing.” We need the mind to analyze the parts only because we consider them as parts.

These meanings are potential only in the sense that they could exist separately if the compound came apart. We say, for example, that a strong shade of black contains weaker shades potentially, or that a large continuous body contains smaller bodies potentially. No one can say that this animal is not actually a body, only that it is not a body alone. Nor can anyone say that it does not actually sense, only that it is not only a sensing thing.

In the same way, a human being actually contains all the meanings present in the nature of an animal, with the added capacity to understand in actuality. But there seems to be a conflict between those earlier meanings and actual understanding of general ideas, since opposites cannot be actual in one existence. We answer this by saying that “rational” means able to understand general ideas, not understanding them at every moment. In its bodily existence, a human being understands them only after leaving behind this material existence and many of its coverings, and reaching another kind of existence: finally becoming a separate substance. This happens when the soul becomes intellect, and its sense-based form becomes the meaningful matter for an intellectual form that is both intellect and understood.

#### Chapter 9 — Answering Doubts About the Union of Form and Matter

One objection was raised by the author of the *Notes on the Abstracted Principles* against a contemporary thinker. The view of union that he opposed is very different from ours. He says that the mind analyzes even simple things into broader kind and difference. For example, it analyzes color into a broader kind and into what distinguishes it: what makes sight unable to tell black apart from other colors, or white apart from other colors. If the account of union were right, these features would have matter and form. Yet Avicenna clearly says that features do not have matter and form.

The same analysis, he says, applies to separate substances. Each is a particular substance, with “substance” as its broader kind and a difference that sets it apart from others. So it would need matter and could not be separate from matter. This is absurd. Their words “matter” and “form,” then, cannot mean what this account takes them to mean: matter is not the part the mind analyzes as broader kind, nor is form the difference that distinguishes it. Otherwise, everything other than the God, whose existence depends on nothing else would be made of matter, since all possible things can be analyzed into broader kind and difference.

I say that the difference between what we call simple and compound is not what this thinker supposes: that in a simple thing the meaning of broader kind and the meaning of difference have one existence, while in a compound they have several. If so, a human being such as Zayd would not be one existing thing but many things with only a made-up unity, like an army. Things that exist separately cannot become one existing thing just because we consider them in a certain way. The subject of numerical unity cannot also be the subject of multiplicity, even under two different descriptions.

Of course, one numerically single thing may be made of many meanings: one in its being, many in its meaning. The real difference between simple and compound is what we have already explained. The meaning corresponding to broader kind and matter may exist on its own, without the meaning corresponding to difference and form. A seed can exist before it becomes a plant, and reproductive fluid before it becomes an animal.

When a form appears after matter is ready for it, the existence matter had on its own ends, and a better, fuller existence takes its place. This new existence carries all the meanings that applied to the matter, in a nobler way, and adds a new meaning: the feature that most directly distinguishes the new kind. Everything that followed from the matter now follows from the new form. But the earlier matter itself is not the broader kind that unites with the form. The mind can strip the broader kind of its difference and call it intellectual matter, which is one in nature with the external matter. In the same way, when the mind strips the difference of its broader kind, it is called an intellectual form. This exists in the mind, not in the outside world.

When philosophers say that features or separate substances do not have matter and form, they mean that these things do not have external bodily matter. They do not mean that the mind cannot analyze them into meanings related as matter and form. After all, they compare the relation between a nature and its existence to the relation between matter and form. Avicenna says that the God, whose existence depends on nothing else is simple in reality, while everything else is a compound. Calling matter and form “matter” and “form” in both simple and compound things is not merely using the same words for unrelated meanings. Their meanings have something in common: the matter of a thing is what does not complete its nature, while its form is what completes it.

The objector then says that the claim that elemental forms remain in compounds conflicts with the accepted view of philosophers. He also says that the ruby’s form does not spread through all its parts, as we claimed, and that philosophers do not agree on this. Rather, he says, the form of a ruby spreads through its compound parts, which have the mixed qualities, but not through its simple elemental parts. A thing may spread through some of its parts but not others, or in one respect but not another. A line, for example, extends across a surface only in length; a surface extends across a measured body in length and width, but not depth.

I say, first, that proof is what matters, not reports about what the great philosophers believed. Second, even if elemental forms remain in the ruby,[^V05-49] they are not part of the ruby’s form or of its matter considered as matter. Matter is itself indeterminate. If a ruby’s form could exist in other matter—or even without matter—it would still be a ruby. We will explain more fully in our discussion of mixture why none of the elements exists in its pure form in natural kinds such as these.

Here is evidence that the elements, if they exist in the ruby, have no part in the nature of ruby, just as the elements and mixtures do not make up the nature of an animal. Bahmanyar says in *The Book of Attainment* that we see bodies made up of elements and mixtures sensing, moving by choice, feeding, growing, and replacing what they lose. These bodies must have a particular bodily nature that their parts do not have. A body without this nature cannot produce a special action. Growth, feeding, and replacing what is lost belong to the body as a whole. Each mixture or elemental part does not itself grow, feed, or replace what it loses. In the same way, sight belongs to the eye as a whole, not to each part of it; growth belongs to the body as a whole, not to each of its parts.

So animals and plants have a particular bodily nature that their parts do not have. Their parts exist in potentiality, since whatever is one in actuality has its parts in potentiality. That is the end of Bahmanyar’s argument.

Dawani then says that a passage he quotes from Avicenna’s *The Alai Wisdom* does not prove what he wants. Avicenna may mean that matter and form are one in their being as broader kind and difference, not as matter and form. Dawani adds that to say they are one in being means only that the unity of their combination is real, not a made-up unity like the unity of an army or the number ten. Their combination makes one thing.

Dawani also explains a passage from *The Directives* and its commentary this way: when matter takes on the form of water, it becomes water only incidentally, just as a body becomes black when blackness enters it. The union here is like that between an accident and its subject, and is merely incidental. It can still produce a third thing from them, as happens with accidents and their subjects.

I say that Dawani’s first point in fact admits what we are arguing. We do not claim that matter is one with form in every way it exists. We claim that it has a way of existing that is itself the form. That existence is the very existence of the form in the outside world. The form also has another existence in the mind, apart from matter.

Dawani’s second point—that the unity of matter and form in a compound is a real unity, not a made-up one—is badly mistaken. A unity is real when one thing, through its own existence, is many things. Their unity is actual; their multiplicity is potential. That is exactly what we mean when we say form itself is the real subject of the meaning that applies to matter.

Dawani’s third point is also wrong, for two reasons. First, it conflicts with his earlier admission that their unity is real. When two things are truly one, their union is in their very being, not merely incidental. What is incidental is figurative, as careful thinkers admit, including Dawani himself. The difference between form and an accident is clear to anyone who understands the matter.

How could anyone say both that the union of a body and water is incidental, since water is water through its form, and that the union of broader kind and difference is also incidental, when broader kind and difference exist through one being? Matter and form are the same in this respect when we consider them without restriction. Some people may have been confused by the philosophers’ claim that broader kind is a general feature of its difference and difference a special feature of its broader kind. They failed to distinguish features of a definition from features of existence, and thought that the first was a real relation of subject and attribute. That is false. The existence of a difference is the same existence as the broader kind it completes. They differ only in definition and meaning: the meaning of “rational,” for example, is not the meaning of “animal,” though the existence of rationality is the very existence of animality.

The objector then says that when Avicenna defines form as the nature through which a thing is what it is, he means that form causes the body to become actual. So in “the body is what it is through form,” the word “through” means “because of.” But in the definition of nature as “that through which a thing is what it is,” he says, “through” denies causation: a human being is not human because of its nature. He also says we cannot identify a body’s form with its nature. Nor can we define a difference by the nature of a thing. If we could, then because matter and form are also one, we could define matter by the same nature. If form were the whole nature, and also the difference, matter—which is broader kind—would fall outside the nature, which no one would accept. And if form were the nature, it could answer the question “What is it?” and so would itself be taken as a broader kind. That would make difference answer the question “What is it?”, which is a contradiction.

I say that “through” has the same meaning in both places: it points to a cause, while denying that something else is the cause. When we say a thing’s nature is what makes it what it is, we mean that its identity does not come from something else. Likewise, when we say that a body is what it is through its form, we mean that once the form exists, the body needs nothing else for its nature.

There is no reason to reject defining a thing’s form by its nature. The claim that we cannot define a difference by the nature of a thing confuses the meaning of a difference with what it is, or confuses a general definition with a strict one. There is no rule against defining a thing’s difference by its nature: for example, we may define “rational” as “human,” since the meaning of “human” applies to everything rational and nothing else, and some people may find “human” more familiar than “rational.”

Still, a difference cannot be strictly defined by a nature, and it has no definition of its own—especially in our view, where the form corresponding to a difference is itself a kind of existence. Nature lies outside existence in meaning and definition, but is one with it in actual being. Someone who says that a thing’s form is its whole nature does not say this only because they share one existence, so it does not follow that matter must also be the nature or can be defined in the same way. The objector’s claim that if form is the whole nature it can answer “What is it?” is also false. “Human,” for example, is the whole nature of an animal, but it is not the answer to the question “What is an animal?” That argument confuses nature with existence.

The existence of every thing is its whole nature and is one with it, but existence itself cannot answer the question “What is it?” You already know that the form of a thing is its very existence, and that its existence is the existence of all its essential features in the primary sense, and of its incidental features in a secondary sense.

Avicenna states in one of his treatises that a thing is what it is through its form, not its matter. He stresses the point: a bed is a bed through its shape, not its wood; a sword is a sword through its sharpness, not its iron. Philosophers say that the purpose and result of gaining wisdom is for the human soul to become an intellectual world, like this world of the senses. They argue that the world is a world through its form, not its matter. When the forms of all existing things—the heavens, the elements, and what they contain—are present in the soul in an intellectual way, the soul becomes an intellectual world. From this, we see that each thing’s form completes its nature, and that its form’s existence is the existence of its nature.

One final reminder: in this important question and in others, we rely on proof, not merely on what philosophers have said. When their words agree with the proof, they give the heart greater confidence. But language can easily cause confusion. This question is so difficult that, among the well-known philosophers after the earliest thinkers, including Avicenna and his peers, I know of no one who accepted this principle fully and firmly. It depends on several ideas that go against common opinion. Existence itself is present in every existing thing, and one existence can become stronger or weaker. A substance can change in its very being and become more complete. In a thing, matter is the weaker side of its being, while form is the stronger side.

Other such ideas are that some bodily forms, such as the soul, though natural forms, can become matter for forms separate from matter and unite with them, so that they become one; and that one simple thing can itself be many natures, present in a higher and nobler way than each nature exists on its own. These and other difficult questions underlie this view.

The words we have quoted from Avicenna, Bahmanyar, and the commentators on *The Directives* are places where God made the truth come through their words. We quoted them to challenge those who deny this possibility and find it impossible. But the widespread view in books such as *The Healing* is that a natural compound contains two parts that both exist in actuality as separate things.

Avicenna says in *The Metaphysics of The Healing* that every simple thing is the nature of itself, because nothing receives its nature from something else. If something received a nature, it would become the form of what received it, not the thing that receives the nature. A compound is not what it is through form alone, because the definition of a compound does not contain form alone; it points to everything that makes the compound what it is, including matter in some way. This is how we distinguish nature from form in compounds. Form is always one part of the nature of a compound. A simple thing’s form is also the thing itself, since it has no parts. But a compound is neither its form alone nor its nature alone. Form is clearly only part of it.

Nature is what makes a thing what it is, and a thing is what it is because its form is joined to matter. Nature therefore means more than form alone. Nor is a compound simply this meaning: it is the total made from matter and form. Nature is this whole combination, while form is one part of it. In another place, Avicenna says there are several kinds of union. One is like the union of matter and form: matter has no existence on its own in one respect, but becomes actual through form. Form is outside matter and neither is the other; the whole is neither one of them.

Another kind is the union of things that can each exist on their own but combine into one thing, either by being joined or by changing and mixing together. A third kind is the union of things where one cannot exist in actuality without the other, while the other can. The first becomes actual through the second, and together they form one whole, as with a body and whiteness. In none of these kinds are the things that unite identical with each other or with the whole, and none can be predicated of another in the same sense. A further kind is when one thing unites with another so that the first can become the second, rather than simply being added to it. The mind can understand a meaning that may be many different things, as Avicenna explains when discussing how broader kind and difference unite.

I say that any statement by philosophers that matter and form differ, or that the nature of a compound differs from its form, can be understood in a way that does not conflict with their unity. In the first case, they differ when considered as matter and form, but not when considered as broader kind and difference. In the second, “form” means the form as it exists in the outside world, where every natural form is made up of potentiality and actuality, coming-to-be and passing away. When they say form is identical with nature, they mean form as it exists on its own in the intellect.

#### Chapter 10 — More on the Union of Matter and Form: Objections and Replies

Dawani says: You know that proof shows that anyone who understands general ideas must be separate from matter. If the soul exists as one with the body and becomes separate only at death, as you suppose, then no universal form could take shape in it. It would follow that no one could understand general ideas until after death. And how could something physical change into something separate from matter? If the soul is one stage in the body’s changes, then the effects of being separate could not exist until that change had happened, just as the effects of reproductive fluid are not in food, the effects of an early embryo are not in reproductive fluid, and the effects of a developed body are not in that early embryo. That is his argument.

I say that proof does not show that human beings, at the moment they first come to be, already understand general ideas as general ideas, in an intellectual existence shared by many and wholly free from size, position, or place. The nature of a thing that exists outside the mind is particular in actuality, though it can become universal in the mind. We can remove it from matter and its features by thought. In the same way, the human power to understand is potentially able to know and to become separate from matter. It can remove matter and its features from itself and then understand general ideas in actuality. We have proved that the one who understands and what is understood become one in the act of understanding. Unless what understands becomes the universal in actuality, it cannot understand a universal in actuality.

In short, in every act of understanding, the one who understands and what is understood are at the same level.
