How weak, then, is the claim that the bodily being of an embryo, so close to the states of nonliving things and plants, already has a form that understands itself and separate universal ideas in actuality. The claim that we could understand general ideas only after death also needs qualification. If death means that the soul turns away from the natural world, then every act of understanding requires such a turning-away. Full understanding requires full detachment; limited understanding requires only limited detachment. The soul may be separate from matter in one respect and connected to it in another. Its unity is not like the unity of a point, which cannot be one with many things. The soul has a unity that brings together many levels.

If death means that the body can no longer serve as a body for the soul, this is not required for the soul to become an intellect in actuality. Nor does becoming an intellect in actuality prevent the soul from guiding a natural body through some of its powers, which are like rays from the light of intellect. This is much like the soul before it reaches full maturity, especially when the body is weak and its matter worn down. A flash from the Active Intellect is enough to guide it. In sleep, for example, the soul is separate from the body in one respect, yet it still guides the body through some of its powers.

The objection that something physical cannot change into something separate from matter rests on a denial that a substance can grow stronger or change in its very being. We have already proved that it can. As for the claim that understanding general ideas would not be possible before this change, we have said that what first appears in the soul is the power and readiness to understand. This power—the potential to exist as intellect—is like the soul’s difference, just as the potential to exist as a body is like matter’s difference. At first the soul is a potential intellect. After many changes in its own being, it becomes an intellect in actuality.

Dawani then says: We do not accept that a true union between what is separate from matter and what is physical is impossible when the separate thing governs and directs the physical thing, as the soul does the body. Their belonging to different kinds does not prevent their union. If difference in kind does not prevent a true connection between parts of a ruby, why should it prevent a true union in being between matter and form? Sharif Jurjani allowed a true union between a substance and a feature, as in a bed. Why not between one substance and another? The Active Intellect is not one with bodies because it does not govern them. If it did, it would be a soul, not an intellect. And if the soul can unite with the body, someone could also allow the intellect to unite with a body. Whatever answer you give applies to both.

I say that Dawani himself admits that a natural compound must have one real unity. Merely having a relation between two things does not make them one being. Otherwise, every pair of things in this world would be one, since every two bodies or features have at least a spatial relation. The relation of governing and directing is weak; by itself, it cannot give two related things a real unity. The relation of bringing something into being and acting on it is stronger, yet it still does not make the Active Intellect one with a body. We now know what gives a natural kind its real unity: its material and formal parts must be one in being. Other relations do not do this.

The Active Intellect is not a soul governing bodies because its being is too high and pure to be joined to bodies simply as bodies. The soul, by contrast, is connected to the body as a soul. And the relation of being present in something, as a kind is present in its feature, does not in Dawani’s view make them one being, while the weaker relation of guiding and directing does. That is strange: presence is a stronger relation than guiding or moving. You may ask: if guiding and directing are not enough to unite soul and body, how can you say they are one? I answer that their relation is not only one of guidance. In one respect, the soul is one with the body; in another, it acts on the body as nature does. Nature completes matter and is one with it in one respect, while also acting through it on something else.

Dawani says that their belonging to different kinds does not matter here. I answer that things that differ in kind cannot make one true thing, because each kind has its own way of being. The same applies to things that already exist as separate species. But broader kind and difference within one nature do not differ in this way. They differ only as the vague differs from the specific. Broader kind and difference are not two separate things; likewise, the relation of broader kind to its species, the relation of a broader broader kind to a difference, and the relation of a species’ difference to its broader kind are not relations between separate beings.

Matter and form, when considered specifically as matter and form, differ in kind, since each is restricted in a way that prevents it from being the other. But this is not what makes them one. They are one because, in another respect, they are broader kind and difference. Dawani’s example of a bed made from a substance and an accident is also wrong if he means wood and the bed’s particular shape, as we explained earlier. If he means its measured size and shape, then these do not differ in kind: their relation is like that of broader kind and difference. His claim that if soul can unite with body then intellect can unite with it has already been answered by the difference between these relations.

Dawani then says: If soul and body are one, the forms in the soul must have a particular size and place, and so cannot be universal. A difference of aspect does not solve this. I say that a difference of aspect alone would not solve it. But the soul can change in its very being and pass from one state to another, such as being connected to the body and being separate from matter. A single soul also has different powers and levels. Tell me, for the sake of your loved one: are not touch and reason both powers of one soul? Yet they differ greatly. The human soul has many levels, though it is one being with one existence. In some respects, it is connected to the body; in others, it is connected to intellect.

Dawani then says that our account would mean prime matter is not actually real. It would also mean that no kind exists in actuality, since every kind is one with the features that apply to it. And if matter is one with form in the outside world, it cannot be an external part. Then the difference philosophers draw between matter and broader kind would disappear: broader kind can be predicated of a thing, but matter cannot.

I say that a union into one being does not mean that one of its parts does not exist. It means that both exist through one being, not through two separate beings while they are joined, though one or both may also exist separately. Matter’s being one with a form that we can truly say belongs to it does not mean that it is one with the form when considered as external or intellectual matter. As intellectual matter, it is a part of a definition and is not predicated of the whole. It is one with the form, and we can truly say that it is the form, only when we take it without that restriction, leaving out the other part.

As for a kind and a dependent feature we can say belongs to it, we do not say that they are one in their very being, but only in an incidental way. As for being an external part, we said earlier that matter and form also have another way of existing in the outside world. This is also the difference between matter and broader kind: matter, considered as matter—as the capacity and readiness to receive a form—exists in a way separate from the form. But when we consider matter simply as the broader kind, without that restriction, we can truly say it is the form. As we have explained many times, matter and form each change and follow one another in different ways. Consider prime matter and bodily form.

Prime matter is, in one respect, a receptive power and a cause that prepares the way for form. In another respect, it depends on form, and form gives it its existence. How can what depends on something for its existence also be prior to it? This shows that earlier matter is not numerically the same as later matter. Earlier matter can receive a form, but it does not exist by itself; it is made actual by a form. So the earlier form that makes matter actual is not numerically the same as the later form that depends on matter in order to come to be.

The same applies to other matter and forms. This distinction resolves many difficulties. It answers the question of how the mixture that makes up reproductive fluid is held together. It also answers the question of how an animal’s body, which is its matter, can remain after the soul that is its form has passed away: as you now know, the body that remains is not the same individual body as before.

It also answers the question of how forms of the elements might remain in the matter of living things, since these forms can be brought out by striking or distilling the material. The matter that contains the forms revealed by such processes is not the matter that has already been given a single, uniting form. We will return to the question of whether elements remain in compounds in our discussion of mixture.

These two great thinkers did all they could to explain this issue, and each used every argument in his store of knowledge against the other. Yet neither reached a clear and complete account. One denied the actual existence of visible parts and organs, such as bone and flesh, in an animal. He also denied the actual existence of both soul and body in a human being, and denied matter and form in every kind of body. The other, by contrast, set aside—or even denied—the real unity of natural kinds, and failed to distinguish a body’s becoming water from its becoming white. He called the union of matter and form merely incidental, like the relation between a subject and its feature.

The reason both missed the mark was that they had not grasped what it means for something to exist. One supposed that existence was just the act of being, multiplying with each nature and general meaning. On this view, there is no real union between two things. The other supposed that each thing exists by being joined to the general mental idea “existing.” Neither understood that existence is a reality in the outside world that can grow stronger or weaker and become more or less complete. For matter to take on a form is for its existence to become stronger and more complete. As existence becomes stronger or more complete, it can take on meanings that did not apply before.

So a real union between two things must involve both unity and difference: unity in their existence and difference in their meaning and nature. There is nothing incoherent about this, despite Dawani’s claim that combination requires parts to differ and union rules out such difference.

I say: The real weakness in that objection is arguing over a label after the intended meaning has been made clear.

#### Chapter 11 — More on the Causes of Changing Natural Things

We have already discussed the four causes and their rules in a general way, not only as they apply to things that change. Here we consider how they apply to changing things, in a way that makes it easier to understand natural things. Every body has a material cause, an agent cause, a formal cause, and a final cause. Natural philosophers do not have to prove that every changing thing has all four causes. That proof belongs to the philosopher who studies divine things. But anyone who studies changing natural things must understand what these causes mean and accept their roles as starting points.

First, the agent in nature is the source of motion in another thing, insofar as that thing is other than the agent.

“Motion” here means any change from potentiality to actuality, in whatever matter. If the soul treats itself, then the part doing the treating acts on the part being treated: it treats the sick part as a doctor, not as a patient, and the patient receives treatment as a patient, not as a doctor. This example works only on our view that the soul has different levels. The thing that acts and the thing acted on must differ in a real way; a difference in how we describe one thing is not enough, as it is when we speak of the knower and the known.

An agent cause of this kind either prepares motion or completes it. The preparer makes matter ready to receive a form by changing, warming, or ripening it. The one that completes the process gives the form, which is the end of the motion. The first acts directly on matter and touches it; the second is separate from it. The one that gives forms to natural kinds—especially noble kinds such as heavenly bodies and human beings—must be a source outside the world of nature. Natural science does not need to know what this source is, or whether it is one or many. It can take as given that something gives these forms.

Both the preparer and the giver are causes of motion. They bring matter from potentiality to actuality. In a way, each is also a form, but neither is the form that comes to exist through the motion. The preparer is a particular form, lower in being than the form toward which the motion tends. The giver is a universal form, nobler than the form it gives.

There is also a source that stirs or draws the agent to act. It is one of the principles of motion. It is the cause of the soul-form that begins voluntary movement, and is therefore the cause of the cause. It may also produce a series of changing forms in the soul, such as repeated intentions. These forms then directly cause movements of the animal body, which we call voluntary movements. It may be more accurate to say that voluntary movement takes place in the soul, not in the body. We might call movement in the body “compelled” and movement in the soul “voluntary.”

In short, what stirs the agent is the cause of the cause. This is what “agent” means in natural science. It is a narrower meaning than “cause of existence,” which includes both changing and unchanging things.

As for the receptive cause, matter, all material causes share one feature: they bear things that are distinct from their own nature. This is not true of a necessary thing bearing its necessary features.[^V05-50] Matter also relates to a compound in two ways: as one part of it, and as the matter that receives its form. Its relation to the whole is like the relation of a body to a white body, a compound of body and whiteness. Its relation to form is like the relation of a body to its whiteness. As a part, matter is prior to the whole in being, and helps make the whole what it is.

Avicenna says matter’s relation to form can be understood in three ways. First, neither one is prior to the other as cause or effect. Second, something may depend on another thing in order to be actual, while that other thing is prior in its own being and does not depend on matter. Yet, when it exists, it makes its matter actual. Many things are made actual by one thing and also make another thing actual. Sometimes what they make actual is separate from them; sometimes it is mixed with them.

I say that Avicenna almost states the truth plainly here. He need only have said that the thing that makes matter actual depends on its own causes by being separate from matter, but makes matter actual by joining with it. Such a thing has two ways of existing: one separate, the other joined. It is a substantial form. Avicenna also says that it helps make matter actual by being joined to it, or that it is the whole immediate cause that makes matter actual.

The full explanation appears earlier in this book, in our discussion of how matter and form are linked. This can be true only if we accept that a substantial form exists both in matter and in intellect: in one way it makes matter actual from within, and in another way it makes it actual through a separate cause.

The third kind is when matter already exists in actuality and then receives something that depends on it. That something is called an accident or feature, in contrast with a form that completes the substance. The first kind would mean that matter and form exist together; the other two mean that one is prior and the other follows.

Avicenna says the first kind is hard to find. Perhaps an example is the soul and prime matter when they together make a human being actual. I say that the right approach is to divide the account into two kinds and use only the last two. We are discussing the essential principles of natural things, and natural things cannot be made from things that have no relation to one another.

The example of prime matter and soul can still be understood in this way: prime matter is matter for the soul through an intermediary, while the soul is its guiding form, or form for a form. The soul completes and makes matter actual; in that respect it comes before matter in being. Prime matter has such weak unity that it can be later than some things and earlier than others. But if we do not consider prime matter in relation to the soul, or the soul as a soul connected to prime matter or to a compound made from it and another feature, then neither term applies: matter is not matter, and soul is not soul, because neither is being considered in relation to the other.

Avicenna then says that matter may relate to a compound that comes from it in other ways. Sometimes matter alone is enough to be the material part of a compound; sometimes it needs other matter added to it. This may mean simple gathering, as people make up an army or houses make up a city; gathering and construction, as bricks and wood make a house; or gathering, construction, and change, as when elements make natural things. The elements do not make natural things merely by being gathered, touching, and taking a shape. They must also act on one another and be acted on, until they settle into a single, even quality called mixture. Then matter is ready for a specific form. That is Avicenna’s account.

I say that matter for any one form must be one, whatever kind of form it is. In all these examples, one form belongs to the compound. Where the parts do not change, the matter is the whole amount of them together, made one by a kind of continuous unity, as bricks and wood make up the matter for the shape of a house. Where the parts do change, they become ready for one form after the change. Taken together before the change—even before the parts touch—they can also be considered as one thing for one matter. In that case, their arrangement is the form, while their material parts are the things themselves, considered apart from that arrangement.

Avicenna then says that matter can mean matter for generation, for change, for gathering, for construction, or for construction together with change. “Form” may mean the feature that makes matter a certain kind of thing; it may mean the kind itself; it may mean shape; it may mean an arrangement, as with an army or a set of premises; it may mean an order that is preserved, such as a law; it may mean any feature whatever; it may mean the nature of any thing, whether substance or feature; it may even mean a form understood apart from matter. The form taken as one of the principles is, in relation to the compound, the part that makes it actual, while matter is the part that does not. Form may also be the cause of a class rather than a species: for example, the shape of a bed or the whiteness of a white body.

I say: when matter receives something, such as being brought together or combined with something else, what it receives is in fact a form that makes it a definite kind of thing. They complete a compound nature, whether it is made from matter and gathering, matter and construction, or matter and change. The result is the nature of a gathered thing or a changed thing, even if it is not a substantial kind and its form is not a substantial form.

Every meaning called “form” is shared in the sense that it makes something actual, whether it is a substance or a feature, and whether it belongs to a kind or a class. Each gives some degree of completion to matter and helps make the compound actual. Yet things differ in how fully they exist. Unity, as we know, is existence itself. A thing with fuller existence has stronger unity, farther from multiplicity and even from the potential for multiplicity. A thing with weaker existence has weaker unity, so it may be one together with actual or potential multiplicity.

Sometimes a thing’s unity is also the source of its multiplicity, as with number, prime matter, motion, and time. Their form is so immersed in matter that form and matter almost seem the same in being and in how we consider them, because their existence is so weak. At the other extreme are kinds whose existence and unity are so strong that they cannot be made from potentiality and actuality, or from lack and completion. They are forms without matter, as if matter had disappeared because they are wholly actual and complete.

The final cause is what a form exists in matter for. It is a real good, either in itself or for something else, or a good that only seems good. Every action that comes from an agent in itself, and not by chance, aims at what is good for that agent, whether it truly is good, absolutely or in some respect, or only seems good.

#### Chapter 12 — How These Principles Relate to One Another

You have learned that one nature may exist in more than one way and have more than one relation. The final cause, in one way of existing, comes before the action and even before the agent as an agent. In another way, it comes after the action as its result. So the goal is a goal in one respect and an agent in another. Form, too, is an agent in one respect, since it makes matter actual; it is a formal cause in another, since it makes a thing the kind of thing it is; and it is a goal in another, since motion from matter comes to an end in it. Matter, because of its potentiality, may also be matter for another form. In this way, one thing can include all the kinds of cause, without being made of several parts—except for matter and form, since potentiality and actuality are different, even when they relate to one thing.

Avicenna says: The agent cause is caused by the final cause, since the agent brings the goal into existence. And the goal is caused by the agent, since the agent acts for its sake. The goal moves the agent to become an agent. If someone asks, “Why do you exercise?” and you answer, “So I can become healthy,” that is an answer. If they ask, “Why did you become healthy?” and you answer, “Because I exercised,” that is also an answer. Exercise is the more remote cause of health, and health is the goal of exercise. But if someone asks, “Why do you seek health?” and you answer, “So I can exercise,” that is not a good answer.

If someone asks, “Why do you exercise?” and you answer, “So I can become healthy,” that is a good answer. The agent is not the cause of the goal’s being a goal, nor of what the goal is in itself. It causes the goal’s nature to exist in the outside world. That is Avicenna’s account.

I say that the difference between a thing’s nature and its existence does not make its nature a cause of anything unless some kind of existence is also involved. A thing cannot cause something to exist unless it itself exists. Existence alone acts on things; a nature as such does not. So the goal does not make the agent act just by being a nature. Its prior existence does. For example, a person eats for the sake of being full. Fullness must exist for the person before eating, so that the person eats for its sake. Then fullness exists again after eating. The earlier existence of fullness moves the agent to bring about a later and more complete existence.

In one of its existences, the goal is a formal part of the agent considered as an agent. It is not the goal that makes itself a goal; it is the agent that brings its existence into being for itself. Sometimes the goal’s existence in itself is the same as the existence that the action produces, as with goals brought about through motion and change. At other times, the goal’s existence in itself differs from its existence for the agent, as when the Active Intellect is the goal of the soul’s development. Its existence in itself is not the same as its existence for the soul, which comes after the soul has developed.

We now know that each of the four causes has two principles, whether in relation to two things or to one thing considered in two ways. Matter and form each cause both their partner and the compound: each is a close cause of one thing and a more remote cause of another. They are not close causes of the compound in the same way. They are close causes when they make it what it is; in another sense, they are more remote.

The same applies to the agent. It may prepare matter, and then it causes the matter close to the compound, but it is not a close cause of the compound itself. Or it may give the form, and then it causes the form close to the compound, but not the compound itself. The agent is also a remote cause of the final cause, since it causes the form, which entails the goal. And the goal is a more remote cause of the form’s existence in matter, since it moves the agent to bring about the compound.

So the nearest principles of a compound, simply as principles, are matter and form as its parts, even though they complete it in different ways. They are called “material cause” and “formal cause,” rather than simply “matter” and “form,” when they are considered in relation to the compound. But sometimes matter and form are also causes through another cause. Matter is a cause of the compound through its form when that form is an accidental feature in a compound made of different things. The matter is the cause of that feature, which makes the compound a certain kind of class. In that respect, matter is a cause of a cause. Still, as a part of the compound, it is a direct cause.

Form is a cause through another cause in natural compounds where form makes matter actual and matter is a close cause of the compound. Form is then a cause through matter, but it remains a direct formal cause. So both matter and form are causes of the compound in two ways, and each is a cause of the other in one way, though the way differs for each. They cause the compound in two ways that differ in how near they are and how they make it complete. Matter is a cause of the compound through its form only as matter for that form; form is a cause of the compound through its matter only as the form of that matter.

These rules apply only when we consider the compound as a compound and treat matter and form as different. This difference is still compatible with their being one in a certain way of existing. Do not confuse these two points.

#### Chapter 13 — How Causes Enter Our Questions and Proofs

God, whose existence depends on nothing else, has no cause. He is the source of all causes. Some things have some causes but not others: a kind of separate thing may have an agent and a goal, but no matter or form, since it is itself a complete form. Other things have all four causes: compounds, natural kinds, and all things that move or change, which natural science studies. The natural philosopher must understand all their causes and include them in proofs, especially their matter and form, to understand their effects fully.

Someone who studies mathematics does not need to know matter, because matter and motion do not enter into its questions. The mathematician does not need to know the source or goal of motion, or matter at all. Of the causes, only formal causes matter in mathematics.

When someone asks a question, the question may already include one of the causes. The answer must then use another cause, or the thing would be the cause of itself. If the question includes the agent cause, as in “Why did Zayd kill Amr?”, we can answer with the goal: “So he could take revenge.” Or we can answer with the motivating cause: “Because someone signaled him,” or “Because Amr had taken what was his.” This is the cause that prompts a choice and brings the action about. But it would not make sense to answer with the form or the matter. The form of the action is the act of fighting itself, and we are asking what caused it to exist. A thing cannot cause itself. The only exception is if the form is the highest goal, such as goodness itself. Then form can move the agent by its own meaning. Even so, it is not the direct cause that makes the action exist in matter. It makes the agent act, not the action as it exists in matter.

If the question includes the goal, as in “Why did he become healthy?”, we can answer with the agent cause: “Because he took medicine.” We may also give the material cause together with the agent: “Because his body had a strong constitution.” Matter alone is not enough.

A formal cause alone is rarely enough to end the question. If we answer, “Because his constitution became balanced,” the question may continue: “Why did that happen?” The answer may have to give matter or an agent.

If the question concerns matter and its readiness, as in “Why can the human body die?”, we may answer with the final cause: “So that, when the soul is complete, it can leave the body.” We may also answer with the material cause: “Because it is made of opposing elements.” But we cannot answer by naming an agent cause if the question is about a readiness that does not itself come from a form. An agent does not have to give matter any readiness it can have. If, however, “readiness” means full preparation for a form, an agent may provide it. For example, if someone asks why a mirror can reflect an image, we can answer that a craftsperson polished it. Its basic readiness belongs to matter; its final preparation may come from an agent.

Matter is never asked about without also assuming some form. The question is really about why the form exists in matter. If the question is about form, matter alone is not enough. We must add the readiness that comes from an agent. We can also answer with the goal or the agent.

If you set aside figurative answers and ask for the real one, the full answer includes every cause that the question has not already named. When you have given them all, and end with the true goal, the question stops. God is the one who grants success.

Let us end this part by discussing mixture and explaining it fully, as it follows from what we have said about the union of matter and form. We promised to give this account.

#### Chapter 14 — What Is Mixture, and How Does It Come to Be?

People usually discuss this question in natural science. I include it here for the reason I have given, and perhaps for another: the nature of a thing and the way it exists are suitable subjects for the divine sciences.

Mixture is a quality we can feel. It belongs to the first group of qualities we feel through touch, and stands between the four basic qualities: hot and cold, wet and dry. It is intermediate in such a way that, compared with intense heat, it is cold; compared with intense cold, it is hot; compared with dryness, it is wet; and compared with wetness, it is dry. In a mixed body, every part has the same mixture as every other part. The mixture differs only because it belongs to different parts of the body: most philosophers say it differs both in the underlying thing and in its quality, while in our view the difference is only in the thing that bears it.

As for what produces mixture and what receives it, the common view is that the elements are broken into small parts, mixed, and brought into contact. They act on one another, and the force of their qualities is weakened. The whole then acquires one even, intermediate quality, called mixture, while the different forms of the elements remain as they were.

They say this interaction can happen only if the elements touch. Otherwise, they would either have no spatial relation at all, whether touching or not, or they would have another spatial relation, such as facing one another at some distance. The first option is impossible, because we have shown that every physical action and reaction depends on a particular spatial relation between the agent and what it acts on. So only the second option remains.

If the relation between them is not contact, they must face one another at some distance. Suppose a heater warms another body across an intervening body. Either the middle body warms before the more distant one, or it does not. The second is impossible, because the effect of heat cannot reach a farther body before a nearer one. The first option proves our point: if the middle body is not in contact with the heater, there must be another body between them, and we can repeat the same reasoning until we reach the body that touches the heater. If the heater touches the middle body, then heat first reaches the body in contact with it, and reaches the next body through that one. So every natural action and reaction requires contact.

This is Avicenna’s argument in *The Healing*. Razi objects that it conflicts with what Avicenna says in *The Soul*. There he answers someone who denies that visible forms pass through the air without changing the air. Razi says it is not obvious that every body that acts must touch what it acts on. Although this happens with most bodies, it is not necessary. Some bodies may act on others without contact. No one can prove this impossible, or prove that the two bodies must have a special spatial relation. One body may affect another without touching it. We would only be surprised because we are used to bodies acting by contact, just as we would be surprised if all bodies were used to acting at a distance and then saw one act by contact. If it is not impossible in itself, if our proven account requires it, and if no proof rules it out, then it is possible.

Razi says that a naturally bright or lighted body can affect a body facing it that can receive its image, such as the eye, even if a colorless body lies between them. It affects the eye without changing the intervening body, because that body is transparent. He also says this in the section on the premises needed to understand halos and rainbows. Clearly he wants to show that one body can affect another without contact or touch. Yet in his chapter on mixture, Avicenna tries to prove that physical action and reaction require contact.

Razi is also puzzled by how the sun can heat the earth without heating the bodies near it, such as the heavenly spheres, and how it can light the earth without lighting the transparent bodies between them. How can an intelligent person insist that action and reaction require contact in the face of these problems? That is his argument.

I say that Razi is too quick to challenge Avicenna and other philosophers without examining the question carefully. He claims that Avicenna contradicts himself, but that has not been shown. What Avicenna denies in one place is that all action and reaction between bodies must involve contact. What he says in the other place is that certain kinds of action and reaction, such as heating and cooling, require it. A rule that applies to one kind of action does not have to apply to another.

The way things act and are acted on differs in different cases. An action can happen all at once, or gradually. The kind that Avicenna says requires contact is the gradual change that comes about through motion. The agent must directly reach and act on the thing that changes. Elements act on one another in this way, bringing about changes that complete them, such as the first qualities we feel through touch: heat, cold, wetness, and dryness. Avicenna does not say that actions such as light spreading or an image appearing require contact.

The truth is that there are natural effects, mathematical effects, and intellectual effects, just as there are natural, mathematical, and intellectual things. A natural action comes only from a natural agent that exists in time. It requires a change in matter that can be changed. A mathematical action does not involve a gradual change in matter, even though it may still relate to motion or nature. It requires only size and position, not motion or change in either the agent or the thing affected. An intellectual action requires no time or moment, and no motion or change. It requires only the agent itself and, if there is a recipient, the nature of what can receive it.

Examples of natural action include movement and change: heating, cooling, making something black or white, feeding, growing, and generating. Examples of mathematical action include lighting, illumination, reflections, facing relations, and shapes such as squares, cubes, and circles. These arise all at once and require some size and measure. Examples of divine or intellectual action include existence itself, giving existence, generosity, creation, and mercy. The same division applies to the things we perceive and the kinds of knowledge we have.

Even the actions of human senses fit these groups. Some are natural, such as touch and taste. Some are not natural, such as sight and imagination. Others are neither natural nor mathematical, such as judgment and understanding.

With this distinction in mind, we can say that every natural action by an immediate cause happens through contact and direct action between the agent and what it acts on. A natural agent is immersed in matter. Its own existence is the same as its existence in matter, spread through it, so that one part of the agent exists in each part of the matter. Just as matter exists as something extended and divisible, a material thing is also divided along with the matter in which it exists. This is true of natural forms such as fire and earth, and of their qualities and dependent features.

The animal form, simply as animal, is not like this, whether it belongs to a heavenly body or an earthly one. We have already explained that a thing’s power to act depends on its existence. What depends on something else in order to exist also depends on it in order to act.

If heat spread through a fire could warm another body without touching it, then its existence would not depend on being spread through the matter of the fire. But we have shown that it does. So what exists by spreading through matter can only act by spreading through the matter it affects. Such an agent cannot be separate in its being from the thing it affects. This is a useful principle, and it applies in many places.

If you examine the matter, you will see that different levels of action match different levels of being. How a thing acts and brings something into being depends on how it exists and how it relates to matter. So Avicenna was right: a body warmed by fire does not become warm unless it touches the fire, so that the heating power reaches it as the power of heat exists throughout the body and matter of the fire. The same applies to cooling, moistening, drying, and other natural actions.

Lighting, darkening, images, and shadows do not belong to this group, as we have said. So Avicenna’s two statements do not conflict. As for the sun’s warming the earth, drying wet soil, whitening a bleacher’s cloth, and darkening his face without touching them, I say that the sun’s first and direct effect is one thing: light. This effect happens all at once in bodies that can receive light. Once light exists in matter that can receive both light and heat, it can produce heat, because heat is related to light. The body then becomes warm. The heat dries dense matter and darkens it, or purifies and brightens fine matter, depending on how each kind of matter can receive it.

In short, a hot body such as fire does not make another thing hot except by contact and touch. But it does not follow that something can receive heat only by touching a source of heat. That is a separate claim, and it is not known to be impossible. A body may be affected by another without direct contact. Something that can be heated may receive heat without touching the heater, or from a source of heat that is not itself hot. The same applies to motion: not every moving thing must be moved by something that is itself moving. Something may be moved by a mover that is not moving, and not by direct contact, but by acting from a distance. The souls of the heavenly bodies, for example, move through intellectual causes by desire and support.

With this understood, a cold and moist body may gradually change in both qualities through causes inside or outside it, such as light from the stars and moving air. Its coldness may shift toward heat, and its moisture toward dryness, until it reaches a balanced quality between the four basic qualities of touch. It can then have mixture without any elements being mixed together. One form has one mixed quality; no small parts need to be brought together and touch. Anyone who rejects this must prove that such a change is impossible. So far, we have found no proof that elements need to be assembled, broken into small parts, and brought into contact for mixture to arise. There are strong arguments against the accepted account, and serious problems with its claims.

One well-known problem is that the theory seems to require three things: the agent that breaks down the elements, the elements that are broken down, and the breaking or mixing itself. The thing broken down cannot be the quality, because a single quality does not itself grow stronger or weaker; only the thing that bears it does. Nor can the agent be a quality, or else the thing broken down would become the agent after it was broken down—or would be both agent and patient at once. So the agent must be a form, and what is acted on must be matter.

This is difficult for two reasons. First, when hot water is mixed with cold water, the cold is overcome by the heat, but there is no separate heating form that is itself the source of heat. Second, even if form acts, it acts through a quality, just as nature moves through a tendency. A water-form, for example, heats through its heat. So we are back to the problem of making the quality both agent and patient.

You may object to the first problem. When fire forces heat into water, perhaps the water’s nature, made ready by an unusual state, is the real source of the heat, not the nature of fire. As we explained when discussing causes, something that prepares the way is not the true cause. But the second problem remains. Matter can be receptive to change, yet it becomes ready for a particular change only through a quality that belongs to it. Otherwise, every kind of matter could receive every form. So we still face the question of whether the thing broken down is the quality.

You may object that the agent and the thing acted on can exchange roles. But that does not answer the question of what first causes the change.

Here are further arguments. The philosophers in Aristotle’s school say that if a body of a particular kind is reduced to an extreme degree, it loses its specific form and keeps only bodily extension. If so, when a new form comes to be in it, the result is one body with one specific form. This shows that a mixture cannot arise by shrinking the elements while their different forms remain in place.

Another argument is that the elements naturally tend to separate and move toward their own places. Something else must hold them together. That must be one substantial form that is present in and governs the elements. It cannot be an accident, since an accident cannot govern a substance. Nor can it be a separate body with a different place, since such a body cannot act on matter without contact, as we have already shown. This is especially clear in natural bodies that are always preserved and ready for perfection. Their being held together is not by chance or force. A specific form must be prior to them in being, and it must preserve and complete their matter. It must be one form, or the natural kind would have no unity.

We have shown that one true nature cannot be made actual by two forms, and one form can give actuality to only one matter. Matter becomes one or many according to its form. Just as one matter cannot be made actual by two forms at once, one form cannot make two matters actual at once, except in order, one after another. So every natural kind has one form and one matter. It follows that the different elemental forms do not remain in a supposed mixture.

Once a natural compound has one form, everything it does as one kind must come from that one form. The first things these elemental kinds produce are tendencies and motions, and these belong to the first tangible qualities. The quality we call mixture must therefore come from the one form that makes the compound a kind, not from several different elemental forms. Actions that differ in their nature must come from different forms.

Another proof, from our earlier discussion, is this: when matter is ready for a more complete form, the new form produces all the actions and effects of the earlier form, along with further effects of its own, because it completes what was lacking in the earlier form. If a new form arises in a mixture, that form must be the source of the mixed quality. If the elemental forms also remained, they would be useless and do nothing. But nothing in nature is useless. So the elements do not remain in living compounds as they were.

Another argument concerns the many examples of the same kind of mixture. A mixed quality spreads through every part of the body, and its instances are the same in kind but many in number. What causes these many instances? It cannot be the nature of the quality or one of its features, since one nature and its feature are the same throughout. The material cause cannot explain it either. Prime matter is one in all cases. Nor can the elemental matter explain it. If the elements caused the many instances, they would do so either through their shared bodily nature or through their different forms. Their shared bodily nature is one, so it cannot explain different instances. And if their different forms caused different instances, then the instances of mixture would differ in kind, not merely in number. But they do not.

It would also follow that the number of mixed qualities must match the number of elemental forms, so there could be only four mixtures, just as there are four elemental forms. But philosophers say there are as many mixtures as there are combinations of small, touching parts. The fourth possible cause would be form. If the forms were the complete forms of natural kinds, each one would be single, and one form has one effect. If they were the elemental forms, each form would have its own effect. An effect relates to its agent cause by being produced by it, and to its matter by being received and held in it. But the mixed qualities do not differ in their nature, or by a feature that follows from their nature. Nor do they differ by form, because forms are what make kinds different in their nature. We have shown that form is the completion of a thing’s nature, like its final difference. If forms differ in being and nature, then the natures and things themselves differ too. But that is not the case here.

The fifth possibility is that a separate body causes the difference. But if its relation to every case is the same, it cannot explain why they differ. We would need another cause, and then another, without end. So the numbers of mixed qualities in things of the same nature do not differ in their full nature. They differ only because we divide them in thought or break them into parts, as we do with a single continuous thing. Such a thing can be divided into parts only by an act of measurement, whether its continuity is essential or incidental.

Therefore, the subject that bears a mixed quality must be one continuous body. The subject of one continuous quality is one continuous thing. If it is one continuous thing, it cannot contain forms that differ in kind. Philosophers already use this proof against Democritus: things of different kinds cannot make up one continuous body. So the mixed quality has only one source: one form.

Another argument follows from the same point. If the elemental forms remained in living compounds, then water and air would remain in molten iron, copper, gold, and other metals as they melt in fire and then return to their former state. But our experience tells us that water and air do not remain in melted metal. To say that they disappear when the metal melts and return when it hardens is even less plausible.

If the elements remained in natural compounds, the fiery part of a ruby, for example, would have two forms. One body would then be both fire and ruby, which is impossible. We have already discussed this argument.

#### Report

This is the same argument Avicenna raised against himself. We will discuss his answer and why we do not accept it later.

#### Chapter 15 — What Avicenna Says Here, and the Reply

You may be impressed by what Avicenna says on this subject. In a chapter of *The Healing*, he writes that some people in his time had come up with a strange view. They said that when simple elements mix and act on one another, they shed their forms and take on a single new form. They then have one matter and one form. Some said this new form stands between the elements’ own forms and prepares the mixture to receive the specific form of a natural compound. Others said it is itself a new specific form, while mixture is only a condition that comes with it, not a form. Avicenna then gives this argument: if that view were true, fire would act evenly on the whole compound. But when a piece of meat is distilled, it separates into a watery liquid that drips away and an earthy ash that does not. He says he tested a piece of meat in a distilling vessel and found that it separated in this way.

The parts of the compound must either be alike in their readiness to drip away or differ in that readiness. If they are alike, then either all of them drip away or none do. If they differ, then that difference must come either from their own nature or from something else. If it comes from their nature, the parts have different forms. If it comes from something else, then either the difference is necessary, which brings us back to different forms, or it is only an incidental feature. In the latter case it could disappear, and then there could be a compound whose parts were alike in their readiness to drip away. The whole piece of meat might then drip away, or all of it might turn to ash. The argument fails.[^V05-51]

Avicenna gives another argument. If the forms of the simple elements destroy one another, then either they are destroyed at the same time, even though each is destroyed because the other exists. In that case, both forms would exist while they were absent, which is impossible. Or one form is destroyed before the other, in which case what has already been destroyed cannot destroy what destroyed it.

The first argument is very weak. Someone could deny that all natural compounds have parts that differ in this way. The experiment applies only to compounds with a further, secondary mixture. We can also choose the last option in Avicenna’s argument: the parts differ in how they drip or settle because of features outside their own nature. This would mean that some bodies drip away entirely, while others settle entirely.

But many metals, such as gold, silver, and lead, have similar parts. This does not mean that meat must have similar parts. Meat is not alike throughout, whether we consider the animal as a whole or its limbs. The same is true of the organs called “simple.” The claim that every part of a simple organ must have the same name and definition as the whole is figurative. It means that each part appears to the senses to be like the whole.

The parts of meat, for example, that differ in how they respond to fire are not first-level elemental parts. They are more like secondary parts that differ in form or in incidental features. Our view—that the simple elements shed their forms in natural compounds—does not require every part in every compound to shed its form. Some compounds may have first-level parts with different qualities. Fire may melt some, harden others, and evaporate still others. If the argument is applied to a compound such as meat and bone, we can say its parts differ in nature and form. This does not show that elemental forms remain in it. If the example is gold or ruby, we can deny that its parts differ in how they respond to fire. The argument depends on a difference in how the parts respond, but neither experiment nor proof establishes that difference.

Avicenna’s final argument also fails, for two reasons. First, it does not apply to the view we defend. Our view is not the new doctrine he reports: that after the elements have been broken into small parts, mixed, brought into contact, and made to act on one another, they shed their forms and take on a new one. We do not say that natural forms must arise from matter made up of all four elements. A single element is enough. Under outside causes, it can change in its active or receptive quality until its form becomes one of the forms of natural things, such as minerals, plants, or animals.

Second, the same difficulty applies to the view Avicenna himself accepts. He says that when two things weaken one another, either both forms are destroyed together, or one is destroyed first. The first option seems to make each form destroy the other even as it is destroyed. The second seems to make the thing that has already been destroyed destroy what destroyed it. If the answer is that the weakening quality only prepares its bearer to lose its form, and need not remain once that loss occurs, the same answer could be given in the argument against us.

But this answer does not settle the matter. A preparatory cause cannot take the place of the cause that actually brings about an effect. If the cause is a form, then if one form causes another form to perish, the form that is destroyed would also be destroying the form that destroys it. If the cause is a strong quality, then the quality that breaks down another must itself be broken down by that quality. The same problem arises if one form is said to cause a change in another quality, since forms act through qualities. The direct causes of changes and motions are qualities and tendencies.

#### Chapter 16 — Further Reflections, and Replies to Objections to Our View

Avicenna adds: Let us ask what destroys the substantial forms of the elements when they come together. He then makes an argument much like the second one we have just discussed. In brief, when water and earth come together, each destroys the other’s form. This is impossible. If something outside them destroys their forms and gives them a new one, either they must still exist when that happens—in which case the question begins again—or their forms need not be destroyed at all. Simple elements could then produce natural things without any mixture. That is the rest of his argument.

This, too, is not an argument against us. It applies to the view Avicenna reports. Our view does not require natural things to come from elements that have first been brought together and made to act on one another. What is needed for new natural forms is mixture, not the physical mixing of parts. Mixture is a simple quality between the qualities we feel by touch. It prepares matter to receive one of the forms of natural things: mineral, plant, or animal. Before that form comes to be, the matter is held together by another form, either the form of a natural thing or the form of an element, along with an outside cause that changes the element’s quality from its extreme toward the middle. The mixture that results suits the natural thing being formed. The more complete and noble its form, the closer the mixture that prepares its matter comes to the true middle and balance.

There is no need for the parts of the matter to be physically mixed. This is not impossible or far-fetched; proof requires it.

Avicenna also raises an objection against himself. If the substances of the simple elements remain in mixtures, then fire would still be there, only cooler, and water would still be there, only warmer. The mixture would then gain an additional form on top of the forms of the elements. Since this new form spreads through every part of the mixture, each elemental part—for example, each part of the fire that has changed but not perished—could receive the form of meat. So each simple element could take on the form of these natural kinds merely by changing its qualities. No physical mixing or mixture would be needed. That is Avicenna’s argument.

This is the same argument we used earlier against the claim that elemental forms remain in natural things. It is a strong argument. Avicenna tries to answer it by saying that it applies equally to the common view and to the new view he reports. He says that, in the common view, the elements must come together for the compound’s form to arise, because they act on and affect one another. Their qualities first change; then they shed their forms and take on a new one. If their parts did not combine in this way, there would be no point in combining them. So when they combine, their qualities change by becoming stronger or weaker until they settle at the mixture. A new form then comes to be. What seems to happen after the mixture forms is possible only because the elements’ qualities have changed.

Avicenna says that if this change in quality happened to one element on its own, it would receive the new form by itself, though it could not receive it before. The change in quality happens only when its small parts act on and affect one another in specific arrangements. He also says that a new form cannot arise in matter unless other causes preserve it. This, he says, answers the objection for both views.

I say that all these words do not solve the problem for either view. They only show that the problem applies to both. The new view could answer that the original objection is this: if elemental forms remain in the compound, each part would be both an element and a ruby, for example. That objection applies only if the elemental forms remain. The objection Avicenna says applies to both views is a different one.

The new view could also answer that parts need to come together and combine at first, so that their qualities can change. Once those qualities have changed, each part is ready to shed its own form and take on a new form from the natural kinds. There is nothing impossible about a single part receiving that form after its extreme quality has been softened by change.

However one looks at it, our view faces no such problem. We do not need the simple elements to be broken into parts and mixed in order to produce the mixture that prepares matter for a new form.

Dawani’s answer to the problem was discussed and refuted earlier. Here is another reason his reply fails. He says that the complete form spreads through some parts of the mixture, which bear the mixed quality, but not through others. He compares this to a line or surface, which extends through some directions of its subject but not others.

That answer does not help. Every part of the mixture bears the quality produced by the elements’ interaction and change. The quality is the same in kind throughout the mixture, though it is present in many separate parts. The philosophers themselves say that the mixture in fire is of the same kind as the mixture in water. The mixture is not numerically one across the whole compound; it has many instances, one in each part. If the new form spreads wherever the mixture does, the result is the problem already described: one body would have two distinct specific forms.

The only way to avoid this problem is to accept that elemental forms do not remain once a natural form comes to be. This is true whether the mixture requires physical combining, as the common view holds, or does not, as we hold.

Someone might object that our view leaves each natural kind with only one matter and one form, so it is not a compound but simple. There is no contradiction here. We call these bodies compounds because a new form comes to be in matter that existed before it, or because their secondary mixture is mainly made up of elements, as in plants and animals. In any case, names alone do not decide what things are.

#### Chapter 17 — This View May Not Be New

Perhaps this view was held by the earliest philosophers. Avicenna reports the First Teacher’s words in *The Healing*, in the chapter before the one where he argues that the forms of the elements do not remain. The First Teacher says, “Mixtures exist potentially.” This seems to mean that the forms of mixtures do not actually exist, but exist only as possibilities.

Avicenna interprets the First Teacher as saying that their powers do not perish. By “powers,” he thinks he means the forms and natures that are the sources of the later forms and qualities. Once an obstacle is removed, they produce their own effects. Avicenna says some commentators mistakenly took “power” to mean readiness to receive a form.

I say the commentators understood him correctly. Their reading matches what the First Teacher says and what he seems to mean. Avicenna gives no proof that their interpretation is wrong. The fact that readiness exists together with the passing away of a form, while a thing’s actual power exists together with its form—and is its form—does not rule out their reading. A thing’s readiness does not exist on its own. It belongs to matter that has some other form, not the form it is ready to receive. So when the First Teacher says that mixtures exist potentially, he means they exist as possibilities in a thing that already has another form.

He need not mean that they themselves are actual forms with powers that remain. They can be possibilities within the matter that bears the form of something else. Having the possibility of something does not mean that thing is already present.

There is a difference between the power of a thing and the power to become something. The power of a thing exists together with it. The power to become something exists in matter that can receive it, when that matter has another form suited to it.

Now that we have seen the arguments against the elemental forms remaining in minerals, plants, and animals, especially in bodies whose powers spread throughout them, we can explain why. The elements, like the heavenly spheres, can in principle receive life and knowledge. But their low level of existence and their openness to conflict and decay prevent them from receiving the form of animal life. Their form spreads through their bodily matter. And bodily things, simply as bodies, are divisible, press against one another, and can be opposed. Whatever has an opposite within it is liable to be destroyed by that opposite. So what has an opposite within it cannot receive life.

Life is when a thing can perceive and move by a choice that comes from perception. Perception means that the form of something is present to the perceiver. But a body, simply as a body, cannot be present to anything—not even to itself. Its being present to itself would mean that it was also absent from itself. And its being united would go together with its being divided. Whatever can be undone cannot have a strong and complete kind of existence.

That is why a body cannot perceive itself or anything else. Whatever can perceive itself or something else must have a kind of existence beyond being a body alone. A thing that has even a limited share in life must have an existence less open to conflict and decay. The simple elements, because they oppose and break down one another, are far from receiving life.

When their extreme qualities are softened and their power to oppose and destroy is reduced, they gain a quality that lies between the extremes. This middle quality can, in a way, gather the four qualities into one and free itself from each of them. In this combined state, matter can receive another kind of existence and a kind of life, much as the heavenly spheres contain what lies below them in a way suited to their higher nature.

The more bodily matter moves away from the extreme ends of the four qualities and toward a shared middle, the more complete a form it can receive, and the fuller the life it can have. At the height of this balance, a body receives the greatest perfection open to bodies: the thinking life of a human being. This kind of life belongs to the stars and spheres, whose substances are not in conflict or decay, except through changes in how they are positioned in relation to one another.

The first form that elemental matter receives is a mineral form. It has some effects like life: it holds the body together and protects it from what would break it apart. Water, earth, air, and fire quickly spoil one another when they are near each other. Minerals can remain near other elements and natural things for a long or short time because their form protects them from being quickly changed by them. This is one effect of life and endurance.

As matter becomes more balanced and moves farther from the opposing extremes, it receives stronger effects of life. A plant form comes to it. Along with keeping its structure, the plant can replace what it loses and grow in size. Some plants also produce another of their own kind. Their reproductive power separates from their matter a portion ready to take on a form like theirs.

In short, plants have powers beyond those of minerals. Each plant differs in these powers in degree and in kind. Their development is gradual. Some grow without seeds and do not preserve their kind through fruit or seed. Water, earth, wind, and sunlight are enough for them to grow. They stand near the level of minerals. Other plants have greater powers, in an ordered progression. At a certain point they develop fruit and preserve their kind by producing a seed that can grow into another like them. This is an added power that completes them and distinguishes them from lower plants. It grows stronger until the difference between one level and the next is as great as the difference between the first and second levels.

Once you understand this, you can see that the forms that follow one another in existence differ in rank, nobility, completeness, and lack. The stronger and nobler a form is, the farther it is from the opposed elements. The higher and more complete it is, the more it leaves behind the shortcomings of lower forms. An animal does not need a separate plant soul: its animal soul can do, in a more complete way, what the plant soul did. In the same way, the plant soul preserves a plant’s body without needing another form to protect it from extreme heat, harmful cold, and other dangers.

Together, these points show that the opposed forms of the elements do not actually remain in any of the three kinds of natural beings: minerals, plants, or animals. If those forms remained, their own nature would prevent a more complete form from coming to be. Matter is to form as lack is to completion, and matter and form are joined as one being. The same is true when something incomplete becomes complete, as when a short line grows longer or a curved line expands into a half-circle or a full circle.

#### Chapter 18 — How Natural Beings Rise toward Perfection

Existence is one realm, and the whole world is like one great living being, with its parts connected. This connection is not spatial: their surfaces do not touch. It means that each level of perfection is next to the level above it in the order of existence. There is no empty gap between them where we could imagine an unfilled level of greater or lesser perfection. We do not allow such gaps. We will prove this from two principles: the possibility of a higher level and the possibility of a lower one. The first principle comes from the First Teacher; with God’s help, we have set out the second. We will discuss both later. They apply to kinds of things that come to be, though not to every individual.

You might object that levels of strength and weakness are endless, as levels of size are. If every level had to exist, then an endless number of levels would have to fit between any two fixed limits. That is impossible, especially if the levels are ordered. I answer that this would follow only if each level had to exist separately. But several levels may exist together in one being, without separation. A single person, for example, has different levels of existence: rational, animal, and plant powers, as well as natural powers. Each group has levels within it, yet all belong to one being, as we will explain in the study of the soul.

With this in mind, consider how the elemental world develops and rises through existence, from its lowest level until it comes near the worshipped God. Here, proof matches what we observe. The matter of the elements is as low and incomplete as possible; only complete nonexistence is lower. By itself, it exists only as potential, ready to receive forms. The first form it receives is extension in length, width, and depth. Matter cannot exist without bodily form, and bodily form cannot stand on its own without a specific form. The simplest specific forms are the forms of the elements. After receiving bodily form, matter receives the four elements, either all at one level, as is commonly held, or one after another, each arising through a change in the one that came before it.

If the elements arise one after another, the first must be the lowest, since this whole series rises from the least complete to the most complete. It is hard to say which of the four elements is lowest. After the simple elements, matter receives a mineral form, which is a higher level. A simple element is quickly changed by another when they are next to each other. If they differ in both the quality that acts and the quality that receives the action, the weaker one turns into the stronger one. Minerals are different. They may last for a long or short time because their form gathers the elements into one thing at a higher level. In a way, they are like one element balanced between the four opposed qualities. Mineral kinds then differ in how fully they exist and how well they can receive noble effects. Some are lower and close to the level of the elements, such as gypsum, lime, and sal ammoniac.

Other minerals are higher and closer to the level of plants, such as coral. Between these extremes are countless kinds that differ in how well they receive effects and bring about actions. Nature keeps rising through them, until matter becomes ready to receive a form that produces more effects than a mineral form: the plant form.

The plant can feed itself and grow in every direction. A mineral only preserves its matter. A plant draws in matter that suits it, takes it in, and gives it a form like its own. This allows the plant to grow as an individual. Some plants do more: they aim to continue their kind—not as the same individual, which is impossible for this kind of being, but as the same kind. Through their power to reproduce, they separate from their matter a portion ready to receive a form like their own.

Plants therefore have powers beyond those of minerals. Their levels differ in number and degree, and they rise through them gradually. Some grow without seeds and do not preserve their kind through fruit or seed. Water, soil, wind, and sunlight are enough for them to grow. These plants stand close to the level of minerals. Other plants have greater powers, in a clear order. At a certain point, they produce fruit and preserve their kind by producing seeds that grow into others like them. This is an added power that completes them and sets them apart from lower plants. It grows stronger until the difference between one level and the next is as great as the difference between the first and second.

This progression continues. Plants grow in rank until they reach the highest level of plant life, close to the animal world. Noble trees such as the olive, grapevine, and coconut palm approach this boundary. Their male and female powers are still mixed together, not distinct, so they can still advance. They have not yet reached the highest level of plant life that meets the animal world.

As matter advances farther, it enters the animal world. It can no longer increase as a plant. A small increase takes it out of plant life and makes it an animal. Its male and female powers become distinct; it can move, and it receives tools suited to its needs. The date palm reaches the edge of animal life through the ten special qualities described elsewhere. Only one step separates it from an animal: it is fixed to the ground and cannot move toward its food.

A saying of the Prophet points to this idea: “Honor the date palm, your aunt, for it was created from what remained of Adam’s clay.” Once a plant can pull free from its place, move toward its food, and use new tools to meet its needs, it has become an animal. These tools grow more numerous and refined across the animal world, just as plant powers did before them. Animals gain greater abilities until they can feel pleasure and pain. They enjoy what helps them and suffer when harm reaches them. Then God inspires them to recognize what they need: they seek what benefits them and avoid what harms them.

Some animals near the lower edge of animal life do not mate or produce and raise their young. Worms, flies, and other low forms of insect life arise in other ways. Animal abilities then grow, as plant abilities did. A power of anger also appears. It drives an animal to defend itself against harm. Its weapons match its strength: a strong power of anger is given strong weapons, a weaker one lesser weapons, and an animal with little strength or courage receives no weapon at all. Instead, it is given ways to escape, such as speed, agility, flight, or tricks, as in deer, fish, birds, rabbits, and foxes.

You can see this for yourself in the animals that have horns like spears; something used to shoot or throw, like arrows; teeth and claws like knives and daggers; or hooves like clubs and axes. Animals too weak to use weapons, or too timid to fight, are given ways to escape. Across wild animals and birds, this wisdom is clear.

Human beings receive none of these weapons. Instead, they are guided to make and use them. All these things are placed at their service. We will discuss this later. Let us return to the levels of animal life. Animals that find mates, seek offspring, protect them, build nests or dens for them, and feed them—either with milk or by bringing food—are more complete than those that have none of these abilities.

These powers continue to grow until animals reach the edge of human life. At that point, they can be trained. Training gives them a skill that sets them apart from other animals. The skill grows stronger in noble animals such as a trained horse, a falcon taught to hunt, or a dog taught to understand. At the highest level, an animal can imitate human beings without being taught, as monkeys do. Some are so quick to learn that they need only see a person do something once before they copy it.

This is the highest level of animal life. A small increase beyond it takes an animal out of that level and into the human world, where it can reason, distinguish, speak, and use tools. When a human being reaches this level, the person turns toward knowledge and longs to learn. God grants new powers, habits, and gifts that allow further growth, just as in the earlier levels.

At the lower edge of human life are people said to live at the far ends of the inhabited world, in the north and south—for example, the farthest Turks said to live in the lands of Gog and Magog, and the farthest Zanj and similar peoples. The author says they differ from monkeys by only a small degree. Human understanding and discernment then increase until people reach the middle regions, where they are quick-witted and ready to acquire virtues.

For most philosophers, nature’s work in the visible world ends at this point. In our view, the work of nature and the material world ends at the animal level. From there, the soul begins its work, and imaginal forms—free of this material world of change and decay—begin to develop. The soul can then gain virtues beyond those of animals and, through choice and effort, acquire forms of reason. In time, it reaches the highest world and the higher angels.

This is the highest level of a human being as a human being. Here, all beings come together: the first joins the last, and the last joins the first. This is called the Circle of Existence. Its first half is the arc of descent, and its second half the arc of ascent. A circle is a single line that starts from a point and, through movement, returns to that same point.

The Circle of Existence is a single, connected whole. Its many levels are made one. It gives clear proof that its maker and source is one, and that He is wise, powerful, generous, and giving. If the realm of existence is one, its builder must also be one. “God surrounds them all.” When you understand this much, you will see the state you were created to reach. You will see the higher level linked to your own, moving you from one level to the next until you rise, stage by stage, toward the life that lasts.

You will gain true belief in the life beyond this one and see what your eyes cannot see. You will move on to noble sciences whose beginnings you have already learned, and to the tools that prepare the mind for them. These include sharpening the understanding, strengthening reason, and learning about the kinds of things and their natures. From there, you will move to the divine sciences. Then you will be ready for God’s gifts. His light and peace will come to you, and the restlessness of nature and its pursuit of bodily aims will grow quiet.

You will see the levels of existence and understand the stages through which you have risen. Each level depends on what came before it. When nature prepares the next level by means of those before it, the next level in turn gives something back to those earlier levels. A human being reaches full perfection only after gaining all the levels that came before. When a person is complete and has reached the highest human level, the light of the highest, divine realm shines on him. He may become a wise person who receives insight into intellectual truths and heavenly judgments, or a prophet who receives revelation through direct vision, because of the strength of his inner life. Then he becomes a link between the higher world and the lower world.

The first force behind these human changes and advances is longing and choice. A desire to gain knowledge may lead a person along a straight path, toward full happiness. But it may also turn a person away from the right path for many reasons, which would take too long to explain.
