Just as bodily nature can draw someone toward what harms the body—for example, a craving for clay or something similar—so the human soul can long for knowledge that does not complete it or lead it toward happiness, but instead distracts it and keeps it from reaching its full potential.

In such cases, the soul needs care from a spiritual doctor: a guiding prophet, a wise guide, or a teacher. In the first case, the body needs a physical doctor to guide it to the right treatment. People therefore have a great need for prophets, teachers, and guides. It is rare to find a person whose own powers are so outstanding that, without guidance, they can lead him to true happiness. Such a person appears only after a long time.

When such a person does appear, it is by God’s gift, working through lower causes, chosen actions, and earned progress. The oil of his soul is like a natural lamp that could almost shine even before the flame of teaching, reflection, and practice touches it. He is God’s light in the darkness of the earth and one of His great signs. As God says, “A light and a clear Book have come to you from God.” Later, God willing, we will prove this point. Here, our aim is to show that all existence, from top to bottom and bottom to top, forms one connected chain. Each part is linked to and joined with the others. Despite their many separate forms, all things are one—not as bodies are joined when their edges and surfaces touch. The whole world is one living being, more like one soul, whose active powers—intellects, souls, and other things—are like the powers of a single soul. Those who truly understand know that the soul’s powers form a many-sided unity. They are not separate beings that serve as tools of the soul, as some have thought; nor are they one being scattered across many places and effects, as others have thought. We will explain this in the study of the soul.

You should know that every natural body has matter and form, and that matter itself has matter, until we reach matter that is pure potential: it has no actuality of its own, but exists only through something else. Its form, too, has another form, until we reach pure form: wholly actual, with no potentiality and no lack. As we said earlier, the human being is the last being within the natural world.

A human being gathers within himself the truths of the higher and lower worlds. He also bears the truths of God’s names and attributes, which make him fit for his greater role as God’s representative in the greater world, beyond his lesser role in the natural world. In this high station, all the angels bow to him at God’s command. If the highest company of angels bows to him, how much more the lower world? This is why God says that He has placed everything in the heavens and earth at your service, and that He created the human being in the best form. He praises Himself for creating the human being: “Blessed is God, the best of creators.”

By God’s help, the second part of the second journey of *The Four Journeys* is now complete.

## Notes

[^V05-1]: A broader kind needs a defining feature because it is indeterminate. A species needs one because it shares its broader kind with other species and must be distinguished from them. A defining feature, however, is already definite in itself and distinct from the other defining features and species of its broader kind, so it does not need another defining feature. It would need one only if its broader kind were also a broader kind in relation to it, which is not so: in relation to a defining feature, a broader kind is only a general feature. And if the broader kind were a broader kind of the defining feature…

[^V05-2]: The argument only implies that body is not connected in itself by geometrical dimensions, nor separated in itself by the corresponding separations. It does not imply that body lacks continuity or separation altogether. The latter would be the problem, not the former. So the objection does not follow from what was established. Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V05-3]: Natural science studies the states of body insofar as it changes. Essences and the ways things exist are not intrinsic features of body considered as something that changes; they belong to existence itself and must therefore be studied in the higher science, divine science in the broad sense. — Isma‘il

[^V05-4]: Things with causes are known only through their causes. Since God is the cause of all things, nothing can be known except through knowledge of Him. Knowledge of God therefore comes before knowledge of things, just as His existence comes before their existence. — Isma‘il

[^V05-5]: Debate relies on accepted or widely held beliefs, so it gives only probable knowledge, not proof of the truth. But it can compel an opponent who resists the truth. By removing that opposition, debate can still serve as a way of establishing the truth. Think this through. — Isma‘il

[^V05-6]: Good advice shares in this benefit. God says: “Call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good counsel, and argue with them in the most gracious way.” Take note. — Isma‘il

[^V05-7]: The triangle described here would rule out a square root. If each of its two sides were made of five units, its remaining side—the hypotenuse—would be the square root of fifty units, by the theorem of the Bride’s Figure. That would require an indivisible unit to be divided. Understand this. — Isma‘il

[^V05-8]: The rule being discussed is that half the shadow of a thing is the shadow of half the thing. The reason to reject it as a universal rule is that it would mean every thing that casts a shadow has a half, which would itself imply that an indivisible part can be divided. The rule would then depend on the very division it is meant to prove. Think carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V05-9]: If a thing has no units other than the units that make up its parts, then the properties of the whole are also the properties of those units. The properties of ten people, for example, are the properties of the individual people who make up the ten. This makes the argument stronger: if a body is made of indivisible parts, its continuity exists only in what we sense, not in reality—unless that continuity has some real effect. Think this through. — Isma‘il

[^V05-10]: On closer inspection, this example can be taken to show both a cause without its effect and an effect without its cause. The bucket’s movement is an effect, and the movement of the hook is one part of its complete cause. If the slower hook pauses, the bucket would continue moving without its complete cause, since removing one part removes the whole cause. But if the absence of a cause brings about the absence of its effect, then the hook’s pause causes the bucket to stop: the cause is present without its effect. That is why the author first speaks of a cause without its effect, and later of an effect without its cause. Understand this. — Isma‘il

[^V05-11]: The first case seems to show a cause without its effect; the second, an effect without its cause. The author may have reversed them by mistake. Take note. — Isma‘il

[^V05-12]: Some philosophers say that a body has boundaries only because it ends and has no further extension. They consider boundaries to be non-existent, since the body’s ending is a kind of non-existence. And since the place where a boundary exists is, in that respect, non-existent, the boundary itself must also be non-existent. — Isma‘il

[^V05-13]: If bodies and their parts can be joined or separated according to their nature from the beginning, then some cause must account for why bodies are separate and many while their parts are joined and one. The agent’s relation to both is the same, so there must be something that makes one state occur rather than the other. The only such cause can be matter, which is ready to receive different forms. Each of these bodies must therefore be made from matter and form; it cannot be the first matter, as Democritus thought.

    The argument we have given is enough to establish matter. The idea also follows from two principles already proved: individuals of one kind are distinguished by features that come from matter, and a kind without matter exists as only one individual. Further, reason sees no difference between the first state of a thing and a later state when it comes to whether its nature allows it to be separated or affected. If body is not by nature opposed to separation and change when it first exists, it does not become opposed to them later. If it refuses separation in a later state, this is because something has been added to it; then the refusal is due to that added feature, not to body’s nature itself. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-14]: Aristotle’s followers and most theologians say that shared matter is a substance that is not itself a body. The Illuminationist philosophers, Democritus and his followers, and al-Nazzam say that shared matter is a body. On the first view, a body is made of substance together with accidents; on the second, it is made of body together with something else. Think carefully about the difference. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-15]: This is the material intellect, also called the “hylic intellect.” It is the matter of the second, intellectual stage of existence, just as prime matter is the matter of the first, bodily stage. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-16]: I say: this would require a sixth kind of substance beyond the five generally recognized. This receptive substance cannot be an intellect, because intellect is fully actual whereas this substance is pure potential. For the same reason it cannot be a form; nor is it prime matter or body, as is clear. If it is not the soul, it must therefore be a sixth kind, contrary to the author's own division of substance into its kinds. And if this substance is distinct from the soul, then it is not the soul that receives intellectual forms, gains perfection through them, and rises toward them. It would instead be this receptive substance that does so. But the consequence is plainly false, as the author himself must agree.

    The right view is that this receptive substance is the soul. The claim that one thing cannot be both actual and potential, or form for one thing and matter for another, holds within one and the same level of existence. It does not rule out such differences between levels. The proof for prime matter depends on their belonging to one and the same level—the level of bodily things that arise and perish. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-17]: “In reality” here means in the actual nature of things. This applies whether the people in question call the basic parts accidents, as Dirar and al-Najjar do, or do not call them accidents, as al-Nazzam does. For him, a body is made of colors, tastes, smells, and other sensible qualities, but he treats all of them as substances, as explained above. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-18]: The alternatives depend on whether separation is something positive—the coming into being of two identities—or something negative—the loss of a continuous identity in something that could be continuous. In the first case, separation and continuity are opposites because they require a subject. In the second, they are opposites as absence and possession: separation is the absence of continuity in something capable of it. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-19]: If “continuity” here means the feature that divides quantity—the state of being divisible into parts that share boundaries—then geometric body is continuous in itself, while natural body is continuous only through it. This does not refute the claim, since natural body is continuous in itself in another sense: its substantial form is what lets us imagine three dimensions crossing at right angles. Keep the two meanings distinct. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-20]: That is, natural body. The three dimensions taken together make up geometric body. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-21]: It is clear that several ideas may be drawn from one way of existing only when they do not contradict one another. We cannot draw opposed meanings, such as blackness and whiteness, or what must exist in itself and what is only possible, from one and the same existence. The present case belongs to this second kind. Do not overlook it. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-22]: This is joined to the words “drawn from.” In other words, readiness and potentiality cannot be among the features belonging to a form just because it is a form. That reinforces the preceding statement. On this reading, the two-part pronoun in the Arabic should more naturally have been singular, agreeing with the word “that which,” or feminine, agreeing with its meaning.

    Another reading joins it to “from the aspect of form and completeness.” The two-part pronoun then refers to form and completeness together, and “formal meaning” means what is associated with form, as something that necessarily has it. The statement would then mean that potentiality and readiness cannot be drawn from what has form and completeness precisely because it has both. This is like the preceding examples of what necessarily has whiteness, or of what necessarily exists.

    On the second reading the sentence adds a new point, rather than merely confirming the earlier one. But it requires strained wording: a noun should have been used where the pronoun stands. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-23]: Rather, the answer is that in intellects the receptive aspect is what they are, while the active aspect is their existence. But their receiving does not depend on a capacity that was present before they existed. So in reality their receiving is not a separate aspect from their acting. Understand this. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-24]: This is true if a cause brings about a dependence that was not there before. But there is no problem if the cause only makes us describe something as dependent through another thing. In that case, what truly depends is the intermediate thing, not the thing linked to it. For example, a general nature may seem to depend on a particular subject because of a feature of that individual. There is no difference between a particular subject and a general subject if the cause only explains how the dependence is described. But if the cause actually brings a new dependence into existence, then that is impossible in either case. There is a real difference between these two accounts: one merely attributes dependence to something through another; the other claims to produce dependence where it did not exist. If, after all particular features are set aside, the intervening cause must be of the second kind, the author’s distinction would hold. Perhaps this is what he means. Consider it carefully. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-25]: The first route is the one we began with, through potentiality and actuality. The second is based on the way the parts of an extended body share the same nature, features, and necessary properties. There is no doubt about this second route. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-26]: We have already seen criticism of the argument from matter, and the same reply applies to Avicenna’s claim that matter’s actuality is the actuality of its potentiality. That does not show that matter is composite. This argument is not enough for the present point unless one proves that matter, when stripped of all form, is fully actual and complete—not merely actual as potentiality and indeterminate readiness. Proving that is extremely difficult. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-27]: The cause of a thing must exist alongside it. But a thing’s readiness to receive something does not exist alongside the thing received. If what is ready for the thing produced it, then it would have to produce it without first being ready for it. Otherwise it would not exist alongside its effect and so would not be its cause. But if it produced the thing without being ready for it, then it was not ready for it at all. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-28]: These infinitely many matters would have to exist together. Each earlier matter must receive the matter that follows it, and the whole series must receive the final matter under discussion. A receiver must exist alongside what it receives. The infinite series would therefore have to exist all at once, which is impossible. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-29]: I say: bodily form is a particular kind of nature. Its unity is general and vague in time, and it also has a particular unity beyond time. It is the form’s particular, timeless unity that gives existence to matter—not its general unity in time. In that general sense, form exists only through its particular instances, which begin in time and are preceded by matter and duration. How could form in that sense be the source of matter? The form’s role in giving matter existence must therefore belong to its timeless existence and unity, which is particular and numerically one. So a universal unity is not being treated as the cause of a numerically singular effect. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-30]: Meaning: if someone argues, to show why a body must occupy a place and have a position, that it is impossible for a body by itself to be without place and position, we reply: any impossibility here comes from the impossible consequence—that all bodies would be confined to a single body. But that consequence follows just as much from saying that a body by itself has no place or position as it does from saying that a species, such as human or tree, by itself has no place or position. For if humanity alone, for example, had no place or position, all bodies would be confined to one: the human being just mentioned. Think this over; there is more going on here. — Ismail.

[^V05-31]: The accidents of a sword are effects that follow from its form. A sword has what iron has, and more. The additional features must come from a further principle that completes it. So a change in accidents reveals a change in the underlying substance. — Ismail.

[^V05-32]: I say: the reason is that blackness and whiteness, like other colors, keep the same name and definition in every part. A half-sphere, by contrast, is not a sphere, and neither are its other parts. This is because blackness and similar qualities cling closely to body and exist in it as body, which is divisible in itself. Shape is not as closely tied to body. A body simply as body is not its subject; it must be considered in some further way, such as being enclosed by one or more boundaries. — Ismail.

[^V05-33]: Nature is lower in rank than the soul because it descends from it. Nature is therefore less clear in its existence and more clouded. — Ismail.

[^V05-34]: Existence flowing from what is higher to what is lower is the same as the lower thing’s love and longing for what is higher: it perceives the higher thing’s beauty. That is why it has been said, “If the lower did not love the higher, it would disappear.” Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-35]: Otherwise, if the image were within God, He would have to be separate from the image within Him and would therefore be a form. We have already shown that before creation He is simply Himself. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-36]: This argument is sound for a whole that has no existence apart from the existence of its individuals, as in this case: the whole series of things that come to be exists only through those individual things. But the argument fails for a whole whose existence is distinct from the existence of its individuals. Understand this distinction. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-37]: This argument is sound for a whole that has no existence apart from the existence of its individuals, as in this case: the whole series of things that come to be exists only through those individual things. But the argument fails for a whole whose existence is distinct from the existence of its individuals. Understand this distinction. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-38]: By “taking the definitions of things,” he means drawing them from the separate intellectual forms, which are the realities of things, while sensible things are only their reflections and images. A reflection of a thing is not the thing itself: the reflection of a human being is not a human being. Here “other than” means “distinct from,” and what is added to sensible natures means their qualities and states. Another possible reading is that definitions are taken without regard to whether the things are sensible or intellectual: each essence has two instances, one sensible, changing, and perishable, the other intelligible, enduring, and timeless. Reflect on this. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-39]: “The whole” means intellect, since what is below it exists in a nobler way within it. “Its judgment” means the soul, since the soul is the source of nature’s action in coming to be and existing. Do not overlook this. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-40]: On the downward path of existence, nature comes before prime matter, its motion, and the measure of its motion. So it does not fall under time. — Ismail.

[^V05-41]: Nature gradually brings something into existence, step by step, producing one thing after another. “Things that share in a kind” means the individuals of one kind. They exist through the conditions of readiness produced by the motion through which time is measured. Understand this. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-42]: Their existence depends only on active causes; readiness or potentiality has no role in bringing them into existence. Consider this. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-43]: The “coming to be” at the start of this discussion means coming to be in time, since the world consists of bodies and bodily things, which come to be in this way. But creation by the complete Creator has no beginning, just as the Creator Himself has no beginning: creation exists through His existence and lasts through His permanence. Understand this. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-44]: The First Emanator is the sacred outpouring, also called “Be,” the all-embracing mercy, the divine will, the Real through whom things are created, the Breath of the Merciful, the Cloud, or the Water over which the Throne was established. Perhaps this philosopher’s “water, air, fire, and earth” refer to different aspects of that first outpouring. Every created thing must have, in its own way and according to its level of existence, a ripening heat, a solidifying cold, a shaping moisture, and a preserving dryness. The First Emanator, in its unity, contains these meanings, and so may be called the elements. It is also said that created things need creation, provision, life, and death. For this reason, a report says that the Throne is carried by four pillars: Israfil, Michael, Gabriel, and Azrael. This hint should be enough for a thoughtful reader to understand the intended meaning. Think carefully about it. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-45]: “Universal nature,” in this passage, means the complete human being, who contains the full range of meanings. It is therefore a universal nature and a separate intellectual form. This is a subtle but elegant argument for divine forms—the intellectual forms of natural kinds—like those Plato, the divine philosopher, taught in agreement with his teacher Socrates, as we explained earlier. Understand this point. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-46]: Matter is different: an individual remains the same even when its matter changes. This shows that matter does not enter into the definition of the individual. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-47]: Avicenna says: “If bodies changed through coming-to-be and passing away beyond their bodily form, so that the bodily form in water, for example, remained the same when it turned into air, then bodies would have a shared formal principle of this kind, followed by further formal principles specific to each body.” — The author.

[^V05-48]: Then the parts would be joined together. If the compound has no actual plurality, its combination is a union into one being. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-49]: Their remaining in the compound would not conflict with the union of matter and form in a ruby. I say that this may be true, but it does not prove the claim that if the elemental forms remained in the ruby, one thing would be both fire and ruby, since the ruby’s form spreads through its subject, as the author said earlier. The weakness of this proof is clear. Consider it carefully. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-50]: Although necessary features belong to the things that require them, those features are not separate from those things. So what requires them is not their material cause. — Isma‘il.

[^V05-51]: Perhaps something can cease to be what it is in itself while still remaining possible in reality. What is possible in itself is not always possible in the real world. Think carefully. — Ismail.

    Ismail adds a note about the manuscript source: These marginal notes were copied into the book of the late Hajj Mirza Habib Hashemi Khoei, author of *Sharh Nahj al-Balagha*. At the end of that handwritten book, the following words were copied exactly:

    “This is the end of the notes I found by our teacher’s teacher, Muhammad Ismail—may God raise his rank—in his book, both in his own hand and in other hands. They were with our teacher al-Rashti in natural philosophy—may God prolong his life—not with our teacher in divine philosophy, al-Tabrizi—may his honor increase and may God grant him success. He copied them while sitting in his home over several days. He finished on Saturday, the tenth of Jumada al-Thani, in the year 1248 after the Hijra.

    May God guide us to His path, forgive us, our parents, our teachers, and all those whose rights we owe, and forgive all believing men and women, living and dead, for the sake of Muhammad and his pure family. Place us beneath the banner of their rule, by their right, O Answerer.

    Our aim is to leave a mark that remains after us,
    for I do not see the world lasting forever. (1248)”
